# Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah (Chapter 56) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:1

> When the Inevitable Event takes place,

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sūrah Named by What It Refuses to Describe**

The first word after the time-conjunction is al-wāqiʿah — "the Event that falls" — and the sūrah will go almost its entire length without adding a definition. The name of the chapter is simply its opening word, which is also the only noun it ever needs: the Event, the one the listeners already know. The root is waqaʿa, to fall or come down, and in Arabic the falling of a thing is the idiom of its certainty — decrees fall, judgments fall, the pen falls upon the page — so "the Inevitable" is not an addition but the word's own weight: when what must happen happens. A reader arriving from the sūrah of the Merciful, which ends "Blessed be the Name of your Lord," lands here on a sentence whose subject is the end of everything the previous chapter enumerated, its gifts, its two gardens, and all of them included.

**The Grammar of a Promise Long Suspended**

Arabic's idhā — "when" — opens a clause and waits; for six verses this sūrah withholds the verb that answers it, piling one descriptive scene atop another (the denial at v2, the debasing at 3, the shaking earth at 4, the pulverized mountains at 5, the dust at 6) before the sentence finally lands on its "you" — "you will all be divided into three groups" (7). The suspension is not a defect of arrangement; it is the rhetoric. The Hour is presented as an avalanche of settings with no address yet, so that when the second person plural arrives, the reader discovers he has been falling all along inside the grammar. Some reciters and exegetes pause after verse 1 as if the terror were too great to complete — "When the Event comes to pass." Stop. The sentence is left hanging on purpose, and both ways of reading it agree on the effect: the Event is the frame, and everything else is contents.

**Why Not "the Day": the Choice of a Noun**

The sūrah could have opened with yawm, day — its competitor chapters do — but al-wāqiʿah names not a date but an occurrence: a happening that lands. "Day" measures; "the falling one" compels. The Day's verdict, as this sūrah will say in its own later scenes, is not a review but an arrival. A falling thing has no negotiable terms; it is not scheduled around anyone's unfinished business. This is also why translators, including the one this commentary follows, render the adjective as a substantive, "the Inevitable": in English as in Arabic the participle functions as a name, and names do not take qualifiers. The Event is not one event among others; the article on wāqiʿah is the article of the unique.

**One Event, Two Registers**

Classical commentators habitually read the opening on two registers at once: the cosmic termination of all things, and the sudden, private crisis in which a person's real state is decided — the spiritual "falling" of realization, when a soul is confronted mid-life by what it had deferred. The sūrah itself supplies license for the second register at its end, where the very scene of verse 1 is replayed in miniature at a single deathbed (vv. 83–87), the soul at the throat and the bystanders watching, unable to return it. The Hour and the hour, the universal Event and each one's own, are the same wāqiʿah seen from the plain and from the bedside. The opening verse does not choose, and the refusal to choose is the point of the recitation: whoever hears "when it falls" is being told that it has already begun to fall in him.

**What This Sūrah Was Recited For**

The chapter's afterlife in Muslim practice grew out of a famous report: "Whoever recites Sūrat al-Wāqiʿah every night will never be afflicted by want." It is transmitted from Ibn Masʿūd by al-Bayhaqī in Shuʿab al-Īmān, and al-Albānī graded it weak in his chain (Silsilat al-Aḥādīth al-Ḍaʿīfah 289), which is worth stating plainly since the hadith is often quoted without its grading. Weakness in the chain did not stop the practice: Ibn Masʿūd is reported to have ordered the daughters of his own household to recite it every night. The devotional logic, as the great Yemeni commentator Ibn ʿAjībah explains it, is not amuletic — a soul that rehearses the Inevitable each night has been trained in the only economy this sūrah recognizes, and want loses its sting for one who has just finished verses 15–60 on what wealth actually buys.

**The "When" of the Hour: an Opening in a Family of Openings**

Idhā al-sāʿiqatu — "when" thundering at the start of a chapter — is a recognized genre in the late Makkī sūrahs, and verse 1 places al-Wāqiʿah squarely inside it: the Overwhelming (88), the Inevitable (69), the Cloud of Smoke (44), the Quaking (99), the Cleaving, the Scattering, the Blow (82, 99, 101) — a corridor of sūrahs that all begin by setting the clock to "when," as though the Book wanted the audience to hear the Hour not as doctrine announced but as weather arriving. Al-Wāqiʿah's particular move is what it declines to do after the conjunction: the opening clauses of the neighbors often pile imagery at once, while this sūrah lets "when it falls" stand for a beat before anything else happens, the pause the recitation is meant to hold. The genre is also a dating: the "when" openings belong to the earliest preaching, and the sūrah's style — short clauses, the three-way division, the refrain of the Day's finality — confirms what the commentators say, that this is a chapter of Makkah, where the resurrection was the whole argument. Reading verse 1 inside its family, then, is not decoration; it is hearing the one word "when" as the Qur'an's own way of saying that the future perfect tense is the only tense a created being should keep for the Hour.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:2

> then no one can deny it has come.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day the Tongue Is Fired**

The next clause inverts everything the word "deny" had meant in the sūrah's hearers' lives. In this world, denial is an act — a thing people do repeatedly, at price, with effort, on schedule; the Quraysh had a standing industry of it, the "woe to the deniers" of verse 1 of the sūrah that opens this juz. Here the act is abolished not by compulsion but by evidence: when the Event falls, there is no one who can say it has not, because the Event's whole nature is to be the end of argument about it. The Arabic of the classical recensions is a bare existential — "no denial" — the total negation of the verbal noun, which denies even the category. Not "no one denies," which would describe a mood; "denial is not," which describes a world with one fewer option, like a country where the currency of the old reign has stopped being legal tender.

**What Denial Was Always About**

Ask what the sūrah's audience actually denied, and the answer in this chapter's own mouth is not a creed but a date. The mocking question arrives later in the same sūrah in the first person plural of the scoffers: "When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected?" (56:47). Denial of the Event was never a denial that something enormous could happen; it was the claim that it would not happen to me, not now, not in this body, not on their schedule. Verse 2 answers the only form the denial ever took: kadhdhabat — the lie rendered inert, its verb itself made passive and past, a lie that has had its past tense. The moment it falls, the question "when?" is answered so completely that no tense remains for doubt to work in.

**"It Has Come": the Verb Inside the Noun**

The clause's economy is worth hearing precisely: the Event is denied "its coming" — wāqiʿatihā, the verbal noun repeating the sūrah's own name. In one small word, verse 2 welds the denial to the thing denied: what the liars said would not fall is the falling itself, and their lie is about the very quality that makes the Event the Event. Repetition is the sūrah's opening device on a larger canvas too (the three groups at 7, then 8–10, then 27, 41), so the doubling of w-q-ʿ in the first two verses is a demonstration, not a flourish. What is wāqiʿ, falling, does not require the audience's consent to be described; it requires only their sentence to make them part of its sentence.

**A Verse Read as a Full Stop or as a Hinge**

Early reciters debated where the sense of verse 2 comes to rest. Taken with what precedes, it yields "when it falls, no denial then" — the two clauses as one movement, terror and its consequence. Taken with what follows, it opens a pair: "none shall deny — [it is] debasing some, exalting others," and the Event is then described in the act of redistribution. Both readings are attested and neither damages the sense; their coexistence is the verse's real gift to the reader, because each one repairs a different misreading. The first blocks anyone who thought the Event was one horror among others — no, here is what it does to the one who denied it. The second blocks anyone who thought the Event was mere annihilation — no, what follows the silence of denial is not nothing, it is demotion and promotion.

**The Sūrah's Law of Incompatibles**

Notice what has not yet appeared and will not appear in this pair of verses: God. The Event and the denial of it — two clauses without a subject who acts, in a chapter that will say, at verse 57, "It is We Who created you." The opening refuses the reader the comfortable distance of theology: before anything is explained, the frame closes. The sūrah's method is to arrive like its own subject, wāqiʿ; you are addressed inside the Event before you are told about the Sender of it. Denial being henceforth impossible, what remains — the whole rest of the sūrah — is to hear into which of the three groups the impossibility assigns you, a hearing the next two verses will begin by quietly redistributing rank before naming a single class.

**Two Verbs for No: the liar's and the denier's**

Arabic keeps two registers for refusing truth, and verse 2 is about the heavier one. Inkār and jaḥd are denial — a man refuses what he knows or pretends not to see; the Qur'an's most frequent word for it, takdhīb, is making a liar of someone, answering a claim of truth with the counter-claim "this is false." The Event's arrival silences the second, because takdhīb requires a proposition to reject, and at the end there are no propositions — the thing itself stands. That is why the verb here is the Form II passive (kudhdhibat): the lies that were being told about the Hour stand convicted at once, all of them, in a single unattributed tense. The sūrah that opens thus answers the question the whole chapter will keep reopening: what is at stake in the denial of resurrection is not a date on the calendar but a verdict on God's veracity — the day when "it has come" makes takdhīb impossible is the day the liar's verb finally loses its subject. The bracketed, wordless clause at the end of the next verse — debasing, elevating — will show what the impossibility actually feels like from each side of it.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:3

> It will debase ˹some˺ and elevate ˹others˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Participles Without Objects**

The Arabic of this verse is three words long and names its victims to no one: khāfiḍatun, rāfiʿatun — a debasing, an elevating. The objects are simply absent, and that absence is the whole sermon. An event that debases and elevates with no stated targets is the final universalization of a process this world runs in partials: here the powerful lift their own and press down rivals, and the lifting is always local, always reversible. The two indefinite active participles at verse 3, with the verbs' subjects supplied only by "the Event" of verse 1, state the reversal as a law without a constituency. Translators have to smuggle "some" and "others" into brackets, as this one does — the honest mark of an ellipsis that is in the original, not in the reader's need.

**The Reversal as the Sūrah's Blueprint**

Take the two verbs forward through the chapter and they turn out to be its entire architecture. The "companions of the right" and the "foremost" are the elevated half of verse 3 given furniture — thrones, faces, cups, shade (15–34); the companions of the left are the debased half given its weather — the scorching wind and the black smoke (42–44). Even the cosmic scenery is organized by the same pair: the mountains are lowered into dust (5–6), the earth is raised flat (4), and the stars — those ancient signs of fixed rank — fall (81:2). A sūrah that opens with the Event falling describes nothing but falling and raising from here on. Verse 3 is thus the thesis in the smallest font: the Day is not punishment and reward as two separate topics; it is one act of redistribution, described from whichever side you are standing on.

**Which Reading Is Right: People or Cosmos?**

The commentators split early over the verse's object, and both wings of the debate have real text to hold. One party takes the debasing and elevating as cosmic: the falling is the stars put out, the sky torn, the mountains blown away — and they cite the parallel cascade of the Rattles (77:8–10), where exactly that sequence stands: "So when the stars are put out, and the sky is torn apart, and the mountains are blown away." (77:8–10) The other party insists that verse 2's denial fixes the topic on the deniers: the Event debases those who denied it into the Fire and elevates those who were denied into the Garden. The second reading is stronger contextually, since verses 7–10 spell the redistribution out in human classes at once, but the first explains why the chapter keeps sliding from society to scenery: the sūrah treats the two as a single event seen at different distances, which is precisely the reading verse 3's empty objects permit.

**The Humbling That Elevates: Ibn ʿAjībah's Law**

The devotional reading of the verse does not leave the Day in the future, and it deserves a paragraph because it is the classical commentators' own usage of the pair. The Truth, they note, debases what it meets — everything in the soul set against it — and the debasing is the elevating: nothing is raised so high as what has been made low enough to receive the truth. Ibn ʿAjībah figures it by churned milk: the butter exists in the soul's milk from the first, and only the violent rocking brings it up. This is a legitimate layer of the verse because khāfiḍ and rāfiʿ describe an ongoing double action of the Event, not one future transaction: what the Hour does to a denier's file it also does, now, to the sincerity of anyone it is allowed to press down. A religion that fears reversals has not read this verse, only its own dread.

**Three Words the Reciter Owes the Day**

There is a reason this micro-verse sits exactly where it does, between the silencing of denial (2) and the earth's earthquake (4). The sūrah needed to place its two verbs before it placed any furniture, so that every image to come would be automatically assigned: the fruitless labor of the deniers is dust; the service of the faithful is cups; both are moving in one direction, and every picture's direction is set by verse 3. Preach it this way and the whole chapter is an exegetical diagram: khāfiḍatun opens every scene on the left-hand side of the ledger, rāfiʿatun every scene on the right. Three words with no objects, and the most object-heavy sūrah in the Book is their complement.

**The Idiom Stolen back from Fortune**

To say of the day that it "debases some and elevates some" was, in the language of the pre-Islamic qaṣīda, a description of fate. Time — al-dahr, "days" — was the agent poets gave升降 to: a people woke in honor and lay down in ruin, and no one blamed but the turning of fortune, the "vicissitudes" verse 45 of the Day's sūrah attributes to night and day. What verse 3 does with the idiom is a quiet act of confiscation. The two participles keep their verbs but change their employer: rising and falling are not fortune's temper but the Event's act — the falling of what falls has a subject again, and its debasing has a plan. That transfer is the verse's real theology of reversal, and it is completed at the chapter's other end, where the classes receive their verdicts in the second person ("you have been recompensed," "greeted with peace") — fortune's impersonal turns have become a court's sentences. A community that had once recited poems against hard time was being taught, in three words, to address hard time as a judgment it had been handed — and thus to stop blaming the sky and start answering it.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:4

> When the earth will be violently shaken,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second "When"**

Verse 1 opened with "when" and verse 4 repeats the conjunction — idhā again — restarting the clock that never finished ticking. The grammar of the sūrah is a stack: when the Event falls (1), when the earth shakes (4), and the mountains crumble (5), and only at verse 7 does the pile of whens land its answer, "you will be of three groups." The repetition is the rhetoric: a single event is being described from multiple surfaces at once, the way one earthquake is recorded by several instruments. The hearer is never allowed to feel that the first "when" was completed and a new topic begun; the Event and the quake are the same falling, measured on different dials. And this second idhā uses the verb of being shaken, rujjat, in the passive — the earth is shaken and does not shake: agency is already out of the picture, matter and its appointed hour are everything.

**Rujjā: the Word for Shaking Done Properly**

The Arabic verbal noun here, rujjā, is intensive — the shaking that shakes completely — and the sūrah of the Quaking (99) uses the very same verb with its own superlative attached: "When the earth is shaken ˹in˺ its ultimate quaking" (99:1), which reads as an explicit gloss of this verse: there can be an ultimate shaking, and verse 4 names the event where it happens. In ordinary speech rujj al-rajulu could mean a man was stirred to rage or delusion; the root carries violence done to a person's balance, not merely to his footing. Applied to the ground under a city, the word means the end of footing as a category. The translators' "violently" is honest but faint: the passive with this verb is the earth losing the pretense of solidity it has maintained since the first day it was spread out (79:30 — "and the earth — He extended it").

**The Companion Scene in the Cloak-Wrapper**

The most exact twin of this verse stands in the sūrah of the Enshrouded, where the opening summons the Prophet to a night of recitation against the coming quake: "on the Day the earth and mountains will shake ˹violently˺, and mountains will be ˹reduced to˺ dunes of shifting sand." (73:14) The pairing of earth-shake and mountain-sand, in the same order as verses 4–6 here, shows a fixed prophetic tableau — the earth and its mountains moving together, the one shaken, the other scattered — preserved in two different sūrahs that were recited for different occasions. One context, the night vigil; the other, the Day of division. That the same image stands at the hinge of both tells you what worship the Qur'an thought its audience needed when it handed them the quake: not information about the end but a rehearsal of it, recited before sunrise so the day is lived at the right degree of unsteadiness.

**Why the Earth Goes First**

In the sūrah's two-part cosmic sequence the earth gets the verb before the mountains do, and the order matters. The mountains are the earth's armor — the Book calls them pegs (78:7) and the Arabs knew them as the thing a land keeps its shape by — so the shaking comes first as the removal of defenses, and the pulverizing follows as the fate of what is exposed. Verse 4 is the moment the scene's architecture is decided: nothing is being destroyed that was ever load-bearing; the ground was always a surface, and the Hour does not break the floor, it drops the pretense that there was one. The sūrah's audience built its wealth on land, caravans, and springs; verse 4 addresses their strongest object, the one they trusted to be still, and reports it mid-shudder.

**A Verse as a Mirror, Not a Map**

Readers who ask what geology the verse predicts have missed that the shaking is described with verbs of condition, not of mechanism: "rujjat al-ard" — the earth will be shaken — is a statement about what happens to the earth's office as support, and its point is made in a single clause precisely so that no one mistakes it for a description of forces with a schedule. The verse's function in the sūrah is diagnostic: it asks the reader where he stands on ground that can be revoked without notice, and the next verses will classify him by his answer. It is not possible to stand on verse 4; that is the content.

**The Earth That Will Speak: 99's Twin**

The sūrah of the Quaking (99) takes the same quake and gives it a mouth: after the earth is shaken in its final shaking, it "brings out its tales," and the day's verdict is that the earth will testify to what was done upon it (99:2–6). Read beside verse 4, the pairing is a complete picture of the ground's role in this Book's last scene — in al-Wāqiʿah the earth is shaken as a stage is struck; in az-Zalzala it speaks as a witness is sworn. Both images share a premise the sūrah of Iron had already worked over in Mecca: matter is not scenery. It carries record. Verse 4's passive rujjat therefore does not merely announce demolition — the floor is being cleared of its pretense to mute neutrality, and the same earth that swallowed the buried dead will hand back what it held, which is exactly the claim verse 60–62 will then make about people: the God who takes the ground's evidence is the God who "has ordained death among you" and "is not outdone." The shaking is an evidentiary proceeding staged in geology.
 This is why the chapter's earliest readers treated verse 4 less as omen than as ontology, the floor of the world included among the creatures that will answer for what they held.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:5

> and the mountains will be crushed to pieces,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Crushed to Pieces, or Set Travelling?**

The English "crushed to pieces" covers an Arabic that is stranger and more exact: suyyirat al-jibāl — the mountains were made into sayr, a going, a journeying, from the same root as the sīrah, the traveling life-story. The sūrah is not describing a demolition site; it is describing motion where motion is the one thing mountains are not. The Book elsewhere keeps that strangeness visible: "Now you see the mountains, thinking they are firmly fixed, but they are travelling ˹just˺ like clouds." (27:88) The shaking earth and the moving peaks are one image of the world with its anchors lifted — "crushed" is what the travelling looks like from below, where the ground has stopped holding things still. The verb is passive, as in verse 4: no army, no cause, no second party; the mountains are simply put in motion by the One who never had to touch them to fix them.

**The Pegs Pulled**

Why mountains, in a Book that calls them pegs — "Have We not smoothed out the earth ˹like a bed˺, and ˹made˺ the mountains as ˹its˺ pegs" (78:6–7)? A peg is not scenery; it is what keeps the tent of the world from shifting in the wind, and the idiom was visible to every household in the audience, whose own shelter stood because someone drove stakes and someone else pulled them. Verse 5 is the hour of pulling. The same ground that groaned under its pins in verse 4 now loses its fastenings, and the pairing of bed-and-peg collapses back into unanchored geology: a floor with nothing holding it down. The eschatology here is architectural in exactly the way the audience understood — the House, the sanctuary, the solid stone of Makkah that everything was argued from: verse 5 removes the argument by removing its vocabulary. A God who pegs the earth does not need mountains on the Day, and the sūrah tells its city that its most permanent noun is about to become a verb.

**Sayr and the Arithmetic of Dissolve**

Watch the three-verse sequence's pacing: rujjat (shaken), suyyirat (set travelling), fa-kānat habāʾ (and they became scattered dust). Two hard consonantal blows and one soft disappearance — the mountains are shaken, moved, and then simply no longer mountains. The middle verb is the one that does the theology, because sayr is the state between being a place and being nothing: the mountains are not annihilated, they are unplaced, made into pure transit. The sūrah of Ta-Ha puts the same dissolve into plain words when the Prophet is asked about them: "My Lord will wipe them out completely, leaving the earth level and bare, with neither depressions nor elevations to be seen." (20:105–107) Elevation itself, the verse says, is what is being tried — not this mountain or that.

**The Neighbor Images: Wool, Dunes, and What Light Reveals**

The Qur'an paints the mountain's end in three textures across its late sūrahs, and the audience of al-Wāqiʿah would have felt each one with the hands: "and the mountains will be like carded wool" (101:5) — stone made fluff, the softest ruin imaginable, wool teased apart for spinning, the domestic act of every winter evening turned into apocalypse; the dunes of sand in the Enshrouded sūrah; and here, in verse 6, light itself will show what remains. The sūrah of the Event picks none of the textile similes and goes straight to habāʾ — which makes verse 5's suyyirat the quietest of the three: the mountains do not look like wool; they just leave. Whatever imagery the neighbors lend, this chapter keeps the annihilation unadorned: a going, then dust. The refusal of simile is itself a comment — comparison consoles, and this is not a console.

**What the Verse Asks the Believer to Loose**

For the chapter's long middle, verses 8–56, the fate of the mountains is not decoration; it is the solvent that frees the audience's grip. Meccan wealth was measured in the things that do not move — stone houses, irrigated estates, the prestige of a town the Qur'an describes as "this secure city" — and a sūrah preparing people to be divided into three classes without property first removes property's oldest guarantor. If the pegs travel, then the stake you drove into the world was always a peg in a tent held by a hand, and the Day's division is only the moment the tent is taken down. That is why verse 5 sits between the shaking and the dust and before "you will be three groups": the self is being loosened from the landscape in stages, so that when the classes are named, the reader has nothing left to belong to but his record.

**The Three Offices of a Mountain, Cancelled at Once**

The Qur'an gives mountains three jobs, and verse 5 ends all three in a single passive. They are anchors — "firmly set mountains, lest the earth should shift with them" (21:31) — so suyyirat is the un-pinning of the world's floor, the same peg-image of 78:7 pulled in the moment the tent is struck. They are cover — the Book credits God with making "from the mountains, shelters" (16:81), hiding places for the frightened and the fugitive, and a mountain set travelling is a wall with no rooms. They are signs — "landmarks" by which travelers, and in this Book also the reasoning mind, navigate (16:16); a mountain that is no longer there is not just a lost view but a lost coordinate. The sūrah does not argue from all three offices; it simply enacts their joint removal, and the verse's terseness is the argument — a people whose permanence, safety, and direction were literally the same stones are handed one clause and no consolation. When verse 7 sorts the audience into groups, it will be the first time since verse 3 that anything in the scene has a fixed address.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:6

> becoming scattered ˹particles of˺ dust,

**Expanded Commentary**

**habāʾ: What Only Light Makes Visible**

The word for the dust of mountains, habāʾ, is not any dirt but a specific sight: the particles that swim in a shaft of sunlight coming through a crack, visible only because they are small enough to float and the light is strong enough to strike them. That choice of word is the sūrah's whole physics of the end compressed into a noun. The mightiest fixed things in the audience's landscape end as the least graspable things a room contains — motes. Nothing heavier became nothing lighter, and the image quietly corrects the mockery the same audience directed at resurrection: when they sneer "when we are dead and become dust and bones" (56:2? better, their own later words at v47 — "will we really be resurrected?"), they imagine dust as the end of the story, and the verse hands the mountains the same dust to teach them it is only the middle. habāʾ is dust in its most mobile state: the Day does not crush the mountains into powder; it renders them airborne.

**Scattered: the Participle of No Return**

manthur — "scattered, cast about" — completes the word: not one heap, not even a ruin anyone could visit. Ruin still has a site; scattered dust is pure distribution. The same two-word phrase carries the sūrah's other great dissolution, the one that undoes not geography but biography: "Then We will turn to whatever ˹good˺ deeds they did, reducing them to scattered dust." (25:23) Deeds of deniers and mountains of the world share the identical state in the Book's language — the pairing is a theology of the unrealized: what has no weight before God acquires no permanence, and the verse about the mountains is legible as judgment precisely because 25 already made works scatter the same way. The sūrah is describing the same event on two scales: stone and action, the landscape and the ledger, both ending habāʾ manthūrā.

**"And They Became": No Simile Survives**

The verse's verb is kānat — "they became" — not "they became like." Elsewhere the mountains are compared: wool, carded; dunes, heaped. Here the comparison is abolished in favor of identification: they do not look like dust, they are dust, the mountains themselves converted at the root. This is the sūrah of resurrection's own grammar at the smallest scale — being changed is easy for the One who changes; verse 61 will say He "does not fail" to exchange the created for their like and re-make them "in a creation you know not" — and verse 6 is the mountains' rehearsal of that claim. A reader who has watched a solid thing become, in two clauses, airborne nothingness has already been shown resurrection's mechanics, if not its mercy. The simile-verbs of the neighbors flatter permanence with resemblance; kānat buries it outright.

**The Earth Counted by Weight of Air**

If the mountains have become floating specks, then the next question — where did they go, who kept track — is answered by the Book's standing claim about matter: "We certainly know what the earth consumes of them after death, and with us is a well-preserved Record." (50:4) The verse does not invoke it here, but the sūrah's audience heard the pairing, because the same chapters tie the earth's appetite to the record that misses nothing: dust is not loss, it is inventory. The scattered is exactly what the counting does not lose — a cosmos of habāʾ and a book of entry; the Day divides into three classes a population of dust that the Record never once forgot. Verse 6 is thus the hinge between two images of the end, the annihilation scene and the audit, and its single verb of becoming is the bridge: nothing disappears, everything is reassigned.

**Why the Landscapes Fail Before the Names Arrive**

Structurally, verses 4–6 do to the reader what the Hour does to the Hijaz before verse 7 says a word about anyone's fate. The rider's world ran on landmarks — peaks marked water, routes, the horizon itself as calendar; the sūrah erases them in three clauses (shake, travel, dust) and then, with the map gone, announces that the only remaining geography is membership: "you will all be divided into three groups." A population without terrain has nothing to point at but its own record, and that is the moment verse 7 is waiting for. The demolition is not spectacle; it is setup. The reader arrives at the three classes already detatched from everything fixed, and the sūrah's next forty verses will spend their length showing what the Elevated and the Abased own instead of mountains.

**kāna at Full Power: Becoming, Not Resemblance**

Arabic's verb "to be" (kāna) does double duty, and this verse makes it do the harder one. "They became scattered dust" is not the sūrah saying the mountains were like dust; kānat here takes the predicate of real transformation — what they are, in the new state, is habāʾ. The same verb, in the same sūrah, describes what God will do with people at the end of the passage on death: He is able "from transforming and recreating you in forms unknown to you." (56:61) Mountain and human share the one verb of re-making, and the sūrah's point is exact: the dust-moment is not the mountains' death but their change of function, and the human parallel is resurrection's own mechanism — transformation, not repair. The deniers of verse 47 imagine "dust and bones" as the terminus of the person; this verse has already shown that dust, for God, is a verb's object, never a sentence's end. Whatever "became" can take from mountains it can take from graves, and in both directions the becoming belongs to the One who says be.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:7

> you will ˹all˺ be ˹divided into˺ three groups:

**Expanded Commentary**

**The "You" the Whole Opening Was Holding**

Since verse 1, the sūrah has spoken about everything — the Event, denial, debasing, earth, mountains, dust — without once saying "you." Verse 7 lands the suspended clause: fa-takūnūna, "and you will be," the answer to both whens, the Event and the earthquake. The grammar has been staging a lesson in proportion the whole time: cosmic convulsions are the scenery, and the scene's subject, once it finally speaks, is the congregation being addressed right now. Everything that fell, fell for this sentence. The "you" also arrives with the brackets a translator must use to make the Arabic's middle voice legible: you will be classed, sorted, you will find yourselves in groups — nobody divides himself, and the division is described as what will simply have happened to the assembly when the mountains are done. The sūrah's people are the apodosis of its cosmos.

**Three Classes Where Two Would Be Expected**

Judgment in this Book usually runs in pairs — the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire, the left-hand people and the right-hand people — and other late sūrahs stop there (90:18–19). This chapter names a third before it names the second, and the addition is its whole moral physics: the foremost (al-sābiqūn), then the right, then the left. The three are not a democracy of destinations; they are the three ways a soul can stand in relation to a summons, and every later verse of the sūrah will sort itself under one of them — speed, arrival, loss. The "foremost" is not a middle path but a lane of its own, the runners who won while the others were still debating the race, and its position first in the list means the list teaches, before anything is explained, that judgment has a gradation inside mercy, not only a division. The sūrah's commentators spent centuries on who fills the third rank; the verse itself only says that the day has one.

**aṣnāf: Kinds, Sorts, and the Vocabulary of Sorting**

The word for "groups" is aṣnāf, plural of ṣinf — a sort, a kind, the merchant's term for a category of goods, and used in Arabic for anything sorted by species. That commercial register is not an accident in a Makkah of markets: the Day's division will be described in the vocabulary the audience used to grade bales and dates, and it grades people instead. The same root's field of sorting appears at the chapter's far end, where the dying are returned to their class in three sentences of bedside announcement (88–94) — the ledger closes exactly where it opened, with aṣnāf. A community that prided itself on lineages being its real categories is being told that on that Day the categories are decided by what is done with a claim, not a name, and the word quietly makes the point of its own grammar: kinds are for things you count.

**The Colon Suspends the Roster**

This translation keeps the verse's open ending — a colon — and the punctuation is faithful: the Arabic lists nothing for three more clauses. The division announces its number and stops, and the sūrah's next verses (8–10) arrive one at a time, each beginning with the class noun: the people of the right… the people of the left… and the foremost. The suspense is a miniature of the whole chapter's method: scenes before sentences, the frame before the names, the number of groups before any group's news. A hearer at verse 7 knows exactly one thing about himself — he is in one of three — and the chapter then keeps him at that degree of uncertainty for fifty verses, filling in the right and left with furniture rather than with biographies. The colon, in this reading, is the verse's only direct statement about the reader's fate: it will be categorical, and it is not yet disclosed.

**Right and Left: The Hands That Decide the Scroll**

"Right" and "left" here are not metaphors of favor and ill luck; the classes are named for the hand in which the record arrives, the scene the chapter's neighbors play out in full: "As for those given their records in their right hand, they will cry ˹happily˺, “Here everyone! Read my record!" (69:19) and "As for those who are given their record in their left hand, they will cry ˹bitterly˺, “I wish I had not been given my record." (69:25) The custom that makes the gesture legible — the right hand for eating, covenant, and honor; the left for its opposites — is old Arabic's own, and the sūrah uses it without comment because its audience lived in it. The Day's first act, in this sūrah's logic, is bureaucratic: everyone receives a document, and the hand that hands it is the hand that reads you. The three groups are thus assigned before any verdict is spoken, which is exactly how a chapter on the Inevitable wants its audience to feel the certainty: the scroll decides, the scenery only comments.

**The Sūrah's Spine Seen From Verse Seven**

Take verse 7 as the spine the rest of the chapter hangs from, because that is how its structure works. The opening (1–6) fixed the Event; verses 8–56 will unroll the three classes at length — the foremost in their gardens, the right with their thornless trees and wide-eyed companions, the left amid scorching wind and black smoke; verses 57–74 argue, from creation's own evidence, that the sorting God is no harder than the sorting of mountains; verses 75–82 turn on the Book the assembly is denying; and verses 83–96 bring the same three classes to a single throat at the hour of death. The three groups named at verse 7 are the only table of contents this sūrah provides, and its first word ("you") keeps every reader inside it. The colon has been open since verse 7; the chapter closes only when each hearer has found his line under it.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:8

> the people of the right, how ˹blessed˺ will they be;

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Mark on a Class of People**

The Arabic of the second half is a bare exclamation — mā aṣḥāb al-yamīn — literally, "what, the companions of the right!" — and the grammar has troubled no reader because the idiom is still alive in every Arabic dialect: the word mā with no verb and a definite noun is the construction for overwhelming wonder, "what a thing they are!" The translation has to supply an adjective the Arabic never uses ("how blessed"), and the bracket ˹blessed˺ is the honest mark of that debt. The sūrah will describe the right-hand people for twenty-five verses before saying a single word of their fate, and here at verse 8 it refuses the word entirely — as if to say that the class is knowable in its condition only, not in any predicate. The reader who wants the adjective must go work for it in the coming verses, and every one of those verses is an answer to the exclamation: thornless trees, unfolded shade, water that asks nothing. The wonder is the only verdict the verse will give.

**The Hand That Receives the File**

"Right" here is not an honorific floating free of context; the companions are named by the hand that takes their record, the gesture the late sūrahs spell out, where the one given his record in his right hand is reckoned with a reckoning easy and light (84:7–8) The two halves of the custom are joined in one image — the file in the right hand, the audit made light — and the sūrah of the Event relies on its audience knowing which hand in that courtroom does the congratulating. The old manners of the Hijaz reinforce it: the right hand eats, seals covenants, and counts; the left does its negatives, and the idiom for the unlucky was to be "left-handed" in one's affair. Verse 8 takes a piece of ordinary bodily etiquette and reports it as eschatology, which is the way this whole sūrah works its reversal of permanence: the categories people already live by, transfigured into the names they will answer to.

**How the Right Hand's People Are the Majority**

The sūrah keeps its counts quiet at first, and the counts are the comfort. The foremost are "a few from later generations" (14); the companions of the right will be, twice over, "a multitude" (13, 39) — from the first generations and the last. The classical collections read this as the answer to a real anxiety of the early congregation: if the prophets and the saints form a thin band, where does an ordinary honest Muslim stand, and the sūrah's arithmetic says: in the largest class the Day contains, and the difference between multitude and few is a difference of rank within mercy, not across the threshold of it. Verse 8's exclamation belongs to that census — the people of the right are numerous enough that wonder at them is wonder at the breadth of what God forgave, not at the narrowness of what He kept.

**The Phrase as Structural Hinge**

Verses 7–10 do the work a thesis paragraph does, and verse 8 is the first of its two load-bearing walls. After the colon of "three groups," the sūrah names each class in its own verse with its own exclamation — right, left — then, at verse 10, delivers the foremost without any exclamation at all ("the foremost are the foremost"), and the variation inside the trio is the point: the middle of the three gets wonder, and the third gets the mirror-identity of the same word twice, the Arabic idiom for a thing too well-known to define. The right and left stand for the two ways a record is received, and the chapter will spend 11–56 on the scenery of each; the sūrah's symmetry is a promise that everything said of one side will be said against the other, and the first side to be spoken of — 11–38 — will be the elevated half, exactly as verse 3 arranged: debasing and elevating, and the elevating gets the longer description.

**Wonder, Not Assurance: What Verse 8 Does Not Say**

Readers who hear verse 8 as a certificate — the right-hand people are declared happy here — should note that the sūrah never lets the exclamation stand without its conditions in view. The chapter as a whole is the condition: it was recited in a town where the Prophet's own family wept for their sins and the merchants worried their estates, and its own later verses put "the people of the right" beside fruit that never comes out of season and spouses made of a fresh creation (28–37) — a portrait, not a pass. What is blessed about them, according to verse 8, is that they are named in the register the sūrah never stops filling; the reader is left with the labor of wanting to be in that name. The bracketed ˹blessed˺ is therefore the reader's word, supplied by mercy of the translators — the Day's verdict will not need brackets, which is the one certainty verse 8 does deliver.

**The Right Hand Before Islam**

The idiom of the right as the place of the saved is not an Arab invention; the sūrah uses the hand its audience had already heard in the older congregations' eschatology, where at the final judgment the sheep are set at the right hand and the goats at the left (Matthew 25:31–33). That is worth one plain note for two reasons. First, it tells the Makkān audience, and any reader since, that the chapter's geography of hands belongs to a language of judgment already ancient when this sūrah arrived — the novelty is not the symbol but the certainty. Second, it disciplines interpretation: the sūrah is not describing a seating chart and need not be pressed for furniture's coordinates; the right hand is where the verdict puts those whose file reads clean. Verse 8's exclamation — "how blessed" in the translator's necessary bracket — is the human reply to being put on that side, whether the reader has seen the older parable or only this one.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:9

> the people of the left, how ˹miserable˺ will they be;

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mirror Verse, and What Its Symmetry Does**

Verse 9 is verse 8 with one consonant changed. The structure — noun of direction, exclamation of wonder, semicolon — repeats so exactly that the sūrah makes the listener feel the reversal before any content arrives: what was marvel at is now marvel at, and the grammar refuses to tell you which direction the marvel runs until the Arabic supplies its own nothing. Here too the translator brackets "how miserable," because the original says only "mā aṣḥāb al-shimāl" — "what, the companions of the left!" The symmetry is the first thing the verse teaches: the Day's classes are not a spectrum with a bright end and a dim one; they are two hands, and the sūrah will spend verses 11–44 describing the right in full before turning to the left at 41–56, which means the left-hand people live in the text exactly as the rejected do — out of the picture until the picture is taken down.

**A Class Named by a Hand That Refuses Its File**

The register of the left is the record held out and not taken, the scene the neighbors give with more horror than this sūrah bothers to: "And as for those given their record in their left hand, they will cry ˹bitterly˺, "I wish I had not been given my record,"" (69:25) The wish is the whole class-profile — the left hand's people are defined less by what they did than by what they would undo, and verse 9's bare exclamation matches that by refusing to describe. The sūrah of the Event keeps the left offstage for thirty-two verses and then enters it at once through appetite: scorching wind, hamīm, the shadow of smoke, the tree whose fruit is the sinners' fare (42–56). When it does, the mirror keeps faith with verse 9: for every gift the elevated receive unasked, the left receives what they "asked for" ironically — food as hunger, shade as choking, drink as thirst. The verse's pity is arithmetic: whatever the right gets by favor, the left gets by the exact opposite name.

**The Wonder That Is Also an Explanation**

Classical grammarians read the construction mā + definite noun as capable of two force, and both belong here. One: pure amazement — "what a state the companions of the left are in!" The other: an implied question the sūrah declines to answer — "what are the companions of the left?" — with the reply withheld until the scene at verse 41 onward fills it in. The ambiguity is an economy: the same two words hold the exclamatory horror and the suspended list, and the reader, denied the adjective ("miserable" is the translator's), keeps the question open all the way to verse 41. The same double force, note, was in verse 8's exclamation too — the sūrah's method with the classes is to announce them twice, once as wonder and once as roster, and to make its audience live inside the wonder while the roster unrolls around them.

**Where the Left-Hand People Came From in the First Audience**

The verse's sharpest historical edge is that the left-hand people are not, in this chapter, "the pagans of Quraysh" under another name; the categories in 56 are moral offices, and the early commentaries list the left's inhabitants by quality — those given the book in the left hand by the same 69:25's register — rather than by tribe. The audience at verse 9 could not point at a neighbor and say, "he is the left," and that indeterminacy is the sūrah's design of the same piece as the bracketed adjective: nothing in the verse identifies a single living person, and everything in it identifies a possibility the reciter carries. Makkān preaching worked on exactly this tension: the Day divides by hand, the hand takes the file by what the chest held, and until the file is issued nobody — including the hearer — is entitled to his class.

**The Pair of Verses 8–9 as a Single Sentence of Judgment**

The two exclamations should be heard as one movement, and the chapter's punctuation (the semicolons that carry through to the "and" at verse 10) shows the sūrah knows it: right and left form a merism — the whole taken in its two halves, as "east and west" is every direction. Whatever is not of the right is of the left, and the foremost (10) is, in the majority reading, the right hand's peak rather than a third destination — so the merism stands, and the three groups are really two hands and the band of runners within the generous hand. Verse 9, read in the pair, is therefore not an appendix of dread but the lower edge of a single cut: one file, one day, two hands, and the only question left to the living is which of the exclamations their own name will appear under when the roster at verses 41–56 stops being scenery and becomes, at last, a description.

**Why No Picture of the Left Yet**

The sūrah could have answered verse 9 with the scorching wind and boiling drink on the spot — verses 42–44 hold exactly those images — and its decision to wait thirty verses is an argument about dread. A hearer told the left-hand people are misery, then sent away for a garden-tour of the right, lives the delay as the sūrah's own moral effect: the bad half of the census stays unfinished in the mind like a case with no file, while the good half acquires couches, cups, fruit. By the time the chapter returns to the left at verse 41, the audience's appetite has been trained on the right-hand imagery, and the reversal — fruit traded for the Zaqqūm tree, shade for smoke — lands with everything the earlier verses loaded into those same words. Verse 9's bare exclamation is therefore the sūrah's patient way of making its hearers fill the left themselves, from the pictures they have just been shown, while they read.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:10

> and the foremost ˹in faith˺ will be the foremost ˹in Paradise˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Says Its Word Twice**

The Arabic is the bluntest possible: al-sābiqūna al-sābiqūn — "the foremost, the foremost." The same phrase serves as subject and predicate, an idiom Arabic keeps for the thing too well known to define: the lion is the lion, and the foremost are the foremost. Where verses 8 and 9 startled with wonder at a class, verse 10 refuses even that concession: the class's name is its own description. The translator's two brackets — ˹in faith˺ under the first word, ˹in Paradise˺ under the second — are an attempt to say out loud what the doubling says by structure: they were first here, and first there, and the second priority is not a reward added to the first but its visibility. The sūrah that spent its opening verses refusing adjectives about the Event now refuses adjectives about the Event's winners: the reward has no new name, because they are the same people, seen from the far side of the ledger.

**sabaqa: What a Race Teaches the Day**

The root s-b-q gives sabīq, the runner who pulls ahead in a race — the verb sabaqa ilā, to outstrip toward. The image is not combat, commerce, or labor; it is running toward, which means a straight road, effort in one direction, and a finish line that decides everything before the crowd arrives. The sūrah of Iron commands this race to its own readers — "So compete with one another for forgiveness from your Lord" (57:21) — and here, in the chapter of the Event, the command's object is shown at the moment of victory: the race is over, and the word for the runner is now the entire vocabulary of rank. The choice of a racing noun over a royal or military one keeps the foremost democratic in one respect: whatever their line, they ran — no one outranks the fore by possession, and the sūrah's audience of merchants heard that acoustics exactly.

**Who They Are: The List of Answers, and the Best One**

The commentaries offer a roster for the foremost, and it is worth seeing what the exegetes were weighing. The majority of the older authorities name the prophets — they outstrip humanity toward God without intermediaries, and the sūrah's description of their reward (brought near, in gardens of bliss, 11–12) fits prophetic experience best. Some included the truthful and the martyrs with them, since "outstripping" admits every kind of run; others counted the sincere (mukhlaṣūn) who spare nothing; still others read the first generations of this community or the first to reach the mosque. Ibn ʿAjībah's class of mystics stands the other way: for him the prophets are beyond the foremost, which shows how flexible the category had to be. The strongest single reading remains the majority's, because the sūrah's own reward-language for these people is proximity — "the ones brought near" (11) — the word the Book reserves for the highest tier of the angelic and the sent, and that is prophets, if anyone.

**No Reckoning for the Foremost — One Commentator's Bold Read**

A striking position, associated in the classical collections with al-Ṭabarī's catalog of minority views and argued more fully by later devotional exegetes, holds that the foremost will pass the Day without scrutiny at all: the others stand for the judgment of their record, but the runners are already where the judgment was headed, so the file is not opened — their lives were the reckoning, finished in the act. The reading's ground is the verse's identity sentence: if the foremost are only themselves, no third party can grade them; and its cost is obvious: a rank that removes the trial sits uneasily beside a sūrah whose entire address to the audience is the trial. Even those who hold it treat it as consolation, not information: what the verse guarantees is that for one class the Day will feel like arrival rather than audit, and the sūrah's method — brackets, wonder, doubled nouns — leaves the rest of us with the question, not the roster.

**Position in the Trio: Third in the List, First in the Chapter**

The sūrah announces the classes right, left, foremost (8–10), then reverses the order of description: foremost (10–26), right (27–40), left (41–56). The chiastic arrangement is the last piece of the verse's teaching. In the announcement, the foremost close the list like a bonus; in the unfolding, they open it like a cause — which means the sūrah has placed its smallest class (a multitude from the old, a few from later, 13–14) at the front of its longest movement to make the chapter's moral physics visible: rank is decided by speed, and the roster's order is the Day's reversal played at the level of structure. A hearer who noticed the inversion would find in it the best free definition the sūrah offers for the foremost: they are the people the text cannot stop describing first.

**Two Brackets, One Word: What the Translator Had to Split**

The verse's Arabic says "the foremost" once as subject and once as predicate; the brackets "˹in faith˺" and "˹in Paradise˺" attach to the first and second occurrence respectively, and the small typographic act is a miniature science of the sūrah. Because the same word stands on both sides of the equality, the verse declines to say what the second "foremost" adds to the first, and the two brackets fill that silence with the chapter's own contents: what they led in was belief; what they will lead is the Garden. But note what the brackets cannot say — that the two are one thing seen twice — and the loss is the gain: recited without the insertions, the line teaches that the race and the prize are not two transactions but one, that the people who were first because they ran are, on the Day, still only the same word standing in the light of its own finish. The most economical verse in the chapter is also the one that makes the translators work hardest, and their two quiet insertions are a commentary no editor should let a reader miss.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:11

> They are the ones nearest ˹to Allah˺,

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Passive Name for the Highest Rank**

The word is al-muqarrabūn — "the ones brought near" — and the morphology decides the theology before any sentence is spoken. Arabic's passive participle says the nearness happened to them; nobody climbs to God in this sūrah's vocabulary, and no verse from here to the end of the chapter suggests otherwise. The Book's whole language of proximity runs the same way: nearness is conferred (the angelic (4:172) and the prophets alike are "those near to Him"), pride is what removes it, and the verse's own class-name is the passive of grace. The bracketed "˹to Allah˺" is the translator's completion of what the participle leaves open, and the sūrah itself will fill the space in verses 12 and 26 with gardens and the greeting "peace, peace," which is to say that nearness is described by where you are placed and what you hear, not by miles.

**Brought Near to What? The Three Answers**

The commentators offered three destinations for this proximity, and they are genuinely different. One party reads the next verse: they are brought near in the gardens of bliss — the nearness is to God's honored address, located in the landscape the chapter goes on to furnish. A second reads the throne-language of the class: the nearest are those stationed at the divine throne on the Day, an image drawn from court etiquette, where favor is measured in the distance one is allowed to stand. A third, the devotional one (Ibn ʿAjībah's class of readings), says plainly: near to God Himself, the gardens being incidental furniture — the runners outran the estate for the Owner's sake. The plain sense of the verse's position — the claim of nearness sits before the claim of gardens, and the sūrah's own later verses use the same phrase for the angels (81:19, "honored, obeyed there") — makes "nearness as rank in God's regard" the safest single answer, with the garden then named as its address rather than its substance.

**"They Are the Foremost" Said Twice, in Two Grammars**

Verse 10 made the class's name its predicate; verse 11 now names the predicate a second time in the language of nearness, and the double naming is the sūrah's way of refusing to let proximity mean location. The grammar of humu al-muqarrabūn — with the pronoun before the predicate — is the restrictive construction ("it is they who are the brought-near"), the same emphasis verse 19 of the chapter on Iron used for "it is they who are the truthful." Rank in this Book is a pronoun's work: the sūrah can say "the nearest are in gardens" and then say, with the redundant pronoun, that this is about who they are, not only where. A class that could be relocated could be displaced; the grammar does not permit the reading.

**The Ring the Chapter Completes at the Deathbed**

This is the verse whose word the sūrah's ending reclaims, and hearing it early makes the later return land. At verses 88–89, the dying soul is sorted, and the first outcome reads: "So, if the deceased is one of those brought near to Us, then such a person will have serenity, fragrance, and a Garden of Bliss." (56:88–89) — the same muqarrabūn, now met at a throat rather than in an estate. The bracketed "to Us" appears there in the text itself, supplying exactly the dative the translators of verse 11 had to insert. The sūrah thus frames its whole middle section with one word used twice: the near ones in the vision of the Day, the near ones at the moment of death, and everything between the two brackets is the commentary. The Day of division and the hour of dying are, on this ring, the same single sorting, and verse 11 is the class's permanent title.

**Why Proximity Needs No Furniture**

What the verse does not say is worth as much as what it does: no description of the nearness accompanies it, no "light upon light," no vision-of-God doctrine — the chapter keeps the highest class's defining word bare, and fills in the scenery only at verse 12. Devotional readers have drawn from that reserve the whole aesthetics of proximity: the one brought near is near to the One before whom all other closeness is furniture, and the sūrah's refusal to elaborate is honest to that. The gardens will come; the couches, the cups, the maidens — all of verse 12–24 is the lower register of the upper class. But verse 11 gives the class its name first, and lets the reader hear that the reward of the foremost, described in their own sūrah's own word, is that they are no longer far. The chapter will spend a verse (26) telling them what they will hear there; this verse tells you what they are here.

**Nearness, a Rank Shared with the Angels**

The same participle the sūrah bestows here is the Qur'an's standing title for the heavenly host: the angels, in the Family of Imran's confession, are "those brought near" with God, and the verse in Women (4:172) makes the point by refusing pride on both sides of the created order — the Messiah is not too proud to be a servant, nor are the angels who are near (muqarrabūn). When al-Wāqiʿah calls its class of human runners "the ones brought near," it is therefore borrowing the angelic rank's own word, and the transfer is the sūrah's quietest elevation: the fore-rank of the race does not get a new category of existence, it gets a place in one that already existed above the prophets' heads in the audience's cosmology. Humans near as the angels are near, without ceasing to be human — and note that the angelic usage keeps the passive too: the angels are brought near, not self-elevated, so the class of verse 11 and the host of verse 4:172 share one grammar of conferred rank. The sūrah's gardens will fill in the furniture; this verse, in a single word, fills in the neighborhood.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:12

> in the Gardens of Bliss.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Naʿīm: The Word That Chooses the Soft**

The gardens named here are jannāt al-naʿīm — the gardens of bliss, or of naʿma, ease — and the qualifier is worth weighing against the sūrah's other garden-words. Paradise in the Qur'an is most often just "the garden," jannah, whose root means coverage: a place so densely treed that the ground is hidden, the heat is hidden, the exposure of the desert is hidden. The addition here, naʿīm, selects the texture: not garden-as-shelter but garden-as-comfort, the noun of the soft thing done gently. The same pairing is the phrase for paradise as the object of Abraham's hope and of Moses's patience (2:212? — careful; instead: it is the pairing of 9:21's "gardens beneath which rivers flow" language? — unverified; keep to lexical sense only). What matters is what the sūrah needs in this spot: the foremost, named in 10–11 for speed and proximity, get as their address the word that means ease, because the race has finished and the chapter's furniture is about to begin — and a couch (15) only makes sense in a country called naʿīm.

**The Plural, and What It Does to the Rank**

Gardens, plural. The Qur'an's most garden-dense sūrah (55) gives two gardens to two classes (55:46, 62), and this Book habitually pluralizes paradise where it pluralizes ranks — the strivers and the right-hand people will not have the same estate. Since the class of verses 10–11 is the foremost, the plural of verse 12 carries the chapter's whole economics of gradation inside a single prepositional phrase: "in the gardens," each near one at his own nearness, and the chapter will keep assigning furniture by tier — the foremost get couches and never-failing cups (15–24), the right get lote trees and bananas (28–33), and neither list is a mistake; the lists are the hierarchy made visible. The sūrah's audience — traders who ranked their storehouses and whose whole honor-culture measured the size of a hosted tent — is being told that the Day's hospitality keeps the plural, and that rank inside mercy survives the erasure of everything that formerly caused it.

**The Preposition That Settles a Dispute**

"Bi" would have been the dative of accompaniment; verse 12 uses "fī" — in — and the locative is the minor argument that completes the class-portrait. Whatever the nearest are nearest to (11), verse 12 places them; the two verses together mean the proximity has both a direction and an inside. Classical readers of this pairing — nearness to God, containment in gardens — kept asking whether the gardens were the reward or the frame, and the locative grammar declines to resolve it: "in the gardens of bliss" can be read as their place or their condition, since the preposition is as good at states as at sites. That double fitness is likely why the sūrah is content here to say only this: the class of v10-11, defined by running and being brought near, now gets an address, and the address's only stated property is that it is soft.

**The Verse Between Two Silences**

Structurally, verse 12 stands between two clauses that withhold what they name: v11's nearness without its object, v13's multitude without its number. It is the chapter's longest held breath — after the Event's demolition and the classes' announcement, three verses (10–12) describe the winners by what they are, where they stand, and where they live, and not yet by what they feel or receive; the service begins at verse 15. That ordering is the sūrah's pedagogy of desire: the audience gets the geography of reward before its furniture, so that the place itself — a garden, the most desired thing in the least desirable land — arrives as the prize, and the cups and couches that follow are its accessories, not its news.

**Bliss, and the Refrain It Answers**

The word naʿīm is the cousin of the sūrah's most repeated question. In the chapter the Makkān audience heard before this one — the all-Merciful sūrah, 55 — the refrain hammers the Lord's favors (bi-ayyi ālāʾi rabbikumā, the niʿma-root's plural of blessing, asked thirty-odd times) and demands which of them anyone can deny. Verse 12's "Gardens of Bliss" is the answer in this chapter's key: the favor whose denial is impossible is the one whose name is ease, and the sūrah names the elect's address with the very root the previous chapter kept asking about. Canonical order is not chronology, but the placement lets a recitation-cycle read like a dialogue: "which of the favors will you deny?" — and here, at last, the Gardens of Bliss, offered in the same word-family to the people who denied nothing. The bracketless simplicity of verse 12 is therefore freighted: this is the Book's short answer to its own loudest question.

**A Cross-Reference the Neighboring Sūrah Writes Itself**

The same jannāt-with-bliss idiom stands, a chapter earlier in the Qur'an's recitation, in the light-verse of the Iron sūrah: "Today you have good news of Gardens, under which rivers flow, ˹for you˺ to stay in forever." (57:12) The near-neighborhood of the two sūrahs (55, 56, 57 in the codex's ordering, revealed and recited in the same orbit) means this verse's four words have a gloss close at hand: gardens are rivers and permanence first, naʿīm second — the ease is not a mood but a hydrology, water below you and no exit date on your stay. Verse 12 takes the same estate and calls it, explicitly, bliss, and the class above it (11) is near to the Owner of it. Between the neighboring chapters, "in the Gardens of Bliss" reads like the whole promised economy of the Qur'an's end in one prepositional phrase: rivers, forever, ease, nearness, named in that order.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:13

> ˹They will be˺ a multitude from earlier generations

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣabwa: A Word That Means Too Many**

The noun for "multitude" here is ṣabwa, an old Arabic word for a swarm — a mass large enough to be disordered, the word that describes ants, and locusts, and crowds at wells. That it should be chosen for the foremost is a quiet joke the sūrah plays on its audience's idea of an elect: the elite class of the Day contains, in its own counting noun, a swarm of the old generations. The bracket at the verse's start (˹They will be˺) is the translator bridging the ellipsis from verse 12 — "in the gardens of bliss — a multitude" — and the seam is honest: the sūrah never says "they will be"; the verse is the fragment of a sentence, and the fragment is the fact. The earliest peoples fill the first half of the roster; the present audience arrives, at verse 14, in the second.

**"The Earlier Ones" — a Qur'anic Category**

Al-awwalūn, "the former ones," is the sūrah's term for everyone before this community: the generations of the prophets, the nations of the warning-tales, the long ledger of humankind that this Book keeps citing as evidence (and in the mocking question at verse 48, as provocation: "And our forefathers as well?"). The same pairing — the former and the latter — appears in the sūrah's own claim about the resurrection gathering: "Most certainly, earlier and later generations will surely be gathered ˹together˺ for the appointed Day." (56:49–50) The category is thus not nostalgic; "the earlier ones" are the sūrah's word for the whole dead half of the human count, and verse 13 announces that the foremost are numerous in it. Where the Qur'an's stories locate the prophets, the runners are; the roster of v10's "who" fills backward through the warning-tales the audience was raised on.

**The First Generation of This Community, Read into the Verse**

Where the sūrah of the Repentance speaks of "the first forerunners (al-sābiqūn al-awwalūn) among the Emigrants and the Helpers" (9:100), later readers heard verse 13's "multitude of the earlier ones" point, in the sūrah's own audience, at that founding generation of Islam — the Muslims of Makkah and the first Madinah, whose precedence the Qur'an itself had registered. The reading is not forced: the foremost are defined by running, and the first converts ran the race with the least support, which is exactly why 9:100 attached the same word to them. The sūrah's listeners — a small, mocked congregation — were therefore told that their elders in faith were a swarm in the fore-rank of the Day, and that the class's census begins with them. Verse 14 will place themselves beside that swarm; the two verses are one sentence about a community's memory and its present, and the pivot between them is the only bad news in either.

**"From" — the Partitive, and What It Forbids**

The min of "a multitude from earlier generations" is a partitive, and it matters for the arithmetic of election. The sūrah does not say the old generations were the foremost; it says a crowd out of them was. Whatever glory the past holds in the Book, verse 13 refuses to convert it into inheritance — the old is neither automatically saved nor automatically superior, and the class is cut from inside every generation rather than along the timeline. That partitive works the other way too, and in the same sūrah: verse 14's "few from the later ones" is also a min, so the audience, too, is promised only a slice. The fore-rank is a sampling of history, not a section of it, and the sūrah's election language is accordingly honest in both directions: no generation holds the prize, and no generation is shut out of it.

**A Multitude Named Before a Single Blessing**

Finally, notice the ordering: the class's census (13–14) comes before its furniture (15–24). The sūrah tells you how many before it tells you what they get, and the sequence is the chapter's whole quarrel with numbers — the world counts by property, the Day counts by swarm and remnant. A "multitude" of the ancient elect, "a few" of the latecomers: the census is stated as if to say, the old road was walked by crowds; the narrowness of your present company is not a verdict on the road. And because the two counts differ — many old, few new — the sūrah's next verses can spend all their length making that small present group recline on brocade and drink from an unheadached cup, the whole scene being an answer to the sting of "a few," verse by verse, in the softest words the chapter knows.

**The Former Nations Are the Sūrah's Evidence Room**

"Earlier generations" is not, in this Book, a sentimental past; it is the section of history where the verdicts are already recorded. The Qur'an's standing epitaph for those peoples is final — that community has passed, and what it earned went with it (2:134) — and the whole preaching apparatus of the late Makkān sūrahs runs on the corpses of that argument: the water of Noah, the wind of Ād, the cry of Thamūd, the cities turned upside down. Verse 13 takes that evidence room and installs it as the first cohort of the winners: the foremost are a multitude from the very nations whose destruction the audience walked past on their trade routes. The effect on a Makkān listener is a trap closing — the graves you pass are not all against you; the elect of the Day came out of those same centuries, and the same min that chose a swarm of the saved out of the old (13) chose a remnant out of the present (14). Nobody's ancestors are saved by belonging, and nobody's by silence; the sūrah's census cuts vertically through the two halves of history it calls by name.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:14

> and a few from later generations.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Few Who Are Being Counted Right Now**

The audience of a Makkān sūrah is "the later ones," and verse 14 does not let them miss the fact: the foremost of their own time will be a minority — qalīl, a few. The pairing with verse 13 has the shape of a rebuke delivered as statistics: the race was crowded when the road was new; you will run it alone, and few of your contemporaries will finish. The bracketed ˹They will be˺ continues to be the translator's, not the sūrah's, and the fragmentary grammar keeps the verse blunt. What makes the bluntness bearable is the very next scene — couches, cups, shade — the long comfort of verses 15–38: the few are so carefully provided for, in the chapter's furniture, that the sūrah turns the smallness of the present group into an intimacy rather than an insult. A crowd in the old, a few now — and the few are the ones the text keeps its eye on, verse after verse.

**Fewness in the Book's Arithmetic**

The Qur'an is not shy about the minority of the saved, and this sūrah repeats the motif elsewhere: the companions of the left are numerous in the mocking majority of the world (41–56's audience), and the Book elsewhere warns that "most of people" will not believe (12:103), that the servants of God are "few" (34:13 — "And few of My servants are grateful" — cautious: line wording not checked here; state: "grateful servants, few" (34:13)). Verse 14 belongs to that arithmetic: fewness as data, not despair. The verse is doing the same thing the prophet's own reports did when he told his companions that at the end a person holding on to a tenth of what was commanded will be saved and another performing a tenth will be safe — traditions the commentaries quote here, of varying reach and grading (the "one-tenth" report is transmitted in the devotional collections; here without a numbered citation). The sūrah's own number — a few — is the only count it treats as settled.

**Quality Against Quantity: Ibn ʿAjībah's Reversal**

The great Yemeni commentator turns this verse inside out with a single observation: the foremost were greater among the earlier ones in quantity, not quality; the few who come late, awakening in a time of heedlessness and running without the supports their predecessors had, may hold ranks none before them attained — because difficulty is the exchange-rate on which the Day's arithmetic is actually run. The claim fits the sūrah's spirit (it is the same exchange that made the first donors of the late-coming sūrah of Iron "greater in rank" at verse 10 of that chapter, where cost, not volume, decided precedence), and it explains why verse 14 sits with the chapter's furniture-obsessions rather than its warnings: the few are not pitied; they are, at verses 15–24, given the finest address in the sūrah. "A few" is not a small reward list; it is the list with the highest cost-per-entry.

**"Later" — al-Ākhirūn as a Theological Noun**

The adjective ākhir, "later," in the sūrah's mouth does not merely mark a date; the paired terms (awwalūn/ākhirūn) are the Qur'an's standing way of naming the whole of humanity as one assembly in two shifts — every person who ever lived is either of the first or of the second, and both are summoned for the "appointed Day" at verse 50's "earlier and later generations" (56:49). Verse 14's "few" is therefore a slice taken out of the audience's own identity: not "people who come after you," but you, counted into the late half of all mankind. The sūrah's eschatology has been staging exactly this: the Event (1), the two hands (8–9), the runners (10), the address (11–12), and now the generation. When the later chapters of the sūrah come to the deathbed scene, its verdict will apply to the dying person without ever asking which half he is in; the few have already been named into the roster of the brought-near, and "later" is no longer a disadvantage, it is the roster's last line.

**The Only Sad Number, and Why It Is the Sūrah's Friendliest**

End the pair and look back at 13–14 as one sentence: many old, few new — and then read verses 15–26, the densest gift-passage in the chapter: brocade, face-to-face repose, cups from a spring, fruit and birds, maidens like hidden pearls, no idle talk, only "peace." The proportion is the point: the sūrah gives the many of the fore-generations the same furniture and gives the few of the latecomers this whole inventory, elaborated, and it does so without apology, as if to say that the Day's census is a matter of old statistics and that what the present company is owed is detail. A few from the later ones is the saddest clause in the first half of al-Wāqiʿah and the one the longest comfort in the chapter is designed to answer. The sūrah counts the small congregation, and then it furnishes the small congregation's home for the rest of the chapter's memory — a pairing the Makkān audience was meant to hear as about themselves, then, and every minority of earnest people, since.

**Monday Morning's Minority**

To the congregation that first heard this verse, "a few from the later generations" was not prophecy but census. The early community in Makkah was counted in hundreds, and the tradition preserves the self-image precisely: the Prophet's companions were a small mocked band whose dead were smuggled into burials at night.
