Verse 14, read inside that room, converts the social humiliation into a verse of the Qur'an's own list — the few are the chapter's first audience, and the sūrah's tenderness toward them (couches, unheadached cups, maidens, the greeting of peace) is a pastoral detail no later age should skim: the God of this chapter furnishes His minority, not in compensation for their numbers, but as their name. That the same few will be told, at the end, that nearness is what dying already feels like (85–89), closes the loop for them exactly as it closes it for every remnant congregation since: small at the start, small at the end, and named twice by the sūrah that counts them.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:15

> ˹All will be˺ on jewelled thrones,

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Preposition, a Throne, and the Beginning of Furniture**

Verse 12 put the class "in the gardens"; verse 15 puts them "on thrones" — ʿalā, the preposition of elevation and of being seated upon — and between the two the chapter has stopped describing the estate and begun describing a room. The furniture arrives as a claim about posture: they will be upon them, the way a person is upon what is under him, and nothing in the verse asks them to get there. Arabic's sarīr, the word translated "throne," is not a seat of judgment in this usage but a raised couch with a canopy — the piece of royal display around which an eastern household organized honor — and when the sūrah later borrows it for the beds of the Garden it is elevating, with a single noun, the ordinary piece of a well-born home into the furniture of nearness to God (the same word that describes Bilqīs's magnificent seat, 27:38–41, and Solomon's, 38:34–35). Paradise keeps the vocabulary of courts and gives it to everyone.

**Jewelled: Mardūwiyyāt and the Luxury Vocabulary of Revelation**

The adjective here, mardūwiyyāt, is classically glossed in two directions the sūrah's commentators recorded — woven through, or encrusted, with pearls and gold, "inlaid couches." Either way it belongs to the Qur'an's small family of imported luxury words for the Garden's surfaces: silk brocades (the istabraq of 76:21), thick tapestries and carpets (the rafraf and abqariyyāt that clothe the blessed of the all-Merciful sūrah, 55:76), and here the pearl-woven couch. The linguistic fact is unglamorous and important: the Qur'an's vocabulary for the afterlife's surfaces borrows the trade-lexicon of the very commerce that raised Makkah, so that the audience's finest goods become, in their transfigured form, the markers of divine reward. What the caravan imported, Paradise inlays; and the verse's bracketed ˹All will be˺ keeps the census from forgetting that the class of 13–14 — swarm and remnant — is what wears the jewels.

**Throne or Couch? A Question the Verse Refuses**

Exegetes debated whether these are thrones of rank or couches of rest, and the debate is worth recording only because the verse dissolves it. In Arabic usage, sarīr is where kings recline — a bed that rules — and the sūrah takes the one word without either specialization: the elevated are enthroned, which is to say they are seated in high state, and they are abed, which is to say they have no more work. Both meanings coexist exactly as they did in the courts the audience knew, where the throne was a daybed and judgment, rest, and hospitality shared a piece of furniture. To choose "throne" is to miss the ease; to choose "couch" is to miss the rank; the sūrah's word keeps the whole object, and the chapter will proceed, in verses 16 and 34, to recline and to spread beds — proof that it meant the couch's rest as much as the throne's height. The near ones are crowned by being given permission to lie down.

**Ones Upon Their Beds in a Book About the Fallen**

Place the image back into the sūrah's physics: verses 1–6 emptied the world — denial silenced, debasing and elevating, the earth shaken, mountains unpegged, dust in the sunlight. Verses 15–16 complete the same reversal at the human scale: the same Event that made every fixed thing travel leaves its nearest ones fixed, "upon" thrones, in the one posture the sūrah will never revoke. It is a precise theology of the solid: in the world, solidity was the lie the mountains told; in the Garden, it becomes the gift. The elevated are the only beings in the chapter whose position is described as settled, and settledness itself is the point — the Day that debases and elevates is, for these people, the last movement they will ever make, everything after 15 is posture, cup, companion, and greeting. The furniture is not decoration of the reward; reclining is the reward, the end of all rujjā (4), all travel (5), all scattering (6).

**The Passive Estate: What the Verse Does Not Ask Them to Do**

The grammar of 15 deserves one final notice: the people of the near rank do nothing in it. They will be "upon" couches; the sūrah's verbs of motion all belong, from this verse on, to what will be done to and around them — youths will circulate (17), cups will be passed, no headache will happen (19). Nothing is demanded, and that absence is the whole estate's constitution. Al-Wāqiʿah never says the elevated will earn their rest; it says the rest is their state, arrived at, and the chapter's moral urgency lives entirely on the audience's side of the text. That passive is also, in classical reading, what makes the verse a statement about grace rather than wages: the throne is what the brought-near are put on, not what they build, and the sūrah's later "recompense for what they used to do" (24) has to be read against the grammar of this room, in which nobody is doing anything at all.

**The Beds of the Garden and the Bed of the World**

One word-family runs through all of this. The Book asks, in the sūrah of the Cliff, "Have We not smoothed out the earth ˹like a bed˺, and ˹made˺ the mountains as ˹its˺ pegs" (78:6–7) — the world itself is a mattress pinned at its corners for the use of its sleepers — and this chapter's right-hand people will be given "elevated furnishings" (furush, 34), while the foremost get jewelled thrones (15). The sūrah is working the same noun up the ladder of its classes: earth-as-bed for everyone, spread cushions for the right, inlaid couches for the nearest. That is why verse 15 reads less like furniture than like promotion: the elevated are not moved to a different planet, they are given a better mattress on the logic of the same creation, the world's own softness perfected. And when the mountains are unpegged in verses 5–6, the image completes: the bed of the world, whose pegs are pulled for the Day, is replaced by beds whose cushions are jewelled for the people of nearness. The chapter's geology and its hospitality are one argument, conducted in bedding.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:16

> reclining face to face.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Muttakiʾūn: The Grammar of Leaning Done Right**

The verb is ittakā, Form VIII of w-k-y — to lean back, to prop oneself, to be at rest against support — and the sūrah's use is the Qur'an's own settled idiom for the posture of the blessed: the people of the Garden recline (the word returns at 36:56, 44:53, 52:20), a posture the world reserved for sultans and the dead, since in pre-Islamic practice only kings reclined at table while others sat forward. Verse 16 gives the whole assembly the sultan's spine, and in the chapter's own terms this is the completion of verse 3: the elevating has finished, and what it elevated now leans. The root also carries, in Arabic's older usage, the net into which game falls (muttakā is a pit or snare) — a resonance the sūrah's next images will cash in quietly: the birds of verse 21 arrive without being hunted, and the only snare in Paradise is rest.

**Muqābilīn: Nobody's Back Is to You**

The face-to-face — muqābilīn, reciprocal participle, turning toward one another — is the sūrah's single clause of social engineering. Every room on earth has a geometry of backs: those seated behind the honored, the queue, the listener's shoulder, the angle a rival must lean into to be seen. The Garden abolishes backs; all faces are toward all, which means no one is audience to another's honor, and honor itself is no longer scarce enough to face sideways for. The chapter's parallel at 37:44 makes the same clause serve the companions of the right ("facing each other on thrones," 37:44), and 15:47 supplies the psychological equipment it assumes: rancor is first stripped from their chests — "We will rip out whatever is in their breasts of resentment, [as] brothers facing each other on couches" — before the facing can mean anything. Verse 16 in our sūrah does not spell the prerequisite out, because its own 24–25 (no vain talk, only peace) already encodes it: faces that are level are what you can have once nothing in the chest has to be hidden from them.

**Rank Without Queueing**

It is worth stopping on how this verse handles the sūrah's hierarchy, because the solution is subtle. The chapter has ranked classes and will rank them again (the foremost above the right, the right above the left); but inside the class, verse 16 makes rank face itself. No one at the top of the room, no one shelved at the back of the tent — a community of the elevated that still contains the whole vocabulary of elevation (15's thrones) but no longer needs the competition for them, because the couches are jeweled for everyone on them. The Day redistributes height and then flattens the interpersonal distance that height used to create. The classical readers of "facing each other" noted that the phrase rules out one more thing: no one sits with his back turned in anger or indifference toward another guest; even the minor discourtesies of the world — the averted shoulder — are abolished, and the sūrah's paradise is as much an etiquette as a real estate.

**The Room Before the Table: A Sequence That Preaches**

Verses 15–16 furnish, 17–24 serve. The order is the etiquette of Hijāzī hospitality turned into eschatology: shade, then seat, then water, then food — a host who brought the cup before the couch had not hosted, he had fed, and the sūrah's God hosts. Once noticed, the sequence disciplines the whole reading of the scene: what comes to the foremost is hospitality, not logistics, and hospitality is personal — the host's honor is spent on the guest — which is why the next verses will describe servants and vessels in such detail while the guest himself does nothing (15's grammar) beyond receiving. The sūrah is answering, in furniture order, the chapter's deepest claim: the nearest are guests, and a guest is not paid; a guest is treated. Verse 24's "recompense for what they used to do" will land, in that frame, as courtesy, not accounting.

**Faces: What the Sūrah Lets the Elevated See**

Face-to-face in a Book that loves faces means the verse also hints at what the audience of these people will look like and what they will see. The Garden's faces elsewhere in the Qur'an are bright, working, and — highest of all — beholding: "On that Day ˹some˺ faces will be bright, looking at their Lord." (75:22–23). Al-Wāqiʿah does not quote that; but it places its reclining class in the chapter's first mention of nearness to God (11) so that the geometry is complete: the nearest face one another in a room whose door, we were told, opens on being brought near. A mutual gaze among the guests, with a prior dative of God at its back — that is verse 16's quiet maximum. The people here are not shown gazing at God, but nothing in the sūrah's furniture excludes the direction the neighbors' verses give the elevated. In a chapter about a Day when every cover is off, faces turned toward faces, and toward nothing furtive, is the closest the text lets its highest class come to a description of heaven's sight-line.

**The Posture the Chapter Denies Everyone Else**

Read 16 against the sūrah's only other reclining, and the verse acquires its negative. The companions of the left will be, in the chapter's stark mirror, "They will be in scorching heat and boiling water, in the shade of black smoke, neither cool nor refreshing" (56:42–44) — they too get a "shade," and theirs neither cools nor shelters; the right, by contrast, recline in extended shade (30) and on raised couches, and the foremost face each other on thrones without a single discomfort itemized because none can be. Al-Wāqiʿah's technique is to distribute the same amenities between two classes and let one list read as gift and the other as parody of the first. The reclining faces of verse 16 are therefore the chapter's standard for everything "comfort" can mean: rest that is mutual, ease that has no side of the room, and shade — which for the left will mean only smoke — that here is the quiet light of a room in which nobody has to turn around.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:17

> They will be waited on by eternal youths

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wildān: The Page-boys of the Eternal House**

The word for the servers is wildān, plural of wild, the boy before maturity — the household's errand-running class, in any grand Arab or eastern home, precisely because a child serves without the calculation of an adult, and the sūrah keeps that anthropology when it hands the nearest rank its pages. The adjective beside them, mukhalladūn, is the passive participle of khallada — "made to last" — and the double meaning in it is not lost on the commentators: the boys are everlasting and they are age-locked; they will serve forever and will never grow out of the soft cheeks that make them pleasant to look upon. The servant class of Paradise is thus designed at the level of grammar to never age, never tire, never leave, and — the passive is important — never achieve any of this by themselves: even the furniture's staff is endowed, not hired. The sūrah's economy has, at verse 17, no labor problem left anywhere in the ledger.

**The Same Phrase in the Neighboring Sūrah**

The Mount sūrah, whose reward-lists pair with this chapter's so closely that the two seem to be read against one another, has the identical tableau in four words: "And they will be waited on by their youthful servants like spotless pearls." (52:24) — and it then borrows this sūrah's pearl-image (23) for the servers, while here the pearls dress the maidens. The exchange of similes across the two chapters is the Qur'an's own commentary on the vocabulary: youth, pearls, eternal round-service belong to the whole scene, and which object gets which figure is a matter of the scene's lighting, not of doctrine. The sūrah of the Event says "eternal youths," the sūrah of the Mount says "like hidden pearls" — together they tell the audience what the boys are for: the sight of unspoiled, tireless, glad service is itself part of the hospitality; being waited upon by people who cannot grow sullen is the Garden's answer to the world's most quiet fear of one's own household.

**Whose Children They Are: A Debate the Sūrah Leaves Open**

The commentaries raise a question the verse never asks — whose boys these are — and its answers show what a pastoral text is doing with its silences. One party reports a position from Salmān al-Fārisī identifying the servants as the children of the polytheists — the idea being that no child of that community is lost, and their service in the Garden is their station of nearness; another party, reported from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, assigns them to the children of the believers, matching the hope that the faithful's little ones who died young are not dead at all. The sūrah itself says only mukhalladūn — made to last — and its neighboring promise in the chapter of the Storm, that believers will be joined with their offspring who followed them in faith (52:21), covers the parents' grief without naming it here. The disagreement is not a gap in the text; it is the text's way of letting bereaved households hear the verse as the answer they most needed, while the chapter keeps the doctrine minimal: nobody in the Garden is lost, and the errands are run by the spared.

**Service Without Servitude: The Utopia of the Round**

The verb is ṭ-w-f — to go around, the circumambulation-root, the word for the pilgrim's circuit — and "they will be waited on" is literally "they circle around them." The sūrah thus uses for the Garden's staff the one verb of motion that the audience's own sacred geography had made entirely liturgical: service that goes in circles, around honored guests, is in this Book's vocabulary already worship, and the round of the cup mirrors the round of the sanctuary. Servitude is what the round becomes when the server fears the master; verse 17 removes the fear by removing the master's power over the server entirely — the eternal youth cannot be dismissed, cannot be punished, cannot want — and what remains of household service is only its dignity in reverse: the honored, served by the safe. That single adjective, "eternal," is the whole political theology of this verse: every hierarchy in the sūrah that could exploit has had its teeth removed by making the tenure absolute.

**The Youths Who Are Not a Temptation**

Finally, the sūrah's restraint with these boys deserves a sentence: wildān carries no erotic charge in the verse, and the text never says another word about them after 17 — they arrive with cups in the next lines and vanish from the chapter's attention. The aesthetic of the Garden's service is hospitality, not possession; and when the chapter later describes the maidens with pearl and hiddenness (22–23), it grants them the similes of beauty, while the youths keep only the adjective of duration. A hearer who tried to grade Paradise by the number of its beautiful things would find the sūrah has already sorted its gazes: some beauties are described because they are given to you, and one is described only because it will never stop being what it is. "Eternal" is the youths' only ornament; the sūrah's readers were meant to notice that this is, in the end, the most beautiful thing to say about anyone.

**An Estate Where Nobody Ages and Nobody Quits**

Read "eternal" as the sūrah's answer to the two griefs of a household: servants grow old and are pensioned off, and children grow up and leave the house. The Garden's pages are exempt from both exits — their youth is not a phase but a permanent state, and their tenure has no end because their nature does not change. That is the quiet utopianism of verse 17's one adjective: every dependency the world makes people carry for each other — care that ages out, hands that retire, the slow erosion of service by time — is answered by a staff that cannot erode. The near-rank reclines, in other words, in a household no master could afford on earth, and the sūrah knows its audience well enough to let the sentence rest there: mukhalladūn, made to last, is the whole contract.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:18

> with cups, pitchers, and a drink ˹of pure wine˺ from a flowing stream,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Vessels, One Sentence**

The Arabic sets the table in three nouns: kās — the glass that already holds its drink — abrīq — the ewer that pours it — and salsabīl, a word so saturated with motion that translators keep taking it as the name of a spring, when here it is "from a spring" (min maʿīn): the glass, the pitcher, and the flowing source. That ordering — served cup, refilling vessel, living wellhead — is the whole hydraulic dream of a desert people, compressed into a metonymy: the sūrah never describes the liquid in verse 18, only the containers and the plumbing, and the containers are doing the theology. In a Book that will spend verses 57–74 arguing for God's skill from milk and semen and rain, Paradise's drink arrives pre-argued: it is described by its vessels and its spring because the sūrah's point is not what the drink is like, but where it comes from and what it will never do — and verse 19 exists to complete that clause's "never."

**Ewers That Shine: The Etymology of Glass**

The abrīq is glossed by the old lexicographers from baraqa, to flash, to sparkle — the ewers glitter from their own cleanliness, the vessels of the Garden shining because the hands that bear them and the liquid they hold are both without residue. Arabic had, by the sūrah's time, a real glass trade (the Sasanian glasshouses and their exports are attested along the Gulf), and the Qur'an's choice to reach for the vessel-word rather than a gold or silver bowl keeps the image light: what dazzles here is clarity, not precious metal. Gold and silver are this sūrah's irony — the world hoards them, the Garden does not drink from them — and the translators and commentators of the neighboring chapter record the Prophet's prohibition of silk and gold for this community precisely in the context of what the blessed will be given next life. Verse 18 keeps its glitter cheap to the eye and honest to the ledger: ewers that shine because the spring they are filled from is clean.

**The Wine the Sūrah Never Names**

The liquid is only implied: "a cup from a flowing spring," and the translator brackets "of pure wine," supplying from the wider Qur'an what this verse declines to say. That reticence is the verse's most-misunderstood property. Elsewhere the Book promises the near-rank "rivers of wine, delicious for those who drink" (47:15), and the glasses of the companions in the Ranks chapter are filled "from a spring" with identical phrasing (37:45–46), but here in al-Wāqiʿah the noun khamr never appears — because the sūrah's interest is in what the drink will not do, and naming the wine would summon the world's associations with it. The bracketed insertions in the verse's English are the honest trace of that design: the sūrah offers the cup and its stream and leaves to the next line the whole duty of defining the drink by subtraction: "that will cause them neither headache nor intoxication" (56:19). Paradise is described in this chapter as the removal of the reason for prohibition, not as its relaxation.

**A Spring, Not a Cistern**

Maʿīn is the word for a flowing water — from the verb of streaming, gushing — and the contrast the sūrah's audience lived was between the ʿayn, the spring, and the bīr, the cistern: collected rain in a stone tank, finite, brackish, fought over. The Garden's drink-source is the first kind; it flows, it is never drawn down, no one queues at it, and it does not depend on the sky's temper. That one noun quietly answers the whole economic theology of this chapter: provision in the near-rank is not stored, and therefore is not hoarded, and therefore is not power. Where the cistern makes its owner rich, the spring makes everyone's cup full without owner — and the sūrah that opened by pulverizing the mountains has, at verse 18, quietly re-issued its verdict on property: fixed things fall, flowing things are served to the reclining (16). The stream is the chapter's second lesson on solidity, poured into a glass.

**Why the Cup Comes Before the Fruit**

Verses 18–19 serve drink before verses 20–21 serve fruit and birds, and the Hijāzī guest knows why: the cup is the opening of hospitality, the first courtesy, the gesture that establishes the guest as guest before any meal is planned. The sūrah is staging its scene in the manners the audience would have read as competence, and the competence matters: this is a hosted afterlife, and its Host observes — in sequence — the etiquette that Arab honor-culture considered the proof of a household's worth. When the sūrah later says "any fruit they choose" (20), the verbs will keep the same order of the world's hospitality transfigured, and the reader who follows the seating chart of Paradise will find that its etiquette is this Book's most patient argument about God: the nearest are not paid a wage, they are received, and the sūrah knows what receiving means, so it is describing, cup by cup, the God the world's manners were always imitating badly.

**Frictionless Hospitality: The Vessels That Remove Their Own Labor**

Watch what the verse keeps out of the scene: nobody drew the water, nobody carried the ewer to the table, nobody cooled the cup, nobody washed the glass. Every one of those verbs belonged to the world the audience drank in — the slave at the well, the cup-bearer at the host's elbow, the chore at the stream — and the sūrah lists only the objects in motion, which is how it promises labor's removal without ever discussing it. The same technique governs the right-hand people's water, described as "gushing" so that no one must lift anything (31), and their fruit that needs no reaching (33): the Garden's hydraulics are the sūrah's way of answering the servant question that the whole of Makkān society was built on. Verse 18 is thus not a still-life; it is the chapter's second lesson in solidity and its first in labor — cups arrive full because the Event has broken, for these people, every handle that had to be turned.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:19

> that will cause them neither headache nor intoxication.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse of Subtraction**

Having placed the cup, the sūrah defines the drink entirely by what it will not do: neither piercing headache nor loss of mind. This apophatic delight is a deliberate rhetoric. The audience of Makkah knew wine's two bills — the skull's throb the next morning, and the mind's fog that night — and the law had already ringed the drink with restrictions because of them; the Garden's answer to the denier's complaint that God has forbidden pleasant things is not "it will be allowed to you there" but "its cost will be removed there." The pleasures of al-Wāqiʿah are described by their side-effects excised: the fruit has no season and no denial (33), the shade has no heat, the talk has no vanity, and the drink here has neither end. Every comfort in the chapter arrives with its invoice already paid, which is the sūrah's answer, in image form, to the world's only argument against God's generosity — that enjoyment is borrowed against pain.

**Ṣadāʿ: The Headache with a Spear in It**

The word for the headache, ṣudāʿ, comes from a root of piercing — ṣadʿa, to pierce, to stab — so the noun is not "a sore head" but a lance through it, the classical hangover image. Its neighbor in the Qur'an, in the Ranks sūrah, keeps the same two negatives against the same cup: no headache in them, nor will they be intoxicated (37:46–47), a fixed Qur'anic pair for the drink of the near-rank that our verse repeats with new verbs. The redundancy is useful: two late Makkān sūrahs agree, clause for clause, on the precise medical profile of the forbidden thing as the measure of the promised thing. To drink what the world's drinker wakes from is to drink the same drink minus the morning — and the sūrah's audience, including any of them who had tasted the pre-Islamic nights the poets preserved, heard verse 19 as the removal of the only line of the poem that ever mattered, the regret.

**Nuzūf — or the Spring That Will Not Fail**

The second negation carries a variant reading worth reporting for its beauty. Read one way, lā yunzafūn is from anzafa — they will not be light-headed, their minds not seized, the stupor (nuzūf) that wine brings removed. Read with a different consonant, yunzifūn, the verb belongs to wells: they will not run dry, the spring does not draw down. The classical collections record both readings as old (al-Qurtubī and others cite the second as a transmitted alternative rather than an invention), and each fits the verse's company perfectly: the first protects the guest's head, the second the guest's supply. There is no reason the sūrah's audience had to choose, since the two clauses describe one room — a pour with no hangover from a stream with no end — and the verse's grammar, a bare negation attached to the cup of the preceding verse, tolerates whichever force the reciter carried in the mouth.

**A Sufi Poet on the Cup, Borrowed by Commentary**

The later mystical reception of the Garden's drink took the verse's subtraction as a description of the soul's state, and the most famous instance is in the Egyptian Ibn al-Fāriḍ's wine-ode (d. 1235), in which the spiritual cup refines the character of its drinkers, guides the irresolute to resolve, makes the miser's hand generous, and pours forbearance into the man struck by anger — the effects of the drink reversed from the world's intoxication into clarity. The sūrah's commentators are more sober and more exact: what the verse teaches is that in Paradise the sign of divine favor is not that a limit has been lifted but that a weakness has been healed — the head, the mind, and the will all kept intact by the cup that the same God forbade where they would not be. The odes and the exegesis agree on the arithmetic: the promise here is subtraction of harm, not addition of excess.

**Neither/Nor: The Total Negation in Two Words**

Grammatically, lā … wa-lā before two indefinite nouns is the strongest "no" Arabic can build inside a nominal sentence — not "they will not suffer," which might allow one of the two, but "neither headache nor stupefaction, in principle, for them" — the banishment of the whole genus. The sūrah is, in other words, using law-level negation to legislate physiology, and the verse is worth pausing on precisely because it is the first time in the chapter's Paradise that anything at all is said in the negative: the Event denied its denials (2), and now the Garden is defined by its own double denial. The nearest ones, the brought-near (11), recline in a place where the word "afterward" has no application — no morning follows a night that harmed nobody, and the eternal youths (17) exist outside time's interest. Verse 19 is the chapter's small, firm claim that the Garden's pleasures are not this world's pleasures extended; they are this world's pleasures healed, and healing is described, clinically, by listing the removed symptoms.

**The Bodies of the Dying, Listening**

The verse's two removed miseries acquire their weight from the chapter's most persistent setting in reception: a sūrah recited beside the sick and the dying, whose whole audience is bodies under vigilance. For that ear, 19 is not about wine at all; it is a promise about the physiology of the next life — that there will be a place where no body needs watching, where the morning brings no penalty, where the mind is never lent out and returned fogged. The headache and the stupefaction are the two conditions every caretaker knows by heart, and the sūrah names their absence as the drink's definition precisely because its first audience was lying inside those conditions. This is the verse's pastoral precision: the near-rank's cup is described as the end of hangovers in the same chapter that ends fear in the left-hand's fire (41), and both lines are answered to the same question — what will it not cost to be there?


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:20

> ˹They will also be served˺ any fruit they choose

**Expanded Commentary**

**faakihah: The Ripening**

The word is faakihah, from the root that means, before it means fruit, to burst into ripeness — and the same root gives the sūrah's neighbors their verb for delight (tafakkuh, to enjoy), so that "fruit" and "enjoyment" are etymological twins in the Qur'an's hand. Al-Wāqiʿah uses the word without a tree: "and fruits" arrives as sheer produce, unburdened by orchard, harvest, season. The verse's brackets are the translator's work, and they show how spare the Arabic remains: "and fruit they choose" — noun, relative clause, done — following the vessels of verse 18 without a new verb of giving. The sūrah's syntax is a banquet at which the plates keep appearing because the Host's sentence does not end; each new noun is another course placed by parataxis, and the reader is fed by the list's mere continuation.

**Takhayyur: Choice as the Real Provision**

The relative clause — "they choose" — does more work than the fruit. The sūrah's world ran on what one could get: dates in season, grain when the rain came, meat only at festivals or on the camel's death; choice itself was the luxury the market could not stock. Verse 20 promises, before any fruit, takhayyur — the freedom to pick, the absence of a fixed menu — and this is the chapter's consistent method: the right-hand people get their fruit "never out of season nor forbidden" (33), which is the same gift spelled as time and law removed. The provision of the near-rank is defined by the removal of constraints on the appetite rather than by the appetite's quantity; the pleasure is the choosing. What al-Wāqiʿah feeds the audience is, in effect, the answer to the economics of want: Paradise is not everything, it is anything, and the relative clause is the whole doctrine.

**The Measured Portion Said Without Measure**

The sūrah of Ranks describes the Garden's fare with a phrase that reads as a gloss of this verse: "They will have a known provision: fruits ˹of every type˺. In the Gardens of Bliss, ˹reclining˺ facing each other on thrones." (37:41–44). What our chapter borrows is the structure — provision and fruit and the removal of the hangover (19 above and 37:46–47 agree clause for clause) — while dropping its specificity of "known," the measured. That difference is the verse's quiet teaching: the near-rank's fruit is chosen, not allotted; the companions of the right receive fruit that is "abundant" and "never out of season nor forbidden" (32–33), while the foremost receive the open verb of choice. Within a single sūrah, the two lists articulate a real gradation: one class's ease is abundance, the other's is option itself, and the sūrah has arranged for its readers to want the second one.

**Fruit as the Chapter's Own Sign-Language**

The sūrah's middle argument (57–74) will ask the deniers, in turn, about what they ejaculate, what they sow, what they drink — and fruit stands in both halves: the same word for produce names the Garden's course here and the sower's crop there. The technique makes verse 20 legible as the far end of a sign: the fruit the fore-rank chooses is the fruit the farmer of verses 63–67 could lose overnight if God willed it to chaff; the orchard in the near-rank is the world's orchard with the "if We willed" clause permanently spent. The promise and the argument are the same botanical fact seen from two directions — provision with no chaff, because the chaff was the warning. A reader in the middle of the chapter's litany of crops hears 20 as the answer already waiting at the end of the sowing: your wheat is a parable; their fruit is the estate.

**Why the Sūrah Never Says What the Fruit Is**

No apples, no pomegranates, no names at all — just "fruit," and the reticence belongs to the register of the whole scene. The chapter describes the Garden at the level of kinds: fruit as a kind, birds as a kind, maidens as a kind, cups as kinds. Its purpose is not to inventory delights (which would anchor them in the audience's market-prices) but to state the laws of the place: choosing, seasonlessness, harmlessness, unending supply. The sūrah's general nouns are therefore not poverty of image but honesty of scale: it hands the classes conditions and leaves the details to whatever the reciter's own history of want will fill in, so that every ear hears the fruit it has needed most. Verse 20's bareness is the verse's universality, and in a chapter recited to the dying (its reception never strays far from this) the ability of the list to hold every appetite is part of its office: the dying hear, in a kind without a name, the thing they personally were denied.

**The Grammar of "Any"**

The relative construction — mā, "whatever," with the verb of choosing attached — is an open file in Arabic, a noun without extension limits, and the sūrah's restraint with it is the point: no list is closed by verse 20, no inventory is proposed. Compare the chapter of the All-Merciful, which promises the near neighbors of Paradise "two kinds of every fruit" (55:68) — abundance stated as doubling — while al-Wāqiʿah states it as the removal of the word "only": whatever the chooser can select is selectable. The two techniques mark the difference between a catalogue and a constitution; our chapter is writing laws of the place rather than its stock, and verse 20's bare relative is one of the strongest of them, a small function-word that outlaws the sentence "you may not have that." Later, the translators' brackets ("They will also be served") try to supply the verb of giving the Arabic withholds; but in the original, "fruit" simply stands where the sentence needs no verb — the wish is already on its way to being met, grammatically, before anyone serves it.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:21

> and meat from any bird they desire.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Birds, Omens, and the Reversal of the Augury**

The word ṭayr is, before it is any winged thing, the vocabulary of augury: the Arabs read their fate in the flight of birds, and the Book's language inherits the idiom — a man's ṭāʾir is the lot that flies with him, and the Qur'an repeatedly transfers the omen from the bird to God, "your ill omens are with God" being the standing correction of the practice. Verse 21 completes the reversal at the level of the dinner: the birds that used to be read are now served, and the sky's messages have become the near-rank's meat. The irony is precise because it is physical — augury was a science of distance and anxiety, a sign you dared not touch, and the Garden's birds are touched, carved, eaten. Al-Wāqiʿah has spent fifteen verses unmaking the audience's solid world; here it unsets its nervous one, and the guest who once asked the flying things whether to travel now asks whether he would care for the roast. Nothing in the verse's grammar announces the joke; it simply puts laḥm min ṭayr after faakihah and lets the audience feel what a world is like when its signs have stopped issuing verdicts and started issuing dinner.

**Lahm: Flesh in an Orchard**

The menu pairs fruit and meat, the two staples of the desert diet, and does so in the sūrah's spare style — "and the meat of birds as they desire" — with no cook, fire, slaughter, or market in sight. That pairing is the scene's claim to totality: provision in the near-rank is neither vegetarian scarcity nor feast's heaviness but the complete pantry of the audience's own economy transfigured, fruit for the season of the date-palm and birds for the festival of the camel. The absence of the kill is the detail the sūrah will not supply — Paradise's flesh arrives without its blood, a silence the commentators noticed and generally closed with reports of the birds' voluntary availability — and the verse's own grammar supports the quiet: "as they desire" places the entire apparatus between wish and plate in parentheses. The meat is, in the sūrah's register, another object of the wish verb, like fruit: the appetite is the order and the table is the delivery.

**Desire, Said Twice, in Two Verbs**

Verses 20 and 21 give the same gift of appetite a grammar of its own: fruit "they choose" (yatakayyarojn, the verb of picking between options), birds "they desire" (yashtahūn, the verb of craving) — choice for the everyday, craving for the rare. The couplet's variation is the sūrah's way of refusing monotony inside paradise: even in a place without want, the soul still selects and still longs, and the text dignifies both verbs. Later verses will repeat the structure for the right-hand people — fruit "in season, not forbidden" — and the contrast confirms the design: the near-rank's desire is its logistics. Nothing is asked of them, and the sūrah's restraint about how anything arrives — no kitchen, no spring, no storehouse named in this couplet — makes the verbs of want the only machinery the scene permits.

**The Reports on the Birds of Paradise**

The aḥādīth literature that the tafsīr collections assemble here describes the Garden's birds with long necks and extraordinary plumage, and preserves one telling reply: when the companions marveled at their beauty, the Prophet is reported to have said that the one who eats them is more blessed than they are. The report is transmitted in the hadith of al-Ḥākim's circle and the collections, its chains debated — it is cited here, as the classical sūrahs cite it, with caution rather than a numbered claim. [UNVERIFIED: the "more blessed than they are" report — wording and grading vary in the collections and no single number is fixed here.] What the exegetes wanted from it is visible: the scene's aesthetics must not be mistaken for its economy. The Garden's objects are beautiful, but the verse's subject is the guest, and the marvel belongs to the one who eats, not to what is eaten — which is precisely the hierarchy the sūrah's passive furniture everywhere maintains.

**Why Meat Belongs to This Verse and Not to the Argument**

It is easy to skim 56:20–21 as decoration after 18–19's cups, and the placement argues otherwise. The sūrah's next movement (25–26) will define the Garden by its soundscape — no vanity, no sin's instigation, only "peace, peace" — and the birds' verse, immediately before it, is the last word of the body's catalog. Flesh, fruit, drink: the animal functions are complete before the sūrah moves, in 25–37, to speech, plants, water, shade, spouses, and numbers, which is the sequence of a human being restored, sense by sense, to a world with none of its costs. Verse 21, at the hinge, does the dirty work the others avoid — meat, the one food that in this world means death — and does it in three words that mention neither animal nor knife. The resurrection of the body, which the whole chapter exists to defend, is stated here in its least defensible detail, and the sūrah lets the wish-verb carry the burden the audience might ask to see.

**Ṭayr in the Qur'an's Wider Sky**

These birds are the last stop on a journey the Book makes them take. In the dominion chapter, birds are the resurrection's public sign — "Do they not see the birds above them, spreading their wings and folding them in?" (67:19), creatures whose flight is itself an argument about God's hold on what flies. In the table chapter, God commands Abraham to slaughter four birds and lay their parts on hills, and when he calls them they come running (2:260) — the first resurrection demonstration in the Qur'an's sequence, performed with poultry. The sūrah of Jesus gives him clay birds that breathe when he breathes on them. Al-Wāqiʿah's dinner of birds belongs to this same argument's end: the birds that demonstrated resurrection in the signs chapters are, for the near-rank, served at table, the demonstrated raised and eaten, and the chapter thereby completes a circle its audience could feel without being told: the thing God raises, God also provides, and the miracle the deniers mocked is, in the Garden, merely the menu.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:22

> And ˹they will have˺ maidens with gorgeous eyes,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥūr: The Beauty Defect, Perfected**

The word is ḥūr, singular ḥawrāʾ, and in ordinary Arabic it named a flaw: ḥawar is the exaggerated whiteness of the sclera against an exaggerated blackness of the iris — the "fish-eye" that old poets mocked. The sūrah takes that defect and, by making it the epithet of the Garden's company, inverts the whole aesthetic: the beauty of the near-rank's companions is a contrast set at maximum, the white and black of the eye pushed past the point of naturalness into the condition the poets' own diction had reserved for ugliness. Classical commentators felt the problem and answered that the ḥawar of this world is a defect only because the rest of the face is unbalanced; the praised flaw is the one detail that, in proportion, becomes the most striking feature a face can hold. The verse is thus a small lesson in the sūrah's method of healing rather than relaxing limits: it does not describe new eyes, it describes this world's eyes with the imbalance removed — beauty as the same anatomy in which nothing is excessive except what the Host intended.

**Qāṣirāt: The Gaze That Has Arrived**

In its companion chapters the Book completes the image with the detail that carries the sūrah's whole psychology of desire: these are "restraining their glances" — qāṣirāt aṭṭarf (37:48; 38:52) — eyes that have stopped reaching, companions whose gaze is fixed on their own and who themselves look at no one else. The two directions matter together: desire in the Garden is not an intensified appetite but an appetite that has come to rest, and the "wide-eyed" of verse 22 in this sūrah and the "restrained" in its neighbors describe one condition from each side — the eye open and the eye contented. The phrase is the answer to the restlessness every husband and wife of the world knows; the sūrah promises not the addition of beauty to an envious, ranging heart but the removal of ranging itself, which is the chapter's consistent technique: cups with no headache (19), fruit with no season (20), and companions with no wander.

**Served or Serving: The Syntax in Two Hands**

Verse 22 has a famous grammatical fork. The maidens can be read as another item in the gift-list — "and maidens" hanging on the understood "We will have given them" back in the scene's verbs — or as a second subject of the youths' verb of circling service, so that they too wait upon the near-rank. The commentators record both, and each has real support: the gift-reading matches 20–21, where fruit and birds arrive as objects of choosing, and the serving-reading matches 17, where the chapter has already introduced a class of attendants and might be doubling its staff. Nothing in the Arabic decides, and the undecidability may be a grace: the same beings occupy, in the audience's imagination, the place of the given and the place of the giver, and the sūrah lets "maidens with gorgeous eyes" stand between the two grammars without embarrassment, in the one scene where no honor is compromised by serving because nobody is diminished by it.

**The Pearl, the Maiden, and the Sūrah's Swapped Economy**

Verse 23 will dress the maidens in a simile — "like pristine pearls" — but readers should count, from 22, what the chapter has been doing with the audience's luxury goods since verse 15. Jewels belong on furniture, pearls on bodies, gold on couch-cushions; the things Makkah traded and hoarded have been redistributed as surfaces and persons of the Garden, never as possessions of the self. In the near-rank nobody owns a pearl — a pearl is what a companion of the near-rank is like. The chapter's economy of precious objects, read from verse 22, is a complete inversion of the hoarder's logic the sūrah's Iron neighbor legislated: wealth is demoted to décor, and persons are promoted to the worth that wealth used to claim. The "gorgeous eyes" are therefore, in the chapter's arithmetic, more precious than the jewelry they outshine, and that is the verse's contribution to the sūrah's long argument about what solid things are for.

**What the Sūrah Refuses to Say About Them**

The reticence of 22 deserves one final clause of notice: the sūrah gives these companions no portrait — no faces, no hair, no bodies beyond the eye. The chapter that named ewers and couches in detail stops at the eyes of its most intimate promise, and the restraint is its own instruction about the audience's projections: the text will say "maidens" and "gorgeous eyes" and nothing else, leaving the rest to the pearl's simile (23) and to the reader's own history of beauty. The later reception, including the verses recited at deathbeds where this sūrah was a companion, took the hint — the promise is marriage as restoration, the companionship of persons who will not age, refuse, or leave — and the sūrah's silence about the details is what keeps the verse portable to every grief that has lost, or never had, any of them. In the chapter of the Event, everything solid becomes dust; what remains to the near-rank is what can look back, and verse 22 says exactly that, and stops.

**Two Marriage Lists, One Chapter**

The near-rank's companions in 22–23 are not the chapter's only spouses; verses 35–37 give the companions of the right a creation story of their own — made anew, virginal, of equal age — and the two lists should be read as one decision. The sūrah refuses to let any saved class be lonely, and it refuses to let any of them be depicted as receiving a lesser intimacy: the nearest get ḥūr like hidden pearls, the right get wives freshly created for the condition, and the chapter's gradation, which is real everywhere else, stops at the marriage-bed. That stop is itself teaching. The audience of 56 lived in a society where women's status tracked their husbands' rank absolutely, and the sūrah's afterlife distributes companionship by class only in its décor, not in its kindness: every saved household is fully housed, every spouse fully given, and the only inequality left in the room is the one no one in it will feel, because the lower class looks, from inside its verses, exactly as rich as the higher.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:23

> like pristine pearls,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Maknūn: The Beauty Nobody Has Touched**

The simile's adjective is maknūn, passive of kanana — to store, to hide away — and the image it carries is the pearl still in its shell, still in its bed on the sea-floor: a thing of value that has not been handled, aired, priced, or worn. The sūrah's neighbors use the same figure twice more, — the identical adjective that the Mount sūrah lends its youthful servants ("like spotless pearls," 52:24), while the Insān sūrah likens its eternal pages to scattered pearls (76:19) — but here the effect on the verse's grammar is the whole theology: "maidens… like pearls with hidden treasure in them" says that in the near-rank the most precious beings are the untouched ones, and untouchedness — not the shine — is the property being compared. Every other pearl image in the audience's world was about extraction: diving, trading, drilling, stringing. The sūrah borrows the gem and returns it to the seabed, so that its companions' value is defined by what has never happened to them. In a chapter whose first verses strip the world of all its handled things, the hidden pearl is the one object that cannot be seized, lost, or hoarded, because it was never exposed.

**The Economy of Small Precious Things**

Since verse 15's jewelled thrones, the sūrah has been redistributing the audience's luxury goods: pearls as couch-inlay, pearls as persons, gold nowhere. That last absence deserves the note: al-Wāqiʿah, unlike some of its Paradise-painting neighbors, never puts gold in the near-rank's room — its preciousness is all pearl, the one luxury of the Red Sea trade that Makkāns could not grow and had to dive for, and the one gem that needs no cutting. The chapter is quietly making a point about the two kinds of wealth its hearers knew: mined metal, which is worked into ownership, and found pearl, which arrives already finished and is only ever received. Persons like pearls, couches like inlay, drink from a spring no one dug — the whole scene is composed of received goods, and the pearl at its center is the verse's signature: the near-rank does not acquire its companions; they are given, untouched, exactly as the chapter's God gives everything in it.

**Dust and Pearl: The Chapter's Opposing Similes**

Place verse 23 back against verse 6 and the sūrah's architecture appears in two images. The first verse-group of the Event ended with the mountains crushed until they are "scattered dust" — the most exposed, trampled, unvalued of substances — and the reward-section now ends its human catalog with the most hidden, courted, and guarded of substances. Al-Wāqiʿah's Day divides people by their relationship to visibility: the deniers are what the wind makes of everything it can see, and the near ones are surrounded by what no eye was made to reach. Between the two similes sit the censuses and the cups, but the chapter's physics is already stated in the pair: to be unmade is to be scattered, to be honored is to be kept. The pearl is also, anatomically, an answer to the dust — a grain of irritation sealed, layer by layer, into luster, the one substance that is a stored-up hurt made beautiful, and the sūrah's audience, who had watched their mountains lose their surfaces, are being told what happens to pain that God chooses to close over instead of scattering.

**A Simile's Two Jobs: Adorning and Withholding**

Readers of the verse should count what the pearl comparison does and what it refuses. It lends: roundness without seams, shine without fire, smallness that fits the hand, value that needs no setting. It refuses: any description of skin, form, movement, or age. A simile of this kind is a precision instrument for restraint — the sūrah lets the imagination travel exactly as far as the figure carries it and no farther, which is the same technique verse 22's single eye-detail practiced. The classical commentators noted that pearls in their shells are also untouched by the sun's fade and the hand's oil — permanence without maintenance — and that is the one doctrinal content of the image in this chapter: in the near-rank, beauty does not require care, because nothing that harms it is present. The sūrah thus describes eternity again, in the vocabulary of a gem, having already said it in a word (17) and a spring (18).

**The Reception of a Quiet Verse**

One last fact about 23 belongs to this commentary because it belongs to the sūrah's history: in the devotional literature around Wāqiʿah — the night-recitation the reports connect with freedom from want — verses like this one were read less as descriptions than as pledges, small sealed sentences to be recited against the world's handling. The reported virtue of the chapter is weak by the standards of the hadith sciences and was treated by the jurists accordingly — a caution this commentary keeps wherever it touches the practice — but the reception itself testifies to how the verse landed on poor households: people whose daughters were, in the world's economy, liabilities to be buried or brides to be sold heard "untouched pearls" said of the near-rank's companions, and the sūrah's image became, in their recitation, the counter-picture to the exposure that poverty brings. The verse says nothing about that; the history of its hearing says a great deal.

**The Sealed Shell at the Bedside**

And it is precisely the verses of received things — not earned ones — that the sūrah's bedside recitation kept touching: a dying person cannot dive, cannot choose, cannot hold, and maknūn describes a value that survives every hand. The pearl in its shell is, for that ear, the chapter's last word on exposure: what God keeps, nothing open touches.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:24

> all as a reward for what they used to do.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jazaʾ: The Root That Means Matching**

The noun is jazaʾ, from the root of going-between and equivalence — to repay a debt with its like, to settle — and the sūrah uses it here as a bare accusative, a hanging verbal noun that reads as a verdict appended to the whole preceding scene: ˹all this˺ ˹is˺ a recompense. That grammar matters. The verse does not say the reward "exceeds" the deeds, nor that it "pays" them like a wage; the root's semantics is fittingness, the scale that matches. The classical reading of the sūrah's Paradise is built on this double awareness: what the near-rank receives is proportionate — the scene from 15 to 23 is not a fairy-tale surfeit but the exact correlate of lives spent in faith and its practices — and yet the proportionality is not arithmetic, because the matching is of kinds, not quanta: those who were brought near are near, those who chose God over the market choose fruit and birds, those who served guests recline as guests. Jazaʾ is the sūrah's one explicit law of the scene, and it laws it as symmetry.

**"What They Used to Do": The Reward of Habit**

The relative clause's verbs are imperfect after kāna — "they used to do" — and the construction is the Qur'an's regular way of pointing not at acts but at a way of life: the continuous past, the thing repeatedly done until it is who someone is. Al-Wāqiʿah, which has so far named no single deed of the near-rank — no prayer, no fast, no charity — makes their whole recompense hang on this one habitual aspect. The choice is doctrinally sharp: the sūrah rewards character's track record, not a ledger of transactions, and the verses that follow will prove the symmetry it assumes — "did you see what you ejaculate… what you sow… what you drink… the fire you strike" — the same kāna-yafʿalūn rhythm of ordinary repetition on the side of the deniers. Verse 24, in other words, closes the near-rank's scene with the sentence that opens the argument, tying the reward to the only thing the sūrah thinks anyone actually offers God: their habits, done used-to, in a life's continuous tense.

**Hospitality and Wages: Reading 24 Against 15**

The tension in the room is worth naming plainly. Verses 15–23 describe passive enjoyment — reclining, being circled, having cups pressed on them, choosing fruit — and 24 says all of it is "a recompense for what they used to do," and the reader is entitled to ask whether a guest is paid. The sūrah's answer is embedded in the sequence: the recompense clause is appended after the scene as its explanation, not staged inside it as its transaction; nobody in the near-rank is shown settling accounts, and the only agent the verse names acting at all is, again, God. The same appositional construction — "a reward from your Lord, for what he used to do" — closes the sūrah of the Prostration's description of the hidden goods its blessed expect, and the classical readers said the obvious: this book's economy of Paradise is one in which gift and wage are not alternatives, because the Host is also the Judge, and hospitality is what His justice looks like from the guest's side. Verse 24 is that sentence, in three words.

**What the Verse Owes the Deniers**

There is a second edge in 24, aimed back at the beginning of the sūrah. Its opening charged that "man denies Our signs" and that the deniers "used to indulge in sinful conduct" — the same habitual grammar — and the whole chapter will keep the two tracks in one register, "recompense for what they used to do" for the near-rank and the identical logic for the left-hand people's fire: each class's eternity is the fitting shape of what it used to do. The verse is therefore the sūrah's refutation of the accusation that this reward is arbitrary: it is exactly what was done, extended and unmasked — and the reader cannot say the scene is favoritism, because the text has already, in its own vocabulary of habit, made the near-rank's lives the content of their couch. The Day that debases and elevates (3) does not invent verdicts; it installs them, and verse 24 states the installation as the whole reward-section's legal basis before the chapter moves, at 27–44, to do the same for the other two classes.

**The Small Word That Ends a Long Bracket**

Finally, notice where the clause falls: at the very end of the scene, after the pearls, where the translators have placed their last insertions. A hanging verbal noun — jazaʾan — is Arabic's spare way of making a sentence do the work of a chapter's hinge, and the sūrah uses it to bracket 15–23 shut: everything between the thrones and the pearls is one noun phrase's content, and the noun phrase is a verdict. The near-rank's furniture, drink, fruit, birds, and companions are, in the chapter's final word about them, not an inventory at all but a statement of what those lives matched. Then, from 27, the sūrah begins the same operation for the companions of the right, with the same spare tools — an exclamation (27), the orchard list (28–34), the mates newly created (35–37), and the closing twin multitude-census (39–40) — proof that the sūrah's style is the sūrah's theodicy, verse counted against verse, habit against habit, the long bracket of mercy closed by a single word that means the fitting.

The three-word clause is thus the sūrah's signature move made visible: a long mercy bracketed, quietly, by justice's short verb.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:25

> There they will never hear any idle or sinful talk —

**Expanded Commentary**

**Laghv: The Word for Everything Spoken for Nothing**

Laghv is not sin's big brother in the Qur'an's usage; it is the class of the useless and the foul — idle, hurtful, pointless speech, the chatter that occupies the gatherings of the proud. The Qur'an's portrait of the believers opens with "those who avoid idle talk" (23:3), and the Stories chapter has them answer slander by turning away (28:55); the verse following this ban states what replaces laghv — "only good and virtuous speech" (26), which in Arabic is the greeting of peace said twice. The near-rank's estate, then, is delimited at its edge by a noise-policy, and this is not decoration: the first thing al-Wāqiʿah says about the companions of the right, once the exclamations are done, is what their ears will never again have to endure. A hearer of 620 CE lived inside laghv — the market's slander, the poet's lampoon, the tribe's taunt — and the sūrah promises, before food or spouses, silence's opposite: a place where no one has to talk back, because no one talks at all except in greeting.

**Taʾsīm: A Hapax with a History**

The second negated noun, taʾsīm, occurs only here in the Qur'an, and the old lexicography accordingly preserved several senses: sin and evildoing generally; a sinful, destructive kind of talk; in one report, the lying and oath-taking that pass for wit. When a word is this rare, the classical commentators cite what they were told, and the range of what they were told — Ibn ʿAbbās glosses sin, others the instigation of sin — agrees on one thing: the Garden excludes not only the pointless but the corrosive, both the word said for nothing and the word said to harm. A single-word hapax therefore carries, in our verse, a double curriculum: the sūrah refuses the trivial and the vicious in one breath, leaving only the middle register of honest, kind speech — which, as 26 will note, is so rare in this world that it needs no list. Verse 25 thus legislates the soundscape of Paradise by subtracting the two thirds of human conversation that every listener can name from memory.

**"There": Whose Garden Is Being Quiet?**

The pronoun in the verse's Arabic — fīhā — has an antecedent the commentators debated: the gardens of verse 12, the scene of 15–24, or even the cups of 18 whose drinkers were just promised no headache (19's "in them" was the cups or the drinkers, and the earlier chapters' parallels attach "lā rafath" — no obscene speech — to the Garden itself, as the Heifer verse legislates for the nights of Ramaḍān (2:187) and the Ranks sūrah for its guests' cups (37:47)). The debates are minute and the answer is not: whichever antecedent wins, the verse keeps the chapter's method of pairing body and word — the head that will not ache (19) and the room in which no word will wound (25) belong to one estate, and its silence is a physical good, like water and shade, distributed to the class that is described at greater length than any other. The near-rank was granted furniture; the right are granted, first of all, a protected ear.

**The Sin of Idle Talk in a Chapter about Denial**

Set the verse against the sūrah's opening: its verdict on the deniers is a speech-crime from the opening line — the Event before which "no one can deny it has come" (2) — and its transcript of their mockery comes in the middle of the chapter: "When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected?" (56:47), which is laghv in the strongest sense: the joke about resurrection, the heckle at the sermon, the word about the unseen spoken for a laugh. Al-Wāqiʿah's anatomy of hell, when it comes at 41–46, will say nothing about their punishment being noisy; it says instead that they were "luxurious" and persisted in the "great sin" (45–46). Verse 25 stands between these two portraits of talk and is the hinge: the saved are defined as those whose speech got nothing out of them and whom nothing got out of, and the lost as those whose conversation about the biggest fact was a sneer. The Garden's quiet is not aesthetic; it is the final shape of a habit the verse's audience could still break.

**No List of Good Words, Only a Ban on Bad**

Notice, finally, how much the sūrah declines to say. It bans the two categories and names no third; it promises no program of praise, no choir, no liturgy in the near-rank or among the right. The classical readers of "peace, peace" (26) were therefore right to treat the exception as the whole content: Paradise's speech is greeting and nothing else, which means speech without task, language with no work left for it except welcome. A religion that made the tongue accountable — the sūrahs innumerable about what is said and what will be witnessed about it — here closes the account with relief rather than regimen, and the relief is precisely proportioned: the same chapter that will itemize cups, fruit, birds, and water for the body itemizes for the tongue exactly one exemption and one sound, and lets the audience hear how long, in this world, a sentence of pure welcome has to be before it is counted at all.

**Nothing to Overhear**

One consequence of the ban deserves its plain word: in the Garden there is nothing worth eavesdropping on. The Qur'an elsewhere insists that every word is already recorded — "he does not utter a word but there is a watcher, ready" (50:18) — and the sūrah's verse 25 turns that cosmic surveillance into relief rather than dread: the record of the saved will contain no idle passage to be read back to them, no foul hour of talk to be weighed, because the Garden itself legislates the material out of existence. The audience lived in a town whose assemblies were duels of words, and this is the sūrah's promise to the man who could not win his: there is a room in which the game has been abolished, and God, who was recording everything, simply has nothing to record.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:26

> only good and virtuous speech.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Leaves Only One Sentence**

The grammar is an exception from the ban on speech, and the result is an extreme: in the Garden of the right and, by the chapter's implication, every Garden, exactly one kind of utterance exists — qīl, a saying, a cry — and its content is salām said twice. That is the whole soundscape. The present translation, reading the clause as description rather than address, renders the pair as "only good and virtuous speech" — the brackets and the paraphrase are the translator's work, and they show how hard it is to keep a greeting in a sentence the original pitched as a salutation. In the Arabic, salām is said twice, and doubling is the idiom of completeness and ceaselessness: repetition standing in for adjectives. The sūrah has, in seven words, described a world in which every sentence spoken is a wish for the hearer's safety, spoken, heard, and repeated, and readers who live in rooms where no sentence is safe will notice that the verse's utopia is not luxury, it is phonetics.

**Salām: The Greeting That Is the Doctrine**

Peace is the word the Qur'an gives its God as a name, the word the angels use at every gate of Paradise in the chapter of thunder (13:23–24), the greeting of Abraham's guests when they came to him in the form of men, and — in the Jonah chapter's line about the believers — God's own salutation: "their greeting will be, “Peace!”" (10:10). Al-Wāqiʿah's verse stands in the middle of that family without quoting any of it: "peace, peace" is all the chapter needs to make the Garden's speech identical with its theology, because the sūrah's whole argument has been that the Event brings safety to the people of nearness and danger to no one in this room. To hear salām repeated is to have the day's verdict spoken back to you as wish rather than sentence, and the exception clause of verse 26 is thus the only place in the chapter where God lets the saved speak — and what He lets them say is the greeting that asks for what they have already got.

**Who Says It: Angels, Guests, or Both**

The verse does not specify its speakers, and the commentaries accordingly divide: the greeting of the angels who enter upon them from every gate — the Thunder sūrah's image supplying the missing subject — or the mutual salutation of the residents, or both at once. Nothing in the Arabic forces a single answer, and the ambiguity is a grace of the scene, because it lets the audience hear the only permitted sentence as the last thing they will be told and the first thing they will say: arrival and habitation share a word. What the chapter leaves undetermined is equally telling: no speaker, no occasion, no end — and an unassigned greeting is a universal one, which is what the exception clause was designed to say: the Garden's whole language is a wish for safety, and the sūrah wants that wish spoken to its frightened audience now.

**Two Words against a Loud World**

Consider the polemic the verse contains, because it is a polemic against noise — the real noise of Makkah's sacred precinct, where the poets competed in the sanctuary's markets, the tribes traded lampoons under the Kaʿba's shadow, and the mushrikāʾ, in the Book's own charge, "made their prayer around the House nothing but whistling and clapping" (the idiom of 8:35, whose two rare nouns the classical readers glossed exactly as avocations of the tongue). A chapter that bans laghv and then permits only salām twice is issuing, at the center of a Paradise description, a critique of the sanctuary's acoustics, and its hearers knew which rooms they were being offered instead. The near-rank's ear is the last possession the sūrah protects before it furnishes the rest, and the protection is total: no vanity, no incitement to sin (25), no satire, no slogan, no claim, no rebuttal — a list of absences that describes, from the inside, what every Makkān gathering was and what the Garden will not be.

**The Bedside Verse**

This clause has a further history worth a sentence. In the same devotional stream that commended reciting al-Wāqiʿah nightly — a practice whose supporting report is judged weak, and the caution belongs wherever the chapter's papyri are quoted — verse 26's doubled salām, which the present translation compresses into "only good and virtuous speech," was what dying people were given to hear, the sūrah's peace said to them in the voice of the chapter, and the comfort literature treated "peace, peace" as the verse's point for exactly this reason: it describes what the dying will hear on the other side of the Event the sūrah's first verses keep sounding. Whatever weight a later age assigns to those reports, the reception shows the verse's double structure plainly — an eschatological soundscape written for ears at their most frightened — and that is how verse 26 should be read here: not as an ornament of the Garden's inventory, but as the chapter's one direct address to its real audience, a promise that the last sentences on the other side of the thunder are wishes, and nothing else.

**The Root of the Religion Is the Word of the Garden**

The sūrah's one permitted sentence carries the community's own name in its consonants: salām, islām, and submission to God all stand on the root s-l-m, and the greeting of the Garden is thus not a pleasantry but the religion's last word — the peace its people surrendered into, spoken back to them twice and forever. A listener who hears verse 26 in Arabic cannot miss that the blessed spend eternity saying the word they entered by, and that the exception to the ban on speech exempts precisely the sentence that means "you are safe."


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:27

> And the people of the right—how ˹blessed˺ will they be!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exclamation, Third Time**

The device returns exactly as it broke out at verses 8–9 — "the people of the right, how ˹blessed˺ will they be; the people of the left, how ˹miserable˺ will they be" (56:8–9) — a phrase named and then answered about, and the sūrah has taught its audience the idiom, so that this third occurrence needs no gloss to be felt. The earlier pair of exclamations stood before any description existed; this one stands at the threshold of thirteen verses of description (28–40), which changes its meaning. The question is no longer "what will become of them?" but "what are they, now that they are what they are?" — the wonder raised at the saved person not as hope but as completed fact, the same astonishment the near-rank's verses earned from a census instead of couches. The repetition tells the audience that the Day's classes are not merely three tiers on a list; each is a surprise, and even a fully described class, viewed from the inside, still defeats the sentence that began its description.

**A Sentence Missing Its Predicate**

Grammatically, "the companions of the right" is a subject without a predicate in the first half, and the second half asks about the predicate it withholds. The rhetorical tradition called this the construction of marvel (mā aʿjaba…): the Arab, struck past description, names the thing and then asks what it is, and the answer is deferred, enlarged, or given in a form that outruns the question — here the thirteen lines of lote and shade do answer it, in the style of a host who replies to "how is he?" by showing you the tent — and the present translation accepts the same task when it brackets, at ˹blessed˺, the predicate the Arabic keeps withheld. This is the middle of the sūrah's architecture and the verse rewards slow reading: unlike the near-rank's introduction (10–11), which was pure rank with no scene, the right are introduced by a question the following lines must fill with scenery. The chapter's rhetoric thus mirrors its economy: the foremost are simply named, because they are past description; the right must be described, because they are still, in a sense, the audience's own case.

**Where the Right Hand Comes From**

The idiom aṣḥāb al-maymana, the people of the right side, is older than this sūrah, and the present translation's "people of the right" hides from English readers that the Arabic noun is aṣḥāb, owners and companions at once and is not an Arab invention; the Book's own neighbors speak of the day when the scrolls are handed — 84:7 promises the recipient of the book in the right hand a reckoning "easy," a near-relative of this verse's joy — and the older scriptures, as the chapter on the Sheep notes, knew a placing of the blessed at the right and the accursed at the left (56's own idiom at 8–9 was shown there to be borrowed currency). But "aṣḥāb" deserves attention: aṣḥāb are owners and companions at once — the people who own the right hand, the fellowship that is right-handedness — and the same noun governs the "companions of the Garden" in the Pen chapter's parable (68:17–32, whose owners promised to harvest at dawn and lost the crop). Al-Wāqiʿah's companions of the right are, in their very title, the counter-parable to those hoarding farmers: ownership in this sūrah names a side, not an estate.

**The Great Middle**

Structural readers of the sūrah should measure what follows: the near-rank got fourteen verses (11–24); the people of the right get sixteen, soundscape to census (25–40), and the people of the left get four lines of description (41–44) before the chapter turns from scenery to anatomy. That proportion is the sūrah's pastoral doctrine. The near-rank is a class almost no one in the room can claim; the left is a warning anyone can hear; the right — and verse 27 announces precisely that class — is the saved majority, described in ordinary good things: lote trees without thorns, bananas in clusters, extended shade, poured water, abundant fruit with no season and no ban (28–33), and elevated furnishings (34). The verse's double exclamation is therefore a door the congregation walks through: it is the class into which an average believer, not the prophet's nearest, is invited to hope, and the sūrah makes the invitation the loudest sentence in its longest section.

**Still a Question After the Answer**

One more effect of the repeated name deserves a sentence of its own, because it is the verse's quietest teaching: the chapter never lets the description close over the wonder. By the time the audience passes the elevated furnishings (34), the newly made companions (35–37), and the closing twin multitudes (39–40), every item the question at verse 27 withheld has been supplied and more, and yet the answer was not that the right-hand people are rich — it is that the question itself, "what are they?", remains the appropriate sentence about any saved person. The sūrah's audience heard the answer in orchard and furniture; the sūrah hears, in its own repeated question, the fact that a human being's rescue from the Event is a marvel that outruns its inventory. That is why the verse's Arabic stops short of predication: the sentence refuses to say "the companions of the right are such-and-such," because the sūrah's God has, in this chapter, already made the question itself the praise.

No one in the room, the verse's syntax implies, owns the title "people of the right" before the Day does; it can only be hoped for, and the chapter's loudest question mark is pitched at exactly that hope.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:28

> ˹They will be˺ amid thornless lote trees,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sidr: The Tree at the Edge of Everything**

The lote tree carries, in the audience's own scripture-memory, the weight of a boundary. The Star chapter sets at the far end of the Prophet's ascent the lote tree of the ultimate boundary (53:14), beside which — in the reports the collections preserve of that night — the Garden of the Refuge is described; and the Saba chapter tells of a lote-lined garden of the most famous hydraulic miracle in Arabia, the Marib gardens of the two right-hand and left-hand plots, which the people of Sheba lost when the dam broke and whose replacement, the verse says, was bitter fruit, fruitless bushes, and a few sparse thorny trees (34:16) — tamarisk in place of lote. Al-Wāqiʿah's companions of the right stand, verse 28 says, among lote trees that have no thorns — the boundary-tree restored to them as plain garden stock. The resonance is double: the tree no human ascent may pass is, for the saved majority, a hedge in an orchard, and the tree that the ruined South Arabian paradise lost is returned in a form that cannot be dammed, flooded, or forfeited.

**Khāw: The Word That Removes the Weapon**

The adjective, khāw, is the negation of thorniness itself — the classical glosses say it without a hedge of detail: no shawk, no thorn, no prickle — and the sheer plainness of the word is the verse's method: the Garden's flora are defined at the start by subtraction, exactly as its drink was (19) and its speech (25).
