Thorns in the audience's horticulture were the price of fruit's defense: the acacia arms itself, the jujube bribes with small sweet fruit and pays for it in spines, and every hedge that protected a crop from a hungry camel also made the crop impossible to pick freely. Verse 28's thornless lote is thus a single-image answer to the whole economics of defense — in the near-rank nobody needs protection and nobody needs guarding against, and the plant world, the first thing the class is placed among, has surrendered its knives. The Torah's curse on the tilled ground brought forth "thorns and thistles" as the mark of the fall; the sūrah's counter-ground grows its fruit bare-armed, and the audience who farmed terraces with stones in them knew exactly what had been undone.

**An Orchard, Not a Pavilion: The Two Rooms of This Chapter**

Compare the furniture of the near-rank with the flora of the right and the chapter's spatial design appears. Verses 15–24 describe interiors: thrones, cups, ewers, inlay — a pavilion, hosted, served by pages, its company seated face to face on raised stuff. Verses 28–34 describe an orchard: trees, shade poured out, water running, fruit in abundance, beds spread — goods without walls, the open-air wealth of a people who slept under stars and fenced nothing. The sūrah's two saved classes do not receive the same scene at different magnifications; they receive two different scenes, and the difference is itself a theology of enough: what pavilion-luxury says to the court, orchard-abundance says to the shepherd, and the chapter's list of open blessings — unprickled trees, endless fruit, water needing no drawing — is the poorer half of Makkah's wish-list transcribed directly into eternity. Verse 28, first in that list, sets the tone: the Garden's door for the majority is a fence that no longer exists, in a thicket that cannot scratch.

**The Left's Menu, Read Backward**

Set the lote tree against the only other tree the chapter names and verse 28 becomes argument, not botany. The people of the left "will certainly eat from ˹the fruit of˺ the trees of Zaqqûm, filling up ˹their˺ bellies with it" (56:52–53), then drink boiling water on top of it (56:54), and the Ranks sūrah asks which lodging is better, the blessed's or "the tree of Zaqqûm" (37:62) — and the right-hand's lote, thornless and honeyed, is the direct negation of that menu's every property: fruit with no defense versus a fruit that is the defense, food that nourishes versus food that scalds. The sūrah's plants, like its water and shade, are distributed in pairs, and the pairing is its most patient form of preaching: it tells the audience that nature is not innocent, that the same earth grows a lote and a zaqqūm, and that what each plant will be on the Day — provision or punishment — is decided, in this chapter's physics, by which class you are standing in.

**For the Average Ear**

Close the verse where it landed. Its first listeners were not the foremost — the sūrah itself reserves that bracket for whoever ran ahead of them — and they were not the deniers its fury is addressed to; they were the crowd of the middle, the people who fasted, gave little, sinned, and hoped, and verse 27 had just told them their class opens with a question God thinks too big to answer and the answer is an orchard. The thornless tree, for that ear, is the most ordinary and most radical promise in the passage: that their lives, without heroics, will be sheltered, that what they touch of God's creation will not hurt them, and that the meanest goods of their meanest seasons — shade in July, a date off a low branch, water from an open spring — are, in His economy, not consolation prizes but the estate itself. Al-Wāqiʿah, at verse 28, hands the middle of its audience a tree they can lean against, and calls it, once, free of thorns, and then moves on to the acacia.

The right are placed, as the foremost were placed, and placement is this chapter's verb for grace: verse 28's opening bracket ˹They will be˺ supplies nothing but the bare locative the Arabic carries — in lote trees — and the preposition quietly does in four words what the near-rank's fourteen verses took to say, that somebody has put them somewhere safe.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:29

> clusters of bananas,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bananas in the Qur'an: The Translation's Decision**

The Arabic says ṭalʿ, the fruit-spadix of the acacia, and the English here says "clusters of bananas" — a decision with a long pedigree rather than an eccentricity, for the classical collections transmit from ʿAbd al-Razzāq's circle and others a report from Ibn ʿAbbās, through Mujāhid, that the ṭalʿ of the Garden is the banana tree of India (mawz), and al-Ṭabarī records the same identification alongside the plain-botanical reading. The translator brackets nothing, which means the rendering leans toward the exotic gloss, and the reader should register both: acacia pods stacked like beads on the thornless desert tree, or bananas piled one above another in their hands, either image saying the same thing about the fruit of Paradise — it comes already clustered, countable, and made for gathering. The verse is two words in Arabic and this is how briefly the chapter builds its orchard.

**Makhṭūf: The Fruit Nobody Climbs For**

The adjective is makhṭūf, from kh-ṭ-f, to snatch — and the sūrahs gloss it as fruit hung low so that whoever wants it takes it without a ladder, without a tool, the bunches bent down within reach of the shortest hand. The same root gives Arabic's word for a robber taken red-handed (mukhtaṭaf), and the verse keeps the snatching image intact: in the Garden the thing you snatch is free, hanging, and nobody's property — fruit that cannot be stolen because it cannot be owned. Together with verse 28's thornlessness, the two adjectives remove the two costs of harvest in the Ḥijāz: pain and labor. The date-palm of the plain was climbed, beaten, netted, and guarded, and every one of those verbs belongs to the world the Event is unmaking; here the orchard has been pre-harvested by its own physics, and "clusters" (the present translation's word) says the stacks are already gathered for the guest's hand. No verse in the chapter argues harder against ownership in fewer syllables: what you can snatch belongs to everyone, and what belongs to everyone cannot be a hoard.

**Why the Banana Reading Travelled**

The exotic gloss survived in the commentaries because it pleased the logic of the scene: the banana is the tree whose fruit the desert knew only through trade, whose bunches are famously stacked, and — in the reports — whose stalk dies after fruiting so that a new one rises in its place, a tree that is all provision and no orchard-management. Those details arrive in the sūrahs of the tafsīr with the flavor of travelers' tales, and this commentary reports them as what the exegetes repeated rather than as botany verified here. [UNVERIFIED: the banana-stalk-dies-and-regrows detail as description of the Indian mawz — transmitted lore in the commentaries.] What matters textually is that whichever tree is meant, verse 28's adjective (khāw, thornless) is read across both, and the pairing gives the right-hand people fruit that defends nothing, needs no ladder, ripens in ropes, and cannot be fenced.

**The Pod the Nomad Knew**

Return to the acacia reading, and the verse acquires its social color. The ṭalʿ was the desert's serviceable tree: its pods (ḥummāḍ) were eaten, its gum was licked, its shade was counted and its thorns cursed; a thornless ṭalʿ heavy with stacked pods was a poet's fiction of the perfect year. Al-Wāqiʿah grants the fiction to the middle class of the saved as inventory — verse 28's lote and verse 29's ṭalʿ are a pair, hedge-tree and food-tree, both disarmed — and the promise's humility is its own eloquence. The foremost sit on thrones; the right sit at a picnic under their own best trees. The sūrah is telling the congregation that Paradise scales to its dwellers' wishes: the nomad will not miss a palace, because the good acacia, without the thorns, is the palace the audience could imagine, and God chose to describe it without straining their image of the good.

**Clusters and the Chapter's Economics of Enough**

One arithmetic point closes the verse. The right-hand list (28–34) counts: two trees, a shade, a water, a fruit that is "abundant" (32), beds (34) — seven items, and not one of them owned. Verse 29's stacked clusters are the smallest unit of the chapter's anti-economy: fruit gathered is fruit spent, and fruit stacking means the supply outruns the picking. The sūrah that will soon reproach the traders of Makkah for a life spent in "rivalry in increase" (102:1) sets before them, for the right, fruit that increases without rivalry, hanging in ropes that no one cut, in a grove no one fenced. Clusters are abundance the hand can see, and verse 29 gives the audience the quantity of the Garden measured in what their own eyes could count: not gardens, but bananas, stacked, for the taking, forever on schedule.

**What the Two Readings Preach**

The acacia reading and the banana reading do different pastoral work, and the sūra preserved both because each said something the audience needed. Read as ṭalʿ, the verse restores the nomad's own landscape with its harm removed — the tree that pricked the camels and fed the hungry, disarmed, a paradise of home: what the desert owed you in pain it owes you back in clusters. Read as the Indian mawz, the verse promises the thing the desert never had at all — a fruit of the warm lands, brought down by trade, stacked by nature — a paradise of arrival, the caravan's cargo grown on the spot for the very people whose caravans carried it elsewhere. The first says nothing good is lost from your own country in the Garden; the second says nothing good is kept out of the Garden by distance. Al-Wāqiʿah, which has already dissolved the distance between horizon and couch, does not adjudicate; it hangs the adjective "stacked" over either tree, and lets both sermons ripen.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:30

> extended shade,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Luxury the Desert Could Not Refuse**

Shade, in the sūrah's world, was not comfort; it was infrastructure — the date-palm was grown for it as much as for fruit, the tent was engineered for it, and the midday rest (qaylūlah, the sun's ceasefire woven into the day's prayer-rhythm) was a survival tactic. To promise "extended shade" to the people of the right is therefore to promise the middle class of the saved their own highest good, stretched. The adjective mamdūd, passive of madda, to stretch out, prolong, spread, carries the double sense without fuss: the shade is long in space (unbounded, no edges to walk out of) and long in time (noon does not move it, the sun does not retire it). The sūrah's next line about the left will prove how load-bearing the gift is: "in the shade of black smoke, neither cool nor refreshing" (43–44) — the lost get a shade too, in the chapter's grim parallel, and the comparison shows that what makes Paradise paradise, in al-Wāqiʿah's register, is not the presence of the exotic but the absence of the noon.

**Mamdūd: A Passive Verb Doing the Work of a Roof**

Watch the grammar of the four-word block that verses 29–31 form: "clusters of bananas, extended shade, flowing water" (29–31) — three nouns, three passives, and no agent. Nothing sets up the shade, no one dug the stream, no hand stacked the fruit; the participles' voice does in three words what the chapter argued at length against the makers of things: that these goods arrive from a will the guests never met. The same three-word pattern governs the sūrah's argument-half — the water that "comes down from the clouds" (69) and the fire "you kindle" (71) — and verse 30 sits on the Garden side of that seam: the world's shade is a contest of builders, the Garden's is a given. The passives are therefore small theological statements, planted in landscape diction, and any reader tempted to see 30 as pretty description should notice that its syntax refuses the reader a single human verb.

**Shade Against the Sun of That Day**

The Qur'an's eschatology runs on one meteorological fact: the sun of the Standing is close. "When the sun is wrapped up" (81:1) and humanity sweats toward its chin in the gatherings — this sūrah itself quotes no such line, but its audience needed no quote, and "extended shade" enters the inventory of the saved where the heat is the chapter's unspoken adversary (the left's "scorching heat," 42, names it at last). The shade is thus eschatological furniture: what the Day's weather makes of you depends on whose shade you are under, and the Book's most consoling image of God's own protection — the seven sheltered in His shade on the Day with no other shade, preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī's book of charity from the Prophet's list — belongs to the same economy. Al-Wāqiʿah does not cite it; it simply places extended shade among the middle class's goods and lets the audience draw the inference that shade, on that Day, is not meteorology at all.

**No Edges, No Hours**

Extended means, in the reports on the sūrahs of this passage, shade without a sun to move it — the classical readers note that in Paradise there is no shadow to chase the light, because there is no direct sun to cast one, so the "shade" of verse 30 is not the absence of noon but the exemption from it. If only you knew, says this sūrah of a great oath elsewhere (76), and here the economy of the image keeps the same counsel: the saved do not experience time as the world's creatures do, in the slant and the burn, and the verse's mamdūd is the one word that makes the point without metaphysics. The near-rank had no shade listed at all — pavilion-thrones are their cover — and that gradation is instructive: what the nearest have as architecture, the right have as atmosphere; the shade is the middle estate's canopy, spread where the ceiling would be, and it costs the occupant nothing but the dying that they lived.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Cooling**

Finally, where 30 sits. The right-hand passage has moved from thornless trees (28) to stacked fruit (29), and the next line will run water (31) and pile up fruit (32–33) before the beds (34); verse 30, the only atmospheric item in an agricultural list, is the hinge that keeps the inventory from becoming a banquet catalogue. Its effect on the recitation is temperature: the ear is let cool between two clusters of plenty, and the sūrah — which began with the earth shaking and the mountains to dust (4–5), images of a world turned inside by heat and shock — places, mid-Promise, the one word every terrified body reaches for. Extended shade, then, is al-Wāqiʿah's softest verse and, for its first audience, its most physical: the chapter about being raised and debased says, at the center of the reward, that the saved will not be hot. That sentence has been consoling people longer than any of its theology, and it will outlast all the rest of the commentary.

**The Verse That Rebuilt the Qaylūlah**

That midday rest is not a marginal custom in the sūrah's world; the reports preserve it as practice alongside the noon prayer, and the day of the Ḥijāz belonged to it absolutely — the streets emptied at high sun, and the cool hours before dawn and after ʿaṣr carried all other business. Verse 30's "extended shade" is, read against that clock, the abolition of the schedule itself: the saved never again calculate their labor around the sun's transit, because the transit no longer rules. This is the verse's small revolution of time as thorough as the flood-inversion at 31 — the Garden does not merely have pleasant hours; it has no tyranny of hours at all — and al-Wāqiʿah teaches it without a single abstract noun. The audience heard "extended" and understood the end of the qaylūlah's necessity: a life in which rest is not stolen from the day's heat but is the day's shape. The shade is the sūrah's clock without hands.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:31

> flowing water,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Maskūb: Water That Pours Itself**

The participle s-k-b is the verb of pouring — an intransitive image made passive: water poured out, running, ungarrisoned. Arabic's ordinary Paradise-hydrology is different: rivers "flowing beneath" the gardens, tajrī min taḥtihā, the settled irrigation-phrase of an agrarian afterlife, and the choice of maskūb instead of any form of that phrase shifts the water from canals to cascade. The sūrah's neighbors exploit the same verb for the opposite face of the Day: the seas "poured forth" in the overwhelming day (81:6), and the people of the left will, at verse 42 of this chapter, get "boiling water" in the same family of images (hamīm, a different word — but the chapter keeps water hot where they are, poured where the right are). Verse 31, in other words, is not a stream; it is water in the act of giving itself, three lines after fruit that gave itself off the tree, and the passive participle is the whole verse, as in verse 30, a roofless verb.

**Water as the Chapter's Chosen Argument**

No element in al-Wāqiʿah does more work. The sūrah's central interrogation will ask, point-blank, "Have you considered the water you drink? Is it you who bring it down from the clouds, or is it We Who bring it down? If We willed, We could make it salty — will you not then be grateful?" (68–70) — and that argument's premise is verse 31's promise made visible: water is the proof-text because no one can claim to have made it, and the Garden's "flowing water" is the same element stripped of its two terrors — drought and brine. The right-hand people's poured water is the answer to the question the chapter will ask the deniers, and reading 31 after 68–70 makes the architecture plain: the sūrah's orchard and its courtroom are built from one stock of facts, and the water that flows for the saved is the very water the lost drink boiling (55), the thirsty camels' guzzle, in the chapter's cruelest image (55).

**The Flood Inverted**

Arabic's most violent water is also its most nourishing, and the Book keeps the two faces together: Noah's drowning was a rain "poured down" in the Samaritan chapter's words — the heavens opened "with water pouring in torrents" (54:11) — and verse 31's maskūb cannot be heard by any trained ear without that resonance: the water that erased a civilization is, for the right, poured for drinking and irrigation in a Garden where it can no longer rise. The sūrah's theology of rain elsewhere is explicit — "We send down water in measured amounts and then give it to you to drink, and you are no hoarders of it" (23:18) — and the clause "you are no hoarders" legislates the exact miracle of verse 31: water no cistern can claim. Extended shade overhead, ungoverned water below; the two passive nouns of 30–31 describe a landscape without the two things that made landscape a contest at Makkah — sun and storehouse.

**No Cistern, No Quarrel**

The hydraulic society of the Ḥijāz ran on ownership of water: wells with tenancies, springs with clans, the camel-lines that queued, the law of first draw that the tribal code guarded with knives. Verse 31 gives the middle saved class a water with no owner because it has no source to own — it is "flowing," in the present translation's bareness, a participle without a noun for the vessel, without the word "well" or "river," and the grammar enacts the end of the water-law: nothing to fence, nobody upstream. That is why this three-word verse belongs in a sūrah whose hoard-argument runs from the mountains (5) to the sowings (63–67): al-Wāqiʿah's critique of property is not a sermon against riches but a description of the place where riches are incoherent, and poured water is its shortest exhibit. A gift that cannot be stored cannot be hoarded; a stream that cannot be dammed cannot be inherited; the right-hand people drink from the death of possession, and it is called, simply, flowing water.

**The Sequence Rests: Shade, Water, Rest**

Set 31 in its run. Verse 29 stacked the fruit, 30 cooled the air, 31 moves the water, and 32–33 will return to fruit with abundance and seasonality, before the beds at 34 — the passage is built as a walk: orchard, shade, stream, orchard again, and finally the couch. Shade, water, rest are the three needs of a body in the sun, and the sūrah lists them as its Garden's core goods to a class of farmers, herders, and porters. The near-rank, seated indoors with pages and crystal, had their water in ewers (18); the right get theirs in the ground, free, in the open air, and the two versions of one element are the chapter's quiet rebuke to the idea that nearness changes the goods it only redistributes. The Garden is one garden, and what the foremost drink from a cup the right kneel beside, and the sūrah's peace lies exactly in that shared thing.

**A Cup, a Stream, and the Question of Whose Water**

Commentators pressed a detail the verse hides: whether the right-hand people's poured water feeds their trees or answers their thirst, since the next line supplies fruit, and the next the drink. The Arabic lets both stand — "flowing water," no verb, no purpose, no meter — and that purposelessness is the verse's point of etiquette. In the audience's law, water had a purpose assigned the moment it was found: the well irrigates this palm, this clan; the sūrah's sower's argument (63–67) presumes fields that someone owns and someone waters. Verse 31's water is before all assignment: it flows because that is its nature in this estate, not because a schedule says so, and the guests neither dig nor allocate nor watch the level. Al-Wāqiʿah thus does to hydrology what the Event did to geology: the solid things move for the right in the direction they were always going, without owners, and the only hand on the tap belongs to the God who asks the deniers whether they brought the water down (69) — while the sūrah, more kindly, tells the middle of the saved that it already has.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:32

> abundant fruit—

**Expanded Commentary**

**The One Quantitative Noun in the List**

Fruit (faakihah) has run through this chapter since the near-rank's table (20) — the right-hand orchard has been its ṭalʿ all along (29) — and now, at 32, it appears for the first time with an adjective of amount: kathīrah, abundant. Every other item in the right-hand list (28–34) is described by condition — thornless, stacked, stretched, poured, raised — and only the fruit gets a number-word, and a vague one, which means the sūrah's only quantitative promise in the whole orchard is the negation of scarcity rather than a figure of it. Kathīrah answers the economy its audience lived: Makkah's fruit was imported, rationed in the lean months, and priced at the gate — "abundant" is, to a market town, the eschatological adjective. The dash that the present translation places at the end of the line is its way of keeping the Arabic's suspension: "and abundant fruit" hangs, and verse 33 completes it with two negations; the abundance is not even stated before it is qualified. The verse, like the fruit, has no edges.

**Never Out of Season: Killing the Calendar**

The completion is verse 33's pair — "never out of season nor forbidden" (33) — and the first negation does the larger work. Maqṭūʿ means cut off, interrupted, and in the date-country it names the one fact every farmer knows: harvest is a season, and the rest of the year is storage and trade. To promise fruit that is "lā maqṭūʿah" is to abolish the calendar for the middle class of the saved — no glut and no famine, no "out of it," and therefore no preservation, no hoarding by the one who has it in February, no price. The Qur'an elsewhere describes this very blessing as the miracle given to Quraysh — the winter and summer journeys that feed the town (106:1–3) — and al-Wāqiʿah removes the journeys from the Garden entirely, because the journeys existed for what the trees of the right will not need. Time in verse 32 is no longer a ration; it is a season that never ends, and the abundance line is the sūrah's longest answer to short years.

**Nor Forbidden: Killing the Taboo**

The second negation, lā muḥarramah, landed in the audience's ears with a specific clatter, for the pre-Islamic sanctity-laws had made whole categories of food "forbidden" by vow and cult — the beasts and crops dedicated away from tables by the saʾiba, the wāʿila and the ḥāmī of the Heifer chapter's catalogue (5:103), with their rulings on who could eat what in what months. The verse's muḥarramah is the same root's adjective, and "never forbidden" promises the right-hand people an orchard without cult: fruit owed to no one, sacred to no altar, restricted to no gender, householder, or month, and it is the mirror-image of verse 18's cup, which the world's law had made forbidden and whose Garden-version removes the harm instead (19). Abundance plus no taboo equals provision with no priest — the sūrah's quiet republican sentence about food, which every class in the chapter eats out of, from the near-rank's chosen fruit (20) down.

**Abundance and the Religion of Rivalry**

The chapter's own neighbor, the sūrah of Rivalry, charges that "rivalry in increase diverts you until you visit the graves" (102:1–2), and against that background verse 32 reads as the sūrah's counter-diagram: kathrah, the competitive more-ness that consumed the audience's life, becomes, for the people of the right, kathīrah — fruit that increases at no one's expense, in no one's ledger, to no one's credit. The two nouns are one root, and the verse's mercy is that it takes the civilization's master-word and purges it of the contest. No one at Makkah could out-give that harvest or undercut it; abundance that cannot be weaponized is the only abundance al-Wāqiʿah grants the world's appetite permission to want. It is worth hearing how gently the chapter legislates: the fruit is not denied to the poor by being made rare, and the rich are not denied by being punished — everyone simply eats, and "abundant" is the sūrah's single word for the end of envy.

**The Fruit of Two Classes, Compared Once**

Set verse 32 against verse 20 a final time, since the chapter has made the comparison inescapable. The near-rank were given fruit "they choose" — the grammar of preference, a wish with an object; the right are given fruit "abundant, never out of season nor forbidden" — the grammar of sufficiency, a supply with a guarantee. One class receives option; the other, assurance; and the sūrah's gradation is not that the right fare worse — no line in the chapter says the nearest get more — but that their portion was described in the terms of a banquet while this is described in the terms of a harvest, the most basic security a hearer could want. Read beside 69's parallel for the rewarded — where the given book in the right hand comes with "eat and drink, a reward for what you sent ahead, in sweetness" — the two late sūrahs agree that the middle of the saved eats first and asks no questions, and verse 32's single adjective, "abundant," is their shared summary of what enough means in a chapter whose God owns the seasons.

**Abundance in the Sūrah's Mouth**

Kathīr, the adjective's root, is the word the Qur'an uses when the world's numbers impress it and disappoint it: the party that said "we are more in number" (54:45, of the defeated host; the claim echoed by the Day-of-Judgment crowds), and the hoard-counting sūrah's rivalry in increase. In the Garden, kathīrah is applied only to goods, never to people, and never with the comparative edge the world's kathra carries — the abundant fruit outranks no one, and the right-hand census (39–40) that is "many" from former nations is a swarm of mercy, not of victory. The verse therefore reuses the audience's favorite metric and strips it of scoreboard; abundance is the one quantity al-Wāqiʿah blesses, and it blesses it in fruit, where quantity harms nobody. That is why 32 can end the orchard's middle without ending its argument: the many who eat the many fruits are the many whom the chapter's arithmetic was counting from verse 6, and the sūrah has quietly changed what its own word "multitude" means.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:33

> never out of season nor forbidden—

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Negations, Whole Genera Closed**

The clause is built the way verse 19 built the cup: lā plus the first noun, wa-lā plus the second, a double denial on indefinite forms that shuts down two whole categories rather than two particulars. Out of season, cut off (maqṭūʿ), is denied, and forbidden (muḥarram) is denied — time and law, the two frameworks inside which the world eats, and nothing in the Garden's menu is legible in either. The sūrah's audience knew both bars on their orchard: a date is either in its month or it is not, and what is sacred or tabu is either yours this season or yours never. The parallel is close at hand in the chapter of The Raising of Hands, where the rewarded are told their fruit "will hang within reach" (69:23) — fruit that cannot be denied because it cannot be kept from the hand — and the sūrahs of the Mercy and the Event keep the same formulaic pair of negations for the Garden's produce: the two words always travel together because together they exhaust what scarcity is. This verse, alone of the orchard's lines, is a sentence of policy, not scenery.

**What the Sacred Months Taught the Ear**

"Mutaharram" in the audience's usage was not only food-law; the same root named the months in which war was forbidden (9:5; 2:194) — a truce imposed on time itself, when roads were safe and markets held their fairs. That resonance means verse 33's "nor forbidden" is not simply the abolition of taboo but the generalization of the truce: the Garden is the sacred month that never exits the calendar, a place where the rules that made eating an occasion for peace or for priestly veto have been universalized into permanent permission. If the pre-Islamic sanctities fenced food by cult, and the months fenced travel by treaty, the right-hand orchard is the state in which both fences run out of business: nothing is barred, nowhere is unsafe, and the sūrah that borrowed the "people of the right hand" idiom from ancient judgment-lore finishes by borrowing the oldest Arab institution of protected time and extending it without end.

**Joseph's Seven Years, Answered**

The Book's most exact scene of "out of season" sits in Joseph, where Egypt stores grain through seven lean years after seven fat, and famine is the plot's hinge (12:47–49). Every hearer of al-Wāqiʿah lived, in the market's price of a measure of wheat, the memory of a season that cut food off, and lā maqṭūʿah speaks first to the stomach's calendar — the fruit never interrupted — before it speaks to law. The verse's genius is that it requires no miracle to be understood: no reader in the room missed that a garden without a lean month is a garden without a king, without granary-policy, without the rich buying up the year. Joseph's storehouse existed because seasons did; cancel the seasons and the whole apparatus of who eats when dissolves by itself. This is why the sūrah spends the line on negation rather than on description: abundance in the Garden is defined, as its drink was (19), by the subtraction of the mechanism that made the world's abundance a tool.

**The Pair Inside the Chapter**

Read 33 within its own sūrah's system of pairs and its placement tells the story: 19 negates the cup's harms (headache, intoxication), 25–26 negates the estate's speech (vanity, sin), 28 negates the thorn, and 33 negates the two bars on the fruit. The Garden is assembled out of subtractions, and the near-rank's passages follow the same design with different items (no choosing required of season; no law needed but hospitality). The chapter never says what the fruit of the right looks like; it says, and twice (32–33), that the conditions that made fruit a contest are absent. The technique is worth naming, because it answers the perennial complaint that this sūrah's Paradise is furniture-obsessed: it is not obsessed with objects but with deprivations removed, and every noun of the orchard hangs on one of these negations like a fruit on a branch that needs no ladder. The middle class of the saved is promised, in five negations, a world without the five ways the world says no.

**"Will You Not Then Be Grateful?"**

One line later in the chapter's own argument, the sūrah asks its question about rain and drought and closes it with "will you not then be grateful?" (70) — and 33 is that verse's promise-twin, since the object of thanks in the courtroom half is exactly what the orchard half grants without end. Gratitude in al-Wāqiʿah is the right relation to provision that can be cut off; the saved have passed out of the condition that makes gratitude a duty into a state that makes it their weather — no season, no prohibition, and therefore no scarcity to thank for, only the Giver for the gift's sake. That is the quiet reversal the verse completes before the beds of 34 arrive: in the Garden, gratitude stops being the payment for fear of famine and becomes the content of the day, and the sūrah's audience, who could not escape their calendar or their tabus, hear 33 as the abolition of both, and the end of every reason not to say, forever, what the fore-rank say first: al-ḥamdu.

**A Note on the Verse's Afterlife**

The pair of negations outlived the sūrah in Islamic law's own debates about the Garden: the jurists asked, reading 33 against the Heifer's food-rules, whether any ruling of the world — the prohibitions of carrion, blood, and swine flesh (2:173) — has a mirror in Paradise, and the standard answer took this verse as its proof-text: the Garden abrogates prohibition itself, not its permissibility-by-exception that the world knows, where what is barred is licensed only in hunger. Verse 33 thus became, in the commentaries, the locus classicus for a theology of law's end: the saved majority eats with no mufti between them and the tree, and the sūrah, which had spent its courtroom half attacking tabus of cult (5:103's saʾiba was the audience's own inventory), closes its orchard by unhallowing hallowing. "Never forbidden" is the shortest sentence in the chapter for the shortest definition of the hereafter the Qur'an ever gives: a place where the only sacred is the safe.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:34

> and elevated furnishings.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Furush: The Bed Before It Was Furniture**

The noun furush, plural of firāsh, is the Qur'an's own word for the earth as mattress — the verse of Ta-Ha praises the Lord "who made for you the earth a bed (firāshan) and the sky a canopy" (20:53), and this sūrah's opening cataclysm takes that bed away under the feet (4). What the chapter does at 34, accordingly, is not add a piece to an inventory but restore the primal good: the firm thing you lie on, back to being spread, and this time raised (marfūʿah), safe from the shake that unmade mountains. The root's domestic range is telling — firāsh is bedding, the saddle-cushion under the rider, anything spread to receive a body's weight — and the Garden's "elevated furnishings" keeps all of it: the saddle's rest after travel, the mattress after the day's work, the marriage-bed after the long betrothal. Al-Wāqiʿah gives the middle saved class not couches of state but the whole vocabulary of being received by something solid that stays solid.

**Marfūʿ: The List's Last Movement Is Upward**

Every item in the right-hand passage runs in a single direction, and the participle says it: the shade is stretched (mamdūd, 30) outward, the water poured (maskūb, 31) downward at will, the fruit stacked (29) toward the hand — and now the beds are "elevated," the last verb before the mates. The chapter's physics of the Day were vertical from the start: the earth flattened, the bodies "raised and debased" (3), the census sorted by height; and the right-hand estate answers that with furniture that lifts no effort, height without a stair. The near-rank had the higher word — thrones, surur (15), and they themselves were the "elevated" of 3 — while the right get the elevation moved down into their bedding. That is the sūrah's gradation, measured in inches: rank in the room above, dignity in the mattress below, and both in the same chapter's one act of raising, which verse 3 promised would, on the Day, belong to God alone.

**The Beds of This World, Read Backward**

The first audience of al-Wāqiʿah slept in layers of what they owned: a mat, a rug, a saddle-blanket spread at the tent-wall, and the camel's furniture doubled as the house's — furush in the old Arab usage included the saddle-cushions themselves, the very gear of travel converted into bed. A sūrah that promises elevated furnishings to herders and porters is speaking, in their furniture-word, about rest converted out of the means of production: what carried goods by day held the body by night, and the raised beds of the Garden are the same objects finally lifted off the ground, no longer both livelihood and sleep. This is the verse's most concrete kindness, and no theological paraphrase should cover it: the people addressed by al-Wāqiʿah spent their lives on their gear, and the chapter's orchard ends with the gear made into a throne-height bed, in a place with no load to carry, which is what the shade (30) and the unowned stream (31) had already been saying in their registers.

**Why the List Stops Where It Does**

The orchard-run — trees, fruit, shade, water, fruit, beds — ends at 34 without the near-rank's pages and cups, and the omission is architecture, not poverty: the middle estate's passage is an outdoors, and beds are the last object a sky-room can hold. The next verses (35–37) will bring in the mates, and the chapter thus closes its furniture with the one piece that has no height in it: a bed is where a body is received, and after it, the sūrah needs no more "also." The translators' punctuation keeps the rhythm of the Arabic — wa-furushin marfūʿah, "and elevated beds," a phrase hanging in the same accusative as the fruit and the shade — and readers should notice that the grammar refuses to rank the items: strewn water and raised beds sit in one undifferentiated list, seven nouns of equal dignity. Al-Wāqiʿah does not itemize the saved majority's reward in tiers of value; it stacks nouns the way verse 29 stacks fruit, and the abundance of the middle class is, as promised, without order of precedence among its goods.

**Sleep Without Night in a Chapter at the Bedside**

One resonance closes this verse: the sūrah that was, in its reception, recited beside dying beds — the weak report of nightly recitation against poverty, noted already at the chapter's opening, gave way in the devotional literature to bedside use of its nearness-passage (85–94) — ends its right-hand inventory with elevated beds, and the dying ear cannot separate those two facts. The furush of the Garden are, for the household watching a person's last mattress, the chapter's counter-object: a furniture of rest that will not receive a corpse. The sūrah does not exploit that association — no line in it mentions the grave or sleep's brother — but its placement of raised beds immediately before the mates created anew (35) and at the end of the middle class's description gives the bereaved one image to hold against the other, and the image is horizontal, elevated, and empty, waiting, in the chapter's choreography, for the guest to be laid there and raised. That is as much as verse 34 will say to that room, and it says all of it in two words.

**The List Ends on the Body**

Notice the passage's last object before its verbs change: trees, clusters, shade, water, fruit, fruit's conditions — and then beds. Everything in 28–33 was environment, something the saved walk through or sit under; verse 34 is the first item that exists solely to receive a body, and the inventory therefore ends where it must, with lying down. The sūrah has described the Garden as the middle class would actually inhabit it — not as a view but as a rest — and the "elevated" of the beds is the list's final grace-note: the receiving thing is also lifted, so the body that arrives tired arrives, at last, above the ground that shook (4). Environment, then embrace; the orchard closes in a bed.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:35

> Indeed, We will have perfectly created their mates,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First-Person Plural at the Center of the Orchard**

After seven lines of passive, agentless nouns — stacked, stretched, poured, raised — the verse breaks into "Indeed, We created": the chapter's only direct "We" between the opening's "We" of 57 and the argument's, and the royal plural lands on the softest subject in the passage, the spouses. The switch of voice is the sūrah's own commentary on 28–34: the trees and beds were described as if self-assembling because that is how guests receive hospitality — nobody sees the host's hands — and when the text finally shows the hands, it shows them for the marriage. The emphatic inna and the perfect verb together answer the audience's probable question about the source of all this: the list has a maker, the maker is introducing Himself at the moment He introduces the intimate, and the sūrah thus binds, in one sentence, its theology of creation to its promise of companions — the same bind that the argument-half (57) will use for the first creation's proof.

**Anshaʾna: The Verb of Sprouting**

The verb is anshaʾa, which in Arabic's oldest usage means to bring forth, to make sprout — the verb of plants coming up (nashʾ, the rising of growth) — and the present translation's "created… perfectly" for inshaʾan carries that germinating sense; the noun is the masdar of the same root, and the sentence "We made them in [a new] making" reads, in the Arabic, as a cognate-accusative doubling that makes the novelty the point: not repair, not transplant, but a fresh sprouting. The near-rank's verses said nothing about the ḥūr's origin (22–23); the middle estate's mates are expressly newly made, and the choice of a botanical verb for the making of spouses suits an estate that has been described, since 28, as an orchard — in the Garden even the beloved is a growth, with roots in the same poured water (31), and al-Wāqiʿah keeps marriage inside its ecology rather than its furniture.

**"Their Mates": The Possessive That Keeps Marriages**

The pronoun attaches the wives to the husbands as property of relation, azwājuhum in the idiom of the Book (2:25; 23:5) — "their spouses," the same possessive that the Thunder chapter uses when the believers enter the Gardens "and the righteous among their fathers, spouses, and descendants" (13:23). That continuity is a doctrine: the sūrah of the Event does not dissolve the married into the angelic, and the middle class of the saved are promised, not replacement companions, but their own — newly made. The promise of 13:23's family-reunion is not quoted here, but its logic stands behind the possessive; the audience who buried spouses in Makkah heard, at the center of an orchard-promise, a pronoun that does not let death file the marriage. Commentators noted the verse's tact: the near-rank got companions described by beauty, the right by making — the middle estate, whose marriages were worn with age and loss, receives the text's one explicit note of re-creation, as if to say: the love you knew is the love that will be, grown again from the seed of what God first sprouted (56:57–58).

**Perfectly: The Translator's Small Dogmatics**

The present line's "perfectly" renders a general perfection of the making — kamāl in the sūrahs' glosses — and the single bracketed word decides a whole old dispute: whether the new creation here means renewal of youth and beauty, or renewal of the very mode of being (the body of this world re-created as the Garden's body). The translators' adverb takes the first side; the verse's own doubling of the verbal noun takes the second, and both readings are sūrahs' — Ibn ʿAbbās's circle preserves "young again, and of equal age" (which 37 then legislates into the list), while the mutazilite readers noted that "We made them" after "We will raise the beds" answers the resurrection of bodies with the resurrection of persons, and the passage's placement — between beds and virginity — keeps the two questions touching. The bracketed "perfectly" is thus a miniature of the sūrah's whole reception: one word, three centuries of debate, and the audience's grief about aging bodies still addressed, gently, in the adverb.

**Why This Verse, and Not the Next, Carries the Weight**

Modern readers often start at 36 ("making them virgins") and stop there, but the sūrah's sentence with the heavy voice is 35: the divine We, the sprouting verb, the doubled making, and the possessive of marriage all stand here, and what follows is predicate-adjectives (virgins, loving, equal-aged — 36–37) attached to that making. The structure is a claim about agency before a claim about qualities — the text says first that the spouses are God's new work, and only then what the work is like — and that order is the sūrah's reply to the accusation that Paradise's promises are merely appetitive. The appetite is described after the doctrine, and the doctrine is about who makes: every intimacy in the near-rank's chapter belongs to a God who builds growth from nothing, and the verse's emphatic "Indeed" is the chapter's own corrective inserted into its reward-list, so that a listener charmed by the orchard hears, at its center, the making verb of creation reasserted over the making of lovers, once, and without embarrassment.

**The Four "We"s of This Sūrah**

The chapter gives the divine plural to only four moments, and their distribution is its rhetoric. "It is We Who created you — will you not then bear witness?" (57) opens the argument; "If We willed, We could reduce [the harvest] to chaff" (65) and "If We willed, We could make [the water] salty" (70) are the argument's two threats; "We have made [the fire] a reminder and a provision for the travellers" (73) is its mercy-note; and one "We" stands in the promise — "Indeed, We created them [the mates] and made [them anew]" (35). The pattern is exact: in all this sūrah's Paradise, the only time God says We is when He makes spouses. The furniture, the streams, the shade, even the near-rank's thrones — all are described in impersonal passives; the first-person plurality is reserved for the one act the audience needed the Maker to take personal credit for, because it was the one they might doubt: that the marriages of this world arrive in the next intact, and newly made by the same hands that built the orchard around them.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:36

> making them virgins,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Aṭhār: The Word, Its Range, and Its Grammar**

The noun is abkār, plural of ʿadhrāʾ, and the sūrah's sentence puts it in the accusative as a state (ḥāl): "We made them" — in what condition? "virgins." The grammar matters for how the verse should be read: the word describes the manner of God's making, not a status imposed on persons with histories, since the sentence's subject is the Creator who, at 35, sprouted the spouses anew. Abkār in Arabic's wider use is not only sexual; it is the word for the unhandled, the unworked, the prime of anything (the ʿadhrāʾ of youth, of a thing before use), and ʿAdhrāʾ, the unworked, is what a pearl, a wild ewe, and a girl not yet touched by a hand all share in the old lexica's glosses. The present line's single word carries a whole aesthetic of firstness, and al-Wāqiʿah applies it, in the middle estate's passage, to the most intimate of the saved goods — the firstness, the chapter's orchard implies, of a condition that God's fresh making alone can give (35).

**The All-Merciful's Verse Beside This One**

The chapter of Mercy, the sūrah that stands before al-Wāqiʿah in the codex and shares its reward-lists, describes the Garden's companions with a formula close to this verse's: "maidens of modest gaze, who no human or jinn has ever touched before" (55:56; 55:74), the negative of mass (touching) repeated as pure fact." The pair of chapters is instructive, because Mercy's formula adds what our verse suppresses — the prior husband — and adds the gaze, the contentedness — while the Event keeps only the bare state, abkār. Between the two, the tradition's question surfaces: are these new women other than the wives of the world, and who, if remarried, is hers forever? The reports the sūrahs collected answered with a saying transmitted from the Prophet about the woman whose two husbands both enter Paradise — she will be with the one of best character — a tradition the collections grade variously and which this commentary cites only as the exegetes did, with caution. [UNVERIFIED: the "best of conduct" report — grading disputed in the collections; no number fixed here.]

**Virginity as the Removal of the Past, Not of Memory**

The discomfort that modern readers report at this line — and it should be reported, not smoothed — belongs less to the adjective than to the world's habit of grading women by sexual history, a habit the sūrah actually undermines if it is read with 35. Verse 36's abkār is a divine passive's complement: God made; what was made has no prior, and priorlessness is stated of the making, not of the made. The ʿadhrāʾ-quality is an attribute of the new creation's grammar, and the chapter, which has just insisted at 61 that God can "transform and re-create" its audience "in forms you do not know," is not describing a body with a deleted past so much as a body whose past no longer functions as a category. The Garden's spouses do not forget their histories in the text's own sense of forgetting (they are "their" mates, 35, with the marriage kept by the possessive); what is abkār is the present of the relationship — nothing that has been handled, and therefore nothing used up, in the chapter's vocabulary for firstness.

**The Pearl and the Virgin: The Two Classes, Compared**

The near-rank received ḥūr "like hidden pearls" (22–23) — value defined by no hand having touched them — and the right receive mates "made virgins," the same property stated as the absence of prior handling in the estate where it is most existential for the spouses themselves. The sūrah, in its two registers of untouchedness, grants each class the identical core: intimacy without wear, possession without prior owner, and the pearl's seabed secrecy translated, for the middle estate, into human terms. The comparison also shows the near-rank's restraint and the right's precision: the foremost got a simile, and the right got an adjective of condition — and it is the adjective, not the simile, that the audiences have argued about for fourteen centuries, because "abkār," in a list about saved men who had been married in the world, asks the question "who exactly are these?" and 35's possessive has already answered.

**Why the Verse Is the Shortest in the Passage**

One word — the line is two in Arabic with "making" — and the terseness of 36, flanked by the heavy creation clause before it and the pair of epithets after it (37), is the sūrah's way of refusing elaboration. Nothing is said about how, when, or by what mechanism; the text will not let the adjective stand as a noun of possession because the sentence's subject, to the end of the passage, is the verb of making. Exegetes who pressed for detail (the reports about the barren women of this world, the aging restored to thirty-three and then never again, the children who will be caught up at the gates for their parents) filled the silence from other material, and the collections preserve those glosses as glosses rather than verse. The chapter's own answer to the question of the saved body's biography is 35, and verse 36 supplies one predicate; that is the verse, and its brevity is a boundary: the promise is stated once, without scenery, and the sūrah moves on at once, in 37, to the quality that makes it mutual — love and equality of age.

**A Word the Sūrah Never Apologizes For**

The text offers no justification for 36, and that silence is worth more than any apologetic this commentary could supply: the adjective stands as one clause among five, grammatically subordinate to the making (35), rhetorically in service of the estate's completeness, and theologically unexplained. What the chapter does explain, around it, is that every gift in this room — fruit, shade, water, spouses — comes from a God who "ordained death" (60) over the audience he is addressing, and who can "transform and re-create in forms unknown" (61). Verse 36 is one of those unknown forms; the sūrah declines to make it familiar, and readers of every century are left with the sentence the text actually has — made, not inherited; new, not repaired — rather than with the culture of virgins that later imagination, on both sides of the argument, supplied.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:37

> loving and of equal age,

**Expanded Commentary**

**ʿUruban: The Noun That Means Being In Love**

The first adjective is ʿurub (plural ʿuruban, as here) or ʿarīb, and the old lexicography — the lexica preserve it carefully — glosses it as the woman inflamed in love for her husband, ʿāshiqah in plain terms, the same root giving the verb for a wine that "goes to the head." Al-Wāqiʿah therefore places, between "virgins" and "of equal age," the one word in the orchard-passage that names a feeling rather than a property, and the sūrahs' most repeated note on it is this: the adjective is mutual in the real case, since the spouses' list began at 35 with "their mates" — a possessive of reciprocity — and ʿuruban supplies the interior the sūrah will not describe elsewhere even for the near-rank. The All-Merciful sūrah, whose companions are "of modest gaze, who no human or jinn has ever touched before" (55:56), supplies the other half of the psychology: restraint toward all else, flame toward the one. This is the sūrah's only love-word in its two Paradise portraits, and it sits on the middle class's spouses.

**Atrāb: The Age Difference Ends**

The second noun, atrāb, is the plural of tirb, the equal in age, and its pastoral content is precisely calculable in the audience's lives: the Makkān household ran on mismatches — girls given to older men, widows to second husbands, the wife aged beside a younger, the young wife beside the impotent, and every one of these an inequality that the afterlife's intimacy would freeze into permanence if death changed nothing. The sūrah removes the asymmetry: the spouses are created, at 35, "of equal age," and the verse's quiet radicalism is that it lists mutuality as a gift to both parties — a fresh sprouting in which neither partner is the one who waited, aged, or was outgrown. Commentators noticed that atrāb answers also the men's fear on their own account, the shame of the mismatched pair made eternal, and the sūrah's one-word repair of it belongs to the estate of the ordinary saved: the near-rank got pearl-silence, the right got the age-gap abolished, and the chapter's gradation in this domain, as everywhere else, runs through furniture and bodies, not through love.

**The Three States: Past, Present, and Like**

The passage's adjectives, read in order, sort the marriage into time: abkār (36) removes the past — no one handled, nothing used before; atrāb removes the future's drift — no one will outpace the other into age; and ʿuruban supplies the present tense, the love as the estate's daily weather. The sūrah has, in three words at the end of its longest reward-list, described a marriage as the only thing it could be without time's asymmetries, and the structure is too exact to be accident. The chapter's opening did the same with the Day's terrors — the denial silenced (2), the earth shaken (4), the classes sorted (6) — and the middle of its promise repeats the surgery on the most intimate of the audience's categories: the pair is rebuilt, past, future, and present, and each clause removes one way the world's marriages leak. Each removed leak has a gift opposite it: no history to be owned by, no future to drift apart in, and a present filled with the only noun the list allows itself about the heart.

**The Widow's Question in the Commentaries**

The line drew the question the sūrah could not avoid provoking: if the saved are re-mated, whom does a remarried widow belong to in the Garden? The collections preserve a saying raised to the Prophet — cited by the compilers of the Sunan and weighed by the critics — that she will be with whichever husband was best in conduct (ḥusn al-khulūq); this commentary repeats it only as reception-history and flags the uncertainty of its grading; [UNVERIFIED: the report's exact wording and grade vary among the collections.] What matters in our passage is that the questioners found no veto in 37 — the verse's love-adjective, ʿurub, is the very quality the report made decisive — and the sūrah's own materials pull the other way: the marriages of this world are, at 35, "their mates," and 13:23 has the whole family entering, fathers, spouses, descendants, together. The verse's equal-aged mutuality was read, by the commentators with the widows in mind, as the end of rivalry for a place, not the reassignment of it.

**Why Love Is in the List at All**

A final observation about the sūrah's place in the Book. Al-Wāqiʿah is recited in the tradition for provision and in the madhhabs for the dying, and its Paradise has — uniquely among the late Meccan pairs of chapters like it — a word of feeling in the reward-list. The Ranks sūrah describes its companions by the eye (37:48 — modest gaze), the Rising by faces (75:22–23 — bright, looking at their Lord), the chapter of Man by drink and garments; here the middle estate's passage closes on "loving." Whatever the codex's arrangement was meant to teach, the reciter who reaches verse 37 at a bedside finds the sūrah promising, to the family of ordinary believers, not scenery but the re-found tenderness itself, and equal footing for its exercise. The chapter's theology has been, from verse 1, that the Event sorts; verse 37 tells them what the sorted middle class gets to keep from the world — a love with nothing left in it to be embarrassed about — and the single adjective, among all the furniture, is the sūrah's own most human note, placed where the music of its orchard-list resolves.

The word's root also flavors wine in Arabic idiom — love that goes to the head — and the sūrah has already served that drink, minus its harms (19): in this estate even passion arrives side-effect-free.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:38

> for the people of the right,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Preposition That Dedicates the Orchard**

Li, the little dative, opens this verse — "for the people of the right" — and after ten lines of passive scenery it is the sentence in which the sūrah finally tells the reader who the scenery was made for, even though the answer has been announced since 27. The preposition of benefit and assignment does work the audience understood intimately: in the gift-language of the Arabs, "this is for you" is what turns an object into a given, and the sūrah now says it, formally, of the whole estate: the mates with their three epithets (36–37), the beds, the water, the thornless grove — all of it, for them. The bracketing of the present translation ("˹They will be˺" back at 28) anticipated the pronoun; the Arabic saves the dedication until now, so that the list stands between announcement and assignment, and the reader's question "who gets all this?" is answered exactly once, at the end of the answer. That is not redundancy; that is how hospitality is staged.

**An Inclusio Around the Middle Estate**

Structurally, 38 closes the parenthesis that 27 opened: "And the people of the right—how blessed will they be!" (27) … "for the people of the right" (38), fourteen verses framed by the class's own name at both ends, with the soundscape (25–26) serving as a threshold and the twin census (39–40) spilling past the frame into the next block. The sūrah's three estates are each ringed by their titles — the near ones named at 11 and answered at 14, the right here twice, the left at 41 and again at 51 — and the ring-form is the chapter's way of keeping the audience inside the census while it tours the properties. Nothing in this passage is description for its own sake; every ewer, every cluster, returns the reader to the question the exclamations asked, and 38 is the hinge-word of the answer: this, for them — and the question "who are they?" remains, as 27 decided, open until the Day itself.

**"A Promise Made in Public: The Grammar of Assignment**

The translators print 38 as a fragment attached to what precedes, and the Arabic is exactly that exposed: no verb governs the phrase — the list simply turns toward its owners. Exegetes asked why the sūrah does not say "We gave this to them," and the best answer is that the bare dative keeps the giving continuous: a past-tense gift would locate the reward in one act, while "for them" states a permanent relation — the estate is theirs the way a promised dower is owed, without expiry. It is also, once noticed, the grammar of the whole chapter's hope: the saved are those for whom things are said — "for those who fear the Standing" is the All-Merciful sūrah's refrain (e.g., 55:46, 62, whose two gardens are both "for" someone), and al-Wāqiʿah, recited beside the same deathbeds, places its longest "for them" here, in the middle estate's dedicatory clause, where the ordinary believer, who is not the near one, learns the sentence is addressed to them.

**The Class That Was a Census, Then a Room**

Watch what has happened to "the people of the right" across the passage. In verses 8–9 they were only a line in the Event's sorting — "the people of the right, how blessed will they be" — a name inside a list of three groups becoming visible as the mountains came apart (6–7). From 27 the chapter has run the class through an entire estate, and 38 now lets the name close a room they are already standing in: the audience of verse 8 heard the title as verdict; the reader of verse 38 hears it as deed of gift. The movement from verdict-name to dedicated-place is the sūrah's complete answer to its opening's terror, and it is worth measuring for the near-rank as well, who never receive a "for them" clause at all — their passage closed on recompense (24), not dedication, because the near ones need no deed; they were never outside.

**What "For" Excludes**

The dative, lastly, has a shadow: a list dedicated to one class is not available to the others, and the sūrah never softens that. Verse 38's "for" is the same preposition that will seal the left's accommodation, "this is their lodging on the Day of Judgment" (56) — "for" in this chapter is assignment, not invitation, and every "they will not be…" (33) that precedes it confirms that the orchard's boundaries are the estate's. Readers who want the sūrah's Paradise to be universal have to take the whole architecture, exclamations and all: the chapter counts, sorts, dedicates, and then, from 41 on, does the identical operation for the lost with its own terrible "for them." That severity is why al-Wāqiʿah has always been read, in the devotional literature, as a sūrah to be completed, never skimmed; the "for the people of the right" line at 38 means nothing without the lodging at 56, and the chapter's mercy — which is enormous, fruit without season, love without age, shade without noon — is, by design, not free for everyone in the same way, because the way it is given is exactly what the Day is for.

**The Bracket as Blessing**

The present translation's ˹who will be˺, set before the census at 39, is a small artifact of the same dedication: the Arabic's fragmentary lists force English to invent a verb, and every invented verb is one more witness that the reward here is stated, not narrated — given like the shade and the water, without a scene of giving. The sūrah's style and its soteriology rhyme: the right do not receive so much as they find themselves, at the Day, in what was already "for" them (38), the way a guest finds the tent pitched.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:39

> ˹who will be˺ a multitude from earlier generations

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Census Returns, and Changes Its Noun**

The chapter's counting device comes back for the second time, and the nouns are different in a way the translations hide at their edges. The near-rank were "a swarm from earlier generations" and "a few from later" (13–14), the swarm-word laḥq and the few-word qalīl; the right are kathrah, a multitude, "from earlier generations" and again "from later" (39–40) — the same min-partitive, the same paired halves, now filled with the opposite magnitude. Al-Wāqiʿah thus teaches its audience to read the census as a scale: the reader who heard 13–14 and flinched ("only the few?") arrives at 39 and is handed the many. The bracketed "˹who will be˺" is the translator's patch over the Arabic's ellipse — the line is a bare accusative of state — and the bareness is the style of these lists: classes as quantities, quantities as mercy, the arithmetic done by God.

**The Multitude of the Ancient Peoples**

"Earlier generations" — the same phrase that carried the near-rank's swarm — now does new work, because the right's multitude from the old nations is, in the sūrah's own theology of history, the crowd the old prophets actually saved. The near-rank's "swarm from the former" named the champions of each age; the right's multitude is the congregations: the followers of Noah, the rank and file of Abraham's household, the ordinary Israelites of the Exodus, the believing remnant of every destroyed city. The Qur'an elsewhere warns that "most of people" will not believe (12:103), and the sūrah's own courtroom half will call the left "the misguided deniers" (51) — but nowhere does this chapter quote a count of the damned against a hope of the saved, and verse 39's multitude from the former is the quiet surprise that balances the census. The multitude from the former is the sūrah's answer to any despair that only saints are saved: the middle estate is big, and its first citizens are the nameless believers of the old centuries.

**Why Repetition Preaches Better Than Novelty**

The chapter could have described the right's numbers with new language, and chose instead to re-run the device of 13–14 with the adjective inverted. That is repetition with a purpose the audience of a recited text feels: the second census lands faster, because the first taught the grammar, and the inversion is felt as a gift — the mechanism that said "few" for the heroes says "many" for you. Comparative readings should note how rarely the Qur'an's late Makkān sūrahs repeat a structure this exactly inside one chapter; here it is the chapter's argument that the near-rank and the right are one saved humanity at two intensities, and the identical frame, changed only in quantity, makes the unity visible. The census even shares its halves — former and later — with the Day's whole gathering at 49–50 ("Most certainly, earlier and later generations will surely be gathered ˹together˺ for the appointed Day" (56:49–50)), so that the saved classes' counts are cut from the same cloth as the total roll-call.

**"Later Generations" Will Come Next Verse**

A short note of structure: 39 leaves its twin, 40, hanging — the multitude "from later generations" — and the pair should be read as one sentence with a caesura, exactly as 13–14 were. The sūrah's list-technique (each estate: scene, then census, then the next) means 39–40 are the middle class's seal, and the break at the line's end is recitational, not logical; in the mushaf's verse-marks the two are separate breaths. For the listener the pause between 39 and 40 is where "earlier generations" resolves into the audience's own era, and the sūrah has set the pause with intent: the saved multitude of the ancient world is not a foreign nation; it is the parent body of "the later," and the later will be the next verse's subject — Makkah, and then every congregation until the Hour. The census, like the shade, is extended.

**The Numbers as Doxology**

Finally, what the arithmetic does in a chapter whose God raises and debases (3). Every census-line — few, a swarm, a multitude, and at 51–52 the left's own swarms of former and later — is an act of divine counting that the sūrah never rounds up into vagueness. The saved are not "legion," the lost are not "none": the chapter keeps its proportions in public, and the effect on the reciting audience is the opposite of discouragement, because the numbers they belong to are the many, and the many have, from 28 to 38, already been given the thornless trees and the ageless love. A sūrah that began "When the Inevitable Event takes place, then no one can deny it has come" (1–2) ends its middle estate on the line that the Event's biggest class is on the side of mercy. That is not statistics; it is praise, and the sūrah has made the audience recite their own hope as a fact about God's arithmetic, right where the left's swarms will take the reader's pulse next.

**Multitude as Mercy, Not Majority Argument**

One caution the verse's defenders should keep: "a multitude" is not, in this chapter, an appeal to numbers as proof. The Qur'an's own charge against the crowds is sharp — "if you obey most of those on earth, they will lead you away from God's path" (the sense of 6:116) — and al-Wāqiʿah has just finished telling its hearers that the majority of history sat with the left (51–52 will say so in so many words). Verse 39's comfort is therefore not "most people make it"; it is that the saved middle is no elite club, that belonging to a multitude, not a spiritual aristocracy, is the normal shape of deliverance, and that the same warning chapters which say "few are My grateful servants" (34:13) here say the right-hand crowd is vast. Both statements come from one sūrah's God, and the tension is the chapter's moral instrument: do not count on numbers, do not despair of them either; be a member of the many, and let the counting be someone else's job.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:40

> and a multitude from later generations.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Pair**

Verse 39's multitude from the earlier generations leans on this line the way the near-rank's "swarm" (13) leaned on their "few" (14): the census is one statement with a hinge, and the hinge opens toward the audience. "Later generations" — al-ākhirūn — is the sūrah's own term for you: not the centuries of the prophets whose ruins the Makkān trade routes passed, but the communities the chapter's recitation would itself create. The sūrah's grammar keeps the later half in the accusative of state, a bare phrase appended to the scene, and the effect is a sentence the reader finishes by existing in it: every congregation that ever heard al-Wāqiʿah was, at the moment of hearing, a candidate for the multitude its final census line reserves. The text's temporality is a loop — a book about the end of time whose verse about "later" includes all readers to the Hour.

**The Many, Where the Foremost Were Few**

Set 40 beside 14 and the chapter's distribution of hope appears. To the race-callers, the near-rank: a swarm from the former, a few from the later — small then, smaller now. To the middle estate: a multitude both then and now — and the inversion at the audience's end of history is deliberate comfort for ordinary believers. The sūrah will not promise its hearers the first place; it promises them, twice and symmetrically, size: you will be many, and many you will be saved. That is a different pastoral from the prophet's company's pride of precedence (9:100's "first forerunners of the migrants and helpers," cited already at verse 13), and it balances it: the chapter holds both the elite call and the wide gate, and its arithmetic — few, many, many — is one of the Book's plainest statements that salvation history is not a competition in numbers. The few of 14 and the multitude of 40 are saved by the same God, and the sūrah is careful that neither line should flatter or despair.

**What the Word "Kathrah" Has Learned to Mean**

The multitude here answers the chapter's own use of abundance since verse 32, where fruit's adjective was kathīrah, "abundant," and it completes the quiet re-education of the sūrah's favorite root. In the audience's life, kathrah was competitive — the rivalry in increase named by the short sūrah "You are diverted by rivalry in increase, until you visit the graves" (102:1–2), the counting of numbers as a civic sport. Al-Wāqiʿah now attaches the same word to a census of the saved (39–40) and to fruit (32) — multitude without rivalry, quantity that is received, not racked up — and verse 40 is the human end of that lesson: the later generations arrive as many, and their "many" cannot be credited to anyone's strategy, because the census is spoken by God before the Day. The verse's one-word adjective, repeated from 32, is the sūrah's way of converting the community's vice into its comfort.

**Generations, Not Elect**

Notice the noun doing the counting here: qarn, generations — the same as in the near-rank's census (13–14) and the Day's roll-call (49–50) — and qarn in Arabic is a unit of time defined by the people who share it, "the generation of such-and-such," with a neutral ring that elect-language lacks. The sūrah thus describes the saved majority as an era's population, not a club's membership: a multitude from each generation, not a multitude of saints. The commentaries, reading 40 against 13–14, extracted the obvious pastoral: the near-rank are individuals, and the middle estate is whole cohorts, which is why the chapter gives the individuals thrones and the cohorts an open landscape of fruit and rain — the estate sized to the population. And the same word qarn binds 40 to the sūrah's repeated warnings about "the people of the former generations" whose fate was recorded (e.g., the Korah-type destruction narratives in the story-half of longer chapters): generations are the unit of history here, and the saved later generation enters, at verse 40, the same archive as the saved former one, file beside file.

**The Verse the Reciters Inhabit**

The last point is the reception and the verse are the same fact. Whatever the first listeners of Makkah — a persecuted handful in the 610s — could feel at "a multitude from later generations," the line has been fulfilled by the plain historical datum the sūrah's own recitation now proves: al-Wāqiʿah is chanted daily, in every century since, in millions of mouths, and each generation that heard it sat, textually, inside verse 40. No commentary should sentimentalize that — the verse promises multitude, not the multitude's virtue, and the chapter's two censuses for the left (51–52) will show the sūrah counting the lost just as massively — but the structural fact stands: a verse about "the later" written at the beginning of a community is, by the community's survival, one of the most literally vindicated lines in the Book. Belonging to the later is, here, the argument: the sūrah has already proved it has a future; the census leaves the verdict to the Day.

**An Audience of the Future Perfect**

The line's tense is, in Arabic, an ellipsis of state — "a multitude from later generations," with no verb at all — and the effect on a reader centuries along is uncanny: the sūrah's "later" becomes every reciter's present, and the census never closes. Preachers from Umayyad pulpit to modern classroom read 40 as prophecy fulfilled in the community's size, and the reading stays honest as long as it remembers the pair: the multitude of the later is the middle estate's multitude, while the near-rank were counted few in both directions (13–14). The verse is thus neither boast nor boast-refuting; it is an open bracket over history, sized to the congregation that keeps arriving inside it, generation after generation, each hearing itself counted before it has believed.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:41

> And the people of the left—how ˹miserable˺ will they be!

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Exclamation, and the Last**

The chapter now pays the full price of its own rhetoric: the exclamation that opened at verses 8–9 — "the people of the right, how ˹blessed˺ will they be; the people of the left, how ˹miserable˺ will they be" (56:8–9) — and re-run at 27 for the blessed, re-runs for the lost, and the third iteration is the last time the sūrah will use its signature device. The repetition has by now taught the audience to lean forward at the formula, and the lean is the sermon: the saved passages rewarded the expectation with orchards and companions, and verse 41 sets up the identical musical figure to do the opposite — the listener's body learns the pattern as comfort, and the sūrah strikes it as loss. That is the moral beauty of the verse's placement: the sūrahs of the commentators, long accustomed to this sūrah's repetitions, note how often re-echoed structures soften the ear before the blow falls, and here the softening is the sermon.

**The Bracketed Word That Answers 27**

In Arabic the two exclamations are identical: mā aṣḥābu-l-maymana, mā aṣḥābu-l-mashʾama — "what [is the state of] the people of the right/left?" The sūrah says nothing about bliss or misery; the translator decides, and the decision is now twice visible: ˹blessed˺ at 27, ˹miserable˺ at 41 (and at 9), the pair of English adjectives the bracket reveals as exegesis. The verses themselves are open throats, and the chapter keeps them symmetrical — a point worth making in a sūrah so often read as lopsided toward description: its two final classes are introduced by the identical withheld predicate, and the difference is made not in the exclamations but in what follows them. Mercy, in this chapter's arithmetic, gets fourteen verses of scenery and two more in the soundscape; the left gets three lines of weather (42–44) and the cause of it. God's detail, the sūrah's structure suggests, is spent where rescue is possible; description is a form of care, and care is withheld last.

**A Geography of Hands**

"Left-hand people" completes the idiom this commentary traced at verse 8: the Book of Matthew places the goats at the left hand as the sheep at the right (25:33), the ancient Near East knew the left as the inauspicious side (the left hand's eating and cleaning duties, the right hand's covenant-feasts), and the Arabic mashʾama, from sh-m-l — the shmal, the north wind's cold and leftward side — is already unlucky in the pre-Islamic poet's mouth. Al-Wāqiʿah takes a symbol whose badness the audience inherited and, in the chapter's physics, makes it literal direction: the people of the left are the same people verse 6 sorted when the crowd became three groups at the unfixing of the mountains.
