The sūrah does not demonize the left side; it finishes the sorting it announced, and the idiom — borrowed, ancient, bodily — bears the verdict without embellishment, which is why the verse needs no description of its own beyond the three nouns to come.

**"How Miserable": The Absence That Replaces the Answer**

Verse 27's withheld predicate, "how ˹blessed˺," was filled by the readers with lote and shade; 41's ˹miserable˺ will be filled by scorching wind, boiling water, smoke — but the sūrah's design keeps both classes, in their intro-lines, unspeakable: the saved are "too blessed to say," the lost "too miserable to name," and the identical grammar for both refuses the audience a voyeur's advantage in either direction. The present translation's brackets make the refusal uniform — two adjectives, one for each — and the verse's real teaching is in that uniformity: on the Day there will be classes whose condition outruns the chapter's own vocabulary on both sides, and al-Wāqiʿah, which has made its entire argument out of what the solid world cannot be trusted for, says here what it says in 56:74: God makes things, and the verdicts are His, and the human words for them (blessed, miserable) are bracketed inserts doing their best.

**What the Chapter Will Not Let the Listener Forget**

After 41 the sūrah never returns to the right-hand estate's furniture; the ear, once turned leftward, stays there for the rest of the chapter — through the anatomy of the punishment (42–46), the mockery answered (47–50), the menu of the lost (51–56), the argument from semen to stars (57–77), and the dying scene (83–87) that re-runs the classes at the bedside. That distribution is why verse 41's single cry has so often been called, in the preaching literature, the pivot of the sūrah: not the climax — the sūrah gives the left no climax, only weather — but the hinge that shows the audience that everything pleasant they have just memorized has a matching door, and the door is named by a sentence whose content, like its opposite, had to be bracketed by the translator to be heard at all. How miserable, indeed: the sūrah declines to elaborate, and its reticence is the last courtesy it extends — or refuses — to the class it is closing out.

**The Name That Is Only a Direction**

What the left are called is a hand, not a heart: aṣḥāb al-mashʾama, the people of the left side — and the sūrah never tells us, in these verses, what their creed was. The audience of 41 already knows, from the opening's charge (2) and the mockers at 47, who the class contains; but the label stays directional, and that is al-Wāqiʿah's method: the Day sorts by file, and the file reads as a direction of turning. Where other late sūrahs itemize the crimes — the denial of the Repayment (which this chapter named at 2 in its most compressed form) — ours, at its hinge of dread, gives only the hand. The misery, bracketed by the translator, is left to be learned from context: how miserable the people of the left will be, in a chapter that has just spent fourteen verses showing what the same noun's opposite looks like, furnished.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:42

> ˹They will be˺ in scorching heat and boiling water,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Samūm: The Wind That Is a Verdict**

The word ṣabāʿ, rendered "scorching heat" here, is ṣamūm in the Arabic — the wind of piercing, from a root of burning through and through, the sirocco that the audience fled at midday and that their poets made the counter-shade to the garden's. The sūrah's punishment is therefore not invented weather; it is the region's worst day raised to an estate, and the selection tells you everything about al-Wāqiʿah's method of hell: it subtracts from the known, it does not add to it. The near-rank's delights were their goods perfected — cups with no headache, beds elevated, shade extended — and the left's torments are their miseries, kept exactly. Verse 42's "scorching wind" is also, in the chapter's system, the answer to verse 30: the right are in stretched shade; the left are in moving air with no relief in it, and wind in a desert is, precisely, what shade is not.

**Hamīm: The Water That Cannot Be Water**

The second noun, maʾ, boiling water, ḥamīm, is the Book's fixed word for the drink of the lost, and the wider Qur'an supplies its exact profile so consistently that this verse needs no adjectives of its own: the Cave sūrah's parallel has them cry for relief and be answered with water like molten brass that sears the face, an evil drink and an evil companion (18:29) — and the Muhammad sūrah's rivers-passage ends by placing, opposite the four promised rivers, "water that tears their insides" (47:15, quoted above at this chapter's cup-section). Al-Wāqiʿah sets its own poured water — flowing, ungoverned, free — at verse 31, so that the two "waters" of the chapter, thirty verses apart, are the same noun family's two ends: maskūb poured for the right, ḥamīm boiling for the left, and the sūrah's hydrology is its theology of what a gift becomes when the Giver is refused. The translator's bracket ˹They will be˺ supplies, again, the bare locative: in these two elements, the verse says; they live inside weather that will not nourish.

**The Anti-Orchard in Four Words**

Compare the inventory of the estates at equal depth: the right stand "amid thornless lote trees, clusters of bananas, extended shade, flowing water, abundant fruit… and elevated furnishings" (28–34); the left are "in ṣamūm and ḥamīm, in a shadow of smoke" (42–43). The sūrah's parody is structural — where the saved had trees, shade, and water, the lost have wind, water, and a parody of shade; two of the three goods survive into the punishment only as their harmful twins, and nothing like fruit or bed is allotted at all. This is the exegetes' point, made without rhetoric: what Hell gets from the vocabulary of Paradise is the absence of the good things, not their excess — no fire-worms, no inventories of new tortures, just the world's own elements with the mercy extracted. Al-Wāqiʿah, which built its whole argument on creation's water (68–70) and fire (71–73), conducts its eschatology as applied cosmology: the same sky's products, two different relations.

**Prepositions of Placement, Once More**

A small grammatical fact carries the verse's doctrine: "in ṣamūm and ḥamīm," fi again — the same preposition the chapter gave the saved ("in the Gardens of Bliss," 12) and the mates ("for the people of the right," 38). Placement in this sūrah is, as at verse 15, the whole of the afterlife's grammar: nobody is shown walking toward their estate, earning the address, or leaving it; the classes are put in, and the put-in is described only by what surrounds them. That is why the left's two "in" items are elements rather than architecture — the wind and the water are total, un-housed, with no walls and no beds and no pages circling, and verse 42's bleakness is produced entirely by the preposition plus two nouns. The sūrah has no language for prison, because its language is for dwelling, and what it does to the lost is to give them a weather and call it their dwelling-place. The eloquence of that refusal is the verse's real sermon.

**What the Verse Refuses to Add**

Finally, the restraint: the sūrah of the Event never elaborates the torments of 42 — no duration, no degrees, no scene of the damned begging (a scene the chapters of prostration and of the Fire do stage in full dialogue). Verse 42, like 43 and 44, is a clause of nouns; its humanity lies in that laconic quality, and readers who expect the Qur'an's Hell to be baroque will find, here at the end of al-Wāqiʿah's longest description, only three images and a cause ("Indeed, before this ˹torment˺ they were spoiled by luxury, and persisted in the worst of sin" — 45–46). The chapter's logic is consistent: elaborate mercy, summarize judgment; describe the saved as persons with rooms, and the lost as persons dissolved into conditions. What is added, in 51–56, is the left's own menu of zaqqūm and boiling drink — which is still not torture for its own sake but the sūrah's closing echo of hospitality inverted. Even in its terrors, al-Wāqiʿah remains a chapter about being hosted, and verse 42 is what hosting looks like when the guest's name is refused at the door.

**Two Nouns, One Verdict**

It is worth ending the verse on its arithmetic: the left's estate, in this description, has exactly two objects, and both are things the audience drank or stood in daily — wind and water. Everything else the sūrah had offered the saved it declines to negate here: no fruit-line, no bed-line, no mate-line; the punishment does not need invented horrors because the plainest elements, unredeemed, are enough. That minimalism is the chapter's most severe rhetoric — God does not describe what He has built for the lost so much as what He withholds from the building — and it matches the distribution of detail across the whole passage: the right got seven nouns of estate, the left got weather, and the difference in verbosity is, itself, the verdict, stated in the sūrah's own grammar of care.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:43

> in the shade of black smoke,

**Expanded Commentary**

**ʿIhab: The Word for a Sky That Has Caught Fire**

The noun ʿihāb carries, in Arabic's usage, both the leaping column of fire from a blaze and the dense smoke rising over it — the beacon the Bedouin read at distance, smoke meaning household or danger. The line itself says ẓill, "shadow," attached to the smoke, and the old glosses read the sūrah's companion-word yaḥmūm — which the Cliff chapter uses for scalding water: "except boiling water and ˹oozing˺ pus" (78:25) — as black because intensely hot; the present translation prints the color it infers, "in the shade of black smoke." Within our chapter, the domestic sense — smoke above a fire that burns — makes the punishment's skyline exact: the lost stand in the shadow of their own cooking-fires, while the near-rank sit beside water poured for them (31). The sūrah that denied the world any lasting solidity has, at minimum, given every class a skyline; the left's is made of what burning leaves behind, low enough to press on the head. And the "shade" here is, of course, verse 30's word minus its properties; verse 44 exists to do the subtracting.

**Shade Satirized**

The right got "extended shade" (mamdūd, 30) — the one luxury the sun makes impossible at noon in the audience's world, granted to them in perpetual extension. The left get ẓill, the same noun, and its one attribute is that it is of smoke — and verse 44 then deducts the two properties that make shade shade: "neither cool nor refreshing." The device is satire at the level of a preposition: the lost receive the word for every creature's relief, stripped of its contents, and the sūrah thus enacts what its own argument-half (57–74) insists creation is: provision is the gift inside the object, and remove the gift and you have not made a lesser provision, you have made an unhoused menace. Shade that neither cools nor shelters is the verse's whole theology of an afterlife without God's favor: the forms of blessing persist as fossils in the place of refusal — that is why the left's inventory is, item by item, the right's.

**Blackness: The Sūrah's Palette**

Al-Wāqiʿah has kept its colors sparse, and the few it uses cluster here at the end of the description. The face of the near-rank was bright in the neighbors' Paradise (75:22–3, "On that Day some faces will be bright, looking at their Lord" — the chapter's own counter-light, quoted earlier at verse 16); the left's sky is yaḥmūm-adjacent black, the smoke's color; between the two the sūrah painted fire's whiteness ("crystal-white" is the Ranks' wine, 37:46, this chapter's neighbor) and the green of brocade nowhere. The palette is thus moral, not decorative: light and color in this chapter belong to description, black to the undescribed, and the smoke at 43 is the sūrah's only painted darkness — the shadow it throws is, the next verse says, unqualified by anything that would make it weather. The chapter that opened by pulverizing the mountains' color into "scattered dust" (6) ends its two estates with a grove (28) and a soot-cloud (43), and the two images are the whole eschatology it offers: growth or smoke.

**"A Shadow" in a Chapter of Placement**

The preposition returns — "in the shade of black smoke," fi — and the reader of verse 42 will have heard the echo's chain: the saved are "in the Gardens of Bliss" (12), "amid thornless lote" (28), the lost "in ṣamūm and ḥamīm" (42), "in a shadow of smoke" (43). This sūrah's geography knows no open ground, no outside, no neutral landscape: every class is inside something, and the inside is the verdict. Smoke with shade under it is therefore, grammatically, a home; the verse says the lost have a dwelling, and the dwelling is the air on fire — and the near-rank's "elevated" beds (34) have their grim twin in the ẓill of a medium that lifts nothing, shades nobody, and has no floor at all. When the chapter, three verses before, called its classes a sorting at the Event when "you become three pairs" (6), the prepositions began closing around the audience one by one; verse 43, smoke-roofed, is the last ceiling the sūrah builds, and it is built of what a fire that needs no wood still makes.

**What Smoke Hides**

One detail deserves the note, because it is the sūrah's kindness read in reverse: smoke obscures. The Garden's company were defined by sight — faces facing (16), eyes lowered in contentment (37:48; 55:56), and nearness itself the chapter's only name for the vision of God at 88–89; the lost, at verse 43, are placed in the medium that is the end of seeing, a darkness with a shape. The verse's final gift to the left is that they will not be able to see the garden next door; smoke is, in the chapter's economy, the mercy's reverse-image: the near-rank cannot lose what is hoarded in shells (23), and the lost cannot be taunted by what they cannot watch. Al-Wāqiʿah's eschatology keeps its modesty even there: it gives its lost the one comfort of blindness, and gives the comfort no name. That, too, is a kind of describing.

And it is exactly at this point — shade, smoke, the last ceiling of the description — that the chapter turns from scenery to cause (45–46): the left get an explanation, not an inventory, and the verse's smoke is the hinge where the sūrah stops painting and starts testifying.


The smoke also cancels the horizon — an audience that navigated by landmarks and stars is, for the left, left without either — the only class in this chapter with no sky, even one they can no longer use.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:44

> neither cool nor refreshing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Unbuilds a Word**

Ẓalla means to shade, and what it provides is two things the audience's proverbs never failed to enumerate: coolness (barad — "the coolest of shades," as the all-Merciful sūrah describes the Garden's best ẓill, 55:56) and benefit. Verse 44 denies both of the smoke's shadow, and the mechanism is the sūrah's signature: the left receive the word for relief minus the contents of relief, a shade that is neither cool nor useful. The two negated nouns, ḍarīb and nāfiʿ, are classical and debated — the old glosses take ḍarīb in the sense of something that strikes the body with good (dāriba: a spring that is hit by travelers and quenches them, or a shade that "strikes" with its coolness), and nāfiʿ in the plain sense of profit — and whichever etymology the reader prefers, the sense is single: the smoke-shade is a form of blessing preserved as a museum of blessing, everything it used to do for the body canceled, and the cancellation stated in two adjectives at the end of the estate's entire description.

**A Twin Structure in the Neighbor-Chapter**

The Gashiyah sūrah gives the damned their food in the same double-negation mold: "they will have no food except from a thorny plant, which neither nourishes nor satisfies hunger" (88:5–7), lā tusmin wa-lā taghnī against our lā ḍarība wa-lā nāfiʿa. The pair of late Makkān chapters share not merely imagery but syntax: punishment, in both, is defined as the retention of the nourishing category — plant, shade — with its function stripped, and the grammar of negation performs the theft. It is worth holding the two verses together in mind when preaching this one, because the shared construction is the Book's answer to the objector who asks why hell's furniture looks so much like heaven's: the sūrahs say, structurally, that the lost get the world's goods in the same words the saved get, with the doing subtracted — nāfiʿ is the operative word of the eschaton in both chapters, and the lost are those for whom nothing does anything.

**"Refresh": What the Water and the Shade Were For**

Nafʿ, benefit, has been the sūrah's hidden standard since its first description. The right's shade is extended (30), the near-rank's cups do not harm (19), the fruit is never cut off (33) — every amenity in this chapter exists for its use-value, stated in passive participles that promise the body will find them. The left's smoke-shade is the only "place" in the chapter whose properties are enumerated as absences, and the absence-word, nāfiʿ, is the commercial vocabulary of benefit — the same root the sūrah of the Iron spends its half on, the trade that does not lose its goods (35:29; 61:10-11, "a commerce that will save you"), the nafʿ that hoarded wealth never bought for its owner. In al-Wāqiʿah's economy the damned are not tortured so much as disinvested: the elements that profit everyone else are, for them, held out at zero yield, and "neither cool nor refreshing" is the ledger-line of an estate in which nothing pays.

**Three Lines, Then the Indictment**

The structure of the left's section deserves the glance it rarely gets: the exclamation (41), the two-element immersion (42), the parody-shade (43), its two properties denied (44) — three and a half verses of scenery, and then "Indeed, before this ˹torment˺ they were indulged in luxury, and persisted in the greatest sin" (45–46), the cause spoken in two more. The sūrah's mercy-ratio is inverted at the left's door: description minimal, indictment explicit, and the indictment's nouns — the luxury (mutraf, one who lives soft) and the "great sin" (the doubt of resurrection, the chapter's one charge, made plain by 47) — bind the whole scene's scenery to a behavior, so that the smoke reads not as arbitrary torment but as luxury completed: they lived for the pleasantness that neither harmed nor profited no one, and they get pleasantness that profits no one, themselves included. Verse 44, in that light, is not decoration of terror; it is the punishment's definition: enjoyment with the benefit removed, which is what their own lives had already been.

**The Small Word That Ends the Scene**

The last noun of the left's first inventory is "benefit," and that is a ledger-word — the sūrah's whole introduction has been about gain and loss ever since it distinguished those handed their record in the right hand, who face "a reckoning, easy" (84:7, quoted at this chapter's own verse-8 commentary), from everyone else. Verse 44 closes the left's scenery on the word nāfiʿ and then the chapter, remarkably, refuses to escalate: the mockery (47–48), the gathering (49–50), the zaqqūm menu (51–55), the accommodation (56) all swim inside a condition defined twice as unprofitable, and the punishment is not enlarged, only witnessed. That is why 44 should be read as the sūrah's hinge: one line that says, in two negations, everything al-Wāqiʿah will ever say about loss — and then turns, from 57 on, toward what might still become profitable for these very listeners: their ejaculate, their seed, their water, their fire, and the stars.


**Neither Sun-Blaze Nor Bitter Cold: The Chiasmus**

The chapter of Man sets the Garden's climate in the mirror of this verse: in the Garden "you will see neither scorching heat nor bitter cold" (76:13) — the saved lack both extremes, because the shade is balanced; the lost have neither the sun's harm nor the cold's harm, but equally neither the sun's rising nor the cold's relief, because their medium is the in-between that smoke makes. The chiasmus is exact, and it is the sūrahs' standard way of reading 44 against 76: what Paradise gives is not the pleasant but the temperate — the removal of weather as a power over bodies — and what the left get is weather as pure presence, neither punishing usefully nor soothing at all. Al-Wāqiʿah's verse 44, ending on "nor refreshing," thus states the classical definition of hell in this literature: climate without season, exposure without end, a sky that neither rules nor serves.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:45

> Indeed, before this ˹torment˺ they were spoiled by luxury,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mutraf: The Passive Man of Pleasure**

The Arabic's word for the luxury here is takrafuf, the verb ismutrafa — "made soft, dandled, put at ease" — and the morphology is the whole sermon: the damned are not described as people who seized pleasure but as people who were laid into it, formed by it, the passive object of a life of ease, which is how this Book's political vocabulary habitually reads the rich of the old cities. The prophets of the Qur'an hear the phrase before this sūrah: when a messenger summons a town's assembly, the same title — "the eminent" — speaks from prosperity in the accounts of Nūḥ, Hūd, and Ṣāliḥ (e.g., 11:27), and the sūrah of the Heights says that God takes such people "by degrees, from ways they do not perceive" (7:182). That class-worried idiom stands behind verse 45, and when al-Wāqiʿah calls the left "mutrafūn in life," it therefore charges them with a condition, not a hobby: a world arranged so that nothing ever asked anything of them, and a self arranged by that world to find asking unbearable.

**"Before This": The Bracket That Times the Sin**

The sūrah's line is just "kānū fī takrafufi" — they used to be in luxury — and the translation's bracket ˹torment˺ decides what "before this" means: before this punishment, before this Day, before this announcement. The decision is right in context — the left's sin is stated as the antecedent of the estate — but readers should see that the bracket inserts sūrah-history's most famous eschatological claim: the whole life of the damned is one "before." The Arabic of verse 45 is, precisely, an imperfect nominal — they were being in ease — the tense of ongoing background, and the Day arrives like a cut in film; the sūrah has been saying this in images since verse 4 (the earth shaken, the mountains to bits), and verse 45 now says it as plain past tense. Luxury, in that grammar, is not evil in itself; it is what continues up to the moment when continuing ends, and the bracketed ˹torment˺ makes the reader hear their own present as the last bar of a long before.

**Softness as a Theological Problem**

The Qur'an's ethics of ease is subtle, and this verse is a good place to state it, because al-Wāqiʿah does not condemn the body's pleasures anywhere in its pages. Its Paradise is furnished, perfumed, married, and drunk from; what the left get in the smoke is exactly what the right get in the shade, with the thanks omitted. The problem named here is takrafuf, being spoiled: the condition in which comfort becomes the horizon, in which a person no longer expects to be asked, and — in the Book's diagnosis, explicit in the Parable of the Town — in which the very idea of accountability strikes as an insult. The near-rank's opposite property is not austerity but vigilance: "men whom neither trade nor sale diverts from the remembrance of God" (24:37, whose commerce clause this chapter's own verse 25 answers in kind) — and verse 45's mutrafūn are that verse inverted: people diverted by nothing, because everything already belonged to them.

**Makkah's Own Mufassir**

Apply 45 to its first audience and the sūrah's situation becomes legible without any history book: Makkah's governing notables were precisely the class whose careers were built on the sanctuary's peace, the pilgrim trade, and the caravan schedules, and whose hymn at the Kaʿba disclaimed hunger — "We have found our fathers upon a path" — while claiming the house by right. The verse never names them; it just keeps the word their own culture prized, the "soft life," and says: that, continued, is the whole before of the smoke. The sūrah's restraint is worth registering — no accusation of wealth's acquisition, no demand for redistribution stated here, only the sequence "you were dandled, you persisted, now see what shade you are under" — and that sequence is the same one the chapter has run on every class, from census to bed: a habit, then a Day, then the habit's building. The Mufassir's point, in other words, is the sūrah's point: comfort un-thanked is slow stone-setting.

**Why "Indeed" Opens the Verse**

The inna at the head of 45 is the sūrah's own witness-word — "indeed, before this they were spoiled" — and its placement matters: the chapter's two descriptions of the estates so far have been pure gift, unprefaced, and the left's gets the emphatic particle before the accusation, not before the torment. The effect is a courtroom move: the punishment has been stated as fact (42–44), and now the sūrah produces its evidence, with inna, as if to forestall the left's own objection — the same objection they voice at 47, the incredulous "will we really be raised?" — and to tell the audience that the verdict's proof is biographical. Nothing in al-Wāqiʿah's hell needs a demon's résumé; the résumé is the soft life itself, attested by God in the chapter's loudest particle. "Indeed": that is the sūrah's last word about the before, and the mockers, in 47, will spend theirs about the after, and the reader is held precisely between the two "indeeds," which is the whole moral interval this sūrah calls the world.

**Luxury's Silence**

One more property of verse 45 deserves the light it can bear: the mutrafūn, in this sūrah, never complain of what they lost. The chapter's long middle (63–74) will hector them about harvests that can be turned to chaff and water that can be salted, and their own line at 47 is about resurrection, not about dispossession — and nowhere does al-Wāqiʿah say the left will mourn their goods. That silence is not an oversight of the poor sūrah; it is its point. Luxury, verse 45 asserts, is not a thing you own but a life that no longer registers ownership, and the damned's poverty on the Day is, accordingly, not that the gardens are taken from them but that the taking is felt as weather (42–44), a condition like the one that conditioned them. What they were spoiled by is, in the chapter's final grammar, the same as what they are: softened until shape no longer holds, and then re-shaped, without their consent, into the only form a persistent no can take.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:46

> and persisted in the worst of sin.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Istihlāʾ: The Verb of Keeping a Claim Alive**

The second charge's verb, aḥa, is stronger than "persisted" — its root carries pressing, insisting, holding on to a claim, and the sūrah's habitual construction (kānū yuḥiḍḍūna) makes the sin a posture maintained across time, not a single act. What is being described, accordingly, is not the person who once doubted but the doubt as habit, a life organized around keeping a claim from landing. That fits the chapter's entire psychology: verse 45's spoilage was a condition, verse 46's persistence is a decision made again and again under that condition, and the two kānū-lines together are the sūrah's complete anatomy of hardening — circumstance, then repetition — the same grammar the saved were given at 24 ("what they used to do") with the sign reversed. The sūrah has no concept of an eternal punishment that is not also a persistent will; verse 46 is where that conviction sits, in one Form VIII imperfect.

**"The Worst of Sin": Which Sin Is Meant?**

Classical readers asked what al-ithm al-ʿaẓīm names here and answered from the sūrah's own sequence: the immediate context supplies mockery of resurrection at 47–48, and the chapter's opening supplied denial of the Day at 2 (the Day of Recompense whose truth "no one can deny" when it arrives). Many sūrahs preferred the widest reading — the unbelief that the Qur'an elsewhere calls the one sin God does not "overlook," the shirk the Book asserts directly as a wrong of greatness (31:13, Luqmān's charge to his son) — and the "worst" here (al-ʿaẓīm) is the superlative's plain work: within every sin this audience knew, the one that arranges all others against God. The translation's "worst of sin" captures the Arabic's definite-plus-superlative exactly; and the sūrah's strategy lets both readings stand: the persisted-in denial is, in the chapter's own file, at once the resurrection-joke and the theology that made the joke possible.

**ʿAẓīm: The Adjective Shared with the News**

The word "great" is not idle in this sūrah's world. The Qur'an's other late chapter calls resurrection "the tremendous News (anbaʾ al-ʿaẓīm) about which people disagree" (78:2–3), and the sūrah's own last verse puts the superlative on God as its final verb: "So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest." (56:96) The sūrah thus builds a small ladder with one word: the News is great, the sin of mocking it is "the great sin," and the Lord whose name closes the chapter is the Majestic — and the left, in verse 46, are the people who attached ʿaẓīm to the wrong rung: they held fast to what is small in the face of what is not. The adjective is the chapter's moral ruler, and 46 sets against the sūrah's own use of it a lifetime of wrong measurements, persisted in, until the Day measures them back.

**"Persisted" as the Chapter's Verdict on Its Listeners**

Because the verb is habitual, verse 46 reaches past the damned of Makkah into the sūrah's continuing audience, and this commentary should say so plainly: persistence is available to any reader of al-Wāqiʿah, and the verse's function in the chapter's structure is to make the hinge visible. Everything in the two halves of the sūrah is a consequence of one habit: the near-rank recline because they "used to do" (24) the same persistent, unglamorous righteousness, and the left choke in smoke because they "used to" keep a claim alive against the obvious. The sūrah's ethics are therefore, in the strict sense, a theory of habit, and verse 46 is its negative theorem — sin, persisted in, is not repeated but reinforced, each iteration making the next likelier, until the self that holds the position and the position are one, and the Day arrives to find only that unity. That is why no additional crime is itemized here: the inventory is done; what remains is the file's header, and it reads "persisted."

**The Two Verses as One Indictment**

Finally, 45 and 46 should be recited as a single paragraph, since the sūrah bound them with its repeated "indeed... and," and together they state the only two things al-Wāqiʿah will ever say about the lost: how they lived, and how long they lived in it. No curse, no list of outrages, no description of their wrongs to the weak — the chapter that could have supplied all of it from its own vocabulary of justice chooses the bare duple, and the choice teaches the reader where the sūrah thinks perdition actually comes from: not from bad deeds counted, but from a soft life hardened into a settled no. That severity, stated in ten words, is the shortest and, many have felt, the most frightening passage in the whole chapter — a diagnosis without a symptom list, and a God who declines, at the brink of the smoke (43), to say more about the lost than that they kept on. Everything the next verses will add — their question, the answer, the menu — is, in that frame, the sūrah's patience, giving the mockers their own two lines (47–48) even after the verdict has been read.

**The Sūrah's Only Two-Verse Cause**

It is a fact of this chapter's design, and easily missed in the sweep of its images, that the left's entire case is built in exactly two clauses — the condition (45) and the persistence (46) — while the saved right required fourteen of scenery before their twin census. The disproportion is not mercy withheld but judgment compressed: the sūrah needs so little to convict because the accused have already, in its pages, convicted themselves aloud (47–48), and the chapter's structure leaves the court no business beyond stating that the loudness went on. Homiletically, the effect on the reciter is severe in a way no catalogue could be: the listener who has been fed shade and spouses for ten verses then hears that three elements of weather and two lines of biography are all the record against the other party, and is left to measure which side of that arithmetic their own persistence sits on. The sūrah supplies no further evidence, and the absence of further evidence is its final warning.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:47

> They used to ask ˹mockingly˺, “When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Gives Its Enemies Their Own Sentence**

Verse 47 is the chapter's longest quotation of the left, and the translator's bracket announces the tone the Arabic declines to state: qālū inna-hā li-khrāta al-ʿūlā? — "they used to say, 'Is this indeed the first and last?'" in the rough; the line's actual shape is a wonder-question, and the present rendering's ˹mockingly˺ supplies what every sūrahs knew the formula meant: the Book's "will we really be raised (a-innā la-mabʿūthūn)?" with its emphatic doubling is regularly the speech of incredulity, and 56:47 keeps the sarcasm implicit in the letters. The sūrah's method is worth pausing on: having stated the verdict (41–44) and the cause (45–46), it then lets the accused testify at length — three full verses of their own words (47–48) — and lets their sentence stand as evidence of the file it claims is absurd. Their line, in that design, is not a refutation but the exhibit the chapter's argument (57–74) exists to answer.

**Dust and Bones: The Sūrah's Own Cosmology Turned on It**

The objection's materials are the chapter's: "When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones" borrows — the sūrah's audience would have caught the exact phrasing — the vocabulary of 4–6, where the mountains, crushed, "became scattered dust." The mockers speak as if pulverization proved impossibility: we will come apart into turāb and ʿiẓām, and who reassembles sawdust? The chapter has already answered at the level of physics: the same "becoming dust" they cite as their defense is, in the sūrah's telling, the Day's own opening movement — disassembly is not the denial of resurrection but its schedule, and the God who flattens mountains (5) does it in order to re-compose, which is why verse 57 opens the argument-half with "It is We Who created you. Will you not then believe?" The quotation at 47 and the cataclysm at 4 are thus one argument in two moods, the sūrah's audience able to hear the skeptics' dust as the Event's dust without a word of commentary.

**"The First [Life]" — What the Arabic's Question Actually Duplicates**

Readers of the English should know that the sūrah's line contains a word the present translation folds into "resurrected": the baʿth, sending forth, raising, and the object of the wonder — "will we really be sent forth?" — is the same verb the chapter uses for recreation at 61 ("We may transform you and bring you forth (yunshiʾkum) in a creation you do not know"). The mockers' noun is not resurrection as doctrine but resurrection as the word in the prophet's mouth, thrown back doubled; and the sūrah's placement makes the exchange grammatically exact: verse 47's interrogative (a-inna) is answered at 49 with the plain "qul: inna al-awwalīna wal-ākhiriina la-jāmiʿūn" — "Say: most certainly, earlier and later generations will surely be gathered" (56:49–50) — the chapter matching their questioning particles with a command to answer by decree, the two words (baʿth/jamʿ) the only theological vocabulary the sūrah chooses to concede them.

**The Ranks Sūrah's Identical Line**

Late sūrahs record the same objection again and again — 17:49, 25:15, 36:78, 37:16, 79:23 among them — and the closest in form and music is the Ranks sūrah's: "What! When we are dead and become dust and bones, will we really be resurrected? And our forefathers as well?" — in the present translation, essentially identical to ours — so that a listener moving between the two chapters heard one fixed quotation, a liturgical refrain of the skeptical, preserved verbatim. That the Qur'an reuses the sentence shows how real and how tired the objection was, and how the sūrah's answer differs by chapter: the Ranks answers with the single command "qul: al-awwalūna wal-ākhirona la-jāmiʿūna ilā mīqādi yawmin maʿlūm" — the same gathering-clause our sūrah borrows — while al-Wāqiʿah, at 57–74, adds its four interrogatives of semen, seed, water, and fire. The refrain belongs to the audience's history; the answers belong to the hour of each sūrah.

**Why the Mockery Matters**

Finally, the verse's bracket, ˹mockingly˺, carries a pastoral judgment the sūrah itself presses: this is not honest doubt, in the chapter's view, but the doubt of the spoiled (45), a sneer rather than a question — and the difference is everything to al-Wāqiʿah, whose near-rank were also defined by a speech-act, the denial that will be "no one's" on the Day (2). Mockery, the sūrah's ear says, is the mouth's luxury: the same ease that made a people un-ask-able (45) makes their questions into weapons against the questioner, and the chapter's God, who answers with "Say" at 49, treats sneering as claim and claim as evidence. Readers have often asked why this sūrah, with its enormous courtesy about the Garden, is so short-tempered about the question at 47; the answer is its own anatomy — the verse heard, three lines earlier, how the man who was never asked anything asks, and it brackets accordingly. The sneer is the last word the left ever get in this chapter as speakers; from verse 49 the sūrah's God does the talking.

**The Question the Sūrah Answers with Bodies**

Verse 47's incredulous "we" — first person plural, four times across 47–48: we die, we are dust, we raised, our forefathers — deserves one notice, because the chapter's reply from 57 onward is built in the same person: "It is We Who created you... have you considered what you eject (tumnee)? Is it you who create it, or We?" The pronouns lock: their "we" asks whether the body returns; God's "We/you" answers by taking the body apart at its first origin — semen — and asking who made it then. The sūrah's entire argument-half is, in structure, an exegesis of verse 47's grammar: the mockers' plural of disgust receives, line by line, the first-person plural of the Maker, until the same "we" can only mean "we who were once a despised fluid and were made by Someone." Al-Wāqiʿah never says this as argument; it is the mere shape of the sequence 47→57→59, and the shape is the answer the sneer asked for, spoken in bodies the skeptics claimed to own.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:48

> And our forefathers as well?"

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Line, Its Own Verse: The Art of the Follow-Up Sneer**

The sūrah gives the left's second breath its full verse-mark — wa-ābāʾunal-awwalūn — five words, an addendum, and in its brevity the most vivid line of their quotation. Grammatically it is not a new sentence but a tacked-on dative: "will we really be raised — and our forefathers as well?" The incredulity is all in the "and also": the first question mocked the individual's return, the addendum mocks the logistics, the sheer implausible scope — that the buried centuries, the ancestors of the audience's own pride, would have to be recalled too. Arabic's shortness makes the sneer audible; the line is a shrug in five words. And the sūrah's placement — immediately before "Say: most certainly, the earlier and the later will be gathered" (56:49–50) — answers the addendum first: the forefathers are in, the scope is exactly the objection, and the answer is one breath long. The chapter thus treats the ridicule on its own scale: a line gets a line.

**The Forefathers: Makkah's Strongest Noun**

To the first audience, ābāʾ were not merely ancestors but authority — the religion of the fathers was the standard defense of the sanctuary's order ("We found our fathers upon a path," the sūrah's own summary of the Makkān creed-formula in the Verse chapter's words, 43:22–23), and the Karbala of Arabic memory ran backward through them. The left's sneer therefore reveals, while dismissing resurrection, everything else they hold: the ancestors are the ones who must not be troubled; the dead of the old ways should stay in their honored ground; the claim that God re-raises the generations is offensive because it would summon, to the same questioning Day, the fathers who are supposed to be above questioning. Al-Wāqiʿah obliges: 49–50 gathers "the earlier and the later" without exception, and the sūrah's longer argument will insist, in its dying-scene, that "We are nearer to him than you, but you do not see" (56:85), a nearness no forefather possesses. Verse 48 is thus the chapter's quietest demolition of ancestor-culture: it lets the mockers name their idol in a subordinate clause and then books him into the census.

**"Bring Our Forefathers If You Are Truthful": The Book's Taunt Returned**

The Qur'an elsewhere turns this very word back on its users. To the claim that paradise belongs to Israel alone the Heifer chapter says: "Bring your forefathers, if you are truthful" (2:111) — produce your ancestors as witnesses — and in the Poets chapter Moses, defying Pharaoh's court, names the Judge by their own oldest title — the God of their Lord and of their forefathers, Lord of the East and the West (26:26–29) — the forefathers not as defense but as summons. Al-Wāqiʿah's 48 rhymes with both usages without quoting either: the sneer's own logic — "and our forefathers as well?" — assumes the fathers stand outside the summons, and the sūrah's theology of the gathering has already made them the first names on the roll. The audience who heard 48 in Makkah heard themselves, precisely where they felt unexamined: the dead whom no one questions will be questioned first, and the question-mark the mockers put on the fathers will, the sūrah implies, be turned back on the sons.

**Anaphora of Doubt: What Quotation Does in Recitation**

Structural note on the chapter's ear: verses 45–48 run "Indeed they used to be (kānū) spoiled… and used to persist (kānū)… they used to say (qālū: in the Arabic, continuing the kānū-chain)… and our forefathers as well?" — four lines of habitual past carrying the audience along a rhythm of "they used to," and the quote lands inside the rhythm like a stone in water. Recited — and this sūrah has been recited aloud in every generation since — the listener's body is moving with the past-tense procession when the interrogative breaks the pattern, and the break is the point: habit is what the left are, and questioning is what they cannot bear. The verse-mark after "as well?" then opens the "Say" of verse 49 like a lid, and the reciter is made, briefly, to hold the sneer in the mouth before the answer is allowed — a small, severe pedagogy of empathy-by-inversion, in which the audience tastes, for one line, how a claim on God sounds from the side of the spoiled, before they are invited to be glad they are not them.

**The Unfinished Quote and the Completed Answer**

Finally, a typographic observation the present translation makes unavoidable: verse 47's opening quotation-mark stays unclosed until 48's final question-mark, and the sūrah, in fact, runs their sentence across both verses as one utterance. Read that way, 48 is not the sneer's end but its completion — "…will we really be raised, and our forefathers as well?" — and the chapter's two-line quotation stands as a single grammatical unit, the first full speech of the left, and the only one the sūrah records before answering them by decree (49–50), by menu (51–55), and by accommodation (56). Everything afterward is God's monologue about them, not their dialogue with anyone; the forefathers-line is the last word the left speak in al-Wāqiʿah, and it is spoken as disbelief in kinship — the very bond the chapter, from 13 to 40, has been quietly rewriting: the saved are "from" generations, not "of" forefathers, and the Day's family is the census of the merciful, fathers and strangers sorted alike into swarm and remnant.

**The Verse-Mark's Mercy**

One last detail: the sūrah's pause after 48 — its own verse-mark, set at "as well?" — gives the sneer a full stop before God answers, and in the architecture of recitation that stop is the sūrah's mercy to its listeners: the audience is made to live the question's sound, dust and ancestors and all, and then to hear the "Say" (49) arrive as the only possible reply. The left's quotation ends, and ends unanswerable by them; the chapter knows that anyone who has ever felt the weight of that "and our forefathers as well?" — the honest tiredness at the promise, not the mockery of it — stands inside the two lines and can either stay there or move to 49, and the sūrah's pacing, verse by verse, is its way of holding the door to the second choice open.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:49

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Most certainly, earlier and later generations

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command, the First in This Sūrah**

Qul — "say" — is the sūrah's single imperative of prophethood, and its placement is its exegesis: after four lines of the left's own speech (47–48), God does not argue from the heavens; He tells a man to answer, and in doing so makes the resurrection-refutation the prophet's mouthpiece rather than a theophany the audience could demand. Late Makkān sūrahs use qul exactly this way — at the dead end of an objection, the command to say becomes the Book's doctrine of the messenger's office: he is the one who answers on God's warrant, and the answer here is a bare certainty. The bracketed ˹O Prophet˺ is the present translation's courtesy, standard across its text at divine commands to address the people, and readers should let the sūrah's own terseness stand: the sūrah says "say" to no one in particular before 56:57, where its argument begins, which means the chapter's first word about resurrection-refutation is the first word it gives to its messenger.

**"Earlier and Later Generations": History as One Attendance**

Al-awwalūna wal-ākhirūn — the sūrah's fourth census-pair (13–14 for the near-rank, 39–40 for the right, 51–52 to come for the left) — and here the two halves are merged into the single subject of one gathering, which is the verse's whole point. Every earlier list sorted by class; this list sorts by time only, and says: the time-sort ends at the Day. The sūrah's audience thought genealogically — first families, later claims, the ancestors at the front of everything — and verse 49 flattens the genealogy into one cohort summoned to one date, an assembly in which the Makkān clans arrive as "earlier and later" and nothing else, their forefathers not honored out of the roll-call. This is the verse the sneer of 48 was aimed at, and the answer gives it back in kind: yes, and our forefathers as well — inna, verily — the sūrah's doubled emphatic, lam plus inna around the predicate, God matching their "a-innā" (will we really?) with the strongest Arabic assertion available, no proof appended, only the fact.

**The Certainty-Grammar of Verse 49**

The English "most certainly" stands for two Arabic particles working together — the sentence's inna (indeed) and the lam of emphasis (la-jāmiʿūn) in its predicate — a construction Qur'anic recitation reserves for verdicts: not "the gathered will come," but "they are, assuredly, gathering-to-be." The sūrah, which has been describing, now swears, and the shift in register is the listener's cue: everything from 50 to 56 hangs on this lam. Commentators noted that the verse carries no oath-formula (no "by the Lord of such-and-such") — the certainty is asserted, not sworn — while the chapter's only sworn passage is its own great oath to the Book — "So I do swear by the positions of the stars" (56:75). The design places two unshakable statements side by side: the Day's gathering, without an oath; the Book's truth, with one — as if the sūrah's architecture wanted its audience to notice that both rest on the same God's word and no human evidence, the resurrection and the revelation, each "most certainly," and each asked to be taken before being seen.

**"Generations" Again: The Sūrah's Unit of History**

The noun left unquoted above deserves a sentence. The sūrah's "earlier and later" are, in Arabic, generation-words — qarn, "the people of a time" — not "dead" or "souls," and the choice keeps the chapter's census material, corporate, bodily: the unit that rises is a whole era of people, not a cloud of spirits. Al-Wāqiʿah has consistently resisted every temptation to speak of the afterlife as disembodied (the semen, the sowings, the water, the fire in its argument-half all insist on bodies and fields), and verse 49, the chapter's great gathering-line, extends the same policy across history: what is raised is every generation, in its body, together, and the audience listening in Makkah — a town that dated itself by its generations of custodians — hears that their whole chronological pride will file into one assembly-line whose only distinction is the one the chapter has been drawing since verse 6: class, of their own choosing, sorted by what they used to do (24).

**How Verse 49 Ends the Sūrah's First Half**

Readers with an ear for structure should note where the qul lands and where its paragraph ends: the sūrah's description-unit — three classes, two estates fully shown, the left's sneer and its answer — closes at 56 with "This will be their accommodation on the Day of Judgment" (56), and 49–50, the gathering-decree, is the hinge inside the left's own passage that also pivots the whole chapter: from here the text turns from scenery to testimony (57–74), from Day to Book to death (75–87), and the three-class census resumes at the bedside (88–94). Verse 49, in that map, is the chapter's waist: the last verse of the vision, the first of the argument. The "say" is handed to the prophet exactly where the sūrah's showing ends — and the command to speak, not to demonstrate, is the chapter's own definition of what revelation is for the unshown: "most certainly," the word for the half of reality the eye has not yet reached.

**The Answer's Address**

Notice also who the qul makes the speaker: not God in the first person — the chapter's own "We" waits until 57 — but the Prophet, quoting the decree. The left's mockery at 47–48 was aimed at the messenger ("will we really be raised?"), and the answer returns through his mouth, so that the sūrah stages the refutation as the very office the mockers denied: a man, saying God's certainty, to people who wanted proof. Between 48's sneer and 49's "Say" there is no argumentative connective, only the imperative — the chapter's judgment, made grammatical, that the question of resurrection is not answered by evidence at the bar but by the weight of who says it, and that the bar itself is the Day. When the audience later hears the four "Have you considered?" questions, they will discover that the sūrah, in fact, does possess the evidence it declined to produce here; verse 49's bare "Say, most certainly" is the verdict, and 57–74 is the summing-up that follows it, not the proof that earns it.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:50

> will surely be gathered ˹together˺ for the appointed Day.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jamʿ: The Day That Is the Assembly**

The verb's root is the same as the Qur'an's name for Friday's gathering and for the eschaton it foreshadows — the sūrah of Hud calls it the day on which all people will be gathered, a day of witnessing (11:103), and the sūrah of Consultation names it the Day of Assembly about which there is no doubt (42:7) — while our verse's form, la-jāmiʿūna, says it plainly: they will be gathered, and the sūrah adds no place, only the Day, because in al-Wāqiʿah's geography the place is the weather and the address is a class. Verse 50's genius of phrasing is that the gathering is for the Day, not in it — the Day is the appointment's time and the verdict's venue at once — and the audience that readied caravans for known meeting-places heard the line as one summons with a fixed date and no escape clause.

**Mīqāt: Contract-Language for the End of Time**

The sūrah's word is mīqāt, from w-q-t, the noun of measured appointment; the present line's "the appointed Day" renders mīqāti yawmin maʿlūm, a day with a date, and the idiom is the audience's own: a mīqāt is a fixed deadline agreed between parties — the same noun family that carries, in the Heights, the forty nights of Mūsā's tryst with his Lord (7:142). The sūrah thus speaks of resurrection in the register of contracts kept: the Day is not a spontaneous eruption but an appointment the deniers themselves have been served papers for, and the mockers' incredulous "will we really?" (47) lands, grammatically, on a date already set. That is the quiet terror of verse 50: the Hour behaves like a creditor's patience — nothing is rushed because nothing can be renegotiated.

**"The Known Day": Why the Arabic Insists**

Mīqāti yawmin maʿlūm — for an appointed, known Day — and the "known" (maʿlūm) is one of the Qur'an's most theologically loaded adjectives: the Day is known to God, known through the preachers, "known" the way a court date is known to both parties, while al-Wāqiʿah keeps the plain force: this Day is knowable — announced by preachers, calendared in revelation, "known" the way a tryst is known to both parties — and the Ranks sūrah, whose Garden-list our chapter keeps beside its own, attaches the identical adjective to the reward ("a known provision," 37:41), so that Day and recompense share one epithet within the same pair of late texts. The Day's "knownness" is thus part of the evidence: an appointment the accused can produce no excuse for having missed.

**The Bracketed "Together": An Emphasis the Translator Had to Recover**

The present translation prints ˹together˺ because the sūrah's Arabic doubles the gathering for effect: the passive of jamʿa with the reciprocal sense the word can carry (jamʿan, a gathering-together of all upon all — every party in one place, no partial assembles), and the translator's bracket is honest: English needs the word the Arabic gets from root-force alone. The emphasis is what the chapter's census has been building since verse 6 — "you become three pairs" — and 50 states the gathering as total assembly, not triage: the sorting happens inside the one event, in front of everyone, which is why the sūrah will spend its end (83–94) at a single deathbed where the same three-way verdict arrives quietly. The Day that gathers all is, structurally, the day every death already was; the bracketed "together" is the sūrah's way of refusing any reader the comfort of a private resurrection, either saved or lost.

**The First Half's Closing Cadence**

Listen to 49–50 as one line across two verses: "Say, 'Most certainly, the earlier and the later will surely be gathered for the Day of the Appointment'" — inna…la-, qawāfil, maʿlūm — the sūrah's longest run of heavy emphatic particles, and its last before the argument turns colloquial ("Have you considered…?" ×4). That cadence is the chapter's own structural signal: the vision has been stated at its maximum certainty, and everything after 50 is evidence, address, and oath — the menu of the left (51–56) and the four questions (57–74) both unfold from the decree, not toward it. Preachers have read the cadence pastorally for centuries: the sūrah's audience is not invited to weigh probabilities about the Hour but to accept a date — and then, in 57, to look at their own bodies and crops, where the sūrah says, the proof of that date has been quietly growing all along, in fluid and in seed, for anyone who will consider it.

**Three Words for the Date, and a Fourth Already Spoken**

The sūrah's small library of eschatological nouns could be read as a lesson in the audience's calendar: maʿlūm, the known day; mawʿūd, the promised, which the Qur'an's skeptics mock in the same breath they mock the dust — "when is this promise, if you are truthful?" asks the sovereign chapter of the dominion of the same doubters (67:25); and mīqāt, the appointment that does not require their agreement to bind them. Al-Wāqiʿah's contribution to the set is its deployment of all three registers inside two verses — "appointed," "known," and, at 56, "the Day of Judgment" (yawm al-dīn, the Day of Recompense of verse 2 by another name) — a single event named four ways: promised, dated, known, and owed. That quadruple naming is not stylistic surplus. The sūrah is answering verse 47's "really?" from every angle the audience's own contract-vocabulary could supply: you were told, it is on the book, it is a fixed date, and the payment is being withheld by no one — the debt is yours. The Day, in al-Wāqiʿah, behaves like a judge who has already stamped the file at the first hearing (2), and verse 50 says so in the market's own nouns, forty-four verses before the chapter asks the deniers why they take the whole message lightly (81).


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:51

> Then you, O misguided deniers,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn from "They" to "You"**

Through verses 41–50 the left were talked about; at 51 the sūrah turns and talks to them — "Then you, O misguided deniers" — and the shift of person, what the sūrahs' rhetorical science calls iltifāt, is the verse's whole argument. Report addresses a jury; summons addresses the accused. The audience, who have been listening to a description of some third group's smoke, suddenly stand inside the address, because the "you" has no quoted speaker other than the God the chapter has been asserting since 49, and every hearer must decide which "you" they are in. Classical readers of the Qur'an's rhetoric list this turn among its most forceful devices precisely because it happens without warning — no verse-divider announces that the court now speaks to the defendants — and al-Wāqiʿah's placement at the hinge of its second half makes 51 the loudest single line of the left's passage, a line consisting, in Arabic, of nothing but a conjunction, a pronoun, and two nouns.

**"The Deniers," Which Is How This Chapter Began**

Al-mukadhdhibūn closes the sūrah's frame: its second verse charged that at the Event "no one can deny it has come," and the first sin the chapter ever named was the denial of the Day of Recompense — the very verb (k-dh-b, to call false) returns here as the class's title. Al-Wāqiʿah has no other name for the lost: not polytheists, not idolaters, not the rich — the chapter's file on them is a single charge, calling the Day false, and verse 2 and verse 51 are the front and back covers of one dossier. That narrowing is the sūrah's doctrinal clarity: in the late Makkān reckoning, what damns is not a list of misdeeds but the refusal to believe the accounting is real, and 51 says so by refusing to add anything else. The reader who has followed the chapter from verse 1 hears the verdict arrive as a rhyme, not a novelty: you, the ones who denied that this would ever come — here it is coming, and it is addressed to you.

**"Misguided": The Verb of Losing the Road**

The second title, al-ḍāllīna, is the sūrah's word for going astray, and the root is travel-language: ḍalāla is losing the road, missing the way across open ground — the worst thing that can befall a people whose survival is navigation. The Qur'an uses the same word for its messenger's own prehistory, in the chapter of the Morning-Hours, "He found you unaware of the Scripture and the faith, and He guided you" (93:7) — guidance and being-guided are the pair the word lives inside — and so verse 51's insult is, at bottom, directional: these people are not the wicked but the lost, and the Day they denied is, in the sūrah's physics, the point at which being lost becomes a fixed address. The audience of caravans and star-reading understood ḍalāla as the final danger, and the chapter's choice to name their enemies with it — instead of the vocabulary of crimes — keeps the sūrah's mercy intact: every ḍāll could still be hadā, and the smoke at 43 is what it looks like when the road has been refused until the map is taken away for good.

**Vocation as Last Courtesy**

It is worth stating plainly what the address does not do: it does not revile. "O misguided deniers" is an appellation, not an abuse, and the sūrah at this its most hostile moment stays inside the terms it used for them at 2 and 45 — no epithet of their persons, no cursing, no wish. Commentators on the Qur'an's rhetoric note that divine vocatives of rebuke ("yā ayyuhā al-ḍāllūna" — the sūrah of Ya-Sin's "willfully wronged" are a kin-case) function as summons to responsibility, not as exclusion from it: a lost person addressed is a lost person still able to hear. The irony the sūrah permits itself is the irony of timing — "then" — the address arrives in the verdict, when hearing changes nothing, and the reader who is not yet in the verdict is the one meant to take the vocative as a last courtesy rather than a last insult: the name the Day will give you, offered now, while names can still be refused.

**"Then": The Conjunction That Spends No Rhetoric**

Finally, the fa. Arabic's conjunctive "so/then" opens 51 and links the vocative to the eating that follows at 52–55 without pausing for emphasis, and the flatness is the design: the sūrah has spent its intensifiers already — the doubled inna and lam at 49–50 — and the sentence that follows the great oath of the gathering runs, grammatically, like a bill coming due: "then you… will certainly eat… filling… then you will certainly drink… and you will certainly drink the drink of the thirst-mad." The three fa-clauses of 51–55 carry the verdict down a slope of mere sequence, consequence without pathos, and the effect on the reciter is the chapter's most severe rhetoric precisely because it is no rhetoric: the left do not get a curse poem; they get a list of what follows from what they were. Al-Wāqiʿah, which furnished mercy with fourteen verses of scenery, gives judgment its conjunctions, and that is the verse's final teaching — the difference between the two estates is detail.

**The Two Titles, Read Once More Together**

"Guiding-losers who call-false": the vocative stacks its nouns without "and," and in Arabic's economy that asyndeton makes ḍalāla and takdhīb one condition — lostness consisting precisely in calling the guide a liar. The sūrah has, since verse 2, treated denial as an act (no one denies the Day except one who has been spoiled into refusing the claim), and 51's double title compresses the psychology: nobody strays into disbelief, in this chapter's view, by honest wandering; the ḍāll of al-Wāqiʿah is the mukadhdhib who has learned to love the road he lost. That is why the verse needs no further description of them before the menu begins — the two words are the inventory of their sin, and the eating to come, from 52, will be its mirror image: what you called false is now your food, and the road you loved is the smoke that surrounds the table.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:52

> will certainly eat from ˹the fruit of˺ the trees of Zaqqûm,

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Name the Qur'an Invented for Itself**

Zaqqūm is not an old Arabic noun for any known tree; it is the Qur'an's own name for this tree, and the sūrahs recorded the philologists' speculations about its making — from zaqama, to cram the throat, the verb for gulping what will not go down; from the phrase "my provision and my rising" as a pun; and always with the note that the word sounds horrible because the thing is horrible, the Arabic root's conflation of taste and dread. That the name is unique to revelation is itself a piece of the sūrah's method: the left get a botany that exists only in this Book's vocabulary, a tree with no garden anywhere else, and verse 52's ˹the fruit of˺ brackets, supplied by the present translation to make sense of "eat from the trees," mark the gap between the ordinary verb of eating and this extraordinary object. A reader can hold no image of zaqqūm, because there is no arboreal memory to hang it on — and the sūrah, which described its saved with nothing but known trees, describes the lost with the one unknown.

**The Fruit That Answers the Mockery**

Context gives the verse its logic: the left denied being raised (47), the gathering was affirmed (49–50), and the address comes (51), and now, at 52, the verdict is not fire as such — it is eating. The Ranks sūrah runs the same menu earlier in the Book — a tree sprouting in the bottom of the Hell, fruit compared to the heads of devils, bellies filled with it, a drink of hamīm after the eating, and a return to the Blaze (37:64–68) — five notes in the same order our 52–56 will play, and and the point of a food-punishment is that the deniers made their bodies their argument: "when we are dust and bones, will we really be raised?" The answer is a body that eats forever and is nourished by nothing — resurrection granted in its most terrible form, flesh restored only to the function of swallowing. Al-Wāqiʿah's mockers get their resurrection, in the first tense it will be felt: the throat.

**"Eat From the Trees": The Botany of the Two Estates, Compared**

Set verse 52 against its twin in the right-hand passage, and the chapter's agricultural system appears complete. The saved eat fruit (faakihah, 20, 32) that is never cut off and never forbidden, from thornless lote and clustered trees (28–29); the lost eat "from the trees of zaqqūm," and the Arabic's shared construction — min plus tree-language — is the sūrah's own cue to compare, estate against estate, tree against tree. The pairing encodes the chapter's theology of provision: food is not neutral stuff but relationship, and in this sūrah every people get the vegetation their covenant produced — the near-rank fruit of choice, the middle fruit of abundance, the lost a fruit that is the opposite of cultivation, a plant of the root of Hell that the Ranks sūrah says God "made a trial for the wrongdoers" (the same idiom of trial the verse 73 below will re-use for the world's fire). The Garden and the Fire are, in al-Wāqiʿah's vocabulary, two orchards, and only one of them grows in answer to gratitude.

**The Certainty-Lam Returns, Third Time**

La-tā-kulūna: "you will certainly eat," the emphatic lam of verse 50's la-jāmiʿūn repeated and now attached to swallowing. The sūrah has rung that particle three times — gathering, eating, drinking (50, 52, 54) — and the recurrence is what turns a menu into a sentence: no new threat is being introduced, only the certainty-grammar of the verdict being pressed, item by item, into the left's mouths. Commentators note that the Qur'an reserves this doubled emphasis (inna plus lam) for what the deniers find impossible — resurrection, punishment, the Book's truth — and 52 belongs to that family: the thing the mockers said could never happen is the thing they will never stop doing. There is, in the sūrah's arithmetic, a grim symmetry between the two estates' verbs of certainty: the near-rank were given no lam at all — their pleasures were simply "for them" (38) — while the left receive certainty three times over, because the verdict's function at this point in the chapter is evidentiary, not executive: the audience is being shown that the Day's food will be as sure as its gathering, and the swallowing that will not nourish is the guarantee that the raising that will not end.

**The Verse's Afterlife in the Preachers' Hands**

One reception-note completes the entry: zaqqūm became, in the literature of warning-sermons across the centuries, the Qur'an's emblem for the punishment of appetite, and the preaching literature turned the tree into the food of every worldly injustice the audience could name — the devourer of orphans above all, after the Women chapter's line that such a man eats fire into his own belly (4:10). That association belongs to the sermon-stream around this verse, not to its chains of exegesis, and this commentary cites it as reception-history; still, the homilists' move is faithful to 52, because the verse has, in fact, made the damned's first eternal act an act of eating, and any appetite trained on the world's forbidden food can recognize, in a belly full of zaqqūm, its own future. What the sermonizers were after is legible honestly: verse 52 makes the damned's diet a single item, endlessly eaten, and any listener who has ever stuffed in spite of nausea recognizes the eschaton of it. The sūrah's restraint — name the tree, verb the eating, move on to the drink (54) — has done more for that recognition than any elaborated hellscape could, and its preachers, in reaching for the orphan's bite, were filling out the one blank the text deliberately left: why this food, of all the terrors, is the one the chapter chooses for the mouths that laughed at dust.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:53

> filling up ˹your˺ bellies with it.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fullness Without Nourishment: The Punishment of the Apposed Appetite**

The Arabic participle mal'ūna — "filled up" — describes the bellies as being stuffed (mal'a, the verb of filling a skin or a bucket to the brim), and the torment's genius is that the wish for satiety is granted: the left will not hunger in this scene, they will be full, permanently, of what cannot nourish. The Qur'an elsewhere defines the damned's food by exactly this pair of failed functions — in the Overwhelming chapter, "they will have no food except from a thorny plant, which neither nourishes nor satisfies hunger" (88:5–7) — and al-Wāqiʿah, which never says "neither nourishes," states the same truth by the absurdity of the image: fill the belly and offer it nothing but a tree that was never food. Verse 53 thus punishes the appetite not with deprivation but with completion, and readers who know the animal satisfaction of a full stomach will notice that the sūrah has made that satisfaction itself the instrument: the body's one reliable pleasure, turned into its one permanent grievance.

**The Belly as the Sūrah's Moral Organ**

Arabic idiom located greed in the belly long before this sūrah — the language of hoarding is a language of swallowing — and the Prophet's word preserved in the Sunan literature, "no son of Adam fills a vessel worse than his belly," gives the ethic this verse presupposes: the belly is where the soul's excess becomes visible. Al-Wāqiʿah's left are the people of 45 (spoiled by luxury), and verse 53 completes their portrait: the soft life was a life lived for the belly's claims, and the afterlife grants the claims in full, which is what its refusal to grant them would otherwise have looked like. The near-rank, by contrast, were described by the cup that neither aches nor inebriates (19) — pleasure kept at the threshold of harm — while the left receive pleasure past every threshold, filling, and the sūrah's anthropology of appetite is contained in the difference between those two images: a cup you may drink from, a stomach you must not escape.

**"With It": The Instrumental of Nothing Else**

Minhu — "from it/with it" — ties the filling to the tree alone, and the exclusivity is the sentence's sharpest edge: no second course, no water that quenches (that comes at 54, as fire on the fullness), no variety, no season, no choice, nothing "they desire" (compare 21, where the near-rank's birds are "what their souls desire"). The saved estate's grammar of options — choose, wish, select — has here been reduced to a single accusative of instrument: you will be filled, with this. Al-Wāqiʿah, whose whole Paradise is built on the removal of constraints on appetite (no season, no ban, no hangover), builds its Hell as appetite with all removals reversed — one object, no exit — and verse 53 is the verse that locks the reversal into the body. Monotony is the punishment's aesthetic; the bellies are full of the proof that a desire, fed forever one thing, is a prison rather than a pasture.

**Passive Again: Even Their Fullness Is Done to Them**

The chapter's grammar of placement — the saved are "in" gardens, "on" thrones, "amid" lote (12, 15, 28); the lost are "in" samum and hamīm (42) — now adds, at 53, the last and most intimate of its passives: they will be filled. The verb is not "they will stuff themselves"; the bellies are full-ed, and the left, who in life were mutrafūn (45 — people dandled, spoiled, done-to by their ease), receive in the next world the same grammatical fate with the sign reversed: in both existences they are acted upon. That symmetry is the sūrah's most severe claim about the damned self — that its passivity is continuous, a softness that chose, once, to be shaped by nothing else, and is now shaped forever by the one thing it mocked. Al-Wāqiʿah has no theory of autonomous damnation; verse 53 insists that even here the verb belongs to God, and the left are the object — and readers who flinch at that theology should remember that the same passive voice, with the same verbs of filling and receiving, describes the Garden's every gift, and the sūrah's point is that the only active voice left in the whole eschaton was the one sentence at 47: "will we really be raised?" They asked; the answer fills them.

**The Verse Between Eating and Drinking**

Structurally 53 is the hinge of the left's menu: eat the tree (52) — filled (53) — then the drink of hamīm (54) — the camels' guzzle (55) — accommodation (56). Its function is the middle of a stomach: the fullness before the drowning of the fullness, and the sūrah's pacing makes the hearer wait, one short line, between eating and drinking, exactly the pause a diner knows. That pause is why 53 should not be skimmed: it is the verse that makes the punishment somatic before it is hydrological, and the Arabic's brevity — a participle, a pronoun, a noun of bellies — keeps the body in the sentence at full weight. When the drink arrives it will arrive, as the next verse's "then upon that" insists, on top of the fullness, and the sūrah's sequence (fill, then flood) turns the belly into the chapter's last word about the deniers: not their minds, not their gods, their bellies — the organ they trusted, filled with what they trusted it with, closed around forever. The Day of Recompense, in al-Wāqiʿah's final arithmetic, is a day of digestive endings, and verse 53 is the full stop that is not a full stop.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:54

> Then on top of that you will drink boiling water—

**Expanded Commentary**

**"On Top of That": The Sequence of a Host's Care, Inverted**

Thumma ʿalā dhālik — "then, upon that" — is the sūrah's way of keeping the manners of serving in the scene of punishment: in the etiquette the audience knew, the drink comes after the food, the host does not let the guest's throat go dry at the end of the meal, and the Ranks sūrah, narrating the same menu, says it with the same phrase — "Then on top of that they will be given a blend of boiling drink" (37:67), the line our verse paraphrases. That courtesy, exercised upon the damned, is the verse's chosen irony: the left are served, hosted, attended to — their estate is, as 56 will name it, an "accommodation" (nuṭub, hospitality) — and the sūrah thus refuses to depict hell as neglect. Nothing here is forgotten, withheld, or left to chance; the punishment is administered with the precision of a host who has planned the whole meal, which is far more terrible than abandonment, because it means the left are as certainly in God's hands as the near-rank are, and are being attended to with the same intentionality.

**Hamīm, the Third Time: Water as the Estate's Medium**

The word ḥamīm has now sounded through the left's passage at three functions: the element they are "in" (42), the drink they are forced to (54), and the hospitality they will be given at the Day's final summary (93). Water is the chapter's master-medium, and this is where its theology of water completes itself. The same Book that made the deniers' argument about the ordinary element — "Is it you who bring it down from the clouds, or are We? If We willed, We could make it salty" (68–70) — places them inside water boiling and makes them drink water boiling: the salted water the argument threatens is, in the eschatology, already arrived. Al-Wāqiʿah's left do not lack water; they have only water, in its unusable form — and a reader who has felt the irony of being offered a drink you cannot want has the whole verse. The chapter's hydrology is thus its theodicy: the same rain is the proof of mercy to the grateful (70, "will you not then be grateful?") and the instrument of verdict to the refused, because in this sūrah substances carry no blessing of their own.

**"Like Molten Brass": The Neighboring Description**

What this verse declines to say about the drink, the Book says elsewhere, and the sūrahs line the passages up as commentary on each other: the Cave chapter's hell-folk, when they cry for aid, "will be aided with water like molten metal, which will burn ˹their˺ faces" (18:29), and the Dazzling chapter's "boiling in the bellies" image for the tree-food of the sinner (44:45–46) — the same belly that 56:53 filled, boiling with what the neighbors say is molten brass. The present sūrah keeps all such comparison back; 54 states only "of ḥamīm," the noun it has already defined in the estate's air (42), and lets the hearer's memory of the other chapters do the work. The restraint fits the chapter's method throughout the left's passage: scenery in three nouns, cause in two lines, and no gore added to what the audience's own experience of heat, thirst, and bad water already supplies — al-Wāqiʿah's punishment is, repeatedly, the familiar made inescapable.

**The Dash at the Line's End: Enjambment in Recitation**

The present translation's trailing dash is honest to the Arabic, which hangs "min ḥamīm" open toward the simile that follows: you will drink — of boiling water — and you will drink like the thirst-mad camels (55). The sentence enjambs across the verse-break, the one line feeding the next the way the drink is supposed to, and the reciter's breath is held at 54's end exactly as the damned are held between the cup and its effect. Nothing in this sūrah is accidental, and this small fact — a period deferred, a comparison arriving in the next breath — is its rhetorical point: the punishment's sentences, unlike the Garden's, are never finished where you expect them (the shade at 43 has an unfinished property, negated in 44; the eating at 52 runs through filling and drink to the accommodation at 56).
