The left's verses are structured as deferral, and 54's dash is the technique seen whole: a drink you are given, and then given again, and then told, after all of it, that it is what hospitality here means (56).

**The Verse's Place in the Chapter's Cup-Architecture**

Finally, one structural observation that gives 54 its full position: this sūrah frames its entire middle with cups. The near-rank's scene opened with ewers from a spring, "that will cause them neither headache nor intoxication" (18–19), a passage defined by what the drink will not do; the left's scene now closes with a drink defined by what it will do, and between the two the chapter has argued from rain to salt, from clouds to bellies. The cup and the ḥamīm are the chapter's two chalices, and 54 is the rim of the second. When, at 88–94, the sūrah compresses all three estates at a single deathbed, the left's line will be "their accommodation will be boiling water" (93) — the drink once more, at the chapter's end — so that verse 54 stands at the exact middle of the book's longest poem about two ways to be thirsty, and its dash, its enjambment, and its courtesy are the sūrah's own cup handed across fourteen centuries to whoever recites it, inviting the reader, plainly, to be drinking from the other table, where no one is asked to swallow what was made from their own rain.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:55

> and you will drink ˹it˺ like thirsty camels do."

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥīm: The Image the Desert Needed No Gloss For**

The comparison's noun — hum, plural ahyām, from the root of the thirst that unmakes the brain — denotes the camel driven mad by thirst, the animal that will not leave the trough, drinks and drinks and comes up empty, in the old lexicography because the beast's disorder prevents its body from receiving water at all. To an audience whose wealth walked on four legs and whose worst day was the camel's death by thirst, the sūrah needed no further word: "you will guzzle like him" is the entire sentence of punishment, and the translators' bracket ˹it˺ marks the object their verb carries on its own. The simile performs the classic degradation of the Qur'an's damning imagery — humans reduced, not to demons, but to livestock: the chapter of Muhammad says the deniers "eat and enjoy as cattle eat and enjoy" (47:12), in a plain assertion of the same zoology — and here the very act of drinking, which the near-rank perform from ewers with no harm (18–19), is given to the left in its beastly form.

**Two Readings of the Mad Thirst**

The sūrahs preserve a second, stranger sense: him may also name a scorpion whose sting brings a thirst that no water quenches, and a minority reading takes the verse's camels as "the one who cannot drink" rather than "the one who cannot stop" — the mad thirst expressed as the inability to receive, not the compulsion to pour. Both classical senses are reported in the sūrahs' lexica and the verse is said to contain both — an Arabic image that carries the whole physiologies of failed thirst at once: you will drink endlessly, and you will not be watered. This commentary reports the second sense as lexical reception rather than adjudicating, because the sūrah's audience clearly took the line as unproblematically terrible either way; the grammar (washarāba al-ḥīm, an accusative of manner — "with the drinking of the him") leaves the camel's behavior unspecified and the water's failure absolute. Whatever the beast's precise affliction, the left inherit it, and the verse's genius is that the metaphor's mechanics — drink, thirst, drink again — are self-explanatory to anyone who has ever seen the thirsty animal at the dry pool.

**The Sūrah's Similes, Counted**

Al-Wāqiʿah uses similes four times, and their distribution is its aesthetics. For the cosmos: the mountains elsewhere become like carded wool (70:6), while this chapter's mountains are crushed to pieces and become scattered particles of dust (5–6) — and at 75–76 the stars' positions carry an oath instead of a comparison. For the near-rank: "like hidden pearls" (23), beauty defined by untouchability. For the left: "like molten brass" is not here, it is the neighbor's; this chapter gives them only "like the him." The pattern is precise: the saved get a simile from the treasury (the pearl, the thing hoarded and safe), the lost a simile from the pasture (the animal, the thing perishing at the water), and between them the sūrah's oath-sworn stars — the middle of the chapter's sky, unowned. Verse 55, the last of the left's images, is therefore the chapter's lower term: the audience's most cherished livestock, rendered in their most humiliating death, handed to the mockers of resurrection as their likeness forever, and the sūrah's economy of comparison is closed.

**Thirst as Resurrection-Doctrine**

What the left asked at 47 — "when we are dust and bones, will we really be raised?" — receives, in 52–55, an answer in the physiology they trusted: raised, they will spend eternity in the body's three hungers and thirsts, satiated with the impossible (zaqqūm, filling without nourishing, 53) and drinking without drink. The him is the figure of the body that cannot incorporate the world, and the sūrah's eschatology here is almost unbearably concrete: resurrection, for the denier, is the permanent return of the appetite without the permanence of its cure. Conversely the near-rank's drink is defined by what it never costs (19), and the two estates are, in the chapter's own terms, two ways water and food relate to a body. Verse 55 thus preaches the sūrah's whole argument-half (57–74) in an image: consider what you ejaculate, what you sow, what you drink — the arguments are about bodies; the camel at the trough is the body's future if the arguments are refused, and the sūrah knows no theology of resurrection more eloquent than an image every child of the desert could complete untaught.

**The End of the Quotation**

Verse 55 closes, typographically, the sūrah's last word spoken to the left: the present translation ends the line with its final quotation-mark, and after 55 the damned receive no further address, only description — the accommodation (56), the created you (57), the questions, the oath, the bedside. That closure is the chapter's design: the speech of the left, their one sneer quoted at 47–48, has been answered; God's vocative runs through 51–55 and stops at the camel-drinking, and what follows is verdict, argument, and doxology. The audience, who heard the "you" of 51–55 as their neighbors' indictment, are meant to feel the silence that falls after 55 — the moment when addressing stops is when the case is no longer before the court — and turn, with the sūrah, toward the four "have you considered" questions, where the only "you" left in the chapter is the one still able to believe it. The thirsty camel's line is thus, in the most literal sense, the end of the conversation; the diction of the remainder is the diction of what comes after words are wasted.
 And a commentary that has followed the chapter verse by verse can add only this: the sūrah's last image of its deniers is a beast at water, and everything the sermon said afterward, from 57 on, is addressed to the men who still can.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:56

> This will be their accommodation on the Day of Judgment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḍiyāfa: The Word for Hosting, Used as a Sentence**

The noun the present translation renders "accommodation" is ḍiyāfah — hospitality, the guest-right, the whole etiquette of receiving the traveler — and its choice, once felt, changes the verse's temperature entirely. The sūrah calls the Fire's menu a hospitality. The Book uses the same noun, with the same irony, in the Ranks chapter's sequence: "that is their ḍiyāfa, so let them taste it" (37:68), and — for the other side — "Is the accommodation of Paradise better, or the tree of Zaqqūm?" (37:62), where ḍiyāfa appears at the head of the blessed estate's description too. The two usages prove that the word belongs to the chapter's moral vocabulary: hosting is what one does to the arrived, and the Day of Judgment is, in al-Wāqiʿah's design, an arrival at what was always being prepared — the near-rank's "elevated furnishings" (34) and the left's ḥamīm are both, in this sūrah's grammar, things a guest is given. Nothing could be more offensive to Arab honor-usage than calling the Fire a ḍiyāfa, which is precisely why the sūrah calls it that.

**"The Day of Judgment": The Chapter's Name for Itself**

Yawm al-dīn — the phrase of verse 2, the Day whose reality the denier could not deny, reappears at 56 to close the left's first scene, and the inclusio means the whole intervening panorama (the estates, the exclamations, the menus) has been commentary on the two words. Dīn carries, in the audience's speech, both religion and debt — the counting, the repayment, the reckoning the creditor makes in the marketplace — and al-Wāqiʿah has been paying the double meaning through the entire passage: censuses like account-books, rewards "for what they used to do" (24), shade and water as things "extended" like credit, and now the ḍiyāfa that is the debt's final settlement. The verse thus names its own chapter's genre: this is not threat-literature but the reading-out of a file, and the flatness of 56 — a demonstrative, a possessive, two nouns — is the tone of the clerk whose entries are all closed. The Day of Judgment, said at the end of the menu, makes the menu into evidence, and the audience into jurors who have been holding the verdict since 2.

**This: The Deictic of Arrival**

"Hādhā" — this — is the verse's first word, and the pointing is the passage's whole eschatology in a pronoun: the left will not be told about their accommodation in advance forever; at the Day, the deictic arrives, and what was prophecy becomes this. The sūrah, which has spent its description in futurate verbs and state-nouns, ends the scene with the one word that cancels futurity: there will be no more "then" after the drink of the him (55); there will be this. Preachers from the sermon-literature onward made the same observation about the chapter's style — the Qur'an's future tense is a courtesy, a delay, and every "on that Day" is the closing of a distance — and verse 56 is that courtesy ending: the distance at which the mockers had stood to say "when we are dust and bones" (47) collapses into the pointing of the demonstrative, and the sūrah lets its audience feel, for one syllable, what the Day will be like to those for whom it has become the only pronoun. The smallest word in the verse is the one with the most theology in it.

**The Verse the Chapter Repeats at the Deathbed**

Verse 56's sentence returns, phrase for phrase, at the bedside summary: of the misguided denier, "their accommodation will be boiling water and burning in Hellfire" (93–94) — ḍiyāfa again, ḥamīm again, and the Day of Judgment now arrived as one person's death rather than the cosmos' collapse. That double placement is the sūrah's structural key: 56 closes the cosmic scene of the left, and 93–94 reopens the same scene at the scale of a single throat (83), which is how the chapter wants both read — the Day of Judgment is not a period in the calendar but the quality of every dying, already begun. The reader who has traveled 41–56 in the grand style arrives at 93 and recognizes the sentence; the recognition is the sūrah's argument that the two are the same event seen at different distances, and its final warning to the listener in the room with the corpse: the accommodation has already begun to be served. The re-use of ḍiyāfa in both places is what makes the recognition unmistakable, and turns verse 56 into the hinge between the chapter's two halves of terror.

**What the Verse Adds to the Threat: Nothing, Intentionally**

It is worth counting what verse 56 declines: no duration ("eternal" is not said here — eternity in this sūrah belongs to the Garden's nouns, never the Fire's), no angels of punishment, no catalog of torments beyond the drink, no "they will not die." The sūrah ends the left's scene with the flattest possible summative — this, their hospitality, that Day — and goes immediately to "It is We Who created you; will you not then bear witness?" (57), to argument, not to escalation. That restraint is al-Wāqiʿah's most characteristic severity: it gives the punishment its verdict and refuses it its drama, because drama would make the left interesting, and the sūrah's theology requires that they be, finally, only what they made themselves: guests of their own denial, hosted by the One they called liar, in the accommodation they spent a lifetime ordering for themselves, dust and bones and all. Everything the chapter will still say about them — the deathbed reprise of this exact wording at 92–94 — lives inside the flat summation of 56. The Day of Judgment, said once, is said enough.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:57

> It is We Who created you. Will you not then believe ˹in resurrection˺?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The "We" Returns, Now on the Stand**

Through the descriptions the chapter gave creation in polite passives — water poured, shade stretched, mates fashioned — and reserved its first-person plural for three moments: the mates (35), and now, four times in five verses, the argument's opening. "It is We Who created you" is the sūrah's thesis-statement for 57–74, and the fronting of "We" (inna-hu naḥnu khalaqnakum) makes it an exclusive claim: whoever else the audience credited with their making — the stars their soothsayers watched, the ancestors their genealogies honored, fortune they blamed for the chaff — is being answered by pronoun before verb. The bracketed ˹in resurrection˺, the translation's insertion into "will you not then believe," is correct exegesis: the object of belief the verse needs is not creation (which the deniers admitted) but the second act the first makes easy, and the sūrah argues, as it has all chapter, from the conceded to the denied.

**"Created You": Badaʾ, and the Absence of Embryology**

The verb is khalaqa, the Book's workhorse for making-without-precedent, and what the sūrah does not do with it deserves the note. Elsewhere the Qur'an narrates the human origin in stages — a drop, a clinging clot, a lump, bones clothed with flesh, then a new creation (the Believers chapter's famous sequence, 23:12–14) — and here, at the argument's hinge, al-Wāqiʿah mentions no stages, no anatomy, no wonder of formation: "We created you," full stop. The reason is the verse's own economy — it needs only the premise that the audience did not make themselves — and the reader who imports the embryological catalog into 57 is preaching from the other sūrah. The argument's strength is its spareness: any theory of origins, however crude, concedes the point, and the sūrah's proof works on the least-developed biology of the seventh century or the most-developed of ours, requiring only that a human be a made thing.

**The Question Form of a Chapter**

"Will you not then believe?" — a-fa-lā tuʾminūn — is a question whose expected answer the whole sūrah has been extracting since verse 2 (the Day no one can deny) and 51 (the deniers by name). The argument-half closes three times on this form: believe (57), be mindful (62), give thanks (70), each after one of the four "have you considered" interrogatives, and the pattern tells the reader that in al-Wāqiʿah epistemology, faith, memory, and gratitude are the same act at different ages: assent at the first glance, recollection at the second, thanks when the glance has been lived with. Late Makkān preaching rarely reasons; it asks — the sūrahs of the Qur'an's argument style call this the interrogation of shahādah, making the audience testify against their own case — and verse 57, opening the sequence, sets the register: God is not introducing evidence into a skeptical court but reminding friends who have forgotten what they grant daily, in the body, in the field, at the well, by the fire.

**"Then": The Small Adverb That Runs the Chapter**

Fa-lā — the "then" the translation keeps in each closing question — is the sūrah's hinge-word, and it carries its double work from 3 to 96. At the beginning, "when the Event takes place, then no one can deny" (1–2); in the middle, "It is We Who created you, will you not then believe" — the then is temporal and logical at once: at the Day you will believe, and right now the premise should force the conclusion; the sūrah's whole argument lives in that ambiguity, which is why the chapter can be recited at weddings and deathbeds with the same sentences. The translators' bracket ˹in resurrection˺ pins the object of belief, but the verse's own force is the fa: belief here is not a new doctrine but what the already-known Event, when genuinely considered, produces in any mind — the small word does the heavy lifting, and any reader tempted to call this argument circular should notice that the circle is the intended geometry of reminder: creation → Day → creation, each "then" a radius.

**The Verse in the Chapter's Arithmetic of Questioning**

Finally, a census of the half: 57–74 contains four "have you considered" openings (58, 63, 68, 71), four disjunctive challenge-questions ("is it you… or are We?" ×3, with the fire-verse expanded to "who produced its trees"), two "if We willed" threat-clauses (65, 70), and three closing appeals (57, 62, 70) before the doxological fourth (74, "So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest"). Verse 57 is the argument's overture, stating the thesis ("We created you") and the required response ("believe"), and everything after is four variations on that single chord, each in a different register of the audience's daily life — fluid, seed, water, fire — the four things a Makkān household touched every hour of its day. The sūrah's rhetoric of the commonplace begins here: the denier will not be convinced by the heavens (he is a trader, and the caravan's sky is a tool), so God argues from the things his hands cannot deny, starting with the one whose making he would be ashamed to claim.

**Creation as the Chapter's Own Definition of God**

One final fact about 57: in a chapter that has spoken of God as maker of water, fire, harvests, beds, spouses, and deaths, this verse states the thesis in the barest possible form — "We created you" — and then, until the doxology at 74, says nothing more about creation except to point at four ordinary processes. Al-Wāqiʿah's God is not introduced here as omnipotent; the attribute doing the work is creativity, and the required response is belief "in resurrection," not in power. That restraint is the sūrah's theology of nature: the argument's force depends entirely on creation being the one act nobody disputes, and its homiletic force on creation being continuous — the drop, the seed, the rain, the fire are still being made, so the resurrection is not a future miracle intruding on a natural order but the next clause of the order already at work. Late Makkān monotheism, at its most economical, says: you are made, therefore you will be remade, and the "therefore" is the whole of the sūrah's middle, the way the "then" of verse 2 was its beginning.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:58

> Have you considered what you ejaculate?

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Have You Considered": The Verb That Sees**

The formula a-fa-raʾaytum — the present line's "have you considered" — uses the Arabic verb of sight for an act of thought, and the sūrah's epistemology is in the pun: the way to know is to look at what you already do, closely, as though for the first time. Late Makkān preaching is full of this imperative to see (the Neighbor chapter's "have you not seen what your Lord did," 89:6, the Elephant's "have you not seen how your Lord dealt," 105:1), and al-Wāqiʿah turns the eye inward: its four "consider" questions will scan semen, seed, water, fire — the four substances of the household — without once asking the audience to contemplate the stars, which the oath at 75 will do for the Book's sake. The verse is thus the argument's manifesto of method: no cosmological subtlety, only the nearest body-function, held up for inspection.

**Maniyy: The Euphemism That Is Clinical**

The verb tamnū — "you pour out" — is Arabic's own discretion: the fluid (umniyyah) named by its spilling, no anatomy, no stage, no clot. The Qur'an speaks about semen without shame and without detail elsewhere — "from a fluid ejected, issuing from between the backbone and the ribs" (86:6–7, a description that is functional, not physiological) and "did he not used to be a drop of ejected fluid?" (75:37) — and 56:58 stands in the same register: what is asked is not a biology lesson but the admission that the fluid is not, at the moment of its pouring, anyone's achievement. Commentators of the sūrahs read 58's bluntness as pedagogical: the audience's boasting was of lineage (nasab), and the sūrah reaches into the very act from which all their genealogies are made and asks the one question pride cannot answer — did you make that drop? The euphemism makes the question possible for public recitation; the question, once asked in the mosque, unmakes the boast.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

Equally instructive is the verse's silence: no embryology, no "wonders of development," nothing the apologetic literature of later centuries loves to supply. Modern readers are often tempted to hear in "what you ejaculate" a foreshadowing of histology — and the temptation should be named and let go: the sūrah's argument needs the drop to remain what the audience knew it to be, a lost bit of the body, because its logic is about agency, not about complexity. The Qur'an describes the stages in its own places (23:12–14, 22:5), and those descriptions serve the reminder of formation; here the point is narrower and deeper: the fluid is your only raw material, and even it arrives in you from what you do not control — you eat, you drink, you pour, and none of it was manufactured by your will. Argument from the mundane, kept mundane, is the verse's discipline.

**The Second Person Plural of Daily Life**

The verb's subject — "you" plural — includes every hearer in the act being discussed, and this is the sūrah's audacity, felt more in recitation than in print: the assembly, men and women of Makkah, is addressed about the most private thing all of them have in common, and the sūrah will then immediately repeat the pronoun for the seed-sower and the water-drawer (63, 68). The "you" is never accusatory; it is inclusive — the sermon's first move, in this chapter, is to make every listener a witness in their own case rather than a juror in someone else's, which is why the argument-half reads so differently from the denunciation-half: no "they" survives inside 57–74 except in the threat-clauses of verses 65 and 67. The audacity is the method: a God who talks about everybody's bodily secret in the same tone as about everybody's harvest treats no part of the human as too low for the holy, and that, not the semen, is the verse's real scandal to every religious taste that ever separated spirit from soil.

**The Drop and the Day**

One final placement-note: verse 58 sits twenty-eight lines after the census (6–14) and forty before the bedside (83–94), and the chapter's span from "you become three pairs" to "you look on while the soul climbs" is the same span the drop crosses. Al-Wāqiʿah has no doctrine of the body's nobility except this: that from the lowest of its fluids to the last of its breaths, the human is a made thing with a Maker who is speaking to it, and the Day that sorts is only the completion of the making. The mockers of 47 asked, "when we are dust and bones, will we really be raised?" — the sūrah's reply begins at 58 with the premise of the reply: you began in a fluid you did not make; do not stake your eternity on the premise that you will end in a dust God cannot gather. Consider the first drop, and the last question is already answered; that is how a late Makkān sūrah does natural theology — by sending every adult, in public, to look privately at the thing they most certainly have, without once saying the word for what it is.

**The Consideration the Sūrah Actually Asks For**

What 58 commands is not reflection on the marvel of the fluid but a return of attention to the act itself — "have you considered (fa-raʾaytum) what you eject" — and the distinction is the sūrah's whole psychology of memory. The audience's error, on this chapter's diagnosis (45–46), was not a missing piece of information but an unexamined given: they knew where babies came from and had never once let the knowing bear on the question of what they were. "Consider it" is therefore the sūrah's only explicit epistemic instruction, and the bedside passage at 83 will give the same command its mirror — "you look on (yaẓurun)" at the dying throat, watching an exit as nobody watched an entrance — so that the first and last movements of a life are framed by one verb of seeing, and the argument of 57–62 sits between them like the unread middle of a book everyone owns. The drop considered is the whole sūrah's school of attention, and the verse begins its lesson where no pupil can claim exemption.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:59

> Is it you who create ˹a child out of˺ it, or is it We Who do so?

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Or": The Question With No Third Door**

The Arabic's disjunctive am ("is it you… or is it We") frames a choice the sūrah refuses to widen: there is no third answer in which the fluid makes itself, or chance does it, or the ancestors do it — the question's shape is the argument's whole content. Al-Wāqiʿah's theology of creation is, at this point in the chapter, radically simple, and the simplicity is the Makkān point: the audience's gods were sharers, partners "who own" and "who intercede," and the Qur'an's recurring demonstration is that partnership requires, at minimum, one act anywhere that another agent performed — the drop being the case in point, because no claimant — not al-Lāt, not al-ʿUzzā, not the family's own memory — has ever put a soul in semen or taken one out. The verse does not enumerate what God did in the embryo; it asks who did it, and its two-option grammar is how the sūrah closes every loophole at once without arguing a single loophole.

**"Create It": The Verb That Exceeds the Fluid**

To the audience's own craft-vocabulary, "creating" (kh-ل-q) meant what a maker does: shaping leather, weaving cloth, forging the head of a lance — and the sūrahs record that Arab poets could call a fine poet "the best of those who shape (khāliq)" in the idiom of making, which is why the verse's verb stings: it asks whether their making reaches into the making of a person, and quietly answers no maker, however skilled, has ever made what he worked with. The Believers sūrah ends its embryological sequence with exactly this coronation: "blessed be God, the best of shapers" (23:14), a verse that knows the audience's praise-formula and redirects it — and 56:59, in its plainer Makkān accent, does the same work by question: "is it you who create it?" The craft is honored; the creation is claimed; the sūrah never says the maker is nothing, only that making is not the same as giving being, and the fluid's first being is the proof.

**"Or We Who Did It": The Modesty of the Evidence**

The second half of the question is the sūrah's only positive assertion in this pair, and its reserve deserves notice: "or it is We Who do so" — no description of formation, no claim about ensoulment, no timetable, nothing a skeptical reader can test, dispute, or over-read. The sūrah's claim, here and throughout the argument-half, is about the direction of credit, not the mechanism of embryology, and readers who want the Qur'an to "anticipate science" in these lines find only an assertion of divine authorship — which, as it happens, is the one part of the argument no science can retire: whoever maps the stages, nobody made the map out of nothing. The present translation's bracket ˹a child out of˺ supplies the object the Arabic elides ("create it"), and the elision is the iʿjāz the sūrahs admired: "is it you who create it, or We?" — the whole of the soul's biography in six words, the pronoun "it" pointing at the drop and at the child at once, because in the sūrah's grammar those two are the same thing, which is precisely the point the mockers at 47 could not grant.

**"You Ejaculate" / "We Create": The Two Verbs of One Event**

Read 58 and 59 together as the sūrah composed them and the verse-pair becomes a miniature of its whole metaphysics: the human act is named with a verb of pouring-out (tamnūn, yours, daily, unremarkable); the divine act with a verb of creation (khalaqnā, once, for every one of those pourings). Nothing in the couplet says you do nothing — your pouring is real, the sūrah concedes it, in the second person — and nothing says God does it instead of you — the fluid is genuinely yours. The two agencies are on different levels entirely: yours provides the material you did not make, for a form you do not give, and the sūrah's afterlife will resolve the same couplet at scale — "We ordained death among you" (60) completes the pattern: what begins in your body ends in God's decree, and the resurrection the mockers denied is simply the middle of a sentence whose first and last verbs are not yours. That is al-Wāqiʿah's entire theory of the human, stated in ten words before the audience has caught its breath.

**Why the Drop and Not the Star**

The final observation is an argument about the sūrah's choice. Late Makkān audiences respected astrology — Mecca's market knew its star-lots, and the sūrah's own oath by "the positions of the stars" (75) shows the Book can reach into that sky when it wants to honor the Qur'an. For the proof of resurrection, al-Wāqiʿah declines astronomy altogether and selects the semen, because the argument's burden is not to demonstrate power in the far but agency in the near: any denial of the second creation is self-refuting at the first, and the first is the one thing the denier's body re-enacts every night of his life, whether he believes or not. The stars move by laws no one claims; the drop is produced by a self that claims — that is why the sūrah starts its courtroom at the bedside and not the horizon, and why verse 59, the verdict-question of the couplet, needs no evidence beyond the man who hears it, the fluid he spills, and the mother of the child neither of them made.

The couplet's last word, "We," is therefore the sūrah's smallest altar: whoever the hearer has been crediting — sky, lots, lineage, self — is asked, politely and in the first person plural, to name the one maker the drop never had.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:60

> We have ordained death for ˹all of˺ you, and We cannot be prevented

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qaḍāʾ: Death as a Completed Act**

The verb is qaḍā — to decree, and also to finish, to carry through to the end (the same root gives the Day "of Decision," yaum al-faṣl) — and the sūrah does not say "We will make you die" but "We have ordained death among you": a settled matter, filed and closed, its grammar as certain as the decree it states. The choice lets the verse carry two things at once: death as law (every human is inside it now, the way a town is inside a tax edict) and death as act (already finished from God's side, which is why the near-rank can die in the chapter's late verses at peace — for them the decree is a summons, not a destruction). The audience's own poet had written, as poets did, that death is the one thing a man cannot outrun, avert, or buy; the sūrah converts that fatalism into a personal appointment with a first-person verb, and the conversion — fate to decree, decree to address — is verse 60's quiet revolution in the Arabia of "fortune (dahr) does it."

**"Cannot Be Prevented": The Image of the Race**

The Arabic's last word, masbūqūn, is from racing — "We are not to be outrun, forestalled, got ahead of" — and the metaphor fits a people who measured honor in the speed of their camels and the escape of their clients: no one has ever arrived anywhere before God's decision about them. The Heifer chapter's line about those who fled their towns "in their thousands for fear of death" — and God caused them to die, then gave them life (2:243) — is the same doctrine told as story; and the verse 60 here tells it as the unlosable race whose every entry is already decided: you cannot be first to evade what is ordained to you, and — the sūrah's next word — the defeat is productive, not terminal, which is the one sentence the fatalist's Arabic never had. Read against the mockers' "when we are dust, will we really be raised?" (47), "We cannot be outrun" means: your dust is, specifically, our turf; the race you imagined against annihilation, you have already lost on our terms, and our terms say re-creation.

**Among You: The Universality That Levels the Census**

The decree is "lakum" — to you, among you — and its universality is the sūrah's first and only egalitarian axiom stated flatly: every estate of verses 6–14 dies, the near-rank's thrones notwithstanding, because death is the one event the chapter never sorts by class. That is why 60 sits where it does — immediately after the semen-verse: the drop levels origin (nobody was asked before it), the decree levels end (nobody is asked before it), and only the middle, the believing middle, is the variable; verses 13–14 and 39–40 counted classes, but 60–62 count humanity as one. The audience's genealogical pride, which the sūrah had already addressed through the forefathers-line of 48, is answered here from the other end: the ancestors the mockers cite as their defense are inside the decree exactly as their mockers will be, and "whichever form" the next verse (61) speaks of applies to both. Death, in al-Wāqiʿah, is the chapter's great simplifier.

**"And We Cannot Be Prevented" as Comfort, Not Threat**

Modern ears hear the clause as menace; its first listeners, mostly, would have heard the double-edge the sūrah always runs: the God who cannot be outrun in killing is the same who cannot be outrun in raising — verse 61 proves the syntax ("We are not to be outrun ˹in Our power to˺ substitute…"), and the near-rank's serenity and perfume (89) prove the theology. The verse's pastoral register deserves the note precisely because the sūrah was recited at bedsides: "We have ordained death among you" spoken to a dying person is not a sentence of despair in a chapter whose God has just described the dying as "serenity and perfume and a Garden of Bliss" — it is the assurance that what is happening is inside a decree that cannot be reversed, delayed, or bargained with, and therefore needs no more struggle, and the struggle's end is 61's "transforming and re-creating." The threat-mode and the comfort-mode are one verse; the difference between them, al-Wāqiʿah has said from the start, is class — which class you were busy becoming (24, 45–46).

**The Decree's Place in the Argument's Machine**

Structurally, 60 is the sūrah's pivot between its two proofs: 58–59 (the drop) and 63–67 (the harvest) are the arguments from what you do, and 60–62 (death, transformation, the known first creation) is the argument from what is done to you. The decree-verse is the middle and the hinge of that arrangement, and its "We cannot be prevented" answers a specific objection: the mockers' calendar, the sūrah suggests, assumes that death is the end of God's jurisdiction — that dust, once scattered, falls outside the law — and verse 60 asserts, in the racing idiom, that jurisdiction has no finish-line; whatever "you" are becoming, He is not outrunning it, He is ordaining it, and ordaining is continuous, not retrospective. Death in al-Wāqiʿah is therefore never a pause in God's verbs; it is a verb of His, and that single grammatical fact is the whole sūrah's answer, before it ever reaches the stars' oath or the dying-throat scene, to the question of the dust.

**"For You" and "Among You": The Pronoun's Double Hold**

The Arabic's prepositions around the decree — ʿalaykum, among you — give the death sentence a legal ring, the phrasing of an ordinance "against" a people, and the same double hold (for you / upon you) will re-appear at the chapter's end, where accommodation is "for them" the boiling drink (93). The sūrah thus binds the decree's universality and its personal address in one construction: death is not a fate that befalls the third person, it is a ruling filed against every listener by name — and since 57 has just said "We created you," the same "you" who were made are the "you" on whom the ordinance stands, which is exactly the verse's contribution to the resurrection-argument: the Maker's jurisdiction opens with creating and closes with dying, and the mockers who grant the one cannot plead exemption from the other.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:61

> from transforming and recreating you in forms unknown to you.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Verbs of Change, and What Their Order Says**

The Arabic stacks nubaddila (substitute, convert) and nunshiʾakum (bring up into new growth) — the second the same root the chapter used for making the mates afresh (35), the verb of plants springing — and the sequence describes the resurrection as a conversion followed by an origination: what you are gets turned, and then you are raised again in what you do not know. Commentators of the sūrahs debated whether both verbs belong to the next world or whether "transforming" alludes also to this world's punishments-of-metamorphosis (the Sabbath-breakers of the Heifer chapter were converted into apes, in the Book's retelling of their story), and the plain reading of this context — death-ordained, then transform-and-recreate — is eschatological, with no reason the first sense cannot lean, as the sūrah so often does, on the audience's memory of what God has been reported to do to peoples, and the pair thus says: He who has changed nations has changed you once already, and the Day's change is His ordinary idiom.

**"Forms Unknown to You": The Sūrah's Agnosticism About the Body**

Al-Wāqiʿah's resurrection is emphatically bodily and emphatically unspecified, and this verse is the source of the balance. The chapter gives the near-rank couches and the left a menu, insists the self survives the scatter of verse 6, and then refuses, at 61, to say what the returned body looks like: forms that you do not know. The sūrahs pressed the point for doctrine — against materialists of every century who demanded an anatomy for the risen, and against the overly literal who measured Paradise in inches — and the verse's own phrasing is the mediating answer: continuity of person (kum, the same object as the "We created you" of 57), discontinuity of form. That is why the bedside reading of this sūrah pairs 61 with 83–87: the soul climbing the throat is the last moment the known form is the one you recognize; "unknown to you" is, in the same breath, the chapter's promise that dying is not a subtraction from what the hearer can imagine, and its warning that nothing they will be is predictable from anything they were.

**The Verse's Quiet Argument Against the Dust-objection**

Set 61 back inside the mockers' sentence and it answers them at their own hinge. Their objection at 47 was: dust and bones do not reassemble — the materials are too ruined. Verse 61 concedes the ruin entirely: you will not reassemble; you will be transformed and brought up new, in forms not in your inventory of materials at all. The sūrah's resurrection is not archaeology, and 61 lets no reader pretend it is: the "re-creation" (nashʾ) verb, chosen in the same chapter where God "made the mates anew" (35), makes the risen body, like the Garden's spouses, a fresh sprouting rather than a repair — and the mockers' physics is thus answered not by a stronger physics but by a change of category: what God does with dust is not gather it but graduate it. The argument-half's logic (58–59: the drop you did not make) generalizes here: just as the body's origin was not an assembly you performed, its end will not be one you can object to.

**"In Forms You Do Not Know" in the Sūrah's Economy of Surprise**

Notice how often this chapter withholds its images behind the word "know" — "positions of the stars," an oath "if only you knew" how great it is (75–76); the Book "touched by none except the purified" (79); and here: forms unknown. Al-Wāqiʿah's epistemology is that the biggest realities are, right now, informationally closed to the audience, and its ethics is that living as though they were open is what the saved do. Verse 61 puts the post-mortem body inside that same closedness — not a mystery to be solved by the pious, but a form to be received without preview — and the effect on the reciter is instructive: one practices, on this verse, the very relation to the unknown that dying will be. The chapter that opened by telling its hearers what happens when nothing solid stays solid ends its first argument by telling them they are, already, on their way into a shape that has no file in their imagination, and that the Only Shaper (59's verb again) is the one sentence they do need to know.

**Why "Recreate" Shares Its Verb with the Mates**

The final fact of the verse is its word: nush'a is the noun family of inshaʾ, and the chapter has now used the family twice — of the spouses made "in a [new] creation" (35, where the present line's bracket reads "perfectly created") and of the selves made over after death (61). The sūrah's readers have always noticed verbal echoes as architecture, and this one is load-bearing: marriage and resurrection share a verb because, in al-Wāqiʿah's theology, both are the same act — God introducing what is worn into what is new, without cancelling the relation (the mates are still "their" mates; the self is still "you"). Verse 61, on that reading, is not merely the argument's next move; it is the promise-section's hidden stanza: the body that will not fit its imagination and the love that will not fit its history are, in this chapter's single vocabulary, equally a sprouting — and the unknown forms are, for the near-rank, precisely the Garden: what you cannot picture is what you were being made for.

And the clause's mercy within its severity is worth one last plain word: "unknown to you" means that nothing you dread about the body's future is what you will get, and nothing you have earned for it is lost in the changing — the forms pass, the address ("you") does not.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:62

> You already know how you were first created. Will you not then be mindful?

**Expanded Commentary**

**"You Know": The Argument from Shared, Not Sacred, Knowledge**

The sūrah does not say "I will tell you how you were made" but "you already know" — ʿalimtum — and the epistemology of the appeal is the chapter's core method. The Makkān audience's knowledge of their own origins was not anatomical but biographical: every birth in a household was attended, every drop of the argument's first premise conceded by everyone, because everyone came the same way and no family claimed to have made its own members. The Ranks chapter will later press the point in the Book's own terms — man created "from a dust, then a drop of fluid" (the sūrah of Prostration's reminder, 32:7–8, in plain paraphrase) — and verse 62 declines to cite even that: your knowing, it says, is pre-scriptural, lodged in the fact that you exist and were born. The sūrah's evidences are always the audience's own; its sermon style is recollection, and the difference between the two is the entire difference between the Qur'an's proof and the philosopher's.

**Tadhakkur: Forgetting as the Disease the Sūrah Treats**

The closing verb, tadhdhakkar, is the root of dhikr — remembrance — and in the Qur'an's anthropology the sin of the deniers is rarely ignorance; it is inattention, the un-remembering of what is conceded. Al-Wāqiʿah has already staged it: the Event "no one can deny" (2), the forefathers the mockers cite as if they were beyond summons (48), the Book they "take lightly" (81). Verse 62's "will you not then be mindful" prescribes against that disease with the sūrah's own name as medicine — the Qur'an, in its self-designation, is "the Reminder (dhikr)," sent so that what the soul already knows is not lost (the Book's claim, paraphrased in the Heifer and the Qāf chapters: "a Reminder to whoever has a heart" — the Qāf verse's exact idiom, 50:37) — and the verse's placement right after the "unknown forms" of 61 makes the logic complete: the future body cannot be known, but the past origin can be remembered, and remembering the lesser is the sūrah's way of making the greater credible.

**The A Fortiori Engine of 57–74**

Read 62 as the argument's thesis-key: if you were made once from nothing you did not prepare, the second making is not harder but easier, and the mockers' difficulty ("dust and bones?") is therefore not an objection but a forgetting. The Book states this syllogism aloud elsewhere, in the chapter of Ya-Sin, where the man who crumbles the bones is answered: "Who will give life to bones while they are decayed? Say: He will give them life who produced them the first time" (36:78–79, cited in plain paraphrase because the sūrahs count them as one argument) — and al-Wāqiʿah, which never quotes Ya-Sin, runs the identical demonstration inside its own four "consider" questions: first creation (57), drop (58–59), decree of death (60), transformation (61), and the reminder of the knowing (62), five rings of one chain. The sūrah's argument-half is thus not four proofs but one proof — the a fortiori of the made — turned in four materials.

**Why "Then" Ends the First Movement**

Verse 62 is the last line of the chapter's first argument-movement before the sowing-passages (63–67) begin, and its fa-lā — the "then" of all three closers (57, 62, 70) — closes the movement on the listener's obligation, not God's: the sūrah's verdict after creation-and-death is "be mindful," a verb the audience can perform in the next breath, in the market, at the well. That is why 62 needs no threat, no promise, no image; its two clauses complete 57's question ("will you not believe") with its memory-variant, and the third, gratitude (70), will close the whole half like a chord. The sūrah's rhetoric here is at its plainest and, in the classical judgment, its strongest — late Makkān passages that argue without ornament are rare in the Book, and commentators of the sūrahs treated 57–74 as a specimen of the "argumentative sūrahs (ḥujajiyyāt)," whose verse-divisions fall on the audience's duties (believe, remember, thank) rather than on God's acts.

**The Verse at the End of the Census It Began**

One structural circle deserves closing: verse 6 began the chapter "and you become three pairs," and verse 62 ends the first argument with the same second-person plural — "you were created," "you know," "you will be mindful" — the three classes of 6–14 now reduced, in the reasoning, to one "you," which is the sūrah's final statement about the Day's sorting: the classes are the future tense of the shared past. Every census-line from 8 to 52 ("the people of the right," "the people of the left," "the foremost") subdivides a humanity that 62 insists is continuous in origin, and the effect is that the chapter's terror and its comfort come from the same fact: you, who know where you came from, are being addressed about where you are going, and the sūrah's one question at the hinge — will you not be mindful — can be answered by anyone, in any of the three pairs, while the knowing lasts. That is al-Wāqiʿah's whole pastoral, and 62, the plainest verse of its middle, is where the sūrah says it without images.

**The Verse That Answers 75 Before It Comes**

One structural grace: al-Wāqiʿah will soon swear "by the positions of the stars" that this is "a noble Quran, in a well-preserved Record" (75–77), and verse 62 has already supplied the epistemology the oath will seal. The chapter's knowledge-claim is not that God reveals the unknown but that He re-presents the known — first creation, ordinary death, the fluid, the seed — under the title of Reminder, and the sūrah's own answer to the mockers' sneer is that everything it asks them to believe is already in their hands, awaiting recollection. "Will you not then be mindful" is thus the verse that tells the reader how to read the coming oath: the Book is noble because it is true, and true because, in the end, it is the audience's own memory spoken back to them — the last verse of the sūrah's first argument before its four questions begin their rounds, and the hinge on which the whole middle turns.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:63

> Have you considered what you sow?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Consideration: From the Body to the Field**

The argument-half runs on one repeated imperative — "have you considered" — applied in order to what you eject (58), what you sow (63), what you drink (68), and what you kindle (71), and the sequence moves outward from the body through the field to the sky and the hearth, the four domains a Makkān household actually inhabited. The second, at 63, is the one the audience's wealth was built on: grain was the south's money as much as its food, the storehouse was the bank, and the sūrahs of the South Arabian terraces are a civilization's autobiography told in irrigation. By placing sowing next to semen the sūrah has made both into the same class of evidence — acts every man performs and no man completes — and the verse's brevity ("what you sow," two words in Arabic, the verb zaraʿa and its object) does the rest: the sermon will not allegorize the seed here, though later readers loved to; it simply puts the furrow beside the womb as second witness to verse 57's thesis, "We created you," in a court of one exhibit per room.

**"What You Sow": The Grammar of the Human Share**

The verb's subject is you — zaraʿtum, your own agricultural past — and the sūrah concedes the sowing before it contests anything, which is the argument's fairness and its depth: nobody is told their labor is illusion. What will be contested in the next verse is only the harvest's making, and that division of labor — your act, His completion — is the verse's real subject, the one that occupied centuries of kalām: the theologians of acquisition (kasb) and creation read 63–64 as a proof-text for the middle doctrine that the human "sows" while God "grows," and the verse-pair's syntax, a concession in 63 and a rebuttal in 64, is precisely why both schools could quote it. Verse 63, read on its own, is thus a sentence about work: the sūrah's audience — traders who did not farm and farmers who were traded upon — is told, without a single word of reproach for labor, that the whole of their exertion sits inside a process they neither originate nor sustain.

**The City Without Fields**

One contextual fact sharpens the verse: Makkān scripture is honest that the sanctuary-town was agricultural nonsense — the Heifer chapter's own account of Ibrāhīm's prayer makes the valley's barrenness the premise of the settlement ("a land without cultivation," 14:37), and the city's fame was the trade that carried grain into it, not the grain it grew. "Have you considered what you sow" therefore addresses, at 63, an audience for whom sowing was a profession of neighbors (the Ṭāʾif farmers, the Yemeni terraces) and an import, and the sūrah's argument reaches them through the thing they bought weekly: to live at Makkah was to depend, every loaf, on rain and growth you could not station guards around. The verse's choice of sowing over reaping — the act of putting seed in the ground, the gesture of pure expectation — fits that listener exactly: Makkah's piety, like its economy, was a bet placed on what someone else's fields and heaven's water would do.

**Seeds Elsewhere in the Book**

Al-Wāqiʿah cites no seed-parables here, but the audience's scripture was thick with them, and the verse's "what you sow" is a compressed intertext: the Heifer's "a seed that splits the sprout" (6:95, plain paraphrase), the Heifer's own parable of the grain that puts forth seven ears, a hundred in each (2:261, paraphrased), and the Victory chapter's sower whose shoot "He sprouts, then makes it grow strong" (48:29, in plain paraphrase), and the Kafrūn sūrah's devastating "you certainly know" structure — "not are you worshippers of what I worship" — which verse 68's format will imitate. The Qur'an's agriculture is, across these passages, a single argument: growth is the clearest case of un-owned power, and the seed, because it must leave the hand to live, is the object that best dramatizes trust. Verse 63 places that whole scriptural stock, without quotation, behind four words — "have you considered what you sow" — and lets the listener's own familiarity with the Book's farming do the exegesis.

**The Verse Before the Threat**

Finally, notice what follows and why: 64 asks who grows it, 65 says "if We willed, We could make it crumbled stubble, and you would keep on wondering." The sūrah's second consideration is thus the only one of the four followed by an explicit "if We willed" clause — a threat hung on the field and not on the fluid — and the asymmetry makes verse 63 the hinge between the two registers of the argument: the body is God's proof, but the harvest is God's leverage, because the audience's survival, unlike their generation, could be bargained with, taxed, salted, and lost, and the sūrah exploits that vulnerability. To "consider what you sow" is, in al-Wāqiʿah's court, to consider the thing that can be taken; semen cannot extort a verdict from a denier, wheat can, because he feeds his children from it and knows it. The verse is gentle in form and ruthless in placement: the second question in a chain whose second answer is famine.

**Sowing as the Second Chapter of One Argument**

The verse also marks the argument's change of judge. Semen testimony (58–59) convictsdirectly: nobody made himself. Field testimony (63–65) convicts indirectly, because sowing is the one domain where the audience would cheerfully admit dependence and still deny resurrection — their agricultural humility and their eschatological pride coexisted without embarrassment, and verse 63, standing between the two, makes coexistence impossible by forcing the same verb-pair onto both: you do the first half of each process, and neither of you has ever done the second. That is why the sūrah keeps "consider" for both halves: not to shame the farmer, but to catch him, mid-sentence, in what the sower and the begetter share — the act whose outcome is not theirs — and a hearer who has considered one must, the verse implies, consider the other, which is the entire design of 57–74 stated at its second hinge.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:64

> Is it you who cause it to grow, or is it We Who do so?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Nashz: The Root That Grows Crops, Mates, and Bodies**

The verb of the challenge — "is it you who cause it to grow?" — carries the sūrah's own root for sprouting: n-sh-ʾ, to bring up, to cause to rise, and the same root has already done duty twice in this chapter and does so once more below: God "made the mates in a [new] making" (inshaʾan, 35), "We could transform and bring you up in a form you do not know" (nunshiʾakum, 61), and here, the growth of what the farmer buries (tunshīʾūna? — the sūrahs read the verb as the raising of vegetation). One root, therefore, runs from the re-creation of spouses to the resurrection of bodies through the daily sprouting of grain, and the sūrah is making its case with morphology as much as with questions: in al-Wāqiʿah's vocabulary, marriage-freshness, harvest, and the risen body are the same act of God, one verb wearing three contexts. The earth's sprouting is also, elsewhere in the Book, the earth's speech — the Quake chapter has the ground "tell its news, because your Lord has inspired it" (99:4–5) — and the mockers at 47, who dismissed the reassembly of dust, are answered by the dust that rises every spring on their own terraces, speaking, testifying, sending up what nobody put there.

**An Open Question the Hearer Cannot Exit**

The disjunctive form — "is it you… or is it We" — is identical to verse 59's, and the repetition is itself the argument: the audience, having conceded the "or" for the child's creation, cannot withdraw it for the crop's growth. The verse demands no doctrine, only the choice between two options its hearer already knows, and the sūrah is confident the field will answer "We" the way the womb did. What the sūrah also declines is a third option — no "nature" slot is offered, not out of discourtesy but because the audience's nature was their weather, and verses 68–70 will argue the rain separately and reassign it in the same form. By its shape alone, 64 closes the loophole the denier always tries, the concession that God made the first push and then things just grow: the question has already put the whole growing, this season's included, inside the same unanswered "or."

**The Labor Theology of a Trading Town**

For an audience whose grain arrived by contract and caravan, 64 makes a point of economics as much as physics: the sūrah does not deny the farmer's labor (63 said "what you sow") and does not credit him with the increase — and that division, stated in the market's own terms, is a sermon against the one vice the caravan civilization practiced at scale: pricing what it did not produce. The Book's hoarding-laws elsewhere — the hoarding of gold and silver that is not spent in God's way, with its tidings of a painful torment (9:34, in plain paraphrase), a theme the Iron sūrah's hardening-verse sounds at 57:16, already noted in this commentary — provide the moral half of the argument; 64 provides the metaphysical one: growth is not a thing you do, so the increase you priced was never yours to begin with, and the Day's census (8–14) sorts people by what they did with what was given, which is the only ownership the sūrah ever admits.

**"We Cause It to Grow": The Continuity of Creating**

The present tense of the divine verb — "We are causing it to grow," the sūrah answering in its own imperfect — puts the making of the crop not in a past-deity but in ongoing act, and that tense is the sūrah's contribution to its own resurrection-logic: if growth is something God is doing now, the second growth (nushuʾ, 61) is a continuation of the same verb, not a new argument. The classical readers of these verses, in the kalām schools, read 64 as a proof-text for the moment-by-moment renewal of creation — that the crop's ripening is not a machine running down but a gift being given each hour — and the verse's plain sense bears them out without technical vocabulary: the seed that was "sown" is not a finished product awaiting the market, it is a process owned by Another at every stage, which is precisely why 65 can threaten to crumble what has not yet been given at all. A sūrah that said "We grew it once" would license deism; "We are causing it to grow" licenses only gratitude or greed.

**The Growth the Chapter Is Really About**

One last reading of 64 inside al-Wāqiʿah, and it is the argument-half's whole purpose: the verse is not finally about farming, because the sūrah's subject since verse 1 has been the Event's credibility — the Day that raises, gathers, sorts. The growth-verse, like the semen-verse, is a reductio of the mockers' physics: they who dismiss the reassembly of dust rely daily, for their bread, on a rising they cannot perform even in the single seed they held in their own hands, and the sūrah offers no cosmology beyond that mirror: "is it you who cause it to grow?" The denier's honest answer — no, it is We, says God for him — is extracted in five words, and the extraction is the demonstration: a man who grants the second cause of wheat has already, by the same act of counting, granted the first cause of bodies, and the resurrection at 47 is no more than wheat, believed. The argument's audacity is its plainness, and 64, its second and sharpest beat, is the plainest of the plain: every harvest is a resurrection nobody objects to, arriving without a witness, twice a year, for free.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:65

> If We willed, We could simply reduce this ˹harvest˺ to chaff, leaving you to lament,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥuṭāmah: The Sound of the Thing Crushed**

The word the present translation renders "chaff" is ḥuṭam — an object of the root meaning to crumble, to bruise to fragments, the same word used for dates crushed in the hand and, in the Sijjīl passage of the Elephant chapter, for the birds' stones that make the army "like eaten straw" (105:5, plain paraphrase — the Book's master image for a harvest or an army reduced to fodder). Ḥuṭam is not a botanical state but an aftermath: what remains when the crop is broken down past use, and the sūrah's threat is therefore not drought, not blight, but the specific horror of the granary-age — labor completed, produce present, and then crushed to stuff no animal will take. For an audience whose calendar, debts, and dowries all hung on the harvest's yield, the verse names the total loss available to a God who "willed," and the word's sound — the emphatic ḥ, the heavy stops — performs the breaking the meaning describes.

**"If We Willed": The Sūrah's Second Divine Leverage**

This is the argument-half's second law-nashāʾ clause — "if We willed, We could make it salty" at 70 is its twin — and both serve the sūrah's central claim that nothing in the created order is owed. The clause is not decoration: al-Wāqiʿah has just argued growth as continuous divine act (64), and 65 states the corollary in the negative — if the sprouting is His giving, the crumbling is His un-giving, possible at any hour, no mechanism required, no malice needed, only will. The classical theologians read these verses as proof-texts for the doctrine that nature is custom, not compulsion — that God sustains the world's regularity moment by moment and could interrupt it at any point without contradiction — and the pastoral intent of the verse is simply to keep the audience inside that contingency: the chaff is one will away from your barn, and your security was never in the barn. A sūrah whose opening shook mountains and unpegged mountains (4–5) has, at 65, brought the same physics of contingency home to the field-gate.

**"Leaving You to Lament": The Verse Turns the Farmers into a Chorus**

The Arabic continues — fa-tazallūna tujaʿjʿūna, the present line's "leaving you to lament" — and the verb of the lament, its rare root of the hoot and the howl, the owl's cry, gives the scene its sound: the sūrah does not describe the loss; it stages the noise of losers, a people left keening in their own fields. The choice completes the verse's movement from threat to theater — We willed, We crumbled, you cry — and the crying, as the next two verses make explicit, is quoted (66–67), in the first person, so that the audience hears their own hypothetical voices inserted into the recitation. This is late Makkān preaching at its most inventive: the sūrah does not curse the farmers with loss but lets them rehearse the lament aloud, an exercise in grief performed on a condition God has just lifted, and the listeners, caught reciting the keening with the congregation, have said "we are ruined" about a harvest still standing. The lament is pedagogical; the chaff, a rehearsal.

**Wonderment or Bewilderment: Reading tujaʿjʿūna**

The sūrahs' lexicographers weighed the verb hard, because it is one of the Qur'an's rarest: among the senses transmitted are being amazed, wondering at the turn of things — and bewilderment, the stupefied running to and fro of people for whom no explanation holds. Both readings fit the verse, and they are not the same emotion: wonder looks at the crushed harvest as a sign; bewilderment looks for someone to blame. The sūrah's placement favors the compound: the lament of 66–67 that follows does not wonder, it accuses — "we are afflicted, we are deprived" — and the verse therefore lets the audience feel the slide from stupefaction to grievance that, in this chapter's anthropology, precedes every "why" aimed at heaven. The rare word thus earns its rarity: it names the exact second in which a person stops processing a loss and starts performing it, and the sūrah, recited beside dying beds for fourteen centuries, knows that second better than any sermon about it.

**The Verse the Surah Answers with Gratitude**

Finally, 65 belongs to the chapter's economy of thanks. Its last argument-question, at 70, ends "will you not then give thanks?" — shakūr, the grateful servant of whom the Saba chapter said few are (34:13, cited already at this chapter's verse 14) — and verse 65 is the verse that defines what gratitude is for: not for the harvest enjoyed (that is 64's growth, conceded) but for the harvest not yet crushed. The sūrah's theology of shukr, in this passage, is the flip side of its theology of will: every morning the chaff did not come is the morning God did not will, and the grateful are those who read the barn's silence as speech. Al-Wāqiʿah has, from its thornless lote trees to its never-cut fruit, described the Garden as provision with the "if We willed" clause spent forever — and verse 65 stands in the argument-half like the world's one paragraph on why the clause has not yet been spent on you: read it as mercy, the sūrah says, while it is still the condition and not the outcome, and let the lament be only a rehearsal, keening over standing grain, the sound of a people God chose not to make an example.

**Chaff and the Dust of Verse 6: The Chapter's One Reduction**

Al-Wāqiʿah has already crushed something in this chapter: the mountains, at verses 5–6, became "scattered ˹particles of˺ dust," and the chaff of verse 65 is that same reduction run through the human scale, stone and stalk alike returned to unsalable fragments. The sūrah's physics of loss is one verb wearing two objects — the geology of the Day and the agriculture of the week — and the audience who heard the mountains flattened at the opening should hear, at 65, their own fields flattened on the same terms: the Event is not a foreign catastrophe visiting a stable world; it is the world's own contingency, spoken once about stone and once about grain, with lament (65's "you would keep on wondering") as the human residue of both. The chaff is the sermon's smallest proof and its largest: what can be reduced in a clause is not, in any final sense, yours.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:66

> “We have truly suffered a ˹great˺ loss.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lament Quoted, No Verb of Saying**

The sūrah slides into the farmers' own mouths without the courtesy of "they said": verse 65's "leaving you to lament" ends in the participle, and 66 begins, raw, the quoted cry — the present translation keeps the opening quotation-mark and leaves it, correctly, unclosed until the end of the two-line lament. The absence of qālū matters to the design: in the sūrah's other reported speeches the verb frames the distance ("They used to ask mockingly" — 56:47), while here the audience is spoken for, their hypothetical grief put into first-person plural as though the sūrah's own mouth were theirs. Rehearsed in recitation, the cry belongs, for that moment, to every hearer; the chaff is still a threat and not yet a fact, but the loss is already voiced — a late Makkān sermon's boldest move, letting the damned speak in their own future before anyone is damned, and the quotation's missing frame is the technique seen plainly in the text.

**A Loss Named in the Grammar of Certainty**

The cry carries, in Arabic, the full asseveration — inna plus the lam of emphasis — "truly, we are the utterly-bested," and the sūrah has just used the identical particles for its own decree: "Most certainly, earlier and later generations will surely be gathered" (56:49–50). The parallel is unmissable once named: the lament of the lost speaks, under threat, in the very grammar of certainty God uses over them, and their "truly" will be as confident then as the oath was now — the sūrah implies that on the Day even the ruined will believe, and believe with emphasis, their unbelief having been the temporary, not this confession. The bracketed ˹great˺ supplies an intensifier the Arabic's root carries contextually, and readers should hear the double emphasis, divine and human, echoing across seventeen verses: what God swears, the loss swears too, and the sūrah's audience is being asked which "truly" they will be the first to accept.

**Loss as the Sūrah's Recurring Ledger-Word**

Khasāra — the cry's noun — is the chapter family's commercial term, and al-Wāqiʿah, which has priced the whole Day in trade-language (shade "extended" like credit, "benefit" denied to the left — 44, "for what they used to do" as payment — 24), puts on the farmers' lips the one word a merchant cannot mishear: loss, the verdict on a tijāra, a transaction that failed. The Book reserves the same noun for its gravest bankruptcies — "they bought error for guidance, and their trade did not turn to profit" (2:16, plain paraphrase), "the losers are those who lose themselves and their families" (the sense of the Az-Zumar tally, 39:15) — and the lament at 66 is, in that idiom, the smallest case of the largest: a field is the classroom, and the loss they keeningly confess is rehearsal for the one the sūrah is really counting. The keening of the farmers, in a chapter recited in a market-town, is translated without remainder; Makkah understood a bad trade better than it understood resurrection, and the sūrah says so by letting it use the traders' own arithmetic for the Day.

**The Two-Voice Lament: 66 and 67 as One Speech**

Verses 66 and 67 form a single utterance split by a correction — "We have truly suffered a great loss. No, rather, we are deprived" — and the bal-clause (next verse) is the sūrah's microcosm of repentant accuracy: the first cry mourns the event ("loss"), the second names the agent and the share ("held back from what was due"), and the whole movement, in two breaths, is the sūrah's theory of what grief must learn before it learns anything else. Rhetoricians of the sūrahs preserve this shape for divine address elsewhere — the bal that redirects a wrong self-pity to true self-knowledge — and the lament's mini-drama, three words then three words, is the chapter's compressed sermon: the crop-tears begin as "what happened to us" and end, when honest, as "what we were kept from." The unit of 63–67 has therefore a complete plot — sowing, growing claimed, chaff threatened, keening, confession — a parable in five verses, with no moral attached because the moral is the structure: everything the farmer does, he does inside a will that can, in one clause (65), turn his year into his lament.

**The Verse at the Bedside, Once More**

This sūrah's reception has, since its first generations, gathered around dying rooms — its near-rank passage (83–89) recited at the throat where the soul climbs, its censuses read over the buried — and verse 66 belongs to that history in a way the commentator should record and the theologian should weigh: a cry for the living too, since everyone's harvest, at the end, is the body's. The keening over crumbled grain and the silence at a deathbed are, in al-Wāqiʿah's arrangement, the same sound at different distances, and the sūrah's choice to voice the loss before it threatens the resurrection says its final word to the mourner: the loss you are suffering is the loss you were shown, first, in a field — and the Book that names fields, not philosophies, in the mouth of the bereaved, has already told you where it wants your grief to end: not at "truly a loss," but at the sentence about the class that was never deprived at all, whose fruit is "never forbidden" (33), and whose shade — the lamenters, in the sūrah's physics, can already feel it — is extended.

And the sūrah's bracket ˹great˺, small as it looks, is the translator's one permission taken in the two-line cry — the Arabic's bare "loss," unmodified, is already the groan of the whole market-town, and the intensifier only marks where the listeners would supply it themselves.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:67

> In fact, we have been deprived ˹of our livelihood˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**"No, Rather": The Correction Inside the Cry**

Bal — "in fact, rather" — opens the second half of the lament, and in one small particle the sūrah stages the movement from sorrow to knowledge: the first cry called it "a great loss" (66), an event that happened to them; the correction names it ḥirmān, deprivation — being held back from a share. The change is the whole pastoral point: loss is the language of accident, deprivation is the language of allocation, and the farmers, mid-keening, are made to say the theological word — that what they lost was never simply theirs, that the year's yield was a portion (ḥaẓẓ) from which they have been excluded, not a possession they misplaced. The sūrahs glossed maḥrūm in exactly this register: cut off from the due share, as the disinherited are, and the lament's bal is thus the sūrah teaching its audience, in their own hypothetical mouths, to read every bereavement of the ledger as a notice from the Allocator.

**The Root That Unites 33 and 67**

Read across the chapter, verse 67 answers verse 33 at the level of letters. The people of the right were promised fruit "never out of season nor forbidden (lā maqṭūʿah wa-lā muḥarramah)" — fruit on which no taboo has ever been passed, the root ḥ-r-m negated as a bar — and the keening farmers end their sentence as "deprived (maḥrūmūn)," the same root passed against them as exclusion. Al-Wāqiʿah's theology of provision lives in that rhyme: ḥarām in this chapter means, both times, the power to withhold — the Garden is what no hand is forbidden from, and the Field-world is what any hand may be cut from — and the sūrah has placed the two uses to bracket its argument-half, so that the saved orchard (28–34) and the lost lament (63–67) are spelled from one consonant-root. The keening, heard in Arabic, is therefore unintelligible to the believer except as a definition by contrast: they mourn, and the mourned estate is the one whose fruit "no one is barred from," a sentence recited forty verses earlier in the same breath the sūrah now asks the lameters to complete: "we — we are the barred."

**The Deprived and the Pride of Sanctuary**

Makkāns lived under a name that made ḥurmah their claim to the world: their town was the sanctuary (ḥaram), its truce-months the heritage of Ibrāhīm's prayer ("a land without cultivation" endowed with safety, in the Abrahamic petitions the Book preserves — 14:35, 2:126, plain paraphrase), and their pride was that of people no one could touch at the House. The lament's word, maḥrūm, takes that pride apart at its root: the same ḥ-r-m whose sacred security they cited, whose fruit-taboos they kept for their altars (5:103's cultic refusals, at this chapter's verse 33), becomes in their cry the noun of being shut out — and the sūrah thus converts the city's master-concept into its warning: the sanctuary protects nobody from deprivation, the taboo feeds nobody, and "we are barred" is the confession Makkah's entire sanctity-culture was built to prevent its people from ever having to say. To recite 67 in that town was to speak its vocabulary back to it with the sign reversed.

**Silence After the Cry: How the Sūrah Closes the Sowing**

No divine reply follows 67 — and that silence is a compositional fact, not an omission. The semen-question ended with a closer ("will you not then believe," 57); the water-question will end with "will you not then give thanks?" (70); the fire runs to doxology (74). The sowing alone ends on the humans' own voice, mid-lament, unanswered, and the effect on the recitation is the sūrah's harshest device: the farmers' "in fact, we have been deprived" is left ringing, and the next thing the audience hears is not God addressing the keening but God moving, without transition, to "Have you considered the water you drink?" — as if to say: the lament over the chaff will get no answer in this life because its answer is the next verse, already beginning — the water that is as contingent as the grain, and that can also, in one will (70), be made salt. The silence of 67's close is the chapter's most articulate pause: the deprived are not consoled, they are continued, and the sermon's rhythm tells the listener that grief over fields receives, in this Book, not a theodicy but another question to consider.

**The Livelihood the Bracket Names**

The present translation's ˹of our livelihood˺ completes the cry's object, and the word it chooses — rizq, provision — is the sūrah's final lesson for the keening. Rizq, across the Book, is what a creature is given to live on, and the Qur'an's fixed answer to every anxiety about it is that the Giver and the grower are one: "there is no creature on earth but that upon God is its provision" (11:6, in plain paraphrase), a verse whose grammar of guarantee (ʿalā) matches, in reverse, the lament's "we are the deprived (maḥrūmūn)" — those cut off from what was owed, in their imagination. The bracket thus turns the cry into doctrine: livelihood was the thing they mourned, and the sūrah, by supplying the noun, says plainly that rizq is what the whole sowing-unit — the sowing, the growth claimed at 64, the chaff at 65, the keening at 66, the exclusion here — has been dissecting all along: provision is in the hand that gives it, and the only deprivation is the loss of that knowledge, a sentence Makkah's farmers and Makkah's merchants would each have heard in their own tongue, and which the chapter will, from 68 on, rinse once more in water, before it sets down fire, and praise.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:68

> Have you considered the water you drink?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Consideration, and the Only Passive One**

The argument-half's four questions are built on four things — what you eject, what you sow, what you drink, what you kindle — and the third breaks the pattern in a way the reciter feels before the analyst names it. Semen, seed, and fire are things the audience does: verbs of human making run all three ("what you ejaculate," "what you sow," "you kindle"). But the water verse says "the water you drink" — the only clause of the four in which the listeners' contribution is reception. The sūrah has moved from what you make to what you merely take in, and the escalation of the argument is in the grammar: nobody is required to pretend they did not sow, and it is almost impossible to pretend you did not need to drink. Verse 68 is thus the point at which the sūrah stops contesting credit and simply asks, out loud, about the one commodity that has never belonged to anyone in Makkah — and does it in the flat idiom of "have you considered," as if reminding, not teaching, which is this chapter's whole manner.

**Makkah Drinks from a Miracle: The Zamzam Subtext**
