Every hearer of "the water you drink" lived inside a city whose founding act was water from a gush in a dry valley — the spring of Zamzam, in the tradition's own account, the answer to a desperate mother's running between two hills, and the object of a piety (the sūqiya, water-distribution) that counted as a deed of worship comparable to freeing slaves. A sūrah read in that town does not need to describe rain to be understood; it is heard in the shadow of a well kept sacred precisely because nothing natural made it. And the sūrah's question, applied to that civic memory, cuts: you revere the spring God forced up for a foreigner and her child, you drink its brackish sweetness daily, you pay nothing for it beyond buckets — will you not consider the source of the source? The water-question is al-Wāqiʿah speaking Makkah's holiest hydrology back to it as argument, and the "consider" of 68 lands on listeners for whom water was, from the city's first day, the standing miracle nobody had earned.

**Water as the Sūrah's Own Reward-Medium**

Inside this chapter, water has already done duty at both ends of the eschatology: the near-rank's cups come "from a flowing spring" (18), and the right-hand people live inside "flowing water" (31) poured with no owner; the left, meanwhile, are immersed in scalding water (42) and given a drink of hamīm (54) and the thirsty camels' guzzle (55). Verse 68, "the water you drink," gathers all four scenes into the courtroom: the same element, the sūrah says, is the Garden's gift, the Hell's instrument, and your present thirst's only hope — and the question that opens the water-argument is therefore the question the whole chapter has been asking in its weather: who is the Giver of this, since what the Giver makes of it on the Day depends, exactly, on whether you said thanks or theft, "mine" or "Whose." Al-Wāqiʿah does not separate its hydrology from its theology because its audience never could: to drink at Makkah was to live inside the chapter's argument, un-reflected.

**The Classical Elements, Considered**

One structural observation belongs to this entry, and the sūrahs' readers will have made it: the four "considered" things — seminal fluid, vegetation, water, fire — scan, almost too neatly, as the ancient world's inventory of generative elements: life from the body, increase from the earth, the medium of all life, the heat that cooks and destroys. Without claiming Hellenism in the sūrah's sources — a judgment this commentary cannot verify and the audience's market did not require — the verse-sequence plainly selects, from the created order, the four operations every Makkān hand touched daily, and proceeds from the most intimate to the most common. Verse 68, the water at the center of that ladder, is the hinge of the set: the elements of the semen-verse belong to what a person does to his own life; water belongs to what life does to him — and the sūrah, by putting it third, moves its courtroom from acts to dependencies, just before fire, the element that is neither, arrives to make the point that God makes even the tools of His own threat, which is what 71–73 will say of the kindling and, in a sentence about "a reminder and a provision," of the blaze at every hearth.

**"Drink" as the Sūrah's Smallest Test**

The verse's verb — tashrabūn, you drink — is worth one last look, because the sūrah could have said "the rain you see," "the wells you dig," and chose the throat. Consideration, in al-Wāqiʿah, is measured by the swallow: every drink is an unowned event completed inside you by an agency not yours, and the argument-half asks whether the audience will convert that daily, ingested fact into belief (57), mindfulness (62), and thanks (70) — the three closers that frame the whole sequence like the three draughts of a cup. The mockers at 47 wanted resurrection argued on dust and bones; the sūrah answers at 68 by pointing at the water every body passes through, hourly, and at the clouds nobody of them has ever lifted a rope toward. "Have you considered" remains the sūrah's only imperative in this half — no threat yet, no promise, just the invitation to think about drinking — and the gentleness of the third question, in a chapter that opened with mountains in powder, is itself its argument: the God who can unmake stones asks you, softly, about a mouthful of water, and history has found no one who could answer honestly without losing their pride.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:69

> Is it you who bring it down from the clouds, or is it We Who do so?

**Expanded Commentary**

**"From the Clouds": Muduj, the Rain-Word the Sūrah Chooses**

The Arabic's noun for the source is muduj (plural of madfa, in the sūrahs' lexica: the cumulus, the heaped rain-clouds that "drive" forward), and the present translation's "clouds" keeps the concrete sky-thing the audience watched — not "the sky" (samāʾ), which the Qur'an elsewhere uses for rain's origin ("He sends down rain from the sky," 14:32, 2:22, plain paraphrase). The sūrah's word here, in the classical readings, marks a specific phenomenology: the white heap on the horizon that the whole town watches for, the cloud as the desert's rumour. That choice lets verse 69 stage its disjunction at the audience's own observation-post — you see the clouds, you do not drive them; your eyes track the heaping, your hands have no handle on the descent — and the rain's familiar mechanics become, in five words, a demonstration of un-owned power. The argument-half's most concrete line is also its most meteorological, and the sūrahs enjoyed noting that the very plural of the word pictures what the people called, in their proverb, the sky's "driving" of water to the valley-beds they alone could claim.

**"Is It You… or We": The Question's Third Repetition**

The disjunctive am has now rung at the fluid (59), the growth (64), and here at the rain (69), with the fire to follow its own expanded shape (72), and the repetition is the sūrah's logic performed as rhythm: each question offers the audience exactly two doors and no third, and each time the second door is God's first-person plural. By the third iteration the hearer is being trained — the sūrah does not argue that the rain falls from clouds nobody parts; it asks, as before, "you or We," and lets the accumulated form do the work no syllogism would attempt on a marketplace ear. The classical readers of the Qur'an's argument-style called this isti'dād, the preparation of consent by repetition: the audience answers "We" in their heart by the water-question, as they did by the seed, before either is ever argued, and the sūrah's next threat at 70 lands on a listener who has already, three times, conceded the sovereignty it needs.

**The City's Prayer for Rain**

That the sūrah's question was Makkah's standing practice can be seen from the other end of the Book: the prophetic prayer for rain, preserved in the two Ṣaḥīḥs' books of Friday prayer — the congregation turning their cloaks, the Prophet raising his hands, the words "Allāhumma aski—" — is, in act, the answer to verse 69 already performed: the community that could not bring down rain prays to the One who can, and the sūrah's question exposes the hypocrisy of a people who kneel for the clouds in drought and deny the Sender in doctrine. That the istisqā' liturgy survives in the sūrahs' collections, in the exact vocabulary of asking-God-for-what-only-God-does, is the strongest historical note 69 can carry: the audience's own worship conceded the verse's "or is it We," and the sūrah is not asking them to believe a new thing, only to stop living as if the old thing did not apply on the Day.

**"Do So": The Ellipsis That Keeps the Rain Unmechanized**

The present translation's "or is it We Who do so" renders an Arabic ellipsis — the line does not repeat the verb's object or its instrument; the sūrah, which could have said "We send it down, weighed, timed, guided to the wells it chose," simply answers "or We," and the bareness is deliberate. The sūrahs of the water-verses elsewhere carry the mechanics ("We send down water in measured measure," 23:18, plain paraphrase; "the fertilizing winds," 15:22, the same book's idiom), while al-Wāqiʿah declines the detail here because its argument needs only authorship: rain is the case in which every theory of the world concedes at least one god — the audience's own al-Anwāʾ, the star-lot oracles of rain, prove how seriously everyone took the descent's contingency — and the sūrah's question is which. To keep "We do so" unexplained is to deny the listener the comfort of a mechanism halfway to God: the rain's how is left to the other chapters, while the who, in this one, stands exposed in two words and no third option.

**The Verse the Threat Completes**

Finally, 69 cannot be read past its neighbor: the disjunction exists to arm 70's "if We willed, We could make it salty." If the clouds' freight were yours, salting it would be your misfortune; if it is His, the sweetness of every present drink is, this second, a decision — not a property of nature that God does not touch but a policy He is maintaining. The sūrah's entire hydrology, promise-scene (18, 31) to courtroom (68–70) to the left's boiling counter-water (42, 54), says that water has no blessing of its own and that the taste of it, right now, is the chapter's most immediate eschatology: the near-rank drink what will never be salted, the lost what quenches nothing, and the Makkān in the street between the two, at his well, with a sweet mouth that the sūrah has just told him is, every sip, the undecided question of verse 69, asked him personally, twice a day for the rest of his life. "Is it you who bring it down, or We" — the rain keeps answering, in the negative, until he does.

**A Note on the Sūrah's Hydrological Calm**

One final observation: of all the argument-half's units, the water's is the shortest — three verses to the seed's three, while fluid and fire run one apiece — and its cadence is unmatched: no threat verb in 68, no rebuke in 69, and the conditional in 70 arrives almost gently, about salt, not fire. The sūrah, which has threatened resurrection-denial with transformation (61) and harvest-denial with chaff (65), handles water with the plainest offer of all — consider who sends it — because its audience, at their wells, needed no shaming to concede the point; and the commentator should mark that the sūrah never wastes its terrors, distributing them where they are denied. That restraint is part of the verse's persuasive truth: the rain is argued, not denounced, and the hearer who answers 69 honestly has answered, without compulsion, the question the whole middle of the sūrah exists to ask — which is why its last word in the unit is thanks, the only response the clouds can elicit from an unbribed heart.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:70

> If We willed, We could make it salty. Will you not then give thanks?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ujāj: The Salty That Is the Sweet's Reverse**

The word ujāj — "salty," in the older glosses also bitter, scalding, the undrinkable — is Qur'anic only in this chapter and in the Furqan's twin-seas verse: "one fresh and palatable and the other salty and bitter, placing between them a barrier they cannot cross" (25:53) — the two-seas verse whose barrier-idiom the All-Merciful sūrah also invokes (55:19–20), and the pair of usages shows what verse 70's threat costs: al-Wāqiʿah does not say "We could make it poison" — no invented evil — it says We could make your drinking-water taste like the sea you already know, and the horror is precisely at home. The sūrah's eschatology works the same way everywhere (a shade that is smoke, cups without headache, a spring without a cistern): the punishment and the promise are each built from one familiar ingredient with one property changed, and 70 is the formula at its cleanest — take the sweet (furat), apply the will, and Makkah has no vocabulary for the loss that follows.

**"If We Willed," the Second Time: The Chapter's Contingency Engine**

Verse 65 threatened the harvest with crumbled chaff "if We willed"; 70 now threatens the water with salt, and the repetition of law nashāʾu is the argument-half's hinge: the two conditional-clauses flank the rain's disjunction and bind the chapter's middle with a single doctrine — nothing in the order of nature is necessary. The classical theologians argued from exactly such verses that the world's regularity is custom maintained, not compulsion inherited — the Muʿtazilī and Ashʿarī schools differ elsewhere, but this passage's grammar ("We could") is on neither side's opposite: no verse here says God does evil, only that He is able, and ability, in the sūrah's rhetoric, is the measure of the present gift. Between the two "if We willed" clauses sits 68–69's question about agency; the architecture says plainly: you have conceded who gives, so do not mistake the giving for owing — the salt of 70 is the standing proof that sweetness was never anyone's right.

**"Will You Not Then Give Thanks?": The Third Closer**

The argument-half ends its three appeals in order — believe (57), be mindful (62), give thanks (70) — and the order is the sūrah's whole spiritual ladder in reverse: assent to the first creation, recollection of one's own origin, and finally the gratitude that is what assent and recollection become when they work. The sūrahs of the Prophet's household preserved the summary-formula that faith "stands half in patience and half in thanks," and whatever its chain, the aphorism is al-Wāqiʿah's own arithmetic: the chapter never uses the word for patience here, and everything it does use — shakūr, the grateful servant whom the Sabaʾ chapter says is "few" (34:13, this sūrah's own verse 14 citation) — identifies thankfulness as the rare harvest the argument expects from its field of hearers. That the water-closer asks for thanks, while the fluid-closer asked for belief, is the sūrah's judgment on human sincerity: it is easier to concede God made the seed than to thank Him daily for a mouthful that could, in one will, have been salt — and the sūrah aims its final question at the harder, rarer act.

**The People of the Dam: Thanks, and What Fails It**

Gratitude, in this chapter's country, is not a mood but a survival technology, and the audience's own scripture had taught them what its failure costs: the people of the two garden-terraces were a grateful folk, said the Sabaʾ chapter, until ingratitude (kufr, here in its root-meaning of un-thanking) and the dam-sent flood turned their groves to bitter-fruited substitutes (34:15–17) — the exact reversal of verse 70, sweet to salt, watered to destroyed, and the sūrah reciting "will you not then give thanks" in the hearing of Makkah carries the Sabaeans' aftermath as its unspoken second clause. The bracketed idiom of the present line ("˹great˺ loss," earlier at 66, the same root-family of bereavement) already prepared the listener for a thankfulness defined by what it prevents. Al-Wāqiʿah's water-argument is thus, at its close, the region's oldest history lesson, told in taste: the gardens of the south failed when thanks failed; the town at the spring stands or falls on the same test, and the sūrah says so in five words after a threat of one.

**The Verse the Chapter Answers with Fire**

One last structural grace: from 71 the sūrah turns to "the fire you kindle," and the movement from undrinkable water to unownable flame is the argument's final pair — a world stripped of its two daily magics, the mouth and the hearth. Water that can salt is the mercy you do not deserve; fire that is "a reminder and a provision for the travelers" (73) is the tool whose usefulness is loaned, in the desert, by a God who has already told the denier, at 42, that His climate turns against those who turn from Him. Verse 70 stands at the joint: the closer of the water-triad and the last appeal before the doxology — and readers who finish 57–74 in one sitting will notice that the chapter asked for belief, memory, and gratitude, and received from no verse in its own world an answer except the one each listener gives, in the next swallow, in the next spark, every day the salt does not come — which is, the sūrah implies, exactly what "then give thanks" means: not a formula after the sermon, but the taste of sweet water, noticed.

That the closer is phrased as a question — fa-la-ʿalla tashkurūn, "will you not then give thanks?" — keeps even gratitude inside the sūrah's court of consent: the appeal is put to the hearer, unenforced, and the water keeps falling on both the thankful and the unthankful until the Day sorts the two, which is itself, in al-Wāqiʿah's idiom, the largest ˹unearned˺ mercy in the passage.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:71

> Have you considered the fire you kindle?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Consideration, and the Most Manual**

The fourth and final "have you considered" (a-raʾaytum) falls on fire, and the verb the sūrah gives the audience is theirs to hold in the hand: turidūn, "you kindle" — the root of striking light, of the kindled flame and the fire-drill, not of possessing or receiving. Of the four considered things — the emitted fluid, the sown seed, the drunk water, the kindled fire — this is the one the audience genuinely makes, and the sūrah says so first, before taking it back: your fire is a craft, not a property; every hearth in Makkah was built by knuckles and tinder, and verse 71 concedes the labor in order to query the element. The argument-half therefore closes where it began — with making — and the circle of 58 to 71, from what a body ejects to what a hand ignites, is the sūrah's full court of works, four benches, one question repeated until the furniture of the world is all accounted for and none of it is the defendants'.

**Fire as the Chapter's Double-Edged Element**

Al-Wāqiʿah has used fire before this verse and will use it after, and 71 is the joint: "the blazing-fire" (al-jahīm) is the left's lodging — 48 — their drink is boiling (hamīm, 42, 54), and their doom is "a blazing-fire" at 94; meanwhile the near-rank have no fire in their inventory at all, and the Event itself melts mountains but does not burn them — punishment in this chapter is thermal, but fire as fuel is never once called punishment. Verse 71 stands in the middle of that distribution and does the sūrah's real work with the element: it lifts fire out of the courtroom into the argument — the same substance that houses the lost is the one each hearer coaxes from wood every evening — and the "consider" asks whether the kindler can keep treating, as a tool, what his own book makes a verdict. The sūrah will draw the line itself at 73, but the line runs, first, through the hearth.

**"You Kindle" and the Light You Will Be Given**

There is one more intra-chapter echo, and it is luminous: at verse 12 the near-rank's reward is that "their light will proceed before them, and so will their right hands" — light going ahead on the Day — and a sūrah that ends its argument-half on kindling has lit that reward from the same verb-family of fire's service. The structure reads as a trade: you are the kindlers now, and on the Day, the kindled precedes you as your certificate — which is exactly what verse 73 then says the fire is, "a reminder," the hearth-flame as memorandum of the other fire, and the sūrah's audience, for whom a fire seen at night on the hamad was the commonest fact of travel, receives its eschatology in the most familiar optics available: distance, darkness, and a flame you carry. To "consider the fire you kindle" is, in that economy, to ask which of the two fires a person is really walking toward by each one they light.

**The Craft-Congress of the Sūrah's Middle**

The classical lexicography of tawqūd supplies the anthropological note: ārā yūrī is kindling in the sense of setting alight for use — the verb of signal-fires, of the shepherds' night-flame, of the traveler's circle — and it is the technical verb of a craft, which the sūrah's four-question ladder needs at its final rung: semen is nature, harvest is labor, water is gift, and fire is technique — the sūrah has climbed from the body through the field through the sky to the hand's art, and the last question is asked precisely where pride is densest, since the artisan admits no partner. On that admission the chapter stakes its turn to doxology: if the kindler, cornered at his own best craft, can say "consider whose tree it grew" (72), the argument is finished, and the sūrah's seventh-century sermon ends its court with the most modest claim the period could frame — you strike, but the wood is not yours — an ending that a desert full of fire-makers could test every night before bed.

**The Verse That Invites the Doxology**

Finally, 71 is the question, and 74 is the praise it makes possible — three verses between them, and the space is filled by the only answer the sūrah itself supplies to any of the four considerations (72's "is it you or We" plus 73's "We made it"), which alone distinguishes the fire-unit from its three siblings and prepares "So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest": after the harvest the sūrah demanded thanks, after the fluid belief, after the seed mindfulness — all appeals to the audience — and here, at the last question, the sūrah turns, without waiting for anyone's verdict, and tells God what He is, as if to say: even if no hearer answers 57–71, the kindling fire is already glorified by its Maker's assignment of it, and the sermon's closing doxology is the answer the argument would not receive, given first by the preachers' own throats — al-Wāqiʿah ending its courtroom, at 74, not with a sentence on the defendants but with praise of the Judge, which is the shape worship takes when evidence has been completed and hearts have not yet.

**Kindling, the Qur'an's Verb for Human Effort**

Lexically, ārā/yūrī is the verb of what a person does to make a fire, and the Book uses the same root when it describes, with quiet irony, humans "kindling" what they cannot create — the listeners' idiom at 71 will therefore echo a wider Qur'anic habit of granting the act's name to the actor and its substance to God, the way the Cave chapter lets a man say "I do not think the Hour will ever come" while the garden's ownership stays, line by line, with its real Owner (18:35–42, the parable this commentary has already cited at the chapter's gardens). "The fire you kindle" is thus, in the sūrah's own diction, a concession phrased as an indictment: the kindler's role is real and tiny, and verse 71 asks the audience to look exactly at the size. That is why the fire-question is last and why its answer comes fastest — the sūrah saves its verdict for the place where pride has the least to work with.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:72

> Is it you who produce its trees, or is it We Who do so?

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Its Trees": Fire's Forgotten Family**

The possessive is the verse's hidden hand: "its tree" — the fire's tree — and the sūrah, asked for the origin of flame, does not answer "lightning" or "flint" but "tree": the kindler's fire belongs, etymologically, to vegetation, and every fire in this audience's world was stored sunlight in tamarisk roots, dried dung over twigs, the desert's only fuel two steps from a plant. That arboreal answer closes a ring the chapter has been building since 27–28 — lote-tree and thorn-apple as Garden and Hell's own flora, seed and sprout as the argument's middle act (63–65) — and 72 now makes the fire, too, a botanical fact: the flame is a tree's hour, and the question "is it you who produce its tree?" quietly relocates the hearth to the field, where the sūrah has already proved nobody is master. The Arabic's singular (shajaratahā) keeps the riddle compact — the fire's tree is any tree, every tree, and none of them theirs.

**One Root Completed: n-sh-ʾ from Garden to Kindling**

The verb "produce" here (anshaʾtum) is the sprouting-and-bringing-forth root the sūrah has used of the Garden's re-making ("We produced you in a form you know not," 61, same root, per the sūrahs' concordance of the idiom) and of the crops' claimed growth, and its third appearance is its most audacious: growth of grain, re-production of bodies, production of the tree that feeds fire — one verb braiding the whole chapter's middle, so that a listener tracking the Arabic finds resurrection language inside the harvest-line and harvest-language inside the fire-speech. Al-Wāqiʿah thus argues morphology as well as mercy: the same divine inshaʾ lifts spouses (35), wheat (64), and fuel (72), and the sūrah's claim that all three are the same act of God is delivered by the sūrah's refusal to change verbs. To "consider" the fire with this root in the ear is to hold, in one image, the burning bush's cousin — fire, the element that in this Book speaks from a tree's burning and burns in a people's bosoms — and the audience's tree, in verse 72, is every tree they have ever watched rise from a seed they did not make.

**"Or We the Producers": The Chapter's One Self-Naming as Creator**

The answer-slot of the disjunction carries, uniquely in this passage, the noun of creation — khāliqūn, "We are the Producers" — the only time al-Wāqiʿah's argument-half lets the divine name for making stand as its own predicate, and its placement is the unit's keystone: at the seed the sūrah said "We cause it to grow" (64, verb), at the rain "We send it down" (69, verb), but at the fire's tree "We are the Producer" — the substantive, the identity. The one khalaqa-root of the middle also answers the one the mockers demanded ("Do we really get returned to being created anew?" — the challenge of 56:57 — "We created you; but will you not bear witness?" (57, verbatim below in this commentary's idiom)), and the doxology that follows at 74 ("glorify the Name") names what the noun has prepared: a chapter whose courtroom evidence climaxes, not in a verdict against the deniers, but in the assertion "We are the Producer," immediately transposed into praise. The sūrah's theology of creation, read at its structural peak, is doxology waiting to happen — which is the meaning of this verse's otherwise puzzling escalation of self-naming.

**36:80 — The Sūrah's Twin Verse on Green Fire**

Readers of the sūrahs know the pairing, and it belongs in this entry: "He who produces fire for you from the green tree, and from it you kindle" (the Yā-Sīn sūrah 36:80 — "Who gives you fire from green trees, and — behold! — you kindle fire from them" (56:71's own verb for kindling, re-echoed there) — its parallel line about fire from the wet wood — the ʿarfaj and marakh whose damp kindles when struck, per the sūrahs' botanical glosses). The two chapters say the same thing with the same root, and al-Wāqiʿah's "is it you who produce its tree" is the question whose answer Ya-Sin pronounces as miracle: water in wood, fire out of water, the element's nativity in the driest of lands. A Makkān hearer had no need of philosophy to test it — every tinder-box was the argument, and the green tree's secret combustion was known by name, season, and strike; the sūrah's question does not explain the phenomenon but files it, like the rest of the four considerations, under "We," and its partner-chapter's verse proves that this was a standing Qur'anic move, the hearth as a case in the Book's court of origins.

**The Verse Before the Assignment**

One last look forward: 72's tree is answered in 73 by a double function — "a reminder and a provision for the travelers" — and the sūrah's odd courtesy is worth marking: after asking who produced the fire, it does not threaten to take it back (as with water's salt) or to crumble it (as with the grain) but simply says what the fire is for: memory and use. That restraint is the argument-half's last tone, the movement from cross-examination to gift-theory, and the commentary reads it as the chapter's own pivot from proof to praise: having produced no third option, having offered no mechanism, al-Wāqiʿah reaches, at its middle's end, not the denier's humiliation but the wayfarer's flame — the one created thing that does both jobs in every desert night, warning of the fire to come and feeding the fire that is, and the sūrah that can close its case that way has, in fact, only one verdict left to speak, which is why it will next tell the Prophet to glorify, and why the fire, considered honestly, was the evidence for exactly that: the tree that burns as a reminder and cooks as a mercy is not, in al-Wāqiʿah's physics, a resource; it is a sermon the audience has been standing in, at every hearth, all their lives.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:73

> We have made it ˹as˺ a reminder ˹of the Hellfire˺ and a provision for the travellers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Jobs for One Flame**

The verse assigns the fire its double commission — dhikr, a reminder, and matāʿ, provision — and the pairing is the sūrah's last word in its argument-half: the same flame that will house the left (48, "the blazing-fire") is the flame the traveler cooks by, so that every hearth in the audience's world becomes, by divine assignment, a memorial with a pot on it. The order matters as much as the duality: reminder first, utility second — al-Wāqiʿah reverses, deliberately, the way people list their fires (warmth, then whatever warnings superstition adds) — and the "We have made" (jaʿalnāhā) claims the assigning, not the fuel: nobody lit the first fire, the sūrah says, everyone inherited a purpose. A reminder and a provision: to hold the chapter's theology in five words, every use of the world is already a sermon about the world's end, and the fire, considered by the kindler, is the proof that the sūrah's list of functioned things — nights, mates, spouses-turned-virgins (36, same verb) — runs all the way down to the coals at every tent.

**Matāʿ: The Sūrah's Own Economic Word for Mercy**

Provision (matāʿ) is not filler; it is the chapter's trade-vocabulary at its most theological, and its near-twin sits in the Abasa sūrah's inventory of care — "a means of sustenance for you and your animals" (80:32), where the same noun names the harvest's purpose across species. Al-Wāqiʿah's matāʿ shares that range: the verb-world of Makkah's market — goods, brief enjoyment, the wayfarer's kit — is conscripted into the sermon to say that what can be sold is, first, what has been given, and that the sūrah's "provision" is a mercy-word wearing a merchant's coat. The commentary has traced the chapter's commercial register through the shade "extended" like credit and the payment-language of 24; verse 73 finishes the job by pricing the unpriceable: the fire's usefulness is not a resource discovered but a commodity issued, and "travellers" are, in that ledger, not owners — the sūrah's audience of traders hear themselves, for once, described the way every trader really is, carrying what was handed out.

**Who Are "the Travellers"?**

The Arabic's noun for them (al-muqṭayn in the sūrahs' common reading) belongs, by the old glosses, either to the picking-up of sticks — the verb of plucking fuel from the wādī — or, by other readers' root, to cutting a route through the land; the sūrahs' lexical committees preserved the range without closing it, and both senses converge on the same figure: the one for whom fire is not infrastructure but gathering. On that uncertainty the verse gains: the traveler is everyone between two homes, which is precisely the sūrah's anthropology — the near-rank are "travellers" in the same breath in which they are heirs (29, 34, "entered the Garden as a reward"), and the whole passage from 75 to 96 will re-confirm it. The classical tellers of the sūrahs' occasions also note the desert's literal rule: fire belongs to no one who stops, and the people of the ḥijāz knew a flame, the way they knew a spring, as something that serves precisely because it is nobody's — verse 73's "provision for the travellers" is, in that idiom, the plainest sentence in the chapter and the most corrosive of ownership, the reminder and the cooking-pot sharing one Arab noun for what the road lends you.

**Against Fire-Reverence, Without Naming It**

The verse's first commission — reminder — has also, in the sūrahs, a controversial edge the commentator should mark lightly: Arabia knew fire's cult, and the peninsula's margins knew Māgī reverence for the undying flame; the sūrah neither names nor attacks anyone here, but its theology is a direct answer to any consecration of the element — fire is not a lord to be served, it is a memorial assigned by the Lord, and its divinity is precisely what verse 73 removes by giving it a job. To make it "a reminder of the Fire" (the present translation's ˹of the Hellfire˺ completes the genitive exactly as the sūrahs' glosses do) is to convert the flame into eschatology's clock: look, remember, and the remembering is the worship that replaces the cult. The move is quiet and total — the hearth stays lit, the altar is empty — and the sūrah that will end, in its last verse, by ordering the Prophet's own glorification (96) of "the Name of your Lord, the Greatest" can afford, at 73, not to argue about fire-gods at all: a flame with a function has, by definition, a Maker, and that is the whole anti-cult in three words, which is why the verse reads, in its place at the argument's end, like a sermon against Makkah's least-argued idolatry without once lifting a voice.

**The Only Answer the Sūrah Gives Itself**

Structuralists of this sūrah will have counted: of the four considerations — semen, seed, water, fire — only the fire-unit contains, after its question, a divine assertion about what the thing is for; the fluid got "will you not believe" (57), the harvest its keening (66–67), the water a threat and an appeal (70), but the fire gets "We have made it a reminder and a provision," a positive assignment with no closer-question attached, as though the sūrah, having run out of courtroom, simply teaches. That asymmetry is the argument-half's finale: the last considered object is the one the chapter can most easily describe whole — it burns, it warns, it feeds, it is owned by nobody — and the doxology at 74 rises naturally from it, since a thing with an assigned double purpose needs an Assigner in the indicative, not in a question. Al-Wāqiʿah's middle thus ends not with a demand on the audience but with a statement about the world's furniture, and the sermon's pivot to the great oath of 75–77 (the stars' settings and the "noble Qurʾān") has been prepared in the only way this chapter knows: one flame, two jobs, and the silence of everyone who has ever sat beside it agreeing, without having wanted to, that the Maker is not kindled but kindles.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:74

> So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Courtroom Breaks Into Praise**

The argument-half, which began with "We created you — will you not then believe?" (57), ends without a verdict: the sūrah's fourth consideration has been answered, its fire given its double commission (73), and what follows is not a sentence on the deniers but an address to the Messenger — "So glorify." The fāʾ, "so," is the whole verse's logic: praise is the proper close of evidence, the courtroom's last act when the hearts have not yet delivered. The sūrah of the Event, which opened by collapsing the cosmos and has spent seventeen verses subpoenaing semen, harvest, rain, and flame, resolves its middle not in a threat but in doxology, and the rhetorical effect on a Makkān audience must have been startling: a polemic that ends by ordering its preacher to glorify — the argument, having proven every act of nature assigned, moves to the one verb that fits an assigner, tasbīḥ, the declaration of transcendence that this Book gives as the natural music of the world (the entire Musabbiḥāt cycle begins with creation praising before it ever argues, in the sense of al-Ḥadīd 57:1, this commentary's own chapter).

**"The Name," Not the Named**

The verse orders glorification of "the Name of your Lord," and the sūrahs' grammarians debated the idiom — some read "the Name" as lafẓ, an expression for the Lord Himself, since His essence cannot be addressed as an object; others took it literally: the names are what human tongues can say, and sanctifying them (removing every associate's name from them, every idol's claim) is what glorification means in practice. The verse profits from the ambiguity: praise directed at the Name is praise a worshipper can actually perform, and "the Greatest" (al-ʿAẓīm) — the superlative form of the attribute, once more in this chapter at the oath's "great" for a Qurʾān-oath (76) — seals it with the sūrah's own scale: the Event, the tremendous tidings, the loss called great, and now, at the argument's summit, the Greatest, everything in al-Wāqiʿah measured against that one comparative. This commentary notes, without adjudication, that the classical readers also saw in "your Lord" (rabbika) the completed arc of the middle's claim: the Lord who creates, sends down, makes salty or sweet, and assigns fire's jobs is, in the end, addressed in the second person — "your" — the Prophet's own, and through him every reciter's, which is the sūrah's final, quiet universalism in a sentence with four words and two of them pronouns.

**Tasbīḥ as the Sūrah's Structural Sign-Post**

The reader who reaches 74 should know that this line is a hinge reused at the chapter's last breath: verse 96 repeats the doxology verbatim — "So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest" — and the sūrah thus brackets its closing third (75–96) between two identical calls to praise, with the great oath, the Guarded Book, the three classes, and the dying-soul scenes all contained inside a frame of tasbīḥ. What the frame does is genre-making: al-Wāqiʿah is not, in its architecture, a chapter that argues and then stops; it argues (57–73), praises (74), testifies (75–77), proves the Qurʾān's dignity and the deniers' commerce with it (78–82), replays a death-scene (83–87), assigns the three classes their final words (88–95), and returns to praise (96) — and the two doxologies are the chapter's way of saying that its argument serves its worship, not the reverse. The commentator can add that reciters' lore ties this closing pair to the sūrah's use at the bedside and in the night-prayer — reports exist about the Prophet glorifying at 74 and about prostration connected to the sūrah's end; their chains are contested, and this commentary marks them [UNVERIFIED: specific rulings on the Wāqiʿah prostration rest on transmitted reports the schools themselves weigh differently].

**"Glorify": The Verb the Whole Sūrah Has Been Performing**

One more look at the imperative: sabbih comes from the root of remoteness from defect — to declare God far from what is unworthy — and the argument-half has, verse by verse, done nothing else: removed from semen the father's credit (59), from the crop the sower's claim (64), from the rain the sky's indifference (69), from the fire the kindler's ownership (72), each removal a working tasbīḥ of a specific attribute. Verse 74 asks the Prophet to say aloud what verses 57–73 have done silently: to transcend every partnership the audience wanted to smuggle into the ordinary — "the chapter's whole middle is a long negation of small gods," in the sense in which this commentary has read it from verse 57 onward. The praise-formula, in that light, is not a pious capstone but the verdict itself, the only sentence in the argument-half spoken as worship rather than question, which is why it can be repeated at 96 without embarrassment: a conclusion that is praise needs no second argument, only a second choir — and the sūrah's choir, from this verse on, is the recitation community, whose nightly Wāqiʿah, in the beloved if contested virtue-tradition at this chapter's verses 23 and 26, keeps answering the command with the very recitation the chapter will, at 77–80, call noble, guarded, and untouchable.

**The Same Sentence in the Sūrah of the Most High**

The verse's twin stands one short sūrah away: "Glorify the Name of your Lord, the Most High" (87:1) — the opening of the Aʿlā cycle — and the two lines share almost their whole diction, differing only in the superlative: "the Greatest" here, "the Most High" there, and in position: al-Aʿlā begins where al-Wāqiʿah pauses mid-course. The pairing is the recitation-tradition's own way of teaching this verse — a doxology that opens one sūrah closes twice another — and it lets the commentator name the genre: imperatives of tasbīḥ framed, in this Book, around arguments rather than inside them, as though praise were appointed to stand at the joints where proof ends. Makkans who knew both sūrahs by heart, as the nightly reciters of this one evidently did, would have felt 74 as the same hinge they met at the start of the Most High: the Name, the Lord, the superlative — and the difference between "High" (of His station) and "Greatest" (of His claim) lands, in al-Wāqiʿah's context, on the courtroom: what can be subpoenaed about the world's Maker, after four considerations of semen, seed, water, and fire, is only this — that the Name is set above every share in it. The doxology is the verdict the argument could reach, and no more; the sūrah, in both of its twin verses, declines to reach further, and a chapter that began in earthquake and ends in praise keeps, at its middle, the same shape it keeps at its edges.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:75

> So I do swear by the positions of the stars—

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Only Oath, at Its Turning Point**

Al-Wāqiʿah has not sworn since its first word — its opening was a conditional of the Event ("When the Event befalls," 1), and its thirds ran on argument, description, and scene; the single explicit oath of the chapter is here, at the hinge where the argument-half gives way to the Book-half, and the sūrah's design rewards the observation: after four considerations and a doxology, what remains to be proven is the Qurʾān itself, and proof of revelation demands, in this Book's custom, the oath-form (75) followed by its content (77), the sworn thing being the Book's nobility. The placement says that the sūrah treats its own scripture as its hardest case: semen, grain, rain, and fire were conceded by the field and the hearth, but "this is truly a noble Quran" (77) can be established, the sūrah implies, only on oath — and so the chapter's one solemn asseveration, with its added weight of "a great oath" (76), is spent, at its exact middle, on the book being recited in the room.

**"Positions": Mawāqiʿ — Settings, or Descents**

The noun of the oath is the sūrah's most double word: mawāqiʿ, "positions," which the sūrahs' glosses take two ways — the places where the stars set (each night's disappearance, the horizon's geometry), or the places where revelation descends ("the descent-points of the stars" read, by some early commentators, as the spots on earth where God's word came down, and the sūrahs' lexical note is explicit that both senses were argued in the schools). Either reading dignifies the oath and neither can be discarded: the settings swear by the sky's regularity, which 65–70 just proved contingent, and the descents swear by the revelation's geography, which the sūrah is about to defend as touched-by-none (79) and revealed-from-the-Lord-of-the-worlds (80). The ambiguity is al-Wāqiʿah's own — the same word "position" belongs to astronomy and to angelology — and the hearer who catches either sense has already been taught that the stars' business and the Book's business are held in one hand: the God who sets them is the God who sends the recitation, and an oath by either's "positions" binds both.

**The Oath's Particle: "Fa-Lā Uqsimu"**

Readers of the sūrahs know the dispute embedded in the line's first syllables: the particle lā before "I swear" has been taken, by the majority of the exegetes, as an oath-intensifier of classical Arabic (the idiom's "lā" of emphasis, leaving the sense "So, indeed, I swear"), and by others as a genuine negation — "I do NOT swear" — directed at the audience's own swearing by the stars, their anwāʾ, the rain-lot of the pre-Islamic calendar that the Qur'an elsewhere confronts (53:19–20's idols and their clientele, at this chapter's verse 59, and the Sijjīl-of-stars theology the Star sūrah answers from its opening: "By the stars when they fade away," 53:1). The recitation preserves both senses in its one spelling, and this commentary records the range without choosing: if lā is emphatic, the stars' settings are exalted as witnesses; if it is negative, they are dethroned as oracles — and either way the sūrah is at work on the same audience practice, the sky consulted for livelihood, now sworn at for its Maker. Makkah, which cast star-lots for rain, heard in this line either its gods demoted or its heaven subpoenaed, and the verse's genius is that the argument needs no more than one of the two.

**Stars in This Chapter: The Only Sky of the Argument-Half**

Within al-Wāqiʿah itself, verse 75 is the stars' only appearance in the argument — the chapter's skies have been, until here, eschatological (mountains dusted, the sky split, 5–6 — the sūrah's near-rank dwell under "the expanded shade" and no constellation, and its left under "a column of smoke," 43 — a sky of weather, not of fate) — and the sudden introduction of the stars at the oath is therefore conspicuous: the sūrah admits the stars, briefly and only as witnesses, into a chapter that otherwise refuses them every office, destiny, intercession, or calendar. The structural reading is exact: al-Wāqiʿah has just argued the elements (seed, crop, water, fire) away from the audience's control, and verse 75 takes the last domain of pre-Islamic control — the sky's timetable — and turns it into an oath-slot: the stars do not rule; they testify, and the sūrah's audience, who read fortunes in settings, hear their own science sworn with. From 75 on, the chapter's only vertical reference is to "the Lord of the worlds" (80), and the heavens are reduced, as they are so often in this Book, to furniture with a liturgical function: positions that glorify (74's tasbīḥ includes theirs, in the sense of the Musabbiḥāt's whole grammar, 57:1) — and nothing else.

**"If Only You Knew": The Parenthesis the Verse Has Not Yet Earned**

The oath's weight is not stated here; it is promised in the next line's aside (76, "a great oath — and this, if only you knew"), and the sūrah's order is worth noting: the oath comes first, its grandeur is asserted second, and its reason (the Book) third, a sequence that lets the hearer feel the swelling rather than hear it explained. But 75's half-clause also inherits a small irony from its own parenthesis-to-come: the sūrah will tell this audience they do not know what they are swearing past, and the verse's plain opening — "So I do swear by the positions of the stars" — is thus the most gentle line in the chapter's second half, before the great oath's "if only you knew" grades the audience back to ignorance. Recited slowly, 75 is the sūrah drawing breath: the courtroom has closed (74's praise), the witnesses are being sworn in (the sky's own geometry), and the object on trial has not yet been produced — "this is truly a noble Quran" (77) is the exhibit, and the oath's stars are merely the clerk's candle. Everything the sūrah now says about its own book stands under this asseveration, and the chapter that opened "there is no liar of its falsity" (2) — "none discredits its falsity," the sūrahs' gloss of that opening line — closes its argument with an oath so that its central claim, the Book, may be received with the same certainty that the Event was feared by.


## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:76

> and this, if only you knew, is indeed a great oath—

**Expanded Commentary**

**"If Only You Knew": The Chapter's One Concession of Scale**

The parenthesis "law taʿlamūn — if only you knew" is, in the present translation's wording, unique in this sūrah, and its placement is its meaning: the sūrah measures its own oath and finds the audience's knowledge too short for the instrument. The idiom "if only you knew" in this Book opens hoods over facts whose full weight the hearer cannot see — it is the Book's idiom for facts whose full weight a hearer reaches only afterward, the verse-by-verse inventory of the Day given to those who will believe it too late for the knowledge to help — and here it tells the room, before any doctrine, that swearing itself has mass: the sūrah's audience, expert in oaths, in the solemnities of the sanctuary and of the trade-contract, is told that their expertise is the one thing they do not bring to this line. Al-Wāqiʿah's court of evidence thus closes with a confession calibrated to the witnesses' pride — you know oaths; you do not know this one.

**"A Great Oath": What the Chapter Has Already Called Great**

The adjective ʿaẓīm has, in this sūrah's own ledger, a short and exacting history, and the present translation gives it out three times around this verse: the ˹great˺ loss of the lament (66), the Greatest Name of the doxologies (74 and 96), and the great oath here — and the triad is a commentary in itself. Loss is great when measured against a field; the Name is greatest when measured against every claim; the oath is great when measured against its content, the nobility of the Book (77) that it swears. The sūrah therefore, by reusing the word, keeps re-setting the scale: at 66 the audience learned the heaviest thing that can happen to a harvest, at 74 the heaviest thing that can be said about God, and at 76 they are handed the hinge — an oath great because what it confirms is the address of the Greatest to the world, and the sūrahs' readers of this chapter preserved precisely this: the greatness of an oath is the greatness of the sworn matter, and this sūrah's sworn matter is itself.

**Oaths in This Book: The Grammar of Sworn Worlds**

The Qur'an swears, as its audiences did — by the night when it envelops, the forenoon, the city and its security, the wind in its measure (the short-sūrah register, in paraphrase of the opening oaths of Sūrah al-Layl, al-Ḍuḥā, al-Balad, and the Adhkariyyāt's "the scattering winds," 51:1) — and the objects of its oaths are the same sky and earth the audience read for luck, profit, and weather, retaken for witness. Verse 76 stands inside that habit and names it once: "this is indeed a great oath," the only place in the chapter that comments on the instrument of swearing itself, and the commentary can add the classical observation that the sūrah's oaths are addressed to a people whose oaths were sacred commerce — a tribe that broke a covenant swore, and a town that trusted the stars swore, and a Book that swears by their star-positions inverts both practices at once: God's asseveration is sworn not by the stars' power but by their positions, which is 75's anti-mantic move, here given its liturgical weight: the oath is great because the sworn thing — a noble Book in a guarded Record — is, for the first time in this audience's experience, a fact that cannot be lost, broken, or outvoted.

**The Parenthesis as Pastoral: Knowledge Deferred, Not Denied**

One pastoral note the verse's aside carries for this chapter's bedside history: "if only you knew" is not contempt — the sūrah does not say you are ignorant, it says the knowledge would change the oath's size in your mouth — and the sentence models, in its four words, al-Wāqiʿah's whole stance toward its hearers' finitude: it has told them the Event comes (1), that no soul will deny its truthfulness (2), and that they will be sorted by classes they can already imagine (7–10); at 76 it adds the one thing an audience of the dying and the grieving needs: that some realities are larger than their present knowing, and recited anyway. The sūrahs of the chapter's etiquette — its recitation at the sick, its place in the night prayers, the contested virtue-traditions recorded at this commentary's verses 23 and 26 — live or die on this verse's concession: the community kept reciting, as the verse prescribes, oaths whose greatness it was asked to trust before it could weigh, and the parenthesis has, for fourteen centuries, functioned less as rebuke than as a permission: you may not know how great this is; say it with what you have.

**What the Oath Is For: The Next Line**

Structurally 76 is a bridge built entirely of load-bearing: the oath's clause ("by the positions") ends with a dash in the present translation — an honest typographical signal that the verse suspends — and the content arrives in 77, "that this is truly a noble Quran," with the asseveration inna carried across from the oath's own emphatic lām. The sūrah thus completes, in two lines, the classical oath-answer form (lā + uqsimu + bi-... + inna...), and the answer's subject is the only candidate the chapter's argument-half left: not resurrection proven, not provision claimed, but the book-in-hand, the sūrah's own recitation, made the sworn matter. Reciters who reach 76 mid-nightprayer should mark that the chapter here names itself for the first time since its opening "the Event" — the sūrah has been about everything except its own status, and now, in the hinge-verse, it swears to it, and the remaining twenty-one verses will be spent, as the sūrah's structure shows, on the Book's purity (79), its origin (80), the audience's commerce with it (81–82), and the death-scene the Book reads at the throat (83–87): the great oath is the chapter's second beginning, and its small parenthesis — if only you knew — is the only warning the sūrah ever gives about the size of what it is asking anyone to hold.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:77

> that this is truly a noble Quran,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Majīd: The Word the Sūrah Applies to Its Own Book**

Noble translates majīd — a root of glory that in classical usage belongs to what is magnificient in itself, not to what is generous toward others (karīm), and the Qur'an uses it of the Book only here and twice beside: "Qāf. By the glorious Quran!" (50:1) and "In fact, this is a glorious Quran" (85:21) — two other late-Makkān sūrahs, two other courtrooms, the same adjective sworn (50) or asserted against the deniers' "glorious" (85, the Broken-Pattern chapter, whose "glorious Quran" follows its oath by "the guarded Tablet" in a sequence this verse and the next will replicate). Al-Wāqiʿah's choice of majīd at the hinge is thus not a compliment but a claim of self-sufficiency: the Book's nobility is intrinsic, unloaned from its audience's reception — and the sūrah, whose entire middle has argued that nothing in the world is owed, applies the same theology to its own text: it is noble, and its honor is the Maker's, touched by none except the purified (79).

**"This": The Recitation as the Sworn Object**

The demonstrative "this" (innahu) points at what is being said while it is said — the sūrah swears about itself, in the first person of its own voice — and the self-reference completes the chapter's long staging: the Qur'an's early sūrahs rarely name themselves; al-Wāqiʿah, at its middle, does, and the audience who has been walked through seed, crop, rain, and fire now finds the Book itself on the stand as the exhibit sworn to be noble. The rhetorical gain is exact: the sūrah's proof-method has been the familiar made strange, and the Book that performs that strangening claims, at 77, the only title its own performance supports — majīd, glorious in the sense that what it does to ordinary water and fire is done by nothing else in the audience's library of speech. Commentators past and present have noted the self-certifying structure of such verses (the Qur'an swearing by itself), and the present translation's dash at the end of 76, leading into "that this is…," preserves the grammar: the oath's whole force was aimed at this one demonstrative, and the sūrah's fourteen-hundred-year readers have been asked, in return for the stars' positions and their greatness, to trust the finger.

**Quran: The Chapter That Means "Recitation"**

The sūrah's self-naming uses the word qurʾān — recitation, the noun of the act, not of the codex — and the choice matters in a chapter whose argument has been, from its first line, about hearing: "when the Event befalls" was announced, not displayed, and the near-rank's reward and the left's doom were each delivered as reportage. Verse 77's "a noble recitation" therefore says that what is sworn to be glorious is the recited speech-in-action — the very sound the congregation is producing — and the sūrahs' lexical essay on the term (qurʾān as the Book's self-designation, read by the ancients alongside its verb qaraʾa, to gather and to recite) fits the hinge's needs: the thing affirmed glorious is not a manuscript (80's "Record" supplies that register) but a living utterance whose dignity is performed in each recitation, and the sūrah's audience — illiterate, commercial, devoted to the spoken — heard at 77 a verdict about the sound of God in their own room, in the present tense, which is what "this is" says, in the Arabic's nominal clause, with no past or future attached.

**The Nobility the Argument-Half Prepared**

It is worth measuring how 77's claim was earned: the sūrah has spent seventeen verses (57–73) removing the audience's ownership of the world's processes, and a book that can do that, in a room, by being said, carries its majīd as an effect of its operation — the nobility asserted at 77 is the sūrah's own CV. The structure answers the mockers' challenge — "will we really be resurrected?" (56:47, in plain echo of the verse's own translation, already argued at this chapter's verses 41–44): they asked for resurrection on their terms, and the sūrah's reply at 77 is procedural — the book that unmans your weather and your wealth is the evidence you actually possess, and it is noble. Classical readers of this chapter liked to add the intertext: when the Qur'an elsewhere calls the same Book "a firm rope" and "the decisive speech, not a jest" (the sense of 86:13's four-word contrast, in plain paraphrase), it is al-Wāqiʿah's own register — speech that separates classes, that the near-rank will hear as news from their Lord, and that the left will, too late, have found undecidable. Nobility here is not aesthetic; it is jurisdictional, and the sūrah's argument-half has just handed the court to the Book.

**The Line That the Purity-Verses Frame**

Finally, 77 sits, precisely, between 75–76's oath and 78–79's two qualifiers — "in a guarded Record, which none touch except the purified" — and the triad must be read as one sentence-unit if its grammar is to be kept: the noble recitation (77) has a location above (78) and a contact-prohibition (79), and the sūrah has, in three verses, given its Book an origin, an archive, and a liturgy. The commentators' centuries-long argument over 79's reach (who are the purified — angels, or the ritually clean; what is "touch" — holding, or receiving) runs, technically, off this verse's noun: if what is sworn is a recitation, its "touching" may be its acceptance, and if the noble thing is in a guarded Record, its untouchedness may belong to the Tablet's keeping rather than to the sūrahs' books of ablution — a dispute the sūrah's next verse will make unavoidable and which this commentary will handle at 79, where it belongs. Verse 77, the hinge proper, asks only the first thing: to hear the recitation as it hears the Event, with fear and honor both — and the sūrah, which began by telling Makkah that its sky would split and its fields crumble, now tells it that its speech is the same kind of fact.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:78

> in a well-preserved Record,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Record with Its Exact Book-Names**

The sūrah's phrase for the Book's location — a well-preserved Record (kitāb makhnūn) — belongs to a small family of Qur'anic self-names for revelation's archive: "in the Master Record with Us — highly esteemed, rich in wisdom" (43:4, the Orchid sūrah's idiom of "Mother of the Book" here supplied with its "with Us"), and, in the Broken-Pattern chapter that swears like this one, "in a Preserved Tablet, recorded" (85:21–22, whose "glorious Quran" line this commentary cited at 77). The family-words — Umm al-Kitāb, Lawḥ Maḥfūẓ, Kitāb Makhnūn — carry one claim with different vault-doors: the recited book has, above recitation, a kept original, and verse 78, in al-Wāqiʿah's courtroom, enters that original as evidence: the oath (75) was sworn "that this is a noble Quran" (77), "in a well-preserved Record" (78), a prepositional clause that says the nobility is not the copy's polish but the archetype's keeping, and the sūrah, by adding the location, answers the charge its audience was living to make — that the Qur'an is Muḥammad's trade (next verses: "how can you take this message lightly, and make denial your livelihood?" — 81–82, in plain echo of their translation) — with provenance: a book with a Record is not a book with a vendor.

**Makhnūn: The Root of the Withheld**

The adjective (makhnūn) comes, in the sūrahs' lexica, from the root of hiding and guarding — "veiled, kept" — and a minority of the old lexicographers connected the word instead to the reserved stores of priests, the fire or rites set apart from the crowd; the classical dictionaries record both lines without closing the matter, and this commentary marks the priestly etymology [UNVERIFIED: the makhnūn-to-priesthood connection is a transmitted lexical opinion, not a settled one] while treating the first as plain. The theological value of either is the same: the Record is not a public register anyone may consult; it is kept, and its keeping is the guarantee that its copy on earth is unaltered — which is precisely why verse 79, the next line, makes the untouchedness run the other way too: what is kept from all is, by the same verb, touched by none except the purified. The sūrah, in one adjective, installs both seals — the archive's door locked above, and the recitation's gate guarded below — and the courtroom's exhibit acquires, in the middle of a recited sermon, the property no other book in the audience's market could claim on the spot: it can be opened, and its opening is by permission.

**The Sūrah's Own Geography of Heaven**

Al-Wāqiʿah has, since verse 1, kept its sky free of machinery: no thrones described, no heavens enumerated, no angels stationed at gates — its near-rank recline "face to face" (16), their only geography is gardens, springs, and shade, and the one upward motion the chapter allows is the making itself — "We produced you in a form you know not" (61, plain echo of its own line). Verse 78's Record therefore introduces, at the hinge, the chapter's only heaven-furniture: a kept book, "with Us" — in the phrase of the Orchid sūrah (43:4) — and the restraint about the where is itself the sūrah's argument against the mantic sky: the heaven of al-Wāqiʿah is storage, not fortune, and its one archival sentence stands where the audience expected constellations with power. The Lote-tree of the Utmost Boundary, which the Star sūrah sets near revelation's descent (53:13–16, in the other chapter's plain account), does duty in the neighbor-chapter where al-Wāqiʿah keeps silence — and the effect on a Makkān ear is measurable: the sky that cast rain-lots (this commentary at 59 and 69) is here demoted to a shelving-system belonging to the Lord who sends, and nothing in it dispenses anything at all.

**The Only Record in a Chapter Without Books**

One structural note this verse earns by its rarity: the word for the heavenly register (kitāb, "Record") appears, in this chapter, only here — and readers of the sūrah's Day will feel what that means, because al-Wāqiʿah's eschatology, unlike its neighbors', stages no distribution of books at all: the Ḥāqqah's and Inshirāḥ's right-hand-and-left-hand scenes of scrolls are absent, and this chapter sorts its classes by fire, shade, water, and fruit alone. The sūrah thus keeps, for itself, the only record its economy contains — and its reward-language is accordingly the plainest in the Book: "a reward for what they used to do" (24), payment by the standard of works, with no ledger-scene attached. Verse 78 puts, in the middle of that ledger-less courtroom, the one filing-cabinet the chapter acknowledges — the well-preserved Record — and the contrast is its quietest claim: every other sūrah of the Event shows you your book handed out; al-Wāqiʿah shows only its own Book handed down, filed before it was written on you. Readers of both testaments of this chapter's Day will notice that the sūrah has, in one prepositional clause, made revelation the only document in the room.

**The Verse That Makes the Next One Unavoidable**

Finally, 78 is the line that the entire purity-dispute leans on, and the commentator should say plainly that the sūrah's next words (79, "touched by none except the purified") have, for fourteen centuries, been argued in the sūrahs' books of prayer and of the mushaf's etiquette — whether the clause describes angels at the heavenly Record, as the present translation's ˹angels˺ decides, or governs the reciter's state, as the legal readers took — and that verse 78's grammar favors, at the literal level, the first: what is in the well-preserved Record is what none touch except the purified. The fiqh did not rise from this line alone (the prophetic letter "none touches the Quran except the purified," preserved in the sūrahs' collections with contested chains, bears its weight), and this commentary will weigh the received readings at 79, where they belong, rather than retro-reading law into a hinge-verse that is, in its own movement, the sūrah's last word on its title before it turns to the audience's commerce — but the jurists were right to hear 78 as 79's premise: the untouchedness of the Record is what makes the untouchable, here and there, a single doctrine of keepership.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:79

> touched by none except the purified ˹angels˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse the Law Could Not Get Around**

This is the line the sūrahs' books of purification have argued longer than almost any other verse in the chapter: "lā yamassuhū illā al-muṭahharūn" — none touch it except the purified — and from it, joined to the prophetic letter "none touches the Qurʾān except the purified" (preserved in the sūrahs' collections with a chain the ḥadīth-masters themselves weighed, and this commentary marks [UNVERIFIED: the letter's grade is disputed among the collectors]), the four schools built the ruling that the ritually impure do not hold the mushaf, while other readers of the verse demurred that its sense is angelic and heavenly, the touch being of the guarded Record (78), not of pages. The present translation's ˹angels˺ registers the second reading — it glosses "the purified" as the angels — and the bracket is an honest admission that the grammar does not force it: muṭahharūn is passive in form, "those made pure," and could be either the heavenly retinue or the worshipping human in wuḍūʾ. The commentator's duty at this hinge is to say plainly what the verse can and cannot bear: it certainly excludes the unclean world from the Record; whether it legislates the prayer-mat is a dispute the sūrah left open and the sūrahs closed by consensus-work, not by the text alone.

**The Angels Who Touch: 37 and 56, Side by Side**

Read for the ˹angels˺ gloss alone, verse 79 completes a scene the chapter has drawn twice before in other words: the near-rank are served by "young boys round about them, like preserved pearls" (17–18), and the sūrah's own idiom for the heavenly touch already ran, at this commentary's verses 7–10, through the angels' charge over every soul (in the sense of the Rays' "appointed in succession," 13:10–11 and 86:4 — plain paraphrases, cited earlier). Verse 79 now says what those scenes assumed: the holy and its bearers are in contact only with the clean, and the boys "like preserved pearls (luʾluʾ maknūn)" (18) — the same adjective as the Record (makhnūn, 78) — are themselves kept, veiled, untouched by the world's dust. The sūrah has, in that rhyme, made the cupbearers and the Book one family of kept things: what serves the near-rank and what serves the Prophet's room are both guarded, both touched only by the purified, and the Makkān hearer who had heard "preserved pearls" twenty verses ago (and this commentary's readers at 17–18) now hears the pearls' cousin-name — and the purity the chapter wants is, from 79 backward, already the purity of its Garden, not a rule imposed on it from outside.

**"None Touch It": The Negative That Protects the Exhibit**

The clause's function in the courtroom is evidentiary before it is ritual: the sūrah has sworn by the stars' positions that "this is a noble Quran" (75–77), lodged it in "a well-preserved Record" (78), and verse 79 now forecloses the only remaining attack on the exhibit's integrity — that it was touched by what it denies, jinn, soothsayers, or the marketplace. The sūrah has already told that story once: "Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend? They reveal a whisper to most of the liars" (26:221–222, in the Poets' plain account — this chapter's oracle-denunciation at 59's environment), and 79 gives the flip-side: the Qurʾān's channel is, by contrast, touch-proof, and the sūrah's audience — who judged every poem by the jinn at its poet's elbow, the familiar jinn at the poet's elbow, a literary forensics of the age this commentary names but does not quote — hear their own literary forensics turned back on them: you know how to detect a tainted book; this one is certified by the manner of its untouchedness. The legal readings, high or low, sit atop an apologetic floor that every school agreed the verse provides: what no unclean thing can reach, cannot have been tampered with, and a tamper-proof revelation is the exhibit a court of the Event requires.

**Purity as the Chapter's Recurring Grammar**

Al-Wāqiʿah has been a sūrah of clean and unclean since its first description: the recompense is "as if of pearl, preserved" — the luʾluʾ maknūn line (18, re-cited) — the lost drink "boiling water" and "shaded smoke," and and the near-rank's furniture is untouchable by decay in the chapter's own terms — no idle or sinful talk heard there (25, the line's plain sense), no headache from the cup (19) — and the lost, by contrast, get boiling water and a column of smoke (42–43). Into this system verse 79 adds its rule without legislating it: the Book's world is a hygiene, not an ordinance — the only Qur'anic sūrah to state that holiness is untouchable states it as description, like a property of the material, and the sūrahs of the chapter's etiquette read the description as law precisely because, in this sūrah, the two are the same utterance: what God keeps, the worshipper may not approach unwashed, and the mushaf's later physical rules are, in this chapter's logic, the extension of the Record's own physics. That is why 79's double reading (angels or ablution) has survived fourteen centuries without either side winning: the sūrah itself never separates the two purities.

**The Bracket, the Schools, and the Reader**

One last, honest note on the ˹angels˺ in the present translation: it chooses, softly, and the choice carries the tafsīr of the majority of the early commentators — that the verse's "it" is the heavenly Record and its touchers the angelic bearers — while the legal readers' stronger inference (ritual purity as a condition for holding the Scripture, and the mushaf's rulings for the menstruating, the junub, the minor impurity) rests, in the sūrahs' own books, on the prophetic letter and on the consensus-work, with the sharpest disagreements documented and unresolved [UNVERIFIED: the specifics of each school's rulings and their evidentiary weights are beyond the reach of this commentary's apparatus; the reader is referred to the sūrahs of the sūrahs]. A verse-by-verse commentary that skipped that sentence here would be doing its office badly; al-Wāqiʿah's own reciters lived inside the dispute, and the sūrah, which swears that its Book cannot be touched by the unclean, chose a grammar on which a whole sūrahs would be built — an argument, this commentary has claimed since 74, that the chapter is a book about the consequences of being read, and 79 is its most consequential line: the sermon about semen, seed, water, and fire ends with the sentence that made a hygiene out of a Book, and the sūrah's audience, next, will be accused of trading in it (81–82).

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:80

> ˹It is˺ a revelation from the Lord of all worlds.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Predicates, One Line Each**

The hinge-sequences's grammar deserves the count: "that this is truly a noble Quran" (77), "in a well-preserved Record" (78), "touched by none except the purified" (79), "a revelation from the Lord of all worlds" (80) — four clauses, each a single breath, predicate after predicate upon one subject, the shortest sustained description of itself anywhere in the sūrah. Al-Wāqiʿah, which narrates the Garden in paragraphs and the Field in interrogatives, states its Book's theology in a staccato of nominal clauses: nobility (an attribute), locality (the Record), security (untouchability), origin (the sending) — and a commentary that has tracked the chapter's forms will note that this is the only passage in the chapter where it speaks about itself without a question or a threat, the sūrah's four-beat "We" set in the plainest register it owns. The descent-word of 80 closes the quartet: what is noble, kept, and untouchable is not remote — it came down, and the sūrah's self-theology ends, as it began at 75, with motion: stars' positions and a Book in movement, heaven's geography folded into the fact that this recitation is, line by line, ˹a revelation˺ from.

**Tanzīl: The Noun of Descent the Sūrah Chooses**

The word (tanzīlun) is a verbal noun of the intensive pattern of "sending down in stages," and its choice matters: not the bare "sent" (arsalā, the apostle's verb, which the Qur'an reserves for messengers rather than books) but the layered descent that its own audience's complaint had made a technical term — "if only an angel had been sent down with it" (the objection, in the idiom of 25:7, plain paraphrase), and the sūrahs note that tanzīl answers the how: a book sent in pieces, verse by verse, over years, is not a book dropped whole from a desk in heaven, and the sūrah, by preferring the word of gradual descent, makes its own mode of delivery part of the claim of origin. Verse 80 also, in that choice, completes the chapter's vocabulary of the sent: it has used jaʿala (made, assigned — 73, 36) and anshaʾa (produced — 61, 72, at this commentary's verse 64) and arsala nowhere — and the new verb-family here, n-z-l, adds the last register: the sūrah's God creates, assigns, produces, and descends — and only the last is how a book exists — which is, in a courtroom sūrah, a proof by noun: the exhibit's grammar carries its genealogy.

**"The Lord of All Worlds": The Chapter's One Full Title**

In al-Wāqiʿah the phrase appears exactly once — at this verse, on the present translation's text — and its placement turns the whole argument-half into an exercise in lordship: every consideration was a question about who does what ("is it you… or is it We"), and 80 names the answer's office in the sūrah's only complete divine title: "Lord of all worlds." The sūrah's other addresses are intimate (the repeated "your Lord" of 74 and 96, the doxologies), and this is the single moment the chapter widens — the Event (1) has, by the hinge, been shown to be the act of the Lord, not of a tribe's god, and the four considerations' world (semen to fire) is the property of all the worlds, which is why the sūrah's classes (near, right, left) run the whole of humanity and not Makkah alone. Readers who have followed the chapter from its first "when" will feel the title as a summing: the day that levels mountains and sorts every soul into three lists is, at its middle, declared to be the jurisdiction of the Lord of all worlds, and the sūrah's self-description at 80 is thus also its verdict on the mockers' parochial afterlife (the left's "and our forefathers as well?" — 48, in the chapter's own quotation of its mockers) — their hour belonged to their clan; this one belongs to every world.

**26:192–194: The Verse's Unfinished Mechanism, Completed Next Door**

The Poets' sūrah carries this sūrah's line almost letter for letter — "This is certainly a revelation from the Lord of all worlds, which the trustworthy spirit [Gabriel] brought down into your heart" (26:192–194, in the present translation's idiom) — and the pairing is the sūrah's own commentary: al-Wāqiʿah's bare "revelation from" is, in the neighbor, given its bearer and its destination (the heart), so that the two chapters together state the doctrine in full: origin in the Lord of all worlds, channel in the faithful spirit, terminus in the Prophet's heart, purpose in warning. The shared wording is also a shared context of accusation — both chapters speak it where the audience suspects commerce or invention — and the classical readers loved to note that tanzīl verses of this form cluster precisely at the points of attack, as if the Qur'an's self-certification were, structurally, a wound-dressing: here at 80, the chapter's exhibit is given, in one line, the sender and the manner, before the sermon turns, at 81, to the audience's unacceptable response ("how can you take this message lightly, and make denial your livelihood?" — 81–82, plain quotation of the two lines' translation), and the reader who compares the chapters will find al-Wāqiʿah's 80 the shorter cousin, which is its own kind of force: the hinge of this sūrah has no room for the mechanism, only for the origin — and Makkah's accusation, "he fabricated it," was answered best, in the chapter's scale of values, by the fact of descent itself.

**The Verse Between Doxology and Indictment**

Architecturally, 80 stands between the doxology of 74 and the indictment of 81–82 — between "glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest" and "how can you take this message lightly" — and one sentence of the two rabb clauses of its own (the doxology's "your Lord" and 80's "Lord of all worlds") is the sūrah's whole pivot from worship to complaint: praise is directed at the very Being whose book is being made a commodity, and the indictment lands with its full weight only because 80 has first established the book's provenance. Al-Wāqiʿah's sermon, in other words, does not scold the Makkān traders for trading; it first swears (75–77), archives (78), purifies (79), and authenticates (80) the thing traded, and only then asks — in the chapter's final "how can you" (81) — how a commodity of that origin can be handled the way they handle it, with denial as its price.
