The verse is thus the small hinge on the sūrah's largest late turn, from argument about nature to argument about the Book, and every reader who reaches 96's repeated "glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest" will have passed, at 80, the line where the sūrah's subject changed — nature became news, and news became the news itself, descended from, and about, the Lord of all worlds.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:81

> How can you then take this message lightly,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥadīth: The Sūrah's Self-Name, Here and at the Star-Sūrah's Rebuke**

The present translation's "message" is the Book's word ḥadīth — in its ordinary Arabic, "new talk," and in the Qur'an's own usage, regularly the Qur'an speaking about itself as an event of speech (the idiom is decisive at 52:34 and, for this verse's exact neighbor, at 53:59, where the Star sūrah asks "Do you find this revelation astonishing?" — the same deictic phrase "this ḥadīth," in the present translation's diction, and the same rebuke-context). Al-Wāqiʿah's 81 thus borrows a self-designation the sūrahs had already made accusatory: the Book is a ḥadīth, a new thing said, and the verb that follows — "take it lightly," rendered in the older sūrahs as "treat it as trivial," a verb whose sūrahs' glosses run to making a thing sport and distraction — describes, in the classical glosses, precisely the Makkān manner of meeting the sūrah: not refutation but deflection, the laugh that will not answer. The sūrah's audience is indicted, in 81, not for disbelief argued but for demeanor: the chapter that spent seventeen verses asking them to consider the world has arrived, at the Book itself, at the simplest charge — you handle this as you handle nothing you believe.

**"Then": The Little Word Carrying the Whole Argument**

The verse's conjunction "then" (fa) is the hinge of the sūrah's second half and should not pass unremarked: nobility sworn (75–77), Record guarded (78), touch reserved (79), origin named (80) — "and how, then, can you take this message lightly?" The little fa loads the four predicates as premises and lets the question fall out of them: if the book is what 75–80 has just sworn it is, lightness about it is, in the sūrah's arithmetic, a category error of the size of the Event itself — a small word, then, doing the sūrah's largest bookkeeping. The same fa had, at 74, moved from the argument to praise; here it moves from the Book's status to the audience's conduct, and the pair of fa-clauses frames the sūrah's central turn: everything above was about what is; everything below, from 81 to 87, will be about what is done with it — by the traders who deny, and by the dying whose souls the book reads aloud. Al-Wāqiʿah's architecture, in that reading, is a single movement from nature to news: four considerations of the world's making, one doxology, four predicates of the Book, and then the Book's readers on trial — and the verse that begins the last trial is this one, and its only verb is how they handle it.

**The Crime Is a Manner**

Read against the chapter's opening courtroom (2: "no soul will deny its truthfulness"), 81 shows what disbelief costs in this sūrah's anthropology: not a creed rejected in good faith but a tone adopted toward a message — "taking lightly" is a register of the self, the shrug, and the sūrah, which has described the left's whole sin as mockery at dinner (47, "they used to ask mockingly," in the present translation's bracket), returns, at the hinge, to its first charge against the audience's manners. This is late-Makkān preaching at its characteristic best: no philosophical objection is entertained because none was the real problem; the sūrah's hearers did not disprove resurrection, they yawned at it — and the commentary may cite, in this sūrah's own idiom, the Book's sharpest parallel, the "will not weep, but laugh" (53:60, in the Star sūrah's plain sequence just quoted): al-Wāqiʿah and al-Najm, side by side, put the human problem not in theology but in the face's choice between tears and the smile at news of that weight.

**Laughter, Weeping, and the Chapter's Own Economy of Sound**

Al-Wāqiʿah has, since verse 1, staged a world of sound: the right's greeting of peace — 91, "Greetings to you from the people of the right" — in the sūrah's own salutation, the keening of the harvest-lamenters (66–67, already argued here), the "no idle or sinful talk" of the Garden (25) — and verse 81's "take lightly" enters that soundscape as its worst register, the laugh the sūrah will answer, at 87, with the bare question "if you are truthful, why not pull the soul back?", which is the rhetorical form of shame's silence: not weeping, not laughing, but the choked arithmetic of the dying throat. The sūrah's judgment, across that arc, is acoustic: Makkah's problem is what it does with the voices it has (mocking at 47, keening at 66, trivializing at 81), and the Garden is what it hears will, at last, be free of all three: a people with no idle talk, no need of lament, and no occasion for the shrug, which is to say that the sūrah's ethics of the voice is, in al-Wāqiʿah, indistinguishable from its eschatology.

**The Verse That Names the Audience for the Last Time**

One closing observation on 81's grammar: the second-person plural, which has run through the chapter since its first question to the readers of the Event, is about to disappear for six verses — 83 onward will speak of the soul "at the throat" in the third person, the dying man absent from his own scene — and 81 is thus the last time the sūrah addresses its living audience collectively before it walks them, word by word, into a deathbed. The verse is the sermon's summons before the last exhibit: "you" are charged — not the forefathers (48), not the left of the parable, the you in the room — and the sūrah's manner for that final address is exactly what its content condemns: the plainest possible construction, one question, no threat, no oath, as if the chapter had used all its solemnity at 75–80 and kept, for the charge, only the shock of its plainness — "how can you take this message lightly?" — a sentence a Makkān trader could hear once and not forget, in a chapter that has, by this point, made forgetting about it the sin whose name is denial.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:82

> and repay ˹Allah for˺ your provisions with denial?

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Make Your Livelihood Denial": The Verse's Devastating Idiom**

The present translation's "repay… with denial" renders an Arabic more merciless than its English: tajʿalūna rizqa-kum an tukadhdhibū — "you make your rizq (your livelihood, your portion, your daily bread) to be that you call the Book a lie," and the sūrah, which has spent its whole middle arguing rizq through the grain (63–65), the water (68–70), and fire's provision for travelers (73), arrives here at the sūrah's final exchange-rate: these people have bartered sustenance itself for denial — they eat provision and pay for it with kufr, the root's double sense (ingratitude and disbelief) fused in a way the commentary has been tracking since verse 14. The idiom answers, from the other side, the keening farmers of 66–67, who called the lost harvest "great loss" and themselves "deprived": the living Makkān, says 82, are making deprivation their daily profession — they receive rizq and render back denial, and the bracket ˹Allah for˺ in the present line names, correctly, the one creditor they are actually dealing with. Verse 82 is thus the market-sūrah's closing invoice, the chapter's commercial register (shade like credit, payment "for what they used to do," 24) arriving at its last and worst transaction.

**Rizq as the Chapter's Buried Subject, Now Named**

The chapter's entire argument-half has been a rizq-tract without the word until now: "We made it a provision (matāʿ) for the travellers" (73), the farmers' ˹livelihood˺ bracket at 67, the "fruit and fodder" theology the Abasa sūrah shares with 73's provision-language (80:31–32) — and at 82 the noun rizq arrives, unannounced, in the mouth of the Book's accusation, which is the sūrah's subtlest move: it reveals, at the hinge's end, that its long argument about semen, seed, rain, and fire was not about "nature" but about rizq, provision, the daily givenness of what the audience had made light of as news. The classical readers of this verse drew exactly this conclusion — that the sūrah couples the two unbeliefs, calling God's book a lie and living on His rizq while doing it — and the pairing indicts, in one clause, a piety of the table that ignores a claim of the revelation: a Makkān could reject Muhammad's ḥadīth and still, each dawn, receive the water that could have been salt (70), and 82 says these two receipts are one ledger. To "make your livelihood denial" is, in that light, the most literal description the chapter ever gives of the audience's religion: they are, at breakfast, eating kufr.

**"Denial," Not Unbelief: The Sūrah's Charge Is Verbal**

What the sūrah's verb indicts is takdhīb — calling it a lie, the act of the tongue that 81's "take lightly" set up — and not a concluded denial of the heart; that verbal nuance is the chapter's final pastoral severity: the audience's problem began as manner (the shrug at 81) and ends as speech-act ("He fabricated it," the charge recorded elsewhere of this exact audience, 25:4 and 46:7's "this is nothing but a fabrication of earlier times" in plain paraphrase — the same noun, in the other chapters' mouths, that 82's tukadhdhibūn is answering). Al-Wāqiʿah, uniquely, declines to quote the charge and instead names its verb — you make denial your livelihood — and the effect is that the sūrah never lets its hearers the dignity of a proposition rejected; their position is described as a transaction, repeated daily, in which the commodity is their bread and the coin is "lie," and the commentary has already seen, at 41–48, that the left's sins were listed in the same commercial order (devouring, denying, until the certainty came, 46–48's sequence). The chapter's verdict on Makkah's dispute with the Book is that it was never a dispute; it was a trade in which they were paid in what they refused.

**The Two-Verses' Frame: 81–82 Against 75–80**

Read as a unit, the indictment-verses are the mirror of the oath-qua- tetrad they follow, and the sūrah's antitheses can be tabulated in its own words: there, "a noble Quran… in a well-preserved Record… touched by none except the purified… a revelation from the Lord of all worlds" (77–80); here, "how can you take this message lightly, and make denial your livelihood?" — the Book's four predicates against the audience's two verbs, majīd against muftarā, guarded against belied, revealed from the Lord of all worlds and paid to with denial by people of one small market. The frame is al-Wāqiʿah's largest single composition — nine verses whose subject is the Book's status and its handlers — and its center, verse 78's Record, is the pivot the whole chapter has turned on: everything before 75 was world, everything after 82 is death and sorting, and the hinge-qua-tetrad-with-reply stands in the middle like a courthouse with one case on the docket. That the sūrah never again, in its remaining fourteen verses, mentions the audience's denial — the soul at the throat (83–87) and the three final exits (88–96) proceed without further argument — is the composition's last rhetorical act: the chapter has said "how can you," once, and leaves the question to be answered by dying.

**The Verse the Reciters' Lore Kept for Itself**

One reception-note belongs at the close of this unit, and the commentator should state it plainly and stop there: this pair of verses, 81–82, was the passage the sūrah's early audience heard as its own exposure — the account that made the chapter, in the sūrahs' histories, less a comfort than an indictment for anyone who recited it professionally and traded in its rewards, and the prophetic saying "the best of you is the one who learns the Qur'an and teaches it" (transmitted in the canonical collections, in the Bukhārī's Book of the Qur'an, in this commentary's earlier paraphrase) was preserved precisely as the positive pole of this verse's warning: learning and teaching, rizq in service of the ḥadīth, against making denial the livelihood — and the sūrah's reciter, each night, held the pair of sentences like two cups, and drank. This commentary, which has recorded the contested virtue-traditions of this chapter at 23 and 26, records this pairing here as the one verse-pair of the entire sūrah that cannot be recited aloud in a market-town without its readers, whoever they are, being named by it — a property of 81–82 that its fourteen centuries of readers have, on the whole, quietly accepted, and that the chapter's final "so glorify the Name" (96) is the only adequate response the sūrah itself permits.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:83

> Why then ˹are you helpless˺ when the soul ˹of a dying person˺ reaches ˹their˺ throat,

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Why Then": The Question That Ends the Court**

The chapter's last interrogative is the shortest form Arabic has for a rebuke: fa-law lā — "why then do you not" — a compound of the conditional and the negation that the sūrahs' grammarians gloss as "then why do you not do it," and the sūrah, which has asked in turn about belief (57), mindfulness (62), and thanks (70), now asks the one question that convicts by grammar alone: if this Book were nothing, and you were nothing but yourselves, why do you not, at this throat, do something? The scene is chosen for the exactness of its helplessness: at no moment in the human calendar is the audience's advertised autonomy — the self-possession implicit in "is it you who create it, or We?" (59) — so visibly suspended as at the deathbed, where the strongest hand present does nothing but hold the wrist. Verse 83 is therefore the argument-half's final verdict, delivered as a question: the courtroom of semen, seed, water, and fire closes at the throat, because it is the throat where every plea the chapter refused to entertain — that persons own their lives — comes to trial honestly, once, for everybody, and loses.

**The Soul at the Throat: The Sūrah's One Anatomy**

The idiom (al-nafs balagha al-ḥulqūm, "the soul reached the throat") is the Qur'an's fixed image for dying, and it names a location the living body knows from the inside — the lump, the constriction, the swallow that fails — and al-Wāqiʿah, whose whole argument has been built from what Makkah knows by heart — the ejected fluid of 58, the reaped harvest of 63, the drunk water of 68 — ends on the throat's last passage. The sūrahs' readers of the verse kept the physical vocabulary: al-ḥulqūm is the windpipe itself, and the soul's "reaching" it is, in the sūrahs' tellings, the moment of the taking, the point at which the departing becomes observable and no longer reversible. The sūrah's choice of this anatomy is also its final theology of the body: after the mockers' demand to be "restored to our former state, even after decayed bones" (79:10–11, in the other sūrah's plain report), the sūrah says: your first restoration is happening, hourly, in the room next to this one — no bones required, only breath failing, and the throat as the last door every argument about resurrection must pass through, whether the debater likes it or not.

**"A Dying Person" — the Verse's Deliberate Anonymity**

The present translation's bracket ˹of a dying person˺ supplies what the Arabic withholds: the soul at the throat belongs to no named character, no Israelite or Pharaoh, no particular class — the chapter that has sorted near, right, and left refuses, at 83, to sort, and the dying one is simply "the one whose soul reached the throat," which is to say: you, whoever you are, first. The rhetorical effect is the sūrah's gentlest and its hardest at once — no Makkān hearer can place themselves outside the scene; the anonymity is the universalization, and the sermon's whole preceding argument (classes, orchards, fires, keening farmers) condenses, in this line, into a room where every class is represented and none of the classes matters yet. The sorting resumes at 88, and the structure is exact: first the common door, then the separate rooms beyond it — al-Wāqiʿah tells every hearer their own death before it tells them which death they are practicing toward, which is the ordering a sūrah that ends in praise must have: no reader may flatter themselves past verse 83.

**The Helpless Audience: Family, Physicians, and the Limits of Presence**

One historical note this verse earns: the sūrah's "why then do you not" is addressed to the bystanders, not the dying — you are looking on (84) — and the classical readers weighed the line's reach with unusual honesty, taking it first as the onlookers' impotence (the family that could not pay its weight in years, the physician whose art stopped at the throat), and some of them recorded the sūrahs' report that the Prophet recited this sūrah, and that its recitation at the bedside became, for that reason, a beloved practice in the early communities [UNVERIFIED: the specific reports about reciting al-Wāqiʿah at the dying are transmitted in the sūrahs' collections with chains the ḥuffāẓ themselves weighed differently]. What is certain is the verse's own content: presence at death as the limit-case of all human presence — the strongest thing the living can do, at a throat, is look — and the sūrah, having argued that its hearers own nothing in life (57–73), does not need to argue that they own nothing at death; it simply gathers them there and asks, once, why they do not act — a question none of them, in fourteen centuries of rooms, has ever answered aloud.

**The Verse the Chapter Answers with Its Final Sorting**

Structurally, 83 is the hinge between the Book's trial (75–82) and the classes' exits (88–96), and its placement states the sūrah's last doctrine plainly: the Qur'an and the soul are the same jurisdiction — the book that none may touch except the purified (79) and the soul that none may call back except its Owner are held by the same "We" (85, next verse but one), and the chapter's two late subjects, revelation and death, meet at this line. The mockers had asked, at the chapter's own dinner-table, "when we die and become dust and bones, will we really be resurrected?" (47, re-echoed from the chapter's quotation of them), and verse 83 answers their form of the question by moving the scene from the grave to the bedside: the return they mocked is, before it is a resurrection, this — a soul at a throat, a room of hands that do nothing — and the sūrah's sermon will not proceed past this verse to verdicts (88–96) until the room has been described, because in al-Wāqiʿah's arithmetic no argument about the Book's nobility (77) and no claim about the audience's trade (82) is complete until the dying person has been laid, anonymous, between them, and the reciter asked why they are standing there with empty hands.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:84

> while you are looking on?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Grammar of Watching: Presence Without Power**

The line's single clause — "while you are looking on" — is the sūrah's smallest and most pitiless construction: a plural subject, a verb of sight, and nothing else, and the grammar performs what it describes, since seeing is the only act permitted to the bystanders of a death. Al-Wāqiʿah's argument-half had, at verse 57, charged its audience with not believing, and its closing third now re-describes them in the only posture that ever belonged to the living at a dying: watchers. The classical tellers of the sūrahs read the line's verb exactly this way, as "while you see the one dying and can do nothing," and the commentators after them liked the verse's irony in legal register: a sūrah that had subpoenaed the field, the rain, and the hearth now subpoenaes the bedside, and the testimony it takes from the room is one word long — we were looking — the only testimony no one present has ever been able to give as anything more.

**"Looking On" and "You Cannot See": The Chapter's Twin Verbs of Sight**

Verse 84 hands the audience sight and verse 85 takes it away — "but you cannot see" — and the pair is al-Wāqiʿah's final epistemology: looking on is the humans' true report (you are watching the body), not-seeing is their true limit (you are watching a taking you do not see), and the sūrah lets both stand in consecutive lines as a single lesson about what presence is. The sūrahs' glosses supply the standard explanation of the contrast: the bystanders see the dying and do not see the angels, the soul, the scene of the taking — "you see what is happening to the body; you do not see who is doing it" — and the rhetorical gain for the sermon is decisive: the mockers' sneer at the unseen ("will we really be resurrected?" — 47, in the chapter's own quotation) dies at the throat not by proof but by proximity, because the room at every death contains exactly one unseen fact, and everyone in the room knows, hourly, that it is there, being executed by hands they cannot name. Al-Wāqiʿah's resurrection-argument, its most contested claim, ends, at 84–85, with the ordinary witness of rooms that the unseen is real.

**The Spectators Named: A Chapter's Audience Arrives at Its Own Scene**

One compositional justice deserves the record: every "you" in this sūrah has been Makkah — the questioners of 57, the keening farmers of 66, the trivializers of 81 — and "you are looking on," at 84, is the first time in the chapter that the audience appears, in its own person, inside the scene it was asked to consider: until now the hearer was the courtroom; now the hearer is the family at the bed. The shift is the sermon's completion in its form: no more arguments can be offered about who owns the crop or the fire, because the owners are at a throat, watching, and the sūrah, having walked its listeners from the first fall of the Event (1) to this room, has arrived where arrival was always its aim — not the audience's consent to doctrine, but its recognition of the scene. Readers who recall how this chapter began to be recited at the dying (the lore recorded at this commentary's verses 23, 26, and 83) will see why: the community that kept al-Wāqiʿah at bedsides was, in that practice, answering 84 literally — present, looking, reciting the verse about being present and looking — a loop between sūrah and life in which the chapter's form and the community's custom have, for fourteen centuries, been the same gesture.

**What the Verse Withholds: No Angels Here, Yet**

The commentators must also mark an absence: verse 84 does not yet say who is taking the soul — that is 85's "We are nearer" — and several sūrahs note that the angels of death, familiar elsewhere in the Book ("It is the angels who take the souls of the disbelievers, striking their faces and their backs," in the plain sense of 8:50, and "Our messengers take him, and they are not negligent," at 6:61, quoted already at this chapter's 83), are, in al-Wāqiʿah, left entirely behind the verse's curtain: the sūrah's dying-room has human eyes only, and its unseen is unnamed until the next line. The restraint serves the argument: the chapter has spent its middle insisting that the audience's own sciences (semen, harvest, rain, fire) are agencies of God they do not see, and 84 keeps the same method to the last — the death scene is told as the body's fact, not the angels' spectacle — so that when 85 finally introduces the nearer We, it arrives not as mythology added to medicine but as the medicine's own missing half, the sentence the watching was already shaped to receive, and the sūrahs of the afterlife's details (the striking, the questioning in the grave, the long reports of the collections) are, here, respectfully, entirely absent: al-Wāqiʿah's eschatology is a sūrah of bare, terrible proximities, not of wonders.

**The Line That Makes the Next One Inevitable**

Thus 84's two words — "you looking on" — exist to arm 85's "We nearer than you," the comparative that has made this verse-pair one of the Qur'an's most quoted: the bystanders' nearness is real (they are at the bed, they see the throat) and it is being measured, at 85, against a nearer — and nothing in the chapter has yet been measured without losing to God. The pair is also, in the sermon's arithmetic, the final inversion of the mockers' dinner-talk: they had asked to be sent back in the forefathers' state (47–48); the sūrah's answer is that no one at a throat can even be sent forward, and the only agency present belongs to the One whose nearness the watchers cannot see. Verse 84, read in that position, is the hinge of the whole sūrah's last movement: the room (84), the nearer We (85), the challenge to return the soul (86–87), and then the exits (88–96) — and a commentary on this line should end where the line ends, with the audience's own eyes, since the chapter has, at the last, nothing left to say about them that is not true: they are all, at some hour, verse 84, looking on, and what they most need the sūrah to say about that looking is what it says next, about the One who was nearer than all of them, before any of them arrived.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:85

> And We are nearer to such a person than you, but you cannot see.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Nearest Sentence in a Chapter of Comparatives**

"Qarībun aqrabu minkum" — nearer than you — is the sūrah's only comparative of divine station, and its precision should be felt: the measure is not "nearer than his jugular vein," which is the other sūrah's figure for God's relation to a person in life (50:16, "We are nearer to him than his jugular vein," quoted here in the present translation's idiom), but nearer than you — nearer than the closest family hand, the physician, the son holding the pillow — and the dying man is, at 85, the point of comparison rather than the object. Al-Wāqiʿah chose, at its climax, the nearness that every mourner can verify: everyone in the room was as near as they could get, and the taking happened nearer still — which is why the verse has been, across the centuries, read at bedsides as both rebuke and comfort: the watchers were not the nearest, and what they could not do was being done, gently or terribly, by One who could not miss. The sūrah's whole argument that the audience owns nothing in its processes now arrives, in one line, at the one process every house has watched.

**"But You Cannot See": The Chapter's Final Epistemic Clause**

The closing words — "but you do not see" — repeat, at the throat, the refrain the sūrah has run under every consideration: "a form you do not know" (60), "if only you knew" (76), and now "you cannot see" — and their cumulative function is to teach the audience the shape of the word "near" in this Book: nearness without sight is the normal condition of the world (61–64), and death adds only the degree. The clause also completes 84's pair — "you are looking on… but you cannot see" — and the paradox is doctrine, not poetry: the room sees the body and does not see the taking; the sūrah's hearers, who wanted resurrection argued in dust and bones (47's sneer), are answered with the plainest fact available, that the unseen is not at the far end of a syllogism but at the near end of a bed, and the "but" of the clause is the chapter's last, quiet word about human instruments: your eyes were open at the right time and are still, here, useless — which al-Wāqiʿah treats as news about seeing, not about dying.

**Two Nearnesses: The Sūrahs' Own Readings**

The exegetical tradition weighed the verse's nearness along two lines, and this commentary records both without collapsing them. The first reads "We are nearer" by way of the angels — the "Our" of the taking is God's command executed by the angels of death, whose office the Book states elsewhere in plain terms ("Our messengers take him, and they are not negligent," 6:61, quoted here as earlier at 83, and the striking hands of 8:50, in the Spoils sūrah's plain account) — a reading that suits the sūrah's refusal elsewhere to name machinery (at 84 above, the angels are unseen here too). The second reads "We" as the divine first person directly, and the sūrahs' theology then governed the "how": nearness as knowledge, power, and mercy, not as place or distance, for which the later schools armed themselves explicitly against spatial attribution, and this commentary notes the point without adjudicating the schools [UNVERIFIED: the technical formulations of the nearness-debate belong to kalām literature beyond this commentary's apparatus]. Either way the verse's function in al-Wāqiʿah is identical — it transfers the action of the deathbed from the watchers to the Owner of the soul — and that transfer, not the metaphysics of proximity, is what the sermon has been building its whole middle toward.

**The Predicate That Replaces the Challenge**

Formal readers will notice the turn: through 57–73 every "or is it We" took a verb of making (We create, We grow, We send down, We produce), and at 85 the divine answer-predicate changes to an adjective — not "We take it" but "We are nearer" — and the change of register is the sūrah pivoting from courtroom to scene: the argument asked who does what, and the dying-room says only whose presence is prior. The "We" of the four considerations, in other words, becomes at 85 the "We" that is already there, and the effect on the chapter's theology is profound in a single word: God's agency in the seed and the rain (64, 69) was asserted as making; here it is asserted as nearness, which is the sūrah's way of saying that the two are the same — to make a thing is to be nearer to it than anyone, and the dying, at whom every hand arrived too late, are the standing proof. This is the only verse in al-Wāqiʿah in which God predicates Himself of a scene rather than acting in it, and that restraint is the chapter's last word about the Event's God: the Event was never an arrival; it is a discovery of what was nearer first, which is why the doxologies at 74 and 96 can sit, at either end of the whole final third, without any verdict in between being needed.

**Rayḥān: The Scent in the Nearest Hand**

One link forward completes this entry. If 85's nearer "We" is received, by the angels' reading, as the hands that take, the sūrah will itself supply what those hands carry: for the drawn-near at 88–89, "a welcome (rayḥān), fragrance, and a Garden of Bliss" — and the word rayḥān, at this commentary's verses 18–19, was weighed already for its double sense of rest-of-soul and sweet-plant, so that the first thing the dying near-one receives, in this chapter's ordering, is scent. The long reports of the collections — the soul taken with garments of Paradise and a breath of its fragrance, in the famous narration transmitted from Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib — match, point for point, what al-Wāqiʿah implies at 85–89: the unseen takers carry what they will return, and the nearer One's nearness is, for the saved, a hand with a flower in it. This commentary cites that narration only for its shape, its chains being the province of the ḥuffāẓ [UNVERIFIED: grading of the Barāʾ soul-taking report is disputed across the collections]. But the sūrah's own sequence needs no report to land its effect: you cannot see, it told the watchers, and the verse after the challenge will say what the seeing, when it comes, smells like — which is, in a sermon built on water and fire and the throat, the most human imaginable mercy: the nearest presence, at the last, a fragrance nobody in the room could see arriving.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:86

> Now, if you are not subject to Our Will ˹as you claim˺,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional at the Bedside**

Al-Wāqiʿah's sermon had run on questions since verse 57, and at 86 it abandons even questioning for a conditional: "Now, if you are not subject to Our Will — then bring it back (87)" — the challenge-form of the Book's other great disputes (the Cow's "produce a sūrah like it… if what you say is true," 2:23, and the Jonah chapter's shorter dare, 10:38, both quoted here in the present translation's idiom), transported from the literary arena to the dying-room, where no one has ever performed it. The grammar matters for the whole chapter's trajectory: the mockers had demanded resurrection be proved to their taste (47), and the sūrah now grants them, hypothetically, the one doctrine under which no proof is needed and none is possible — that they answer to no one — and stakes everything on its corollary: if no Lord, no reckoning, and no reckoning, then the soul at the throat is yours to call back, and the verse hangs its "if" open in front of a room that cannot fill it, the gentlest way a book has of executing an argument by experience.

**"Madīnūn": The Word with Two Reckonings**

The Arabic's single adjective (from m-d-n) was preserved by the sūrahs' lexicographers with two construals, and the verse is stronger for carrying both. The first is the one the present translation chooses — "subject to Our Will," read as not being reckoned, answerable, judged (the dīn-family's office: "you are not being requited," hence free of a Lord in the claim) — and the sūrahs' readers also transmitted, as the plainest sense for this verse, "if you are not maintained": madīn as the one whose sustenance is spent on him, the kept, the provided-for — the same audience's own livelihood-word (rizq at 82), turned back on them as a conditional: if you are not, at any rate, kept — and both senses are answered by one scene, because the soul at the throat is neither reckoned with by the watchers nor maintained by them. That the verse holds judgment and provision in one word is al-Wāqiʿah's last theology: to be judged and to be fed are the same Lord's offices, and the audience's pretended autonomy ("as you claim") is, in this sūrah's physics, the single error of denying both — a point the brackets of the present line (˹as you claim˺) mark, in the Book's idiom for zaʿm, an assertion without a witness.

**"As You Claim": The Bracket's Long History**

The translator's parenthetical ˹as you claim˺ carries, in this chapter's commentary, more weight than two words, because zaʿama in classical Arabic is the verb of the unsupported assertion — the claim you make when nobody will check it — and the sūrah's final third is built, word by word, on making sure someone checks: the sūrah just swore by the stars' positions that its message is noble (75–77), filed where the unclean cannot reach it (78–79), and sent by the Lord of all worlds (80), and the audience's counter-position — no reckoning, no Lord, the book a trade — has been called, at 82, a livelihood, not a thesis. To supply "as you claim" is therefore to place the audience's doctrine where the chapter has always placed it: among the things said, at dinner (47's "they used to ask mockingly"), that the dying-room will test at the throat, and the test's result, 86 implies, they know before they hear it — the verse's malice, if it has any, is the mildest kind: it asks the claimers to notice that they are claimers, at a bedside, where nobody has ever advanced a claim by proof, and the sūrah knows that no one, watching the last breath leave, has ever once felt in charge.

**The Sūrah's Last Concession**

Formally, 86 is the argument-half's final grant — the chapter that had refused its hearers credit for semen, crop, rain, and fire now refuses them, provisionally, even the one sovereignty they thought they kept for themselves (the sovereignty of the unaccountable), by conceding it hypothetically in order to expose its poverty: "if you are not subject to Our Will" — suppose it true; the only visible consequence is the impotence of verse 87, and the sūrah has thus, in two verses, executed the classical reductio: the autonomy that cannot reverse a dying is not worth arguing against, only exhibiting. The commentators will note that this is the same method, in miniature, as the chapter's opening: the Event arrives, "its falsity no soul will deny" (2), and every position is tested at the point where it would have to act — semen that cannot choose its shape (59–60), water that cannot refuse its salt (70), and here a will that cannot hold the soul it claims to own — and al-Wāqiʿah closes its long argument, at 86, exactly as it began its whole sermon: by putting its hearers, not in a debate, but in a scene, with their doctrine in their hands and a dying person between them, and asking them — one last, last time, softly, in an "if" — to do with it what they promised themselves they could do.

**What the "Now" Does: fa and the Hinge of the Third Third**

One last look at the opening conjunctions — the fa and the in together, "Now, if…" — because they are the sūrah's structural sign-post: from 83 the chapter stopped arguing about the Book and started, with this fa, moving its whole audience down the hall to the room, and the "if" of 86 is the door the three exits walk through (88, 90, 92, each a fa-amma clause, "as for…", the sorting-formula this sūrah shares with the Qur'an's verdict-passages). The little word marks, in the commentary's count, the last hinge in al-Wāqiʿah's architecture: revelation (75–82) closes under a conditional, and the fates (88–96) open under the next — and the conditional itself is the chapter's final gift to the reader: 86 asks each hearer, individually, which of their own claims would, if true, be able to bring a soul back, and in that question — asked by a sermon at a bedside, fourteen centuries running, in rooms where people have just stopped being able to pretend — the sūrah's courtroom dissolves into its verdicts without a sentence of transition, because the transition was the throat, and the throat is the sermon's last word and its only witness.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:87

> bring that soul back, if what you say is true. Which of the Three Will You Be?

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Imperative Nobody Has Ever Obeyed**

urdidūhā — "bring back that soul" — is the only imperative the sūrah ever addresses to its deniers, and its content is a command to reverse the one process the chapter has spent its whole middle declaring sovereign: if you own nothing in the making (57–73) but your claim to be unaccounted for (86), exercise that ownership at the throat, now, on this one soul, and return it to the body it has just left. The sūrahs' readers marked the form: divine challenges in this Book come in escalating generosity — produce a sūrah like it (2:23), ten (the Hūd chapter's dare), or call witnesses to help (10:38, both in the present translations' wording) — and al-Wāqiʿah's is the shortest and the most local: no literary performance, no assembly, only the reversal of a dying in the room you are standing in. A challenge measured to the strength of the opponents' actual position — "if what you say is true" — and the sūrah, by accepting no substitute deed, makes the verse's silence (no one ever has, anywhere) the last testimony in the courtroom: the chapter does not need to refute the claim of the unaccountable; it hands the claim the only instrument its own doctrine recognizes, the will, at the only scene where the will is on parade, and lets the failure speak.

**"That Soul": The Demonstrative's Intimacy**

Arabic's hādhihi — "this/that" — points, and pointing at a death is different from speaking about deaths: the sūrah has said "the soul" (83) and "such a person" (85) in the general case, and at 87 the grammar comes close, "bring back that soul," the one now in the room, now at the throat of the person whose pillow the strongest bystander is holding. The shift from indefinite to demonstrative is the sermon's final act of address, and it explains why this verse-pair has been, from the sūrahs onward, the passage read at bedsides (the practice-lore recorded at this commentary's verses 23, 26, 83, and 84): the dying person in the room is the referent of the sūrah, and the reciter, whoever they are, obeys the grammar by looking at the throat the verse is pointing at, and asking the room the question the verse asks the room — which none of them, in fourteen centuries, has answered by acting. Al-Wāqiʿah, which began by denying that any soul would disbelieve the Event (2: "no soul will deny its truthfulness"), ends its argument by staging a scene in which everyone present, believers and mockers alike, believes one thing with equal clarity: the soul has gone, and it is not coming back, and the "that" of verse 87 is where the chapter finally catches its audience — in a certainty they share with each other, in a room, over a body, before any doxology tells them what to do with it.

**"Which of the Three Will You Be?": The Line's Curious Shape**

The present translation carries, at the end of verse 87, a question — "Which of the Three Will You Be?" — that the classical editions did not treat as part of the verse's text but as a division-heading supplied by some editors to introduce the sorting-passage that begins at 88 (fa-amma in kana… "as for if he was one of those brought near…"), and the commentary states the fact plainly, since the line's position in this translation makes the heading part of the recited page: readers of this sūrah, here, are given a three-option quiz — near ones, people of the right, the misguided deniers — exactly at the moment the sūrah's sermon turns from the common death to the different fates. The device, whatever its editorial pedigree [UNVERIFIED: whether the sentence belongs to the verse-text in any canonical reading is not the place of this commentary to decide; the recited lines of the sūrahs divide at 87/88 without it], does the sūrah's own work by other means: al-Wāqiʿah has spent six verses describing one death (83–88a) and now asks the hearer, in its translation's heading, which exit is theirs — the same question the chapter put, at 7–10, in the register of "three classes," and the rhyme of the whole architecture closes: the sorting announced at the beginning is, at the end, put to the vote at the bedside, where votes are cast, the sūrah says, by a life.

**The Challenge Answered by the Verdict**

Structurally, 87 is where the chapter stops asking: the sūrah's remaining ten verses (88–96) issue no question at all — they are three conditional sentences (if of the brought-near / if of the right / if of the deniers) with their apodoses of welcome and of boiling water, and the doxology. The transition is itself an argument by form: having issued the only test no claimant can pass, al-Wāqiʿah does not gloat, infer, or pronounce a verdict on Makkah; it proceeds directly to describe the three fates as though no one in the room had failed, because the failure was the room, and the room knew it, and a sūrah that had run its courtroom on questions now has nothing left to interrogate — the throat answered everything. Commentators of this passage's style note that the Book's challenges (the dares of 2:23 and 10:38, re-cited above) are conventionally followed by the audience's continued resistance and a fresh punishment-sentence; here the challenge is followed by a welcome (89) and a guest-room's sentence (93), which is this sūrah's way of saying that the Day's verdicts are not new information: they are the exits the dying are already facing, named from the nearer side, by the nearest One (85), and the reader's task, at the hinge, was never to win the argument of 86–87, but only to hear which of the three doors their own life has been oiling, all along, without looking up.

**"If What You Say Is True": The Sūrah's Own Truth-Test**

The formula "in kuntum ṣādiqīn" — "if what you say is true" — appears, in this Book, almost always attached to a dare (at the Cow's verse quoted above and the Jonah chapter's, but its cousin-noun sits in this chapter's own opening: "no soul will deny its truthfulness (takdhīb)" at 2, the very noun the mockers were charged with making their livelihood at 82), and its placement at 87 completes a ring that has been running since verse 2: the chapter opens with the Event that nobody can disbelieve, and closes its challenge with the truth-test nobody can pass — and between the two, the sūrah has defined truth-telling, in its own vocabulary, as the only thing the human will has ever reliably been able to perform: consider, admit, thank, glorify — the four small verbs of 57, 62, 70, 74. To "bring that soul back" is the one act that would make the claimants' cosmology true, and the sūrah, at 87, is not asking them to perform it; it is asking them, one last time, to tell the truth about it — to say, looking at the throat, "this was never mine" — which is, in al-Wāqiʿah's entire moral world, the exact sentence that opens the door of the people of the right, three verses from here, and the only sentence the deniers of 47–48 had spent their whole lives, at every bedside they attended, refusing to say.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:88

> So, if the deceased is one of those brought near ˹to Us˺,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Participle of the Brought-Near, Read After the Verse of Nearness**

Al-Wāqiʿah has just declared "We are nearer to him than you" (85) — a nearness of presence that none could match and all must endure — and the first class of the final sorting is, in Arabic, "the one brought near (al-muqarrabūn)," the same root with a different morphology, the passive intensive participle: there is a nearness that is owed to every creature, God's being nearer than anyone to the dying, and a nearness that is given, brought-about, honored, and this sūrah has been arguing, since verse 8, that the second kind is a rank: "the forerunners — the brought-near" (11, in the chapter's own census, at this commentary's verses 7–10). The double vocabulary is the hinge of the whole chapter's theology of proximity: near to all as Lord, near to some as welcome — and the dying person of 83–87, whose throat was about to be the scene of the universal nearness, is now, at 88, addressed in the possibility of the particular: which nearness did your life befriend? The sūrah does not say "if he was noble" or "if he was pious"; it says "of the brought-near," a phrase whose passive forces the reader to ask by Whom — and the whole argument-half already answered: only the One whose first-person verbs owned the semen, the crop, the rain, and the fire can bring near, and can refuse to.

**"If He Was": The Conditional That No Longer Challenges**

The sorting-clauses of 88, 90, and 92 all open with "fa-ammā in kāna" — "as for, if he was" — and the conditional particle, at this late point in the chapter, is doing something new with its old work: 86 had used "if" to test a claim ("if you are not subject to Our Will, bring the soul back"), and 88–92 use "if" to sort a file — the difference is between a dare and a verdict, and the sūrah's grammar marks its own transition: the room where the challenge rang (87) is the room whose occupant is now conditionally classified, and nobody is being asked to perform anything anymore. The commentators will note that the Arabic of these verses contains no verb but "was" (kāna) — the copula of settled identity, not of act — and that this is the last verb the sūrah will lend its classes: everything they did has collapsed, at the throat, into what they were, and the sūrah's ethics of habit (it has repeated "they used to" (kānū) again and again, in the left's "they used to ask mockingly" at 47 and the right's "they used to do" at 24) arrives at its tense: past, complete, and, on this side of the verdict, final. "If he was" is the sūrah's plainest and most terrible question, because it asks what has, by definition, stopped being answerable by new action.

**The Bracket's Two Words: "To Us"**

The present translation supplies ˹to Us˺ after "brought near," and the supply is the standard reading — the sūrahs' commentators explained al-muqarrabūn as "those honored and brought close by God (yurjūna wa-yuqarrabūna)," and the divine first-person plural that the chapter has used for every act since 57 (We created, We grow, We send, We produce, We are nearer) reappears, in the bracket, as the destination: the near-rank are not near to a place, a tree, a garden — the sūrah's own Paradise has springs, couches, fruit, and cups, and none of them is here — they are near to the One who gave them, and the bracket marks al-Wāqiʿah's rare mysticism of rank: its highest class is defined by relation to God Himself rather than to His furniture, which is why, in this chapter's own inventory, the near-rank receive the fewest things and the strangest words — "welcome, fragrance, and a Garden of Bliss" (89, next verse) — while the people of the right, below them, are described in paragraphs of orchards and wives. The sūrah's economy of nearness is subtractive: the nearer the class, the fewer the nouns, and the bracket's "to Us" is the last, smallest, largest of them.

**"One of" — Partitive Mercy in a Chapter of Lists**

The phrase "one of the brought-near (min al-muqarrabīn)" uses the partitive "min," and the sorting at 88, 90, and 92 all run on it — a dying person is "one of" a class — and that small grammar has earned its commentaries: the sūrahs read the partitive as the sūrah's mercy on the individual, since "of" allows a man to be counted without being totalled, a servant to be among the near despite himself, and the later schools — pressed by their own doctrines of assurance — debated whether a believer may testify "I am one of the people of Paradise" and commonly answered that the sūrah's own grammar permits humility and hope at once: every "if he was" is a fact only God verifies, and every "one of" is a company wide enough for the imperfect. Al-Wāqiʿah's censuses, at 7–10, had the same shape — "companies," the near-rank "a great multitude of the former peoples and a few of the later" (13–14, the words this commentary weighed at the first rank's entry) — and the chapter's final sorting now returns to that shape, with the same partitive "min," so that the reader who has traveled 1–88 finds the verse's last word on class answering its first: you enter eternity "of" a people, not as a file complete in yourself, and the sūrah, which has stripped every other self-claim since 57, keeps, for the dying, only membership.

**The Sentence That Waits for Its Welcome**

Structurally, 88 is the protasis of a long conditional whose apodosis is the whole remainder of the chapter: "if he was of the brought-near — then welcome, fragrance, and a Garden of Bliss (89); and if of the people of the right — then greetings (90–91); and if of the deniers-of-lie — then boiling water and burning (92–94)" — and the sūrah's last ten verses are therefore one sentence of three branches, the longest conditional architecture the chapter contains, a syntax that performs its own point about death: the scene (83–87) is one event whose consequence is a fork of three doors, and the sūrah, recited at bedsides since its first generation (the lore this commentary has tracked at 23, 26, 83, 84), is structured to be heard as the last words a mourner's sūrah speaks over the body — protasis at the throat, apodosis at the burial. And the first word of the first branch is not "reward" but "welcome (fa-riḥun)," a noun the sūrahs weighed carefully as rest, coolness, relief (and some of them heard, in its near-homonym, "mercy (raḥma)" — the sūrahs of the second reading [UNVERIFIED: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd's "mercy" gloss is transmitted in the sūrahs' marginal notes, not the canonical ones]), and that choice of opening word is al-Wāqiʿah's final theology in miniature: the near-rank arrive, at death, not to payment but to relief, not to wages but to a bath of mercy after a long walk — and the sermon which began by warning a market-town that its fields could crumble and its water turn salt ends by telling its best hearers that what comes next is rest for the feet, a welcome for the guest, and fragrance in the nearest hands, which is — in a sūrah that has measured everything by rizq — the definition of Paradise: provision without the need to be grateful, because the gratitude was already, entirely, answered.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:89

> then such a person will have serenity, fragrance, and a Garden of Bliss.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Nouns With No Verbs: The Welcome as Pure Gift**

The apodosis of the near-rank branch is three words — fa-riḥun wa-rayḥānun wa-jannatu naʿīm — and the sūrah, which has spent its whole final third attaching rewards to verbs of past service ("a reward for what they used to do," 24; "We shall requite," in its repeated idiom of giving-back), gives its highest class, at the throat, three bare nouns and no sentence: rest, fragrance, garden — a welcome, not a wage. The grammarians of the sūrahs read the nominal clause as immediacy: the dying muqarrab does not receive after the Day; what arrives at 89 arrives now, at the moment the soul reaches the throat (83), before reckoning, in the sūrah's own compressed time in which the Event and the dying are the same verb. And the near-rank's estate, unlike the right's in the chapter's earlier panorama (28–34), has no furniture listed here at all — the sūrah reserves its orchards, spouses, and cushions for a different register — because the entry of the nearest is a greeting scene, and the commentary's long observation at this chapter's first description of them (11–14) found the same subtraction: the nearer the class, the fewer the nouns.

**"Wa-Rayḥānan": The Fragrance the Chapter Promised Once Already**

The word rayḥān has stood, in this sūrah's earlier passage, at the cups of the near-rank — "and fragrance (rayḥān), a Garden of Bliss" — and this commentary weighed it at verses 18–19, where its double sense (the sweet plant and the ease of soul) divided the sūrahs: the present verse, repeating the noun at the bedside, decides nothing and doubles the gift: the nearest dying are welcomed with a scent that is also relief, and the sūrah, whose left receive "a column of smoke" (43 — samūm in the other chapters' word, ṣaʿīr here and samūm at 43's neighborhood) a drink of hamīm (93) — and in the other chapter of the mounting hour, the striking wind called samūm paired with hamīm itself (52:16, plain paraphrase) — defines its near-rank by olfaction as sharply as it defines the lost: two economies of smell, the garden's rayḥān against the smoke and the scorched wind. Smell, in the ancient bedside real, is the sense of presence without sight — the oil, the incense, the decay — and the sūrah's final scene of salvation gives the dying one a fragrance nobody in the room can see arriving (85: "but you cannot see"), which closes the loop this commentary left open at 85: the nearer hands, unseen, carry what the saved can verify with the one sense the watchers' failure does not disqualify — and a mourner who has stood at a bath of coolness at dusk, with scent in the air, has already half-understood verse 89.

**"Jannata Naʿīm": The Garden Word and the Question It Will Ask**

Bliss translates naʿīm — the noun of soft, untroubled living — and the sūrah's audience met its family at 45: the left were "spoiled by luxury (mutrafīn)" (45) in the life before, and the naʿīm of the near-rank is the same root's honest form: luxury become gift, softness become garden, the wealth Makkah worshipped re-sown for those who were not worshipped by it. The pairing completes the chapter's economics: it indicted making denial one's livelihood (82, at this commentary's verse), promised gratitude as the rarer harvest (70), and now gives the near-rank the naʿīm whose every hour the unthankful spent pretending was theirs — and the sūrahs' hearers would have known, at the word, the companion-verse they recite when the garden's name is spoken: the Rivalry chapter ends by promising that every person will, that Day, be questioned about the naʿīm — the delights they were given (102:6–8, in plain paraphrase). Al-Wāqiʿah does not pose that question at 89 — the near-rank are past questioning — but by naming naʿīm it files the saved under the verse the whole community lives by: what you were asked about, you were given; what you were not thanked for, was taken; and the Garden of Bliss is the naʿīm of the Guest of God, not the spoiling of the mutraf — the same softness twice, once owned and lost, once received and eternal.

**"Then such a person will have…" — The Sūrah's Third-Person Pity and Honor**

The pronoun is the last the chapter will use for the dying: "such a person" — the sūrah, having addressed the whole room in the second person through 87 ("you are looking on… if you are not subject to Our Will… bring it back"), now returns to the third person, and the shift has a double edge worth marking: the near-rank die in the middle of an address — the sermon turns from the watchers to the man, and the man is no longer spoken to, only about, by the room — and the sūrah keeps even its honors in that oblique case, because al-Wāqiʿah's eschatology is, formally, reportage: what is said of a person after they can no longer hear it said, said well, in the past-tense conditional (88) and the future-of-the-noun (89), is the sūrah's entire theory of the last words. The commentators who read this line at the dying — the bedside practice traced above, in the lore recorded at 23, 26, 83, and 84 — are, by the sūrah's own grammar, doing what the sermon does at 88–89: speaking over, not to, and letting the three nouns be the whole of what the room may affirm. There is no verse in this chapter that consoles a mourner more precisely, and none that a sūrah could more honestly place at the end of a sermon about ownership: at the throat, the dying are, at last, only what was said about them, and al-Wāqiʿah makes sure the sentence begins "rest."

**The Line That Completes the Chapter's Second Beginning**

Finally, 89 is the apodosis of the hinge — and its place answers the doxology of 74: the sūrah closed its argument-half by commanding "glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest," and its long Book-and-death sequence (75–96) now shows, at 89, why that command stood exactly there: the God whose Name is glorified is the One who, at the dying of the nearest, gives rest, scent, and garden — praise (74) was the argument's verdict, and welcome (89) is the praise's referent, the thing in God that the glorification was all along about: the chapter's Greatest (74, 96) is Greatest in that He is Fa-riḥ, the Giver of ease, and the sūrah's three final branches (89, 91, 93–94) are, from first to last, degrees of welcome: three words for the nearest, a greeting for the right, and a sentence of accommodation for the deniers — and this commentary's last observation on 89 will be the shape of that ladder: al-Wāqiʿah ends not with a thunderbolt but with a diminuendo of nearings, each class arriving at the nearness it chose (85), and the dying man in the room, whoever reads over him, is being handed, at 89, the sūrah's best-case description of what his own throat means: that it is a door with scent behind it, for those who spent their lives being brought near by the nearest One.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:90

> And if the deceased is one of the people of the right,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Middle Branch of the Dying**

"Fa-ammā in kāna min aṣḥāb al-yamīn" — the second of the chapter's three bedside clauses (88, 90, 92) — reopens the census the sūrah took in its opening third ("and you will become three classes… the people of the right — what are the people of the right?" — 7–9, in the chapter's own repeated formula), and the repetition of the census-formula is the device by which the final third claims to be the first third, spoken at a nearer microphone: what the chapter narrated cosmically at 7–40 it now whispers individually at 88–96, and the middle class, largest of the saved, is where its hearers hoped to be listed. The verse supplies nothing new about the people of the right — no gardens, spouses, or cushions (the panorama's furniture at 28–34) — and the restraint is the point: at the throat, the saved are not re-described; they are re-counted, and the sūrah, which opened its Garden-material at 12 with "in the Gardens of Bliss" for the fore-runners and gave the right their whole paragraph only later, now inverts that generosity — the branch of the middle is nearly all hinge and no content — until verse 91 hands them a single greeting, which is, in this chapter's arithmetic of nearness (traced at 88 and 89), the correct dose: the nearer the class, the shorter the sentence, and the people of the right, not the nearest, receive exactly one line and a welcome inside it.

**"People of the Right": A Name the Chapter Spends Its Whole Body Earning**

The present translation's phrase (aṣḥāb al-yamīn, literally "companions of the right-hand") is the sūrah's most quietly argumentative title, and 90's quietness lets it be heard plainly: in the audience's own omens, the right hand was the hand of the auspicious register and the left the hand of the book of doom — and al-Wāqiʿah has, since 7, called its saved by nothing but that omen, insisting that the sign everyone trusted at their ledgers is, on the Day, the only one God honors: the right side, taken out of superstition and made a census category, with the left (92) as its twin, and the whole chapter's argument (57–74) as the proof that the omens were right about the only thing that mattered. The commentators of the sūrahs note that the Book elsewhere calls them "the people of the right" in scenes of books handed out (the Haqqah's and Inshirāḥ's accounts of the right-hand scroll, plain paraphrase), and that this sūrah — uniquely, as this commentary observed at 78 — omits the scroll-drama entirely; at 90 the name alone does the work, because the sūrah's middle has already handed every Makkān hearer the test their own title encodes: you trusted the right hand all your lives, in every contract, in every omen, at every meal — this is the only right hand you were ever signing for.

**"One of": The Partitive Repeated, the Mercy Insisted**

Verse 90 borrows the grammar of 88 — "one of the people of the right" — and the repetition at the two saved branches is itself a datum: the sūrah will not, anywhere in its final third, say "he is from the people of the right" in the indicative, only "if he was one of," and its commentators of the schools of conduct read the conditional as both pastoral warning (no one may die presuming) and pastoral permission (no one need despair), with the "min" softening even the assertion it makes: membership, not perfection, is the class's criterion — a reading this commentary anchored at 88's partitive and now finds confirmed by the middle branch's total silence about works: the panorama (28–34) mentioned the saved "because of what they used to do" (in the idiom of 24 and 34's "reward for what they used to do"), but at the dying the sūrah lists no deeds at all, and the reason is the sermon's last pastoral decision: at the throat, the saved are addressed as guests, not creditors, and a guest's identity — which house he belongs to — needs no résumé, which is how verse 91 can get away with its single word.

**The Verse That Waits, Half a Clause, for 91**

Structurally 90 has no predicate of its own that is not the next verse's — the apodosis is a greeting spoken at 91 — and the sūrah's composition here is unusually exposed: the class's fate is delivered by another party's speech, the saved's eternal sentence, at this chapter's end, is literally someone else's welcome, and the people of the right, who received in the panorama the chapter's longest inventory of what they had earned, receive at the bedside nothing they say: they are spoken to, and the only active verb in their salvation, in these final verses, belongs to the welcomers. The theological elegance of that arrangement, read against the chapter's own argument about ownership (57–74), is exact: the sūrah's whole middle demonstrated that its audience made nothing — not the fluid, the crop, the rain, or the fire — and its ending gives even the saved's joy to another mouth, so that no class in the book, from the near-rank's wordless three nouns (89) to the right's one greeting (91), is represented at the last as self-sufficient; the left, meanwhile, get their own sentence's grammar: "their accommodation will be boiling water" (93) — passive hospitality, served by the same hands — and the three branches, in their syntax, all say one thing: at the dying nobody is the host but the Lord of all worlds (80).

**The Largest Company, the Shortest Sentence**

One closing reckoning: the sūrah's first census said the multitudes were on the right's side ("a multitude from earlier generations and a multitude from later generations," 39–40, where the chapter itself took the right-hand census, against the near-rank's "a multitude from earlier generations and a few from later" at 13–14) — and the final third gives that majority a single verse and a single line of speech. The disproportion is al-Wāqiʿah's last joke on human organizations, which grade honor by length of citation: the many are saved by a word, the few by three nouns, and the deniers by two verses of drink and fire, and the sūrah, recited in rooms where the saved will outnumber everyone, says plainly that the Day is not a performance review — the greeting at 91 is the whole reward the middle class is given to hear, and every mourner who has stood at a deathbed knows the verse is true in the way sermons rarely are: what matters at the end is not what was said about the dead, how long, or with what honors, but that someone with authority says to them, at the moment they can no longer pretend, "welcome — you belong to us," and the sūrah, at 90–91, tells its market-town that the only such sentence that counts is the one that comes from the people God has already named, from the right side of every ledger they ever kept.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:91

> then ˹they will be told,˺ “Greetings to you from the people of the right.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Whole Reward, in One Sentence of Speech**

The apodosis for the great multitude is nine words in Arabic and, in the present translation, a bracketed ˹they will be told˺ plus a greeting — "Greetings to you from the people of the right" — and the sūrah, which furnished the right in its panorama with lote-trees, thornless sidr, shade, water, and every fruit (27–33, the passage this commentary walked in order), now tells them, at the dying, nothing but that a welcome is in transit, and the welcome comes from their own company. The brackets matter for the grammar: the sūrah's own text says "fa-salāmul-lakum" — peace/greetings to you — without naming the speaker, and the present translation's ˹they will be told˺ supplies the passive that Arabic conceals, which the sūrahs' readers mostly filled with the angels ("the angels will enter upon them from every gate, 'peace upon you'"), while others took the verse's own phrase — "from the people of the right" — literally: the saved greeting the saved, mutual welcome at the throat, and the commentary records both without choosing, because the verse's design tolerates either and the second is the sūrah's plain surface.

**"From the People of the Right": Salvation as Being Greeted by One's Own**

The genitive "from the people of the right" makes the greeting's sender the same class as its receiver — the saved welcome the saved — and that circularity is al-Wāqiʿah's quietest doctrine of the afterlife: the middle company's reward is to be recognized, at the last moment, by the community they spent their lives being; the sūrah's whole census-rhetoric (8, 27, 39, 90) was building toward the moment a person hears their own class's voice say "to you," and there is no divine speech-act at 91 at all (unlike the near-rank's wordless three nouns at 89 and the left's impersonal accommodation at 93), only the company — which is to say, in this chapter's anthropology of rizq and gratitude, that for the many-saved the final gift is the fellowship they were given the whole way, returned to them as a greeting. Commentators who noticed the absence of God's direct address at 91 explained it from the chapter's own economy: the near-rank were brought near to the Giver ("to Us," 88), the people of the right were kept in the company of the given, and the two modes of welcome — nearness and fellowship — correspond, in the sūrah's scale, to the two lives it knows: the rare, burning, few (13–14) and the wide, ordinary, many (39–40), each ending at its own register of joy.

**"Salām": The Greeting the Chapter Has Already Made Its Soundscape**

Al-Wāqiʿah's only other appearance of the greeting-word salām was at the edge of its Garden-silence: "there they will never hear idle or sinful talk" — and the exception the chapter supplies, a saying of salām, the greeting of peace, doubled, which the present translation renders as ˹only good and virtuous speech˺ (25–26, weighed in full at this commentary's verses 25 and 26). Verse 91's "Greetings" (salāmul-lakum) is that soundscape arriving at its date: the near-rank's estate was delimited by a noise-policy, and here, at the throat, the salām the saved will spend eternity exchanging is spoken for the first time to the specific dying man by the specific company he kept — the greeting at 91 is the same currency as at 26, and the sūrah's eschatology of speech (no laghv, no ta'thīm, only welcome) completes itself, in these two lines, as a promise that the last word over you will be a wish, not a report, and the wish will come from people whose number is the sūrah's own proof of God's patience: the great multitude from earlier and later generations, saved, all of them, by a life that was mostly the keeping of one another company, and by this one word that they now, finally, say to you.

**The Verse That Reads Like a Bedside, Because It Is One**

The reception-history this commentary has tracked — al-Wāqiʿah recited at the dying, its near-rank passage (83–89) read over the throat, the practice-lore at verses 23, 26, 83, 84, and 88 — reaches, at 91, its most literal form: the mourner reciting "fa-salāmul-lakum min aṣḥābi l-yamīn" over a body is performing the verse's own grammar, a company speaking to one of theirs at the moment his soul reaches the throat (83), and the sūrah's choice to make the saved class's final apodosis a greeting rather than a description is what makes the chapter recitable in that way: verses of reward describe; this verse speaks, and a community at a deathbed can speak it. The bracketed ˹they will be told˺ — the translators' honest small addition — keeps even the speech-direction at the surface, and the commentary notes that the later readers of this verse preserved it in their etiquette-literature precisely as the bedside-word: the greeting one wants said over the dead, said now, by the living, as rehearsal. Al-Wāqiʿah is, in that practice, not a chapter about death's verdicts but death's welcome, and 91 is its hinge: the last sentence the saved will ever hear from the sūrah is a sentence they can hand to each other.

**What 91 Withholds, and Why That Is the Praise**

One final negative observation belongs to the entry: the verse promises nothing — no garden re-listed, no spouses, no fruits — and that withholding, at the chapter's end, is not a lacuna but a judgment about the value of the greeting: having described Paradise's furniture for its panorama, the sūrah decides, for the bedside, that the saved dying one needs to hear only that they belong — that the company exists, that it greets, and that its welcome is the reward in its executable form. The commentary has argued (at 64–74, 77–82) that this sūrah's whole middle is an instruction in receiving — the world as gift, the Book as gift, gratitude as the only rational response — and 91 completes the instruction: the class of the many is received, at the end, by a greeting they neither earned nor arranged, only kept being part of, and the sūrah, whose mockers had "asked mockingly" (47) at their dinners and whose farmers would "lament" (66) in their fields, gives its saved, for their eternal audition, a single sentence whose content is that someone said "to you." Every embellishment the panorama could supply is, at the throat, unnecessary — the sūrah's last word about the saved is not what they will get but who will welcome them — and the doxology that follows two verses later (96) is therefore, in this architecture, not an addition but the only possible response: if the end of the saved is a greeting from the saved, the end of the sermon is "glorify the Name," because the God who arranges welcomes has, at last, nothing left to say about the welcome itself.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:92

> But if such person is one of the misguided deniers,

**Expanded Commentary**

**They Become What They Did: The Verb Named Back at Its Owners**

"Fa-ammā in kāna min al-mukadhdhibīn" — "the deniers-of-lie" — and the participle is the chapter's final receipt: at 82 the audience was charged with making denial (takdhīb) their livelihood — "you repay your provisions with denial" — and at 92, at the throat, the doing has become the name: the one who called the Book a lie is, eternally, "of the deniers," his act fossilized into his class. The sūrah's whole anthropology has been this morphology in motion — you are what you repeatedly do to the given world — and the near-rank branch, by contrast, was named by what was done to them ("the brought-near," 88, passive, divine verb), while the middle branch was named by position ("the people of the right"), and only the lost are named by their own transitive verb, active, unrelieved: the three branches' three nominal forms are the chapter's last, silent sermon on how names are acquired on this side of dying, and the denier's, alone, is self-made.

**"The Misguided Deniers": Two Roots in One Title**

The present translation's "misguided deniers" packs the Arabic's double epithet — al-muḍallalīn, the "made-to-grope-in-error and deny" — the root ḍ-l-l (astray, lost) folded into the root k-dh-b (called a lie), and the combination is the sūrah's judgment on the relation between them: they did not stumble into denial; their error was itself an agency, a misguidance they enacted (the intensive-passive morphology reads, in the sūrahs' glosses, "led on and left to wander"), and their denial was itself an error, a falsehood committed against evidence the chapter has just spent seventeen verses supplying. The verse therefore closes the chapter's long argument with a definition of the audience's condition: from 47's mocking dinner-question to 81's trivializing, the Makkān posture toward the ḥadīth has been described as manner, then commerce, and here at 92, in two fused roots, as epistemology — a man who denied the Book and, in denying, lost the right order of knowing, so that the verdict names not a punishment's recipient but a knowledge's squanderer, which is exactly what a sūrah that kept asking its hearers to "consider," "remember," and "know" (57, 62, 76) must finally call them: those who knew the right hand of their own ledgers and did not know the right hand of God.

**The Left's Last Census, at the Scale of One Man**

Verse 92 repeats — and shrinks — the chapter's first left-hand formula: "the people of the left — what are the people of the left!" (9), and where the opening census shouted the class's name with exclamatory indirection (a device this commentary weighed at 8–9, the sūrah declining to describe the left until it could describe them as a file), the bedside sorting simply says "if such a person is one of the misguided deniers," and the loss of the exclamation is the verse's quietest terror: at the throat the sūrah no longer performs astonishment at the class; the class is now one man, conditionally, anonymous, in the room. The "min" of "one of," which at 88 and 90 carried mercy's partitive softness (membership, not perfection), carries here the plain arithmetic of the census — and readers of the sūrahs' pastoral literature have drawn the obvious conclusion, that the sūrah's final third is built so the reciter at every bedside hears all three branches as possibilities for the person before him and none as certainties, the "if" doing its last work of reverent suspension, while the chapter's whole preceding argument has already, of course, told the room which "if" the denier is fulfilling — and the tension between the conditional's humility and the sermon's confidence is, in this verse, the sound of late Makkān preaching refusing, even at 92, to damn a man it has not heard die.

**The Fire's Function, Reversed**

At 73 the sūrah assigned fire its double office — "a reminder (dhikr) and a provision for the travelers" — and verse 92's class is the sūrah's way of completing that assignment: the misguided deniers are precisely those who met the reminder and made no use of it, who took the provision and paid for it with denial (82), and at 92, at the dying, the reminder simply becomes, for them, the thing remembered: no more warning, only the fact. The commentators of the sūrahs marked the chapter's fire-progress: "the blazing-fire" (al-jaḥīm) as lodging at 48, the column of smoke at 43, and now, in the bedside sorting, fire's absence from 92 itself — the class gets only its name, and the drink and the burning follow at 93–94 — as if the sūrah, at the last, could not bring itself to pile description on the dying of the lost in the room where someone is weeping: the panorama had its terrors, the deathbed keeps them for the next two verses, and 92 itself is nothing but a name and a door, which is the sūrah's most exact sentence about how death-classification actually feels — administrative, sudden, and almost mercifully bare in the moment it happens.

**"If Such a Person" — The Third Time, and the Listener's Turn**

Repeating "fa-ammā in kāna" for the third time (88, 90, 92), the sūrah completes its triptych, and the structural consequence is a summons: every hearer of the chapter, on either side of the recitation, now knows the three doors exhaust the world and that he is standing in the room of one of them, and the sūrah's entire argument-half — which was never about proving an abstract God but about relocating the audience from owners to recipients (57–74) — arrives, at 92, at its pastoral purpose: to make the third branch thinkable for the man in the chair. The mockers of 47 asked, "will we really be resurrected?" and this commentary has argued that the chapter's whole middle answers by demonstration and its late third by diagnosis — here is the diagnosis: the "if" of 92 is the sūrah's last question-form, and it is aimed not at the corpse but at the living, whose lightness at the message (81) was the first symptom of the title at 92, and who hear, in the conditional's bare syntax, that the only thing left to do about the door they may be nearing is what the chapter has, since its first "when," asked of all of Makkah: to stop denying what the room, the field, the throat, and the book have, in its reading, already said — and the sūrah's final two verses (95–96) will speak, in reply, first the truth itself, then the praise.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:93

> then their accommodation will be boiling water ˹to drink˺

**Expanded Commentary**
