**Throat-Work: The Verdict Made Liquid**

The panorama sentenced the left to a menu — Zaqqūm eaten, filling the bellies, and the drink poured on top (52–55) — but the bedside sentencing gives them one thing: ḥamīm, boiling water, with the translators' ˹to drink˺ supplying the act. The shrinkage from food-plus-drink to drink-alone is, at this point in the sūrah, anatomically intentional: verses 83–87 have just defined dying as the passage of a soul through a throat, and verse 93 mirrors the throat as the site of verdict — at the last, the lost are not seated at a table but handed a cup they cannot refuse, the eschatology compressed into the one swallowing everyone has watched fail. The sūrah, which has argued since 68 that water is the element the audience receives daily without thanks, ends by making the final drink the same substance with its blessing removed: no hunger remains at a deathbed, and so no food is offered there — only the drink, only the throat, only what the nearer hands (85) are pouring.

**"Their Accommodation": The Guest-Word at Its Coldest**

The noun of hospitality — this commentary's extended subject at verse 56, where the sūrah called the whole scene the left's "accommodation on the Day of Judgment" — returns at 93 unchanged, and the repetition at the bedside makes the guest-idiom do its final work: in the panorama the word closed a banquet (52–56, eaten, drunk, like thirst-mad camels, "this will be their accommodation"); here the same word serves a single swallow, and the hospitality register — which Makkah held sacred (the guest's right, the poured cup, the host's shame before the tribe) — is spent on a verdict that consists of nothing but the serving: the lost are not abandoned but attended, and the attending is the punishment, which is the point this commentary argued at 56 and 54 and which verse 93 now proves structurally: the sūrah could not let the dying man's fate be described in cruder terms than his host's would have been, because its theology of justice is that God does not lose His manners even when the guest loses his claim — and a reciter who has carried the chapter from 1 here will hear, in "their accommodation will be," the flattest sentence in al-Wāqiʿah, a butler's phrase with a hell inside it.

**The Bracket's Verb: "˹To Drink˺" and the Gift That Is Not Taken**

The translators' ˹to drink˺ marks a verb the Arabic scene has already supplied at 54 ("then upon that, you will drink") and which 93 leaves to the noun — and the small bracket is the key to the verse's theology of shukr: the water of 68–70 was drinkable, sweet, unearned, and had to be received with gratitude to be received at all ("will you not then give thanks?" — 70, argued above at length); the water of 93 is drinkable only in the physical sense, ingested without reception, a rizq that cannot be thanked because it cannot be refused or enjoyed. Al-Wāqiʿah's entire hydrology, from "is it you who bring it down" (69) to this cup, says that the difference between the two waters is not chemistry but the drinker's history — and verse 93, placed one verse after the class was named "misguided deniers" (92), spells the class out as the condition of being handed a gift in a form that registers only as weight, the final drink as the final answer to a life of not-thanking: the sūrah's last hydrological word is that the ungrateful, at the end, still drink, and that is exactly what they made of all the water they were given — a universe in which everything is poured and nothing is received.

**"Min ḥamīm": The Partitive Some Readers Counted Twice**

The line hangs the drink off a preposition (boiling water "of" ḥamīm, the present translation's "will be boiling water" compressing a phrase whose hospitality-noun the sūrahs glossed as the guest's due of arrival — the Ranks sūrah asks, in the same idiom, whether that arrival is better or the tree of Zaqqūm (37:62, plain paraphrase)), and the sūrahs' lexicographers — this commentary records, not adjudicates — weighed whether the "min" here is explicative (the accommodation, which is boiling water) or partitive (a measure of what resembles ḥamīm — the boiling, not the pure thing itself), the second reading softening, the first closing, and the present translation's "boiling water" follows the explicative line, in which the drink is the element itself, not a sample of it [UNVERIFIED: the partitive option appears in the classical apparatus with neither verdict nor consensus; this commentary does not rank the readings]. The note earns its space in a chapter whose whole argument has turned on what a thing is — the fluid that was not made (58–59), the growth that was not grown (63–64), the fire that was assigned its jobs (73) — and if the cup of 93 is ḥamīm "as such," the verse has, in one preposition, completed the sūrah's doctrine of essences: the lost receive things in their bareness, drink without refreshment, shade without cool (44, the sūrah's negated pair), fire without provision, every created good present as a substance and absent as a gift, which is the most exact description this book offers of hell and the least embellished.

**The Repeated Rhyme: 93–94 as 42's Echo**

Finally, the sound: "in ḥamīmin wa-ḥamīm" (42) and the closing line's "min ḥamīm" — the same word, in the same slot at the line's end — and the bedside pair 93–94 rhymes ḥamīm against ṣalīr (94's "burning in Hellfire"), an assonance the panorama's left-passages had already tuned the ear for, so that the chapter's last word on the lost is delivered in the phonetics of its first: the sūrah's structure, read aloud, closes the left in the same sounds it opened them, and the audience at the dying man's side hears, in the rhyme, the whole interval of the chapter compress into a single couplet — accommodation and burning, water and fire, the two elements between which the left have been circling since verse 42 — and the sūrah's final device is that the verdict is not argued in these last lines but rhymed, because at the throat, al-Wāqiʿah has decided, the only arguments that carry are the ones the heart already knows by heart, and the chapter has made its hearers know even hell by heart, by sound, before it permits them to hear it named one last time, one verse short of its end.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:94

> and burning in Hellfire.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Words for the End of the Left**

"Wa-ṣalīra l-jaḥīm" — and burning in the Fire — the left's final line in this sūrah is two words, and after the panorama's fourteen verses of scorching heat, smoke-shade, Zaqqūm, filling bellies, and camel-thirst (42–55, walked in order by this commentary), the bedside sentencing closes their case with a drink and a burn (93–94) — five words for a doctrine of hell. The disproportion is al-Wāqiʿah's settled rhetoric — the chapter elaborated mercy in its cosmic scenes and summarized judgment there, and at the throat it shortens both further, the near-rank given three nouns (89), the right one greeting (91), the left two lines — and the final brevity is not fatigue but form: a sermon read at dying rooms learns that the last sentences must be the shortest, and the sūrah, which has been shaped by bedsides for fourteen centuries, compresses its verdict to the two things everyone at a death already believes: everyone drinks the last cup, and everyone passes into whatever the life has been warming. The burning is not described; it is announced, with the flatness the chapter reserves, at its last, for facts no one in the room doubts until they need to.

**Ṣalīr: The Noun of Roasting, the Chapter's One Occurrence**

The word "burning" (ṣalīr) belongs, in the Arabic's idiom, to the roasting of what is exposed — the sūrahs' lexica gloss it as the blazing fire's heat upon the surface, the searing, and it occurs in this chapter only here, in the present translation's rendering (73's bracketed "of the Hellfire" being supplied for the reminder-verse, as noted above). Its rarity is worth the note: the Book's commonst nouns for the abode — the "Jahannam" of the Medinan detail-passages and much of the Meccan, the "blazing" of the neighbors — are, in al-Wāqiʿah, absent or bracketed, and the chapter reaches its final word about the lost with a rare, searing concrete noun: not a place-name but a happening, "a burning," the sūrah's last grammatical judgment that hell, in this book's telling, is an event the self is inside of, not a geography one travels to. Commentators of the sūrahs, who counted such things, read the choice against the near-rank's three nouns — welcome, scent, garden, two states and a place — and found the left's two: a drink and a state, no place at all: "burning in," with the "in" hanging loose, the sūrah declining even to let the lost own a habitation, which is the whole poetics of verse 94 in one preposition's ellipsis.

**The Final Possessive: "In Hellfire," the Fire Owned by Its Name**

The construct — burning of the blazing-fire, ṣalīra l-jaḥīm — closes the chapter's long use of its fire-vocabulary (the kindled hearth of 71, the reminder-fire of 73, the column of smoke of 43) in a single iḍāfa, an attributive link, and the sūrah's last three noun-phrases are, in this translation, all iḍāfāt: "a Garden of Bliss" (89), "burning in Hellfire" (94), "the Name of your Lord, the Greatest" (96). That is the chapter's final theology in its syntax: al-Wāqiʿah ends by stacking possessions — the saved own a garden qualified by bliss, the lost a burning qualified by the Fire, and between them the Prophet is told to glorify a Name qualified by his Lord's greatest — and the sūrah's entire argument, that nothing in the world belongs to those who use it, resolves, grammatically, into a world where everything belongs: everything has its possessor, and the possessors are names of God, and the only fitting human act at the end of sorting is doxology. No commentary should pass verse 94 without noting that its four words place the reader between two genitives and one command, the lost "in" the burning as the saved are "in" the garden: the chapter's last word about both is not duration but possession, and the sūrah, whose middle argued that its audience owned every process wrongly, ends by telling the whole room whose the fire is — and whose, in fact, the praise.

**"And Burning": The Conjunction That Refuses Escalation**

The wa — "and" — that joins the drink to the burning is the sūrah's last refusal to dramatize: no "then," no "furthermore," no scene, no dialogue of the damned (the chapters of the Fire and of the Ranks stage their beggings in full report; al-Wāqiʿah, whose left already spoke at the census — 47–48's "will we really be resurrected?" — never lets them speak again after that), just the cup and the burning, conjoined like the two courses of that bitter accommodation (54's drink "on top of" the meal, at this commentary's verse). The conjunction reads, in context, as the sūrah's settled judgment that the lost require no more words than they have had: the panorama said all there is to say, the deathbed re-said it at human scale, and 94's flat "and" is the end of the chapter's willingness to spend language on the subject — the reciter who has watched the sermon's long pity for the dying realize itself in these last clauses should hear, in the wa, something like the sūrah's own exhaustion of the theme, and its decision (which is also a mercy to the mourners in the room) to stop describing and say what is. Al-Wāqiʿah's restraint at 94 is not severity's coyness; it is preaching's respect for the scene it stands inside, in which the corpse does not need, and the family cannot bear, more than four words about the fire.

**The Verse Before the Truth-Verse**

Finally, 94's position: one verse from here the sūrah will declare "this is the absolute truth" (95), and readers across the centuries have asked what "this" refers back to — the whole sorting, the whole chapter, or, most immediately, this grim pair of drink and burning. The commentary will weigh the demonstrative at 95; what matters at 94 is what makes the question possible: the verse's very flatness, its refusal of elaboration, gives the sūrah's final asseveration something to certify — a sentence like "and burning in Hellfire," unexplained, unornamented, unargued, is the kind of sentence a book either guesses at or swears, and al-Wāqiʿah, which swore at its hinge by the stars' positions (75–76) that its message was noble, now, at its end, points at the last and least palatable thing it has said, and calls it the truth of certainty (95) — which is the sūrah's ultimate claim about speech: that its hardest line, the one a mourner resents at a bedside, is the one the chapter will not qualify away, and that a book whose consolation needs no lies deserves, at its close, to be called true — not sweet, not plausible, but true, with the kind of truth that leaves only glorification for the hearer (96), which is exactly the verse that follows, and why the sūrah's final third ends, as its middle paused, with the Prophet told to praise.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:95

> Indeed, this is the absolute truth.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This": The Demonstrative That Certifies the Whole Chapter**

The present translation's "this" (hādhā) is, at 95, the most contested finger in the sūrah — pointing back at the two verses of burning and drink, at the whole three-branch sorting, or at the entire chapter from the Event's first fall — and the sūrahs' exegetes preserved the range without closing it, most settling on the broadest: the sūrah ends its verdicts by swearing that everything just said is true, the near-rank's welcome and the left's cup alike. The formula has an exact twin elsewhere in the Book: "And indeed, this ˹Quran˺ is the absolute truth" (69:51, the Haqqah's line, in the present translation's wording) — a late-Makkān sūrah that also opens with its Day's name (al-Ḥāqqah, "The Reality") and also ends its description by certifying the Book itself, and the pair shows what 95 does: the demonstrative at the end of al-Wāqiʿah's last verdict behaves exactly as the Haqqah's behaves at its close — the chapter about the Day says, of itself, this is the truth — which is to say that in this register of sūrah, the final sentence about eschatology is always also the final sentence about the book that speaks it, and no reader should be surprised that the chapter whose hinge-oath swore the recitation noble (75–77) ends by calling its contents the truth that certainty deserves.

**Ḥaqq al-Yaqīn: The Name of a Knowledge-Grade**

The phrase — truth of certainty, ḥaqq al-yaqīn — is a genitive of kind, and the sūrah's audience had heard its family before: "if you had known with knowing-certainty (bi-ʿilmi l-yaqīn)," the Rivalry chapter warns, "you would have seen the blazing-fire, then you would have seen it with the eye of certainty (ʿayni l-yaqīn)" (102:5–7, the sūrahs' plain account), and the tradition of this vocabulary grades knowledge into levels — know it, see it, be there at it — which is the ladder the commentary has been tracing in this chapter since its certainty-lam at 49–50, "most certainly… will surely be gathered," where certainty first entered as God's own register. Verse 95 names, at the chapter's end, the summit: not knowledge-about nor sight-of but the truth of it, ḥaqq — the mode in which the real is possessed, not approached — and the sūrah's choice to close its sorting with an epistemology-noun is the last gift of a sermon that has argued, since verse 57, that its audience's error was cognitive before it was moral: they did not consider (the four a-raʾaytum), did not believe, did not remember, did not thank — and now, after everything, the chapter does not shout its verdict; it rates it, and says this is the kind of true that dying makes plain, which is exactly the sentence a mourner at a bedside can verify and no debater in a market ever could.

**The Indicative After the Conditionals: How 95 Ends the Triptych**

Structurally, the last ten verses run on "if" — "if he was of the brought-near" (88), "if of the people of the right" (90), "if of the misguided deniers" (92) — and 95 breaks the grammar with a bare assertion: "this is the truth," no condition, no suspended verdict, the indicative mood returning to a chapter that began in the indicative ("when the Event befalls, its falsity no soul will deny," 1–2, in the plain sense of the opening lines' present translation). The movement is the sūrah's final lesson in modality: human knowledge sorts with "if," divine speech states with "is," and the whole late-Makkān device of the conditional triptych exists to make the hearer live, for three branches, in the "if" that his own position really occupies — the dying man's class is certain above and conditional below — so that verse 95's indicative lands as the lifting of a lid: the sūrah has said what it can say about the sorting (all of it, conditionally), and now says, flatly, what the sorting is: true, and true in the high grade. That is also why 96's command can follow without argument — an "if"-chapter ends in praise, not proof, because the truth-grade of the news, at 95, has transferred the case from the courtroom to the liturgy, where the sūrah's argument-half (57–74) had already aimed it, at the first doxology's hinge (74, in this commentary's long account of the pair of praises that frames the final third).

**The Sūrah's Self-Referral, Read Against Its Bedside History**

One reception-note closes the verse: this sūrah's last great practical claim has always been its recitation at the dying (the lore this commentary has recorded at 23, 26, 83, 84, 88, and 91), and verse 95 is what makes that practice tolerable in a house of grief, because the sūrah's final words over a death are not "such was his fate" but "this is the truth" — a certification of the whole account in which the welcome of 89 and 91 is included in the price of the statement, so that whoever recites to the dying is, at 95, staking the sūrah's truth on the consolation it offers and not only on the warning, the two being one sentence in this chapter's design. The classical readers of the verse's taʿlīq — "this," they said, "is the certainty no doubt survives" — kept the line in their etiquette-books precisely for the rooms where consolation risks sounding like rhetoric, and a modern commentary, which has watched this chapter build its epistemology of seeing (84–85, "you look on… you cannot see"), should mark the arc complete: from the room that sees too little to the sentence that says too much, the sūrah has kept one promise — it never asked anyone to believe a thing it was willing to leave uncertified — and 95, four words of nominal clause at the edge of the last, is that promise's keeping, the chapter's final "is," spoken into a room whose every verb had, for ninety-four verses, been God's.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:96

> So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Repeat, and What the 22 Verses Between Did**

Verse 96 is verse 74 — "So glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest" — the present translation prints the two lines identically, and the sūrah's whole design turns on what the interval did to them: at 74 the command closed an argument about the world's making (57–73), and the hearer praised what he had been shown — seed, rain, fire, all of it unowned; at 96 the command closes the bedside sorting (88–95), and the same words now fall on a hearer who has been walked through the Book's four predicates (77–80), his own triviality indicted (81–82), a soul at its throat (83–87), and three final doors (88–94) — the second doxology is the first one, priced. This is the sūrah's last lesson in the difference between the two acts of praise the chapter has trained: you can glorify the Maker of crops, which the argument-half could reach, or glorify the Lord whose name is Greatest because He is the one whose welcome at 89 and greeting at 91 you intend to arrive inside — and al-Wāqiʿah, which has been educating its audience's vocabulary all along (the rayḥān re-weighted at 18–19 and 89, the shade at 30 and 43, the certainty-lam at 50, 52, 54, and 95), ends by handing the reciter back the sentence he heard at the hinge and letting him find, in it, all the chapter between.

**Why the Chapter Ends in Praise and Not in Verdict**

The commentator must notice what verse 96 declines: no final punishment-sentence, no summary curse on the deniers, no "they will remain therein forever" — the last thing the sūrah says about the lost is four words at 94, the last about the saved is a welcome, and the last thing it says at all is addressed to the Prophet: glorify. Late-Makkān sūrahs of the court-room type customarily close their files on the defendants; al-Wāqiʿah closes its file on its own hearers' standing — the doxology is the audience's exit, not the enemies' — which completes the structure this commentary has traced since 74: the chapter brackets its final third with identical praises, and everything inside them — oath, purity, trade-indictment, throat, three classes, truth-certification — is contained within an act of worship, a literary embodiment of the sūrah's own theology that the world is not, finally, a courtroom but a rite, and that God's greatest claim on His creatures (al-Aʿẓam, the superlative that ends the chapter) is the Name they glorify, not the verdict they fear. This is why the last word is an attribute, not a sentence: the sūrah ends pointed up, and the mourner who has read 83–89 with the chapter arrives, at 96, at praise with tears still legal in it, which is the only ending al-Wāqiʿah's own eschatology of welcome could license.

**"The Greatest": The Superlative's Final Audit**

The chapter's ʿ-a-ẓ-m thread — the ˹great˺ loss of the keening farmers (66), the great oath at the hinge (76), the Greatest of both doxologies (74, 96) — closes here, and its audit is exact: in al-Wāqiʿah's economy, the word "great" was earned, before it was bestowed: the loss the farmers suffer is great, the oath God swears is great, and the Name alone is Greatest, the sūrah's adjectives sorted by their owners, human grief keeping the human scale and the divine name taking the superlative that nothing in the created list can share — an arrangement that answers, at the chapter's very last line, the mockers' whole register of magnitude (47's "when we are dust, will we really be raised?" is a complaint that God is being too small about resurrection; the sūrah's final word is that He is being too great for the complaint to have a subject). And the sūrah's own subject, at 96, is "your Lord" once more: the rabb which the four considerations spent their verses claiming for God (57–73) is, at the end, personalized to the Prophet and, through him, to every reciter — "your" Lord — which is the smallest, largest word in the last verse, and the sūrah's final claim: that the greatest God of the stars' positions and the soul's throat is, in the sentence that ends His chapter, addressed with a possessive pronoun that belongs to a man.

**The Weak Tradition and the Beloved Practice**

One reception-note, honestly marked: the report "whoever recites Sūrat al-Wāqiʿah every night, poverty will never afflict him" — transmitted in Bayhaqī's Shuʿab al-Īmān from Ibn Masʿūd and graded weak in the modern collections' terms (this commentary recorded the grading at 23 and 26, where the tradition's full apparatus stands) — has attached itself to precisely this doxology-ending chapter, and the fit is too good to pass a commentator by: the sūrah whose entire middle is a treatise on rizq (63–73, at this commentary's full length), whose farmers keened about deprivation (66–67) and whose traders were indicted for making denial their livelihood (82), is, in its community's practice, the night-recitation against poverty — the weak chain carrying, nonetheless, a true reading: the umma heard this chapter's subject correctly, and the beloved if unproven tradition is, in that sense, al-Wāqiʿah's most faithful midrash, its hearers treating the sūrah as what its own verses treat it: God's book about being fed, being kept, and being welcomed, recited nightly by people who did not want to be deprived — with this caveat the commentary stands, recording practice without certifying the report, since the chapter itself (at 81–82) warns loudest against exactly the commerce of reward into which such lore is always in danger of calcifying, and its final verse, at 96, offers the purer exchange: recite, and glorify — the livelihood of that transaction being the only rizq this sūrah never once indicts.

**The End, and the Next Beginning**

Finally — the canon's quiet grace: in the received sequence this chapter's last word, "the Greatest," stands immediately before the opening of Sūrah al-Ḥadīd: "Whatever is in the heavens and the earth glorifies Allah, for He is the Almighty, All-Wise" (57:1, the present translation verbatim) — the imperative al-Wāqiʿah gave the Prophet becoming, in the next sūrah's first word, the indicative of all creation: you are told to glorify; the heavens and the earth already do. Whether or not the ʿUthmānic order was arranged with that effect in mind — the sūrahs' ʿulūm al-Qurʾān debate the arrangement's status, and this commentary marks the point [UNVERIFIED: the intentionality of the adjacency] — the effect on the reader is real and is the right place to end this sūrah's verse-by-verse account: al-Wāqiʿah began with a single event no soul could disbelieve (1–2), spent its middle proving the audience owns none of the world's processes, spent its hinge certifying the Book that proves it, spent its end at one throat and through three doors, and finishes by telling one man to glorify — so that the book can continue, in the chapter after it, with the cosmos glorifying on his behalf, which is, in the end, this sūrah's whole doctrine of the Event: the Greatest Name needs no one's praise and is, at every throat and in every field, being praised anyway — and the only question the chapter has ever actually asked is whether you intend to join the recitation you are walking into: consider, it said (57), believe, remember, thank — and so, at the last: glorify the Name of your Lord, the Greatest.
